# Philosophy of Mind · Reference Card

> Open book. This card is meant to be open while you work — including during
> quizzes. Nothing here is something you're expected to recall cold.

The course asks how minds fit a world physics describes: whether experience, aboutness and
self-knowledge are physical, how a thought could cause a movement, what fixes the content of a
belief or a perception, whether the soul is the body's form, and whether a machine could think or
feel. It takes no side. Every position is stated as its defenders state it, with the premise its
critics attack, and no entry records a verdict. Use the card mid-problem to find what a word means
([Terms](#terms)), a position and its traps ([Positions and distinctions](#positions-and-distinctions)),
a formal statement with its symbols defined ([Formal definitions](#formal-definitions)), an
argument's premises in outline ([Arguments](#arguments)), or who said what and when
([Thinkers and texts](#thinkers-and-texts)). Topics another course owns are listed with their
owners in [Assumed, not taught here](#assumed-not-taught-here), and the recurring traps are in
[Pitfalls](#pitfalls).

## Terms

Technical vocabulary in first-appearance order, glossed as the lessons gloss it. Terms with a full
entry below link to it. *First used* is the first lesson that uses the term in this sense; titles,
forward pointers and passing uses in another sense don't count. Letters get reused ($P$ is the
complete physical truth in 1.4 and a family of physical properties in 2.3; D, M, C and E are 1.1's
labels for the triad), so read a letter with its lesson.

| Term | Means | First used |
|---|---|---|
| [marks of the mental](#marks-of-the-mental) | intentionality, phenomenal consciousness and first-person access: the three features that make the mental look unlike anything else in nature; they come apart | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [intentionality](#intentionality) | aboutness: a mental state's being directed at something, even something that does not exist (Atlantis) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| phenomenal consciousness, [what it is like](#what-it-is-like) | there being something it is like to be in a state; [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) sets it against [access consciousness](#phenomenal-and-access-consciousness) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [first-person access](#first-person-access), privileged access | knowing your own current mental states without evidence, inference or empirical investigation, with an authority others defer to; not infallibility | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad) | D, M and C, which cannot all be true once E is added; without E the three are consistent | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| D (Distinctness) | mental properties (and, on some views, their bearers) are neither identical with physical properties nor fixed by them | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| M (Mental causation) | some mental events cause physical events | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| C ([causal closure](#causal-closure)) | every physical event that has a cause has a sufficient physical cause | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| E (No overdetermination) | no physical event regularly has, besides its sufficient physical cause, a distinct further cause | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| overdetermination | an effect with two distinct sufficient causes, like a prisoner shot by two marksmen at once | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| fixed by, nothing over and above | necessitated by: any physical duplicate with nothing further added is a mental duplicate | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [physicalism](#physicalism) | everything is physical, or fixed by the physical | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [substance dualism](#substance-dualism) | you are, or have, a non-physical subject distinct from any body | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [property dualism](#property-dualism) | the things that think are physical, but some of their properties, typically the felt ones, are not | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [epiphenomenalism](#epiphenomenalism), epiphenomenal | mental properties are caused by brain events but cause nothing physical; a thesis about causation, not the same as property dualism | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [reductive physicalism](#reductive-physicalism) | each type of mental state is identical with a type of brain state (the identity theory of [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [type identity](#type-and-token-identity) | each mental kind is a physical kind | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [token identity](#type-and-token-identity) | each particular mental event is some physical event; nonreductive physicalists accept it and deny type identity | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [nonreductive physicalism](#nonreductive-physicalism) | mental properties are identical with no single physical type but are fixed by, nothing over and above, physical ones | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [realizer](#role-and-realizer), realized by | the physical state that plays a mental role in a given system ([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) also calls it the occupant) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [eliminative materialism](#eliminative-materialism) | folk psychology is a false theory, so there are no beliefs or desires (Paul Churchland 1981) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [folk psychology](#folk-psychology) | everyday explanation and prediction of behaviour by beliefs and desires | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [idealism](#idealism) | physical things are constructions out of minds and their experiences | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [neutral monism](#neutral-monism) | mind and matter are both built from a stuff that is neither mental nor material (Mach, James, Russell) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [systematic overdetermination](#systematic-overdetermination) | mental and physical causes each suffice for the same bodily effects, every time: the exit that denies E (Mills 1996) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [Hempel's dilemma](#hempels-dilemma) | if "physical" means current physics, physicalism is probably false; if it means ideal future physics, it is near empty | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [via negativa](#via-negativa) | defining "physical" negatively, as not fundamentally mental | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [Russellian monism](#russellian-monism) | physics describes only structure and dispositions; the intrinsic natures that ground them are experiential or proto-experiential | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| Russellian physicalism | Russellian monism under a reading of "physical" that includes the natures physics leaves out, so D is false | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [hylomorphism](#hylomorphism) | the soul is the form of a living body; one substance, and the human being thinks, through the soul | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| form, [soul](#soul-as-form) | in the hylomorphic sense, the principle by which matter is one thing of a kind, with that kind's powers; a living body's form is its soul | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [formal cause, efficient cause](#formal-and-efficient-causation) | what makes a thing or event what it is, versus what produces it; on the formal-cause reply the soul is the first, not a push beside neural causes | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| motive power | the power by which Aquinas's soul also moves the body (ST I q.76 a.4 ad 2); [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md) separates the appetite that commands from the power in the members that executes | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| [hylomorphic dualism](#hylomorphic-dualism) | the *Stanford Encyclopedia*'s name for the Thomist view, given intellect acts through no bodily organ | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) |
| subject of experience | the one who has the experiences | [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md) |
| [unity argument](#unity-argument) | from experiences had together (seeing red while hearing a whistle) to a subject who is not a heap of parts | [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md) |
| emergent individual | on [emergent dualism](#emergent-dualism), a new individual a suitably organized brain generates, as a magnet generates its field, with causal powers of its own | [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md) |
| [bundle view](#bundle-view) | no subject over and above the experiences, only experiences related to one another | [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md) |
| co-consciousness | on the bundle view, the relation between experiences had together, doing the work a subject would do | [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md) |
| further fact | on the [simple view](#simple-view) of personal identity, a fact about who survives over and above bodily and psychological continuity | [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md) |
| causally isolated system | a system nothing outside acts on; the reply to the [conservation objection](#conservation-objection) says conservation holds only for these (Larmer 1986) | [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md) |
| individualistic power | a power to affect one particular object and no intrinsically identical one, as a key opens this lock; a reply to the [pairing problem](#pairing-problem) (Foster, Unger) | [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md) |
| content | what a mental state represents, the way it represents the world as being | [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md) |
| complete achromat | a person with total colour blindness (Knut Nordby, a colour-vision scientist) | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md) |
| [knowing that](#knowing-that-knowing-how-and-acquaintance) | knowledge of a fact, which telling can pass on | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md) |
| [knowing how](#knowing-that-knowing-how-and-acquaintance) | knowledge as an ability | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md) |
| [knowing by acquaintance](#knowing-that-knowing-how-and-acquaintance) | direct familiarity with a thing or quality, as you know a city you have lived in | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md) |
| [qualia](#qualia) | felt qualities of experience; what it is like | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md) |
| phenomenal concept | a concept of a kind of experience that one can have only by having had the experience; on the [phenomenal-concept strategy](#phenomenal-concept-strategy), a concept of a physical property ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md): the "this" used in thinking of an experience by how it feels) | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md) |
| physical concept | a concept stated in the vocabulary of the physical, functional and psychological sciences, with no phenomenal terms | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md) |
| [old fact, new mode](#old-fact-new-mode) | Mary learns a fact she knew under a physical concept, now under a phenomenal one; no a priori reasoning links the two concepts, a mode of presentation being the way a fact is thought of | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md) |
| [hypothesis of phenomenal information](#hypothesis-of-phenomenal-information) | Lewis's label for the view that experience rules out possibilities physical information leaves open | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md) |
| zombie | a physical duplicate, of a person or a whole world, with no experience; the [zombie argument](#zombie-argument)'s case | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| $P$, $Q$ | $P$ is the complete physical truth, every microphysical fact and law; $Q$ is any truth about experience | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| [ideal conceivability](#conceivability-and-possibility) | conceivability that survives ideal rational reflection, as against prima facie | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| positive, negative conceivability | being able to imagine a situation that verifies the claim / being unable to rule it out a priori | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| primary, secondary (conceivability and possibility) | conceivable as how the actual world is, and verified by some world considered as actual / satisfied by some world considered as counterfactual, given the actual referents | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| [primary intension, secondary intension](#two-dimensional-semantics) | what a term picks out at a world considered as actual / at a world considered as counterfactual, given its actual referent | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| [rigid designator](#rigid-designation) | a term that picks out the same thing at every world where it exists | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| contingent identity | Place's and Smart's description of "sensations are brain processes": true, but could have been false; Kripke: if both terms are rigid, an identity is necessary if true | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| [illusion of contingency](#illusion-of-contingency) | the appearance that a necessary identity could have failed | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| strong necessity | Chalmers: a necessity no world considered as actual violates and no a priori reasoning reveals, unlike water's being H2O | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| [epistemic gap, ontological gap](#type-a-and-type-b-physicalism) | a gap in what can be known a priori / a gap in what exists | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| [anti-zombie](#anti-zombie-argument) | a conscious physical duplicate of ours with nothing non-physical added (Frankish 2007) | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| [Twin Earth](#twin-earth) | Putnam's planet where the watery stuff is XYZ, not H2O, with Oscar's intrinsic duplicate on it | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| logical, metaphysical, physical possibility | free of contradiction / possible in the widest real sense, what the zombie argument needs / consistent with the laws of nature; [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)'s Blockhead is conceivable and physically impossible | [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) |
| [category mistake](#category-mistake) | treating something as belonging to the wrong logical type, like looking for the university as one more building when it is the way the buildings are organized (Ryle 1949) | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| official doctrine | Ryle's name for the Cartesian picture: a mind as a second, invisible thing behind the body's doings | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| "the Ghost in the Machine" | Ryle's deliberately abusive name for the official doctrine | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| disposition | a tendency to behave in certain ways in certain circumstances; to call someone vain is to say how they act and would act | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| [logical behaviourism](#logical-behaviourism) | every meaningful mental statement translates into statements about behaviour and dispositions to behave: a thesis about meaning | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| methodological behaviourism | Watson's rule that psychology studies behaviour; not a thesis about meaning | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| psychological behaviourism | Skinner's science of behaviour; like Watson's rule, untouched by objections aimed at the logical kind | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| [super-Spartans](#super-spartans) | Putnam's people who feel pain but have no disposition to pain behaviour | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| perfect actor | Putnam's case of pain behaviour without pain | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| [identity theory](#identity-theory) | sensations are brain processes (Place 1956, Feigl 1958, Smart 1959) | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| C-fibre firing | philosophers' placeholder for whatever neural process a mental state is identified with | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| nomological dangler | Feigl's term for a fundamental psychophysical law tying a neural process to something non-physical, dangling from the net of science ([argument from simplicity](#argument-from-simplicity)) | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| [topic-neutral](#topic-neutral-analysis) | neither mental nor physical, so neutral between dualism and physicalism (Smart, adapting Ryle) | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| [multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability) | one mental kind occurring in creatures whose nervous systems share no kind of process with ours (Putnam 1967) | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| restricted (species-relative) type identity | human pain is one neural kind and another species' pain another: the local reductions Kim defends | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| avowal | the Rylean reading of a first-person sensation report as an expression on a par with a wince, not a report of an inner episode | [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md) |
| role | the pattern of causal relations a state bears to inputs, outputs and other states; see [role and realizer](#role-and-realizer) | [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) |
| [machine table](#machine-functionalism) | a specification of how each state and input lead to an output and a next state | [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) |
| probabilistic automaton | a Turing machine whose transitions between states are stochastic (Putnam) | [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) |
| platitudes | generalizations about mental states so widely known they count as part of the concepts ([analytic functionalism](#analytic-functionalism)) | [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) |
| Ramsey sentence | the platitudes conjoined, each mental term replaced by a variable and existentially quantified | [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) |
| [liberalism, chauvinism](#liberalism-and-chauvinism) | crediting minds too widely / too narrowly | [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) |
| grain | how finely a role is drawn: everyday platitude roles versus roles drawn at the grain of human psychology ([psychofunctionalism](#psychofunctionalism)) | [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) |
| [China brain](#china-brain) | Block's case: a billion people with two-way radios, each doing the job of one entry in a machine table that describes you | [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) |
| [absent qualia](#absent-qualia) | functional duplication without any qualitative states | [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) |
| second-order property | the property of having some state that fills a given role ([levels and orders](#levels-and-orders)) | [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) |
| [anomalous monism](#anomalous-monism) | one kind of event, with the mental escaping strict law (Davidson 1970) | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| [propositional attitudes](#propositional-attitudes) | believing, wanting, intending: attitudes toward a that-clause content that can be true or false | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| [strict law](#strict-law) | an exceptionless law | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| extensional (causation) | causation holds between events however described; laws hold of events only as described | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| charity | interpreting a person so as to make her largely consistent and right by the interpreter's lights | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| rationalization | explaining an action by the reason that makes it intelligible; for Davidson, causal explanation under a mental description ([reasons as causes](#reasons-as-causes)) | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| ceteris paribus | holding only other things being equal; Davidson's own line on how the mental depends on the physical | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| [supervenience](#supervenience) | no difference in the supervening family without a difference in the base family | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| $M$, $P$ (families) | a family of mental properties; a family of physical (base) properties, the ones that do the fixing | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| $x_w \sim_P y_{w'}$, $P$-indiscernible | for every $F$ in $P$, $x$ has $F$ in world $w$ if and only if $y$ has $F$ in world $w'$ | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| [weak supervenience](#weak-supervenience) | $P$-twins within any one world are $M$-twins | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| [strong supervenience](#strong-supervenience) | $P$-twins in any worlds, the same or different, are $M$-twins | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| isomorphism $f$, $P$-preserving | a one-to-one correspondence between two worlds' individuals; $P$-preserving if it pairs each $x$ with a $P$-indiscernible $f(x)$ | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| [global supervenience](#global-supervenience) | whole worlds compared: strong (every $P$-preserving isomorphism is $M$-preserving), intermediate (if some is $P$-preserving, some preserves both), weak (if some is $P$-preserving, some is $M$-preserving) | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| nomological, metaphysical supervenience | covariation guaranteed by the laws of nature / holding in every possible world; physicalism needs the second | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| [content externalism](#content-externalism) | what a belief is about depends partly on the believer's environment, so belief fails to supervene on intrinsic physical properties ([4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)) | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| [minimal physicalism](#minimal-physicalism) | Kim's name for mind-body supervenience as the commitment physicalists share | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| [ban on mixed worlds](#ban-on-mixed-worlds) | the demand that weak-without-strong supervenience explain why no one world mixes pairings that different worlds allow (pressed by Blackburn) | [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md) |
| [exclusion](#exclusion-argument) principle | no event has two distinct sufficient causes at one time unless it is genuinely overdetermined (Kim 2005); 1.1's E folds this and no systematic overdetermination into one | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| supervenience argument | Kim's fullest exclusion argument, starting from one thought causing another | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| self-stultification | epiphenomenalism's price: if judgments never affect speech, your "I decided" is not produced by the decision | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| [quausation](#quausation) | causing qua, in virtue of being, a certain kind (Horgan's term) | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| [proportionality](#proportionality) | a cause should be specific enough for its effect, and no more (Yablo) | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| determinable, determinate | a general property and a specific way of having it, as red and scarlet | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| difference-making | $F$ makes a difference to $G$ when relevantly similar situations with $F$ have $G$ and those without $F$ lack it (List and Menzies 2009) | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| [realization-insensitive](#realization-insensitivity), realization-sensitive | the effect would survive a change of realizer / hinges on the exact realizer; in the first case exclusion runs downward, from the mental property to its realizer | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| production | causation as bringing about, which Kim (2005) says is stronger than counterfactual dependence | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| [generalization objection](#generalization-objection) | the exclusion argument proves too much, idling every multiply realized property of chemistry, biology and geology | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| [levels, orders](#levels-and-orders) | levels: properties of wholes versus their parts (a table's mass, built from its parts' masses); orders: a role property versus its realizer, had by one thing at one level | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| [functional reduction](#functional-reduction) | Kim's three steps: define the property by its role, find what plays the role in a kind of creature, identify the property-in-that-kind with the occupant | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| causal inheritance principle | an instance of a realized property has exactly the causal powers of its realizer (`philosophy-of-science` 4.5) | [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) |
| [creature consciousness, state consciousness](#creature-and-state-consciousness) | a creature's being awake and responsive, or a subject at all / a particular state's being conscious, as when its owner is conscious of it | [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) |
| [access consciousness](#phenomenal-and-access-consciousness) (A) | a state's content poised for use as a premise in reasoning and for the rational control of action and speech; phenomenal consciousness is P | [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) |
| mongrel concept | Block's term: one word covering several distinct concepts, as "conscious" does | [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) |
| superblindsight | Block's hypothetical: access to blind-field content without phenomenal consciousness of it; he doubted it exists | [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) |
| [blindsight](#blindsight) | after damage to primary visual cortex, accurate guessing about stimuli the patient denies seeing | [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) |
| [reductive explanation](#reductive-explanation) | analysing a phenomenon as the performance of a function and finding the mechanism that performs it | [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) |
| [easy problems](#easy-problems) | problems about functions (discriminating, integrating, reporting, attending), solvable by finding mechanisms | [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) |
| [hard problem](#hard-problem) | why the performance of those functions is accompanied by experience | [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) |
| [explanatory gap](#explanatory-gap) | what even a true psychophysical identity would leave unexplained: why the state is like that | [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) |
| caloric | the fluid theory of heat that kinetic theory displaced | [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) |
| [transparency](#transparency-of-experience) | attending to an experience turns up only what it presents | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| PANIC | Tye's phenomenal content: poised, abstract, nonconceptual intentional content | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| poised | ready to make a direct difference to belief and desire | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| abstract content | content no particular object enters, so a hallucination can share it | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| nonconceptual content | content finer-grained than the subject's concepts | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| mental paint | Block's term for introspectible qualities of the representing itself, not of what is represented | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| [inverted spectrum](#inverted-spectrum); Invert, Nonvert | a case of colour experience inverted from birth with behaviour and colour words unchanged; Invert is the inverted subject, Nonvert the other | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| phenomenal externalism | phenomenal character is as wide as content, so molecular duplicates can differ in it | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| [Swampman](#swampman) | a physical replica of a person made by coincidence, by lightning in a swamp, with no history (Davidson 1987) | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| [transitivity principle](#transitivity-principle) | a state is conscious only if its subject is in some way aware of being in it | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| inner sense | a higher-order perceptual faculty scanning first-order states ([higher-order perception](#higher-order-perception)) | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| HOT, dispositional HOT | [higher-order thought](#higher-order-thought): an assertoric thought that one is in the state; on the dispositional version the state need only be available to one (Carruthers) | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| [empty higher-order state](#empty-higher-order-state) | a higher-order representation with no first-order state for it to represent | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| modest, ambitious (higher-order theory) | an account of state consciousness only / one that also claims to explain phenomenal consciousness (Block) | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| [illusion, hallucination](#illusion-and-hallucination) | something there, seen wrongly / an experience with no ordinary object there | [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) |
| [contrastive method](#contrastive-method) | compare brain activity when the same stimulus is seen and when it is missed | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [neural correlate of consciousness](#neural-correlates-of-consciousness) | a minimal neural system whose states suffice, under background conditions, for the matching conscious states (Chalmers 2000) | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [masking](#masking) | a mask shown shortly after a brief target makes it invisible; near threshold the same display is seen on some trials and missed on others | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [global workspace](#global-workspace-theory) | a limited-capacity stage whose contents are broadcast to many specialist processors (Baars; neuronal version, Dehaene and Changeux) | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [ignition](#ignition) | sudden, nonlinear recruitment of workspace neurons into sustained, recurrent activity | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [subliminal, preconscious, conscious](#subliminal-preconscious-and-conscious) | too weak to be accessed / strong enough to be accessible but not accessed, typically for want of attention / accessed and broadcast (Dehaene and co-authors 2006) | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [integrated information](#integrated-information-theory) | how far a system's causal structure as a whole exceeds what its parts carry separately (Tononi) | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| posterior "hot zone" | the region of posterior cortex where integrated information theory places the human maximum | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [recurrent processing](#recurrent-processing-theory) | feedback activity within and between sensory areas | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [no-report paradigm](#no-report-paradigms) | tracking what is seen without an overt task, by reflexive signs such as eye movements or pupil size | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [overflow](#overflow-debate) | phenomenal consciousness exceeding access | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| partial report | Sperling's method: after a brief array of letters vanishes, a cue picks one row to report, testing how much was available | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [adversarial collaboration](#adversarial-collaboration) | proponents of rival theories preregister divergent predictions with pass and fail criteria; theory-neutral teams test them (Cogitate, 2025) | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [readiness potential](#libet-experiments) | a slow rise in electrical activity measured at the scalp before a self-initiated movement (Libet) | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| sense of agency | the felt authorship of one's own acts | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| callosotomy, split brain | cutting the corpus callosum, the main fibre bridge between the hemispheres, to stop severe seizures; its patients are split-brain subjects | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [unity of consciousness](#unity-of-consciousness) | a subject's experiences at a time being experienced together | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [inattentional blindness](#inattentional-blindness) | missing a visible event when attention is elsewhere (Simons and Chabris 1999) | [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) |
| [user illusion](#user-illusion) | a useful self-representation that misrepresents the machinery, as a desktop does (Dennett) | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| [illusionism](#illusionism) | phenomenal properties do not exist; introspection misrepresents experiences as having them | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| [quasi-phenomenal properties](#quasi-phenomenal-properties) | the physical or functional properties introspection detects and misrepresents as phenomenal | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| illusion problem | explaining that misrepresentation (Frankish) | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| [strong, weak illusionism](#strong-and-weak-illusionism) | phenomenal consciousness does not exist / it exists and only some of its apparent features are illusory | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| [meta-problem](#meta-problem) | explaining why we think there is a hard problem | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| [problem intuitions](#problem-intuitions) | dispositions to judge that consciousness poses a special problem | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| meta-problem challenge | Chalmers: problem reports have a physical explanation, so a realist owes an account of how consciousness figures in it | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| [heterophenomenology](#heterophenomenology) | treating reports as data about how things seem, neutral on whether they are accurate (Dennett) | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| [debunking argument](#debunking-argument) | from an explanation of beliefs that is independent of their subject matter to their lack of justification | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| Mach bands | thin bright and dark stripes seen at each edge of a row of uniform grey bars, which a photometer does not find | [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) |
| structure and dynamics | the spatio-temporal relations and changes physics states ([structuralism about physics](#structuralism-about-physics)) | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| [quiddity](#quiddity), intrinsic nature | the intrinsic, categorical nature behind a basic disposition; physics leaves it out | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| categorical, dispositional property | how a thing is / what it would do (`metaphysics` 4.3) | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| [panprotopsychism](#panprotopsychism), protophenomenal | quiddities with nothing it is like to have one, which together constitute experience, truths about them entailing truths about experience a priori (Chalmers) | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| [constitutive panpsychism](#constitutive-and-emergentist-panpsychism) | your experience consists in micro-experiences, as a party consists in people dancing (constitutive micropsychism) | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| grounding | non-causal "consisting in" (`metaphysics` 2.4) | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| brute emergence | emergence with nothing in the base in virtue of which it happens; Strawson's [anti-emergence argument](#anti-emergence-argument) denies it | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| micro-experiential zombie | your physical duplicate in which every particle experiences and nothing experiences at the scale of the whole (Goff) | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| [combination problem](#combination-problem): qualities, structure, subjects | how a few basic qualities yield smell, colour and pain; why smooth experience sits on a grainy brain; how many subjects make one ([subject-summing](#subject-summing-problem)) | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| priority monism | the cosmos is the one fundamental thing and its parts are grounded in it (Schaffer); [cosmopsychism](#cosmopsychism) adds that it is a subject | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| decombination problem | cosmopsychism's version: how one subject grounds many | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| percept | for Russell, a perceptual experience: an event in the perceiver's head | [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) |
| [original, derived intentionality](#original-and-derived-intentionality) | aboutness a state has of itself (minds) / aboutness borrowed from a mind (words, fuel gauges) | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| [accuracy conditions](#accuracy-conditions) | the conditions under which a representation is accurate rather than inaccurate | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| probe | a classifier trained on a network's internal activations to read off some variable | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| [intentional realism](#intentional-realism) | beliefs are inner states, like genes, that cause behaviour by virtue of their content (Fodor) | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| [representational theory of mind](#representational-theory-of-mind) | attitudes are functional relations to mental representations with content | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| belief box | to believe that $p$ is to have a sentence meaning $p$ in the "belief box", which is a role, not a place | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| [language of thought](#language-of-thought), Mentalese | an inner code of representations with words and a syntax that thinking processes respond to | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| syntax | the formal structure of symbols, and the rules defined over it, that processes respond to ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md): formal shapes) | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| [systematicity](#systematicity) | thinkable thoughts come in families: whoever can think $aRb$ can think $bRa$ | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| [connectionism](#connectionism) | thinking as activation spreading through networks of simple units, with knowledge in the connection weights | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| [theory-theory](#theory-theory-and-simulation) | folk psychology as an empirical theory we explain each other with (Sellars 1956) | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| [simulation theory](#theory-theory-and-simulation) | we predict others by running our own decision-making offline on pretend beliefs and desires (Gordon, Heal, Goldman) | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| [intentional stance](#intentional-stance) | predicting a system as a rational agent with the beliefs and desires it ought to have (Dennett) | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| physical stance, design stance | predicting a system from its physics / from what it is designed or programmed to do | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| real patterns | Dennett's term: patterns there in the behaviour, real enough to support prediction, like a centre of gravity | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| propositional modularity | Ramsey, Stich and Garon's name for folk psychology's commitment to discrete, content-bearing states that cause behaviour | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| dual view | Block: some mental states have phenomenal character and no intentional content | [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md) |
| [naturalizing content](#naturalizing-content) | explaining content in terms that mention no meaning, intention or mind: only causes, laws, probabilities and biology | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| [natural meaning](#natural-meaning) | Grice's sign-meaning, as smoke means fire; it cannot be false | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| indication | Dretske's information relation: $R$ indicates $C$ when, given the channel conditions, $\Pr(C \mid R) = 1$ ([informational semantics](#informational-semantics)) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| channel conditions | the stable background on which a signal's reliability depends | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| misrepresentation | a state whose content does not obtain; the test case every theory of content must pass | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| [disjunction problem](#disjunction-problem) | every actual cause of a symbol is folded into what it signals, so no token can be false (HORSE means horse or cow-on-a-dark-night) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| learning period | Dretske's (1981) period during which a state's content is fixed by what it was trained to signal | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| [asymmetric dependence](#asymmetric-dependence) | the wrong causes produce a symbol only because the right one does, not conversely (Fodor) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| [distal content](#distal-content) | the problem of fixing the distal object, not the proximal stimulus, as what a state is about | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| proper function, selected-effect function | what a trait was selected for (`philosophy-of-science` 4.2); the basis of [teleosemantics](#teleosemantics) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| [producer, consumer](#producer-and-consumer) | the systems that make a representation and that use it (Millikan) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| Normal | Millikan's term: the way that historically explained the consumers' selection | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| [functional indeterminacy](#functional-indeterminacy) | selection cannot tell locally coextensive properties apart (dog, dog-lookalike) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| real-nature theory | a theory of what content is, discovered a posteriori like what a species is, not an analysis of the concept (Millikan, Neander) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| [inferential role](#inferential-role-semantics) | a state's place in inference, perception and action | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| two-factor theory | an inner inferential role paired with an external referential factor (Block 1986) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| holism (objection) | if every inference fixes meaning, any change of belief changes every meaning (Fodor and Lepore) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) |
| intrinsic duplicate | a thing alike in every property that does not depend on its surroundings: "same inside" | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| [wide content, narrow content](#narrow-and-wide-content) | content that depends partly on the environment / content any intrinsic duplicate must share | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| anti-individualism | Burge's name for his content externalism, reaching any concept a thinker understands incompletely ([arthritis case](#arthritis-case)) | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| deference | readiness to accept experts' correction about what one's word applies to; [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md): using a word with the reference one's community gives it | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| individuation, location | what makes a state the state it is / where it is: a sunburn is individuated by the sun and located on the skin (Davidson) | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| [slow switching](#slow-switching) | being moved unawares to Twin Earth and staying until one's "water" thoughts are about XYZ (Burge 1988) | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| derivativists, eliminativists (about wide content) | on [phenomenal intentionality](#phenomenal-intentionality): wide content is built from narrow content plus the environment / there is only shared narrow content plus context | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| [vehicle, content](#vehicle-and-content) | the state or structure that carries a content (a brain state, a notebook entry) / what it represents | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| [parity principle](#parity-principle) | a part of the world that functions as a process we would count as cognitive were it done in the head is part of the cognitive process | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| [active, passive externalism](#active-and-passive-externalism) | external factors that drive cognition here and now, as Otto's notebook does / factors that are distal and idle in what the twins now do (Putnam, Burge) | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| bloat | the worry that, without the condition that entries were consciously endorsed, the mind extends to whatever one trusts | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| preservative memory | memory that keeps the content a thought had when it was formed (Burge 1998) | [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) |
| [naive realism](#naive-realism) | in seeing, ordinary objects are themselves directly presented, and how they look is fixed partly by how they are; [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) calls it the [relational view](#relational-view) | [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |
| good case, bad case | veridical perception / an illusion or an introspectively indistinguishable hallucination | [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |
| base case | the step showing that in the bad case no ordinary object is directly presented | [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |
| spreading step | the step giving the good case the same account, since from the inside the two can be alike | [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |
| [Phenomenal Principle](#phenomenal-principle) | if it sensibly seems to you that something is $F$, you are directly presented with something that is $F$ | [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |
| Exclusion Assumption | being directly presented with an $F$ thing distinct from the object excludes being presented with the object (French and Walters 2018) | [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |
| [sense-data](#sense-datum-theory) | non-ordinary items with the qualities on show, through which you perceive ordinary objects indirectly (Moore's term, 1909) | [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |
| [common-kind assumption](#common-kind-assumption) | a veridical experience and its indistinguishable hallucination are fundamentally the same kind of experience, differing only in their causes; intentionalists accept it, disjunctivists deny it | [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |
| perceptual variation | an object looking different from different standpoints or in different conditions with nobody fooled; not illusion | [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |
| simultaneous contrast | identical greys looking different on different surrounds | [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |
| [intentionalism](#intentionalism) | experience is representation with content; it accepts the common-kind assumption and rejects the Phenomenal Principle | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| indirect realism | perceiving ordinary objects by being directly aware of inner objects such as sense-data; intentionalism posits no inner object | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| strong intentionalism (I3) | no two experiences differ in what they are like without differing in content | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| weak intentionalism, qualia theory | experience has content and also non-representational [qualia](#qualia) (Peacocke, Block) | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| standpoint | Campbell's third relatum: experience as a relation of subject, standpoint and object | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| [disjunctivism](#disjunctivism) | the good and bad cases share no fundamental kind | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| fundamental kind | the most specific answer to "what is this event, essentially?" (Martin) | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| [causally matching hallucination](#causally-matching-hallucination) | a hallucination with the same proximal brain cause as a veridical perception | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| causal argument | same proximal cause, same kind of immediate effect, so a common kind ([against disjunctivism](#causal-argument-against-disjunctivism)) | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| screening off | one factor making another explanatorily redundant ([screening-off argument](#screening-off-argument)) | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| [negative epistemic conception](#negative-epistemic-conception) | a hallucination's character is its being impossible to tell apart, by introspection, from a veridical perception | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| mere appearance | McDowell's bad case: an appearance that is not a fact making itself manifest ([epistemological disjunctivism](#epistemological-disjunctivism)) | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| partial hallucination | seeing some things while hallucinating an aspect of them; Martin's treatment of illusion | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| visually relevant similarities | Brewer: an object seen in certain conditions resembles paradigm $F$s and so objectively looks $F$ | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| [cognitive penetration](#cognitive-penetration) | cognitive states changing perceptual experience itself | [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md) |
| *psyche*, *anima* | Greek and Latin for soul; Aristotle's definition is the "first actuality of a natural body furnished with organs" (*De Anima* II.1): a principle of life, not of consciousness only ([soul as form](#soul-as-form)) | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| [first actuality](#first-actuality), second actuality | a standing capacity, present in sleep / its exercise | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| [vegetative, sensitive, rational powers](#powers-of-the-soul) | Aquinas's ranks by how operations stand to the body (ST I q.78 a.1): an organ working by a bodily quality (digestion by heat) / an organ, but not by a bodily quality (seeing) / no corporeal organ | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| estimative power | the interior sense that grasps what no exterior sense presents, as a sheep grasps the wolf as an enemy (q.78 a.4) | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| imagination (phantasy) | the interior sense that keeps and reworks forms received through the senses | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| motor (as against form) | a mover joined to a body from outside; Plato's three souls would be coherent if the soul were joined to the body "not as its form, but as its motor" (q.76 a.3) | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| substantial form | the form that makes matter the kind of substance it is; a living thing's is its soul ([unity of substantial form](#unity-of-substantial-form)) | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| subject of a power | what has the power: the composite for sensitive and vegetative powers, the soul itself for intellect and will (q.77 a.5); the act is still the man's | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| subsistent | existing in its own right, not inhering in something else; Aquinas's subsistent soul is still not the human being (q.75 a.4) ([subsistent intellect](#subsistent-intellect)) | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| structural hylomorphism | Jaworski's view: structure plays the role of form, and no immaterial power is needed | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| contains virtually | a higher soul has the lower souls' powers without those souls being present as distinct forms (q.76 a.3) | [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) |
| per se operation | an operation a thing performs through itself, in which no body shares | [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md) |
| determinate nature | Aquinas: to be a body is to be some particular kind of body | [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md) |
| phantasm | a sensory image held by imagination or memory, powers with organs; the intellect abstracts from phantasms ([abstraction](#abstraction)) | [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md) |
| [quus](#plus-and-quus) ($\oplus$), quadding | Kripke's rival to plus: $x \oplus y = x + y$ if $x, y < 57$, and 5 otherwise; quadding is computing it | [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md) |
| incompossible functions | functions that cannot both be the one computed, since they disagree on some case | [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md) |
| determinate (Ross) | of one definite form, not indeterminate among incompossible functions ([determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument)) | [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md) |
| [pure function](#pure-function) | an abstract form such as addition or squaring, which Ross holds is wholly present in a single case | [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md) |
| naturalness | Lewis: an objective ranking of properties or functions, so the best interpretation of a system assigns plus, not gerrymandered quus ([naturalness reply](#naturalness-reply)) | [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md) |
| accidental form | a form, such as shape, that a substance has without being what it is; for a statue, fixed by the arrangement of its bronze | [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md) |
| push-or-idle dilemma (K) | a soul that makes a difference to the body is one more sufficient cause (so closure fails or every action is overdetermined) or no cause (so it idles) | [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md) |
| [command and commanded act](#command-and-commanded-act) | the act of will or reason and the bodily act it moves, related as form to matter and making "one human act" (ST I-II q.17 a.4) | [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md) |
| [causal pluralism](#causal-pluralism) | more than one kind of cause, so causes of different kinds (physical, rational) need not compete or overdetermine | [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md) |
| [imitation game](#imitation-game) | Turing's test: an interrogator exchanges typed messages with two hidden players, a person and a machine, and judges which is which | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| win rate | in Jones and Bergen (2025), the share of games in which the interrogator picked the AI as the human; in a three-party game interrogators are right 1 minus the win rate of the time | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| persona prompt | Jones and Bergen's instruction to play an introverted young person who knows internet culture and uses slang | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| [computational theory of mind](#computational-theory-of-mind) | thinking is computation | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| [Church-Turing thesis](#church-turing-thesis) | every effective procedure is Turing-computable | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| universal machine | one Turing machine that can run any other's computation, given its description, time and memory | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| [strong AI](#strong-and-weak-ai) | Searle's name for the thesis that an appropriately programmed computer literally has a mind and understands | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| $T$, $G_T$ | a consistent, effectively axiomatized theory containing enough arithmetic; its Gödel sentence, provably equivalent in $T$ to "$G_T$ is not provable in $T$" | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| $\operatorname{Con}(T)$ | the sentence saying $T$ proves no contradiction | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| mechanism (Lucas) | the thesis that what I can establish in arithmetic is what some consistent formal system $T_M$ proves ([Lucas-Penrose argument](#lucas-penrose-argument)) | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| [consistency reply](#consistency-reply) | Lucas must know the system is consistent, which mechanism plus Gödel's second theorem deny him | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| [Blockhead](#blockhead) | a look-up-table machine that passes a conversation test of fixed length: the route-memorizer of the driving-test analogy | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| [psychologism](#psychologism) | Block: whether behaviour is intelligent depends on how it is produced | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| definition reading, evidence reading | passing the test as logically sufficient for thinking / as evidence of it, whose force depends on how the candidate passed | [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) |
| weak AI | computers as powerful tools for modelling the mind; Searle had no quarrel with it | [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md) |
| semantics | meaning, content: what symbols or states are about | [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md) |
| [observer-relative](#observer-relativity-of-syntax) | existing only relative to someone's interpretation, as Searle held syntax and computation are; opposed to intrinsic (mass, consciousness) | [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md) |
| simulation, duplication | copying a process's formal structure / reproducing its causal powers: a simulated storm leaves nothing wet | [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md) |
| internalization | Searle's answer to the [systems reply](#systems-reply): the man memorizes the whole system and works in his head | [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md) |
| finite-state automaton without inputs | a machine with finitely many states whose next state depends only on its current one; Putnam argued every ordinary open system implements every one ([triviality problem](#triviality-problem)) | [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md) |
| implementation | what it takes for a physical system to run a given computation: any mirroring mapping on the [simple mapping account](#simple-mapping-account), counterfactual-supporting causal structure on the [causal account](#causal-account-of-implementation) | [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md) |
| fine-grained functional organization | organization fine enough to fix all of a system's dispositions to behave (Chalmers) | [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md) |
| form (Bender and Koller) | the observable strings of a language, which is what a text-only model is trained on (Bender and Koller) | [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) |
| meaning (Bender and Koller) | a relation between form and something outside language: the communicative intents expressions can convey, and standing meanings testable for truth against a model of the world | [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) |
| communicative intent | what a speaker uses an expression to convey or get done | [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) |
| intrinsic, parasitic meaning | Harnad: meaning that belongs to the symbol system itself / meaning that exists only in its interpreters' heads ([symbol-grounding problem](#symbol-grounding-problem)) | [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) |
| merry-go-round | Harnad's image for symbols defined only by other symbols, never reaching what they stand for | [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) |
| sensorimotor grounding | Harnad's bottom-up remedy: elementary symbols name categories learned from sensory projections and action | [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) |
| referential grounding | words hooking onto things: the one kind of grounding Mollo and Millière hold matters ([vector grounding problem](#vector-grounding-problem)) | [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) |
| preference tuning | post-training on human comparisons of outputs, which on Mollo and Millière's reading can reward factual accuracy | [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) |
| intervention | changing a network's internal state and checking whether its outputs change to match; with untrained baselines, what moves a probe result from information towards use ([world-model probing](#world-model-probing)) | [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) |
| world model | here, a probed internal state tracking the process that generated the data (Othello-GPT); in `reinforcement-learning` 4.5, a learned dynamics model an agent plans with | [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) |
| [substrate independence](#substrate-independence) | consciousness depends on a system's organization, not its material | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| [computational functionalism](#computational-functionalism) | implementing the right computations is necessary and sufficient for consciousness | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| functional isomorph | a system with the same fine-grained functional organization as another | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| [fading qualia](#fading-qualia), sudden vanishing | the two ways experience could leave a one-by-one replacement of neurons by chips: by degrees, or abruptly at one step | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| organizational invariance | same fine-grained organization, same experiences, as a natural, not a logical, necessity (Chalmers) | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| upload | a scan of a brain run as software; whether it is you (`metaphysics` 6.6) is a separate question from whether it is conscious | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| [indicator properties](#indicator-approach) | features derived from scientific theories of consciousness, each checkable in an architecture (fourteen, Butlin, Long and co-authors 2023) | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| residual stream | the running vector every transformer layer reads from and adds to, read by some as a workspace | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| [gaming](#gaming-problem) | a system trained on human-generated data reproducing the behaviours we take as signs of feeling, which then stop being evidence (Andrews and Birch 2023) | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| self-report | a system's statements about its own experience; in AI the paradigm case of gamed evidence | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| over-attribution, under-attribution | treating an object as a subject / a subject as an object ([moral patienthood under uncertainty](#moral-patienthood-under-uncertainty)) | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |
| motivational trade-off | accepting a noxious stimulus to reach a resource only when the resource is good enough: a marker in animal-sentience research | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |

## Positions and distinctions

**Module 1: The problem, and the case for dualism**

### Marks of the mental

**Three features make the mental look unlike anything else in nature: intentionality, phenomenal consciousness and first-person access.** 1.1 states them; each gets its own treatment in the course.

- **Intentionality** (Module 4). A thought is *about* something, and aboutness survives the absence of its object: you think of Atlantis, which does not exist, whereas a physical relation such as being taller than needs both its terms. Franz Brentano made it the mark of the mental in 1874 ([Brentano's thesis](#brentanos-thesis)); [`phenomenology-and-personalism` 1.1](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/01-01-intentionality-from-brentano-to-husserl.md) describes it from the inside.
- **Phenomenal consciousness** (Module 3). There is [something it is like](#what-it-is-like) to taste lemon or feel a cramp, and a complete physical description of your brain seems to leave it out.
- **[First-person access](#first-person-access).** You know your own current states without evidence or inference.
- **The marks come apart.** Your standing belief that Paris is in France is about Paris and available on demand, but while you sleep there is nothing it is like to have it. A theory can do well on one mark and badly on another.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### What it is like

**A state is phenomenally conscious when there is something it is like to be in it: to taste lemon, to feel a cramp.** Thomas Nagel made the phrase famous ("What Is It Like to Be a Bat?", *Philosophical Review*, 1974): an organism has conscious states just when there is something it is like to be it, for it.

- **Nagel's argument** ([3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)). We can imagine behaving like an echolocating bat but not what it is like for the bat. Science gains objectivity by moving away from any point of view, and with experience, moving away leaves the fact behind. His conclusion was not that physicalism is false but that we have no conception of how it could be true.
- **Not Nagel's coinage.** B. A. Farrell raised the bat question in "Experience" (*Mind*, 1950), drawing a deflationary moral, and T. L. S. Sprigge used the what-it-is-like test in 1971 (3.1).
- **[Qualia](#qualia)**, in the broad sense almost no one denies, are this felt character ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)).
- **Why it matters to illusionism** ([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)). Chalmers (2018) holds that the [hard problem](#hard-problem) needs only that there is something it is like to be us, so [weak illusionism](#strong-and-weak-illusionism) leaves it standing; Frankish agrees that only the strong form dissolves it.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### First-person access

**You know that you are in pain, or what you are now thinking, without evidence or inference, and others defer to your say-so.** The third of the [marks of the mental](#marks-of-the-mental) (1.1): anyone can study your brain on equal terms, but nobody else knows your pain as you do.

- **Privileged, not infallible.** People routinely misreport what caused their own choices (Nisbett and Wilson, 1977).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md)

### Inconsistent triad

**That the mind is not physical, that it moves the body, and that bodily motions have sufficient physical causes cannot all be true, given a fourth claim: that bodily motions are not systematically overdetermined.** 1.1's statement of the mind-body problem. Every theory of mind is, first, a choice of which claim to give up.

| | Claim | In words |
|---|---|---|
| **D** | Distinctness: mental properties (and, on some views, the things that have them) are neither identical with physical properties nor fixed by them | a complete physical inventory of the world would leave something mental out |
| **M** | Mental causation: some mental events cause physical events | your wanting to ask is part of why your hand went up |
| **C** | [Causal closure](#causal-closure): every physical event that has a cause has a sufficient physical cause | trace any bodily movement back and you never have to leave physics |
| **E** | No overdetermination: no physical event regularly has, besides its sufficient physical cause, a distinct further cause, as a prisoner shot by two marksmen at once has | mental causation is not a coincidence repeated every time anyone acts |

**Given E, any two of D, M and C entail the denial of the third.** The exits, as 1.1 maps them:

| Gives up | Family |
|---|---|
| D | [reductive physicalism](#reductive-physicalism), [nonreductive physicalism](#nonreductive-physicalism), [idealism](#idealism), [neutral monism](#neutral-monism) |
| M | [epiphenomenalism](#epiphenomenalism), [eliminative materialism](#eliminative-materialism), occasionalism, pre-established harmony ([`modern-philosophy` 2.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/02-04-interaction-and-causation.md)) |
| C | interactionist [substance dualism](#substance-dualism), strong emergentism ([`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md)) |
| E | [systematic overdetermination](#systematic-overdetermination) (Eugene Mills, 1996) |
| disputes the terms | [hylomorphism](#hylomorphism), [Russellian monism](#russellian-monism) |

- **Without E the three are consistent.** Closure says a physical event *has* a sufficient physical cause, not that it has no other; Mills keeps D, M and C by giving up E.
- **E is narrow on purpose.** It bans only a further cause *besides* a sufficient physical one, and only *regularly*: the occasional double cause, like the two marksmen, is allowed.
- **The families cut across the exits.** Dualists split between M, C and E; physicalists, idealists and neutral monists deny D.
- **Where it is weakest** (1.1): the unstated premise that "physical" means the same in D and in C ([Hempel's dilemma](#hempels-dilemma), answered by the [via negativa](#via-negativa)). E is the other pressure point, and [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) runs the same squeeze on the nonreductive physicalist at the level of properties ([exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument)).
- **Correlation decides nothing here.** Every family on the map predicts that each thought has a neural correlate, so a correlation, however perfect, is not by itself an identity.
- **Ancestor.** Elisabeth asked Descartes how something unextended could push a body ([`modern-philosophy` 1.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/01-04-the-real-distinction-and-the-interaction-problem.md)); closure asks what is left to push once physics suffices.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)

### Causal closure

**Trace any bodily movement back and you never have to leave physics: every physical event that has a cause has a sufficient physical cause.** Premise C of the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad), in the wording of [`apologetics-foundations` 4.4](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/04-04-divine-action-miracles-and-quantum-indeterminacy.md); the same claim as [`apologetics-foundations` 3.1](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-01-naturalism-at-its-best.md)'s "every physical effect has a fully physical cause", and 3.1 there tells how it was earned.

- **Closure alone excludes nothing.** It says a sufficient physical cause exists, not that no other cause does. Excluding a mental cause takes a ban on systematic overdetermination as well (E; [systematic overdetermination](#systematic-overdetermination)).
- **The evidence is historical** ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), Papineau): conservation of energy left room for non-physical forces that conserve energy, and physiology's failure to find any such forces made closure orthodox ([causal-closure argument](#causal-closure-argument)).
- **Who gives it up** (1.1, 1.2): interactionist substance dualists (Richard Swinburne, William Hasker) and strong emergentists positing new high-level forces. Dualist replies: closure is confirmed only where physiologists have looked; or, where physics is indeterministic and the laws fix only probabilities, a mind could settle what they leave open (Karl Popper and John Eccles, *The Self and Its Brain*, 1977).
- **Time-indexed in 2.4:** a physical event that has a cause at a time has a sufficient physical cause at that time ([exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument)).
- **Hylomorphism does not escape it by dissolving contact** ([5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)): closure needs no second thing. A formal cause is no rival for the same effect, but Aquinas's soul also moves the body, by its motive power (ST I q.76 a.4 ad 2), so the formal-cause reply covers only half of Aquinas, and his texts admit closure kept or denied ([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)). Jaworski keeps closure; SEP "Dualism" §3.6 calls denying it the natural response for [hylomorphic dualists](#hylomorphic-dualism) ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)).
- **The same premise against God.** It drives the naturalist's dilemma against special divine action ([`apologetics-foundations` 4.4](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/04-04-divine-action-miracles-and-quantum-indeterminacy.md)), where primary causation answers it; that reply is closed to a soul, a creature among creatures (5.3).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Reductive physicalism

**Each type of mental state is a type of brain state, as lightning is an electrical discharge.** The identity theory of U. T. Place ("Is Consciousness a Brain Process?", 1956) and J. J. C. Smart ("Sensations and Brain Processes", 1959); its case is made in [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md). It denies D of the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad).

- **The raised hand** (1.1): the wanting is the motor-cortex cause under another name, so there is one cause. Price: the wanting must be a brain-state type that a differently built creature could lack ([multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability)).
- **It keeps pain and says what it is**, as kinetic theory kept temperature; dropping the mental vocabulary is [eliminative materialism](#eliminative-materialism) (2.1). A [type identity](#type-and-token-identity) is the strongest bridge law a reduction can have ([`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md)).
- **The founders called the identity contingent**, discovered as lightning's was; [Kripke's argument](#kripkes-argument) presses on exactly that.
- **Kim's exit from exclusion** ([2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)) is reductive: [functional reduction](#functional-reduction), with local identities, at the price that "decision" names different properties in differently built creatures.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Nonreductive physicalism

**Mental properties are identical with no single physical type, since unlike brains could share them, but they are fixed by the physical: nothing over and above it.** Its leading forms are physicalist functionalism ([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)) and Donald Davidson's anomalous monism ("Mental Events", 1970; [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)). It denies D of the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad).
