- **Type, not token.** Each particular wanting is a physical event; what it denies is [type identity](#type-and-token-identity). Denying type identity is not accepting distinctness, since D says the mental is something more.
- **Its crux with [property dualism](#property-dualism)** (1.1). Both reject souls, deny type identity and accept every neural correlation. They split on whether the physical properties necessitate the mental ones, the "not fixed by" clause of D: the nonreductive physicalist says yes, the property dualist takes the link to be a contingent law of nature.
- **Its price** (1.1, 2.4): does the wanting do anything its realizer does not? The [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument) makes the nonreductive physicalist say which of its other premises fails.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Physicalism

**Everything is physical, or fixed by the physical.** 1.1's informal test: any world physically like ours, with nothing further, is like ours in every respect. The "nothing further" clause is needed: a physical duplicate of our world with non-physical extras added would otherwise be a counterexample.

- **What a formal statement needs** ([2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)): metaphysical (not merely nomological) necessity, comparison of whole worlds ([global supervenience](#global-supervenience)), the strong global variant, and a restriction such as Frank Jackson's (1998): any world copying our physical facts and adding nothing is a duplicate of ours outright. Mind-body [supervenience](#supervenience) alone is [minimal physicalism](#minimal-physicalism), which a dualist who holds mental properties distinct but necessitated also accepts.
- **What "physical" means** is [Hempel's dilemma](#hempels-dilemma).
- **Its main opponents in Module 1** are the [knowledge argument](#knowledge-argument) and the [zombie argument](#zombie-argument), each concluding that consciousness is a further fact.
- **Where the profession sits.** In the 2020 PhilPapers Survey's target group, 51.9 percent of the 1,733 who answered accepted or leaned towards physicalism about the mind, 32.1 percent non-physicalism, and 16.6 percent gave other answers (1.1).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Idealism

**Physical things are constructions out of minds and their experiences, so there are not two kinds to interact.** It denies D of the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad) "from the other side" (1.1). Berkeley's version is read historically in [`modern-philosophy` 3.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/03-04-berkeley-immaterialism.md); John Foster defended idealism in *The Case for Idealism* (1982).

- **As a [Russellian monism](#russellian-monism)** ([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)): if both physical structure and the natures behind it are mental, the view is idealism.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Neutral monism

**Mind and matter are both built from a basic stuff that is neither.** Bertrand Russell, following William James, took the stuff of the world to be "neither mental nor material, but a 'neutral stuff,' out of which both are constructed" (*The Analysis of Mind*, 1921, Preface). It denies D of the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad) (1.1).

- **Russell's view from 1918.** *The Analysis of Matter* (1927, ch. XXXVII) defends it: matter's intrinsic character is unknown, not absent. Reading that character as experience came later (Grover Maxwell, 1978; Michael Lockwood, 1989) ([4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)).
- **As a [Russellian monism](#russellian-monism)** ([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)): structure and natures both neutral.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Epiphenomenalism

**Mental properties are caused by brain events and cause nothing physical.** It denies M of the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad). Its modern form is Frank Jackson's "Epiphenomenal Qualia" (1982), which added it to the [property dualism](#property-dualism) of the [knowledge argument](#knowledge-argument): physical events cause [qualia](#qualia), and qualia cause nothing physical.

- **A claim about causation, not about what exists.** Property dualism is not epiphenomenalism: a property dualist can deny closure instead (1.1, 1.2, 1.3). Nor does accepting the [zombie argument](#zombie-argument) make you one: its conclusion is property dualism, and a friend of zombies can be an interactionist who denies closure, or a [Russellian monist](#russellian-monism) ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)).
- **Its price in the raised-hand case** (1.1): "I raised it because I wanted to" is false.
- **It is the one route out of Papineau's [causal-closure argument](#causal-closure-argument)**, which covers only mental states with physical effects ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)).
- **The objection that moved Jackson** ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)): epiphenomenal qualia could not cause our beliefs about them, so no one could know them (pressed by Michael Watkins, 1989; [Jackson's recantation](#jacksons-recantation)). Lewis argued, from the taste of Vegemite, that the [hypothesis of phenomenal information](#hypothesis-of-phenomenal-information) makes the information idle.
- **The fallback from exclusion** ([2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)): the option left to a property dualist who accepts closure and the [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument). Its price there is self-stultification: if judgments never affect speech, your "I decided" is not produced by the decision. Epiphenomenalists reply that knowing a state need not be caused by it.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Property dualism

**The things that think are physical, but some of their properties, typically the felt ones, are not.** Non-physical properties of a physical subject, with no soul (1.1, 1.2). It affirms D of the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad). Its two main arguments are the [knowledge argument](#knowledge-argument) ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)), whose conclusion in Jackson's hands was property dualism, and the [zombie argument](#zombie-argument) ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)).

- **Not [epiphenomenalism](#epiphenomenalism).** One is about what there is, the other about what causes what. A property dualist can deny M, as Jackson did in 1982, or deny C (an interactionist property dualism), and then Papineau's [causal-closure argument](#causal-closure-argument) applies to it as to substance dualism ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)).
- **Not a soul view.** It posits no soul, unlike [substance dualism](#substance-dualism). Conversely, Hasker's [emergent dualism](#emergent-dualism) is not a property dualism, since it posits a new individual.
- **Its crux with [nonreductive physicalism](#nonreductive-physicalism)** (1.1): whether the physical properties necessitate the mental ones. The property dualist says no: the link is a contingent law of nature, so a world physically like ours with nothing added could differ mentally.
- **Supervenience** ([2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)): contingent psychophysical laws, uniform within each world and varying across worlds, yield weak supervenience without strong, so the property dualist can explain the [ban on mixed worlds](#ban-on-mixed-worlds) that Davidson cannot.
- **The identity theory's risk** ([2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)): if "pain" picks out the brain process by an irreducibly mental property, the view becomes a property dualism about an event that is physical ([distinct property argument](#distinct-property-argument)).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Eliminative materialism

**Folk psychology is a false theory, so there are no beliefs or desires.** Paul Churchland, "Eliminative Materialism and the Propositional Attitudes" (*Journal of Philosophy*, 1981). On the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad) it empties M at the root, since there is no wanting to cause anything; it belongs to the physicalist family (1.1).

- **Churchland's argument, in outline** ([4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)): folk psychology is an empirical theory (the theory-theory, from Wilfrid Sellars, 1956); a theory that leaves much of its domain unexplained, has not developed in millennia and fits none of the sciences around it is probably false, deeply enough to be replaced rather than reduced; folk psychology is such a theory, silent on sleep, mental illness and learning; so beliefs and desires probably do not exist. Beliefs go the way of phlogiston.
- **Where it is weakest** (4.1): the claim that folk psychology is a theory. Simulation theorists deny that we predict others with one ([theory-theory and simulation](#theory-theory-and-simulation)). The reply: elimination needs only that our concept of belief sets conditions nothing meets, and Ramsey, Stich and Garon (1990) argued that if distributed networks model memory correctly, nothing does.
- **Its target is beliefs and desires**, not everything mental. Denying phenomenal properties is [illusionism](#illusionism) ([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)); Dennett's "Quining Qualia" (1988) is eliminativism about [qualia](#qualia), on the ground that the concept is confused.
- **Self-refutation.** C. D. Broad (*The Mind and Its Place in Nature*, 1925, ch. XIV) argued that the strongest claim, that mentality is a "delusive characteristic", refutes itself, since a delusion is already mental.
- **Not the identity theory**, which keeps pain and says what it is ([2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Substance dualism

**You are, or have, a non-physical subject distinct from any body.** Descartes is the historical source; his conceivability and divisibility arguments are reconstructed in [`modern-philosophy` 1.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/01-04-the-real-distinction-and-the-interaction-problem.md). On the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad), interactionist substance dualism denies C: the subject's choices make a difference no physical cause accounts for (Richard Swinburne, William Hasker; 1.1). Occasionalist and pre-established-harmony dualisms deny M instead ([`modern-philosophy` 2.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/02-04-interaction-and-causation.md)).

- **Today's dualists argue from the subject, not from God** ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)): the [unity argument](#unity-argument), [non-Cartesian dualism](#non-cartesian-dualism) (Lowe), [emergent dualism](#emergent-dualism) (Hasker), and Swinburne's [simple view](#simple-view) of personal identity. Lowe's self has a location and Hasker's is generated by a brain, so neither is Descartes's.
- **Three objections keep it a minority view** (1.2): closure ([causal-closure argument](#causal-closure-argument)), the [conservation objection](#conservation-objection) and the [pairing problem](#pairing-problem). Its price in the raised-hand case (1.1): a gap in brain physics where physiology has found none.
- **Not every dualism posits a soul:** [property dualism](#property-dualism) does not.
- **Hylomorphism is a different view** ([5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)): the form is not a second thing acting on the matter, as a coin's shape is not a second thing gripping the metal. Commentators split on whether even Aquinas is a substance dualist (SEP "Dualism" §3.6).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Systematic overdetermination

**Every action has two complete causes, one mental and one physical, each of which would have sufficed alone.** The exit from the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad) that gives up E and keeps D, M and C: Eugene Mills, "Interactionism and Overdetermination" (1996). Had either cause occurred without the other, the action would still have occurred ([2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)).

- **Systematic, not one-off.** A prisoner shot by two marksmen at once is ordinary overdetermination; what is in question is double causation at every action. Overdetermination in single cases is [`metaphysics` 4.2](../metaphysics/lessons/04-02-counterfactual-causation.md)'s.
- **Its price: the coincidence charge** (1.1, 2.4). Two complete causes at every action, unless something explains their constant partnership. The physicalist answers that the two are not independent.
- **A physicalist can accept it too:** Barry Loewer (2002) calls such overdetermination widespread and harmless (2.4).
- **In 2.4** the ban on it is a premise of the [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Hylomorphism

**The soul is the form of a living body: the principle by which this matter is one animal with its powers, not a second thing lodged in it.** As a view of mind it is Module 5's. In a living thing the form is the soul, the principle of life, so a fern has one; as a coin's shape is not a second thing acting on the metal but the way this metal is a coin, so the soul is the way this matter is a living body ([5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [soul as form](#soul-as-form)). For Aquinas the human being thinks, through the soul.

- **It sits on no line of the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad) cleanly** (1.1, 5.1). With the dualist, the soul is real and no part of the body; with the physicalist, a human being is one substance; and it denies that the form is fixed by what physics describes. There are not two things to connect, so the contact puzzle and the [pairing problem](#pairing-problem) lapse, but closure does not.
- **On mental causation** (1.1). It is the woman who raises her hand. On the formal-cause reply her soul is the form that makes the neural cause a wanting to ask, not an efficient cause pushing beside it, so closure and the ban on overdetermination do not engage ([formal and efficient causation](#formal-and-efficient-causation)). But Aquinas's soul also moves the body, by its motive power (ST I q.76 a.4 ad 2), so his text allows [closure](#causal-closure) kept or denied ([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)).
- **The physicalist's reply** (1.1; tested in 5.3): once the form's work is stated as a difference to what happens, it either adds a push (deny closure) or adds nothing (deny mental causation).
- **Its classification is disputed.** SEP "Dualism" calls Aquinas-type views "hylomorphic dualism" ([hylomorphic dualism](#hylomorphic-dualism)), while William Jaworski (*Structure and the Metaphysics of Mind*, 2016) argues that hylomorphism solves the mind-body problem. Two families (5.1): hylomorphic dualists keep an immaterial intellect (Aquinas; David Oderberg, 2005; Edward Feser), structural hylomorphists need none (Jaworski; William M. R. Simpson, 2023).
- **Who thinks** (5.1). For Aquinas intellect and will use no organ, so their subject is the soul itself, yet "it is more correct to say that man understands through the soul" (q.75 a.2 ad 2). [`metaphysics` 6.5](../metaphysics/lessons/06-05-what-a-person-is.md)'s claim that forms do not think but composites do is the generic hylomorphist's line, not Aquinas's.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Hempel's dilemma

**What does "physical" mean? If what current physics describes, closure is probably false, since that physics is incomplete; if whatever a completed physics will describe, a mind that moved bodies would count as physical by definition.** Carl Hempel (1969); stated in [`apologetics-foundations` 3.1](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-01-naturalism-at-its-best.md).

- **Against the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad)** (1.1). The derivation is valid, but it needs "physical" to mean the same in D and in C. On the current-physics reading C is probably false; on the completed-physics reading D fails by definition.
- **The replies** ([2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)): tie "physical" to physical theory or to paradigm physical objects (Daniel Stoljar), or define it negatively, as not fundamentally mental: the [via negativa](#via-negativa) (Montero and Papineau, 2005), 1.1's strongest reply.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Via negativa

**Define "physical" negatively: as not fundamentally mental.** Barbara Montero and David Papineau (2005); Jessica Wilson (2006). With it, closure says, substantively, that no fundamentally mental cause is needed for any bodily event: 1.1's strongest reply to [Hempel's dilemma](#hempels-dilemma).

- **The concessions** (1.1). The critic concedes that on this reading the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad) really bites. The defender concedes that it classifies by definition the views on which the fundamental physical is itself experiential, such as [Russellian monism](#russellian-monism).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md)

### Type and token identity

**Type identity says each kind of mental state is a kind of physical state (pain is C-fibre firing); token identity says only that each particular mental event is some physical event or other.** The distinction is [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)'s: the telegram "love and love and love" has two type words and five tokens. "C-fibre firing" is the philosophers' placeholder for whatever neural process turns up.

- **Type identity entails token identity, not conversely** (2.1, 2.3). Token identity is not a weaker type identity: it makes no claim about kinds at all. A type identity restricted to one species is still a type identity.
- **Nonreductive physicalists deny type identity and keep token identity, and so deny D** of the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad) rather than accept it (1.1): functionalists such as Putnam and Fodor ([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)), and Davidson's [anomalous monism](#anomalous-monism) ([2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)). Their reasons differ: Putnam's is that one role has many realizers ([multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability)), Davidson's that the mental answers to standards of rationality nothing physical answers to.
- **Token identity does not answer the exclusion question** ([2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)). A token physicalist grants that the event that is your decision causes the reach; whether it does so *in virtue of* being a decision is the question, and Kim's exit needs type identities, if only local ones.
- **Not type-A and type-B.** Those letters label stances on the epistemic gap ([type-A and type-B physicalism](#type-a-and-type-b-physicalism)).
- **[Kripke's argument](#kripkes-argument) runs on tokens too:** this pain, this brain state.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Russellian monism

**Physics describes only structure and dispositions, what things do; the intrinsic natures that do it are experiential or proto-experiential, and consciousness is made of them.** David Chalmers named the view in 1997. Its sources: in Russell's *The Analysis of Matter* (1927) the causal laws and spatio-temporal relations of events "are more or less known, but their intrinsic character is not known"; for Eddington (1928) the physical atom is "a schedule of pointer readings". The *Stanford Encyclopedia*'s three theses ([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)):

- **R1** [structuralism about physics](#structuralism-about-physics): physics says what mass and charge do, not what they are.
- **R2** realism about [quiddities](#quiddity): properties underlie that structure and categorically ground the dispositions physics describes.
- **R3** those properties wholly or partly constitute consciousness.

The varieties split on R3: Russellian [panpsychism](#panpsychism) (the natures are micro-experiences), [panprotopsychism](#panprotopsychism), [idealism](#idealism) (structure and natures both mental), [neutral monism](#neutral-monism) (both neutral), Russellian physicalism (both physical).

- **On the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad)** (1.1). It keeps M: the wanting is built from the natures that ground the neural cause's powers. It keeps C, and does not break E, since the wanting is not a second cause but part of what the neural powers are powers of. D gets no verdict: true if "physical" means what physics describes (the view is then a dualism or a panpsychism), false if it includes the natures physics leaves out (Russellian physicalism).
- **On the [zombie argument](#zombie-argument)** ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)). With $P$ the complete physical truth, natures included, the Russellian grants that $P \wedge \neg Q$ is conceivable and denies that it is possible: the exit at P2, and the Russellian disjunct of Chalmers's two-dimensional version, which blocks the step from primary to secondary possibility (his type-F). If $P$ is read as physics' structural description only, the zombie world is possible but does not falsify a physicalism that counts the natures as physical: the exit is then at P3. It is neither type-A nor type-B.
- **On Mary and exclusion** (3.5): the zombie and Mary's books capture only our structure, and since consciousness is the categorical side of physical causes, the [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument) has nothing to exclude.
- **The objection** (Robert Howell, 2015; 1.1, 3.5): if a different nature could have grounded the same dispositions, the natures make no difference, so M is kept in name only; the quiddities ride along without causal work.
- **Russell himself was a [neutral monist](#neutral-monism)** ([4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)).
- **Its claim that physics gives only structure** is the metaphysical twin of structural realism ([`philosophy-of-science` 5.3](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/05-03-structural-realism.md)). The [via negativa](#via-negativa) classifies it as non-physicalist by definition.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Unity argument

**One experience of red-while-whistling seems to need one bearer, and no assembly of neural fragments supplies it.** Picture a stadium card stunt: ten thousand fans each hold up one card, together they make a portrait, and nobody in the stands sees it. If your experience were built from many neurons' states, each carrying a fragment, who is across the field? William Hasker (*The Emergent Self*, 1999), as [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md) reconstructs it: (P1) I have experiences whose contents are unified, seeing red and hearing a whistle in one experience; (P2) if I were a complex physical system, each experience would belong to one part of it or consist of states of many parts, each carrying part of its content; (P3) no one part carries the whole content; (P4) states of many parts, each carrying a fragment, do not by themselves amount to one awareness of the whole; so (C) I am not a complex physical system.

- **Weakest premise: P4** (1.2). The physicalist: one awareness of the whole is not a further receiver but the integration of the parts' states, bound together and jointly available for comparison, report and action; P4 looks true only if everything must arrive at one place, the viewer the stadium image smuggles in. The dualist: integration is a fact about access, while P1 reports that one subject *has* both. Concessions: "integration" names a job without yet showing why doing it yields one subject rather than a well-coordinated crowd; a subject without parts restates the unity rather than explaining it.
- **The rival** is the [bundle view](#bundle-view), with co-consciousness doing the unifying.
- **Not Descartes's divisibility argument restated.** Descartes reads "no parts" off introspection, the step Kant's Second Paralogism attacks ([`modern-philosophy` 6.2](../modern-philosophy/lessons/06-02-the-soul-and-the-antinomies.md)); the unity argument argues for it at P4, so the Paralogism objection has to be re-aimed there.
- **Ancestors** (1.2): Leibniz's mill (this course's Boss 1) and the inference from the unity of the "I" to a simple substance that Kant attacked.
- **What follows for Hasker:** the subject is an [emergent](#emergent-dualism) self that brings about brain events the neurons alone would not, so he denies [causal closure](#causal-closure).
- **Whether unity can fail**, as split brains suggest, is [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)'s question ([unity of consciousness](#unity-of-consciousness)).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)

### Non-Cartesian dualism

**You are a substance distinct from your body and from every part of it, but not a purely mental one: through your body you have physical characteristics, including a location.** E. J. Lowe (*Subjects of Experience*, 1996; "Non-Cartesian Substance Dualism and the Problem of Mental Causation", 2006).

- **Against the [bundle view](#bundle-view)** (Lowe, 1996): a seeing cannot be identified except as someone's seeing; take the subject away and nothing makes it this experience rather than an exactly similar one, so the bundle view gets the order of dependence backwards. The bundle theorist replies that an experience may be individuated by its place in a network of co-conscious states.
- **Its answer to the [pairing problem](#pairing-problem):** the self is where its body is, and location pairs it as it pairs gun and victim. Kim's rejoinders (2005): if two exactly similar souls could share a place, location pairs nothing without an impenetrability principle for souls; and a located, extended soul looks like a strange material thing.
- **Not Descartes's view**: Lowe's self has physical characteristics and a location.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)

### Emergent dualism

**A brain organized the right way generates a new individual, the self, as a magnet generates its field; once generated, the self has a causality of its own, including on the brain.** William Hasker (*The Emergent Self*, 1999). The self brings about brain events the neurons alone would not, so Hasker denies [causal closure](#causal-closure) ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)).

- **Not [property dualism](#property-dualism):** Hasker posits a new individual, not only new properties.
- **Its answer to the [pairing problem](#pairing-problem)** is origin and location: this brain generates this self, which is where the brain is.
- **Against [hylomorphism](#hylomorphism)** ([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)). The direction of dependence differs: the brain generates the self, where the form makes the matter a brain. So does the count: a new individual, against one substance. And the origin: for Aquinas the intellectual soul is created, not produced by matter ([`creation-grace-last-things` 2.3](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)). Hasker denies closure; hylomorphists split.
- **Strong emergence** in the sense of [`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md) (5.3).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Bundle view

**There is no subject over and above the experiences, only experiences related to one another.** Hume's version is read in [`modern-philosophy` 4.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/04-04-the-self-belief-and-the-passions.md), and Parfit's reductionism, on which a person's existence consists in a brain, a body and a series of interrelated events, in [`metaphysics` 6.6](../metaphysics/lessons/06-06-personal-identity-over-time.md). Modern Humeans (Parfit; Barry Dainton, *The Phenomenal Self*, 2008) add a relation, co-consciousness, between experiences had together.

- **Against the [unity argument](#unity-argument)** ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)): unified experience is fully described by saying the seeing and the hearing are co-conscious, so no further subject is needed; the bundle theorist denies P4. The whole dispute sits there.
- **Lowe's reply:** experiences cannot be identified independently of their subject ([non-Cartesian dualism](#non-cartesian-dualism)).
- **Against the further fact:** the bundle view against Swinburne's [simple view](#simple-view) is the reductionism dispute of [`metaphysics` 6.6](../metaphysics/lessons/06-06-personal-identity-over-time.md).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)

### Simple view

**Personal identity is a further fact, over and above all bodily and psychological facts: a soul.** Richard Swinburne (*The Evolution of the Soul*, 1986, revised 1997; *Are We Bodies or Souls?*, 2019). Imagine your brain's hemispheres transplanted into two bodies. However much is known about bodies and memories, which survivor, if either, is you still seems a real question, so there must be a further fact ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)). It is the denial of Parfit's reductionism ([`metaphysics` 6.6](../metaphysics/lessons/06-06-personal-identity-over-time.md)).

- **Swinburne argues from identity over time,** not from the unity of experience at a time, which is the [unity argument](#unity-argument)'s premise.
- **His soul reappears in an argument for God** ([`apologetics-foundations` 3.3](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-03-consciousness.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)

### Causal-closure argument

**Conscious states have physical effects; every physical event that has a cause has a sufficient physical cause; those effects are not, as a rule, caused twice over; so conscious states are physical.** David Papineau ("The Rise of Physicalism", 2001; *Thinking about Consciousness*, 2002). It is the [inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad) turned into an argument: grant mental causation and [closure](#causal-closure), add no systematic overdetermination, and distinctness goes ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)).

- **Papineau's evidence for closure is historical.** Conservation of energy left room for non-physical forces that conserve energy; physiology's failure to find any made closure orthodox ([`apologetics-foundations` 3.1](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-01-naturalism-at-its-best.md) tells it).
- **Its reach.** It covers only mental states with physical effects, so [epiphenomenal](#epiphenomenalism) states escape it, and it hits any dualism whose mental states have physical effects, soul or no soul ([property dualism](#property-dualism) included).
- **Dualist replies** (1.2): closure is confirmed only where physiologists have looked; or deny it where physics is indeterministic, since if the laws fix only probabilities a mind could settle what they leave open (Karl Popper and John Eccles, *The Self and Its Brain*, 1977). The quantum gap has a twin in divine action ([`apologetics-foundations` 4.4](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/04-04-divine-action-miracles-and-quantum-indeterminacy.md)).
- **[2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md) turns its machinery on nonreductive physicalism** ([exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument)).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)

### Conservation objection

**A soul that moved the brain would add energy to it, and conservation of energy forbids that.** The older objection to interactionist dualism ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)). Two replies:

- **Conditionality** (Robert Larmer, 1986). Conservation holds for causally isolated systems, and a brain acted on by a soul is not one. That turns the objection into the empirical question of [closure](#causal-closure). Conservation laws follow from symmetries of a closed system, energy from time translation and momentum from space translation ([`analytical-mechanics` 2.2](../analytical-mechanics/lessons/02-02-noethers-theorem.md)), so the reply says a soul makes the brain an open system.
- **Redirection** (C. D. Broad, *The Mind and Its Place in Nature*, 1925, pp. 103-109). Causing need not pass energy: a pendulum's string changes the weight's direction without changing its total energy, and a mind might act likewise. The objection, he wrote, had borrowed "a spurious authority from the august name of the Conservation of Energy."
- **The rejoinder to Broad:** redirecting motion changes momentum, which is also conserved. Leibniz pressed "the conservation of the same total direction in matter" against the view he ascribed to Descartes (*Monadology* §80, Latta, 1898).
- **Not Princess Elisabeth's objection of 1643.** "Energy" is a nineteenth-century concept; she asked how something unextended could push ([`modern-philosophy` 1.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/01-04-the-real-distinction-and-the-interaction-problem.md)).
- **Answering it is not answering the [causal-closure argument](#causal-closure-argument)**, which turns on whether physical causes suffice, not on energy.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)

### Pairing problem

**What makes this soul, and not an exactly similar one, the cause of this body's movement? Physical causes are paired with their effects by spatial relations, which souls outside space lack.** Jaegwon Kim ("Lonely Souls", 2001; *Physicalism, or Something Near Enough*, 2005), crediting John Foster (1991). Kim's case: two guns fire at the same instant and Adam and Bob die; a spatial path, this bullet's trajectory, pairs gun A with Adam. Two exactly alike souls each will at the same instant that an arm rise; if souls are nowhere, no trajectory links the first soul to the first body rather than the second, and since the souls are intrinsically alike, nothing intrinsic does either ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)).

- **Metaphysical, not epistemic.** The question is what pairs them, not how we could know.
- **Individualistic powers** (Foster, 1991; Peter Unger, 2006): the soul has a power to move this body and no other, as a key might open this lock and no identical one. Cost: ordinary powers are general, and it is unclear that a power could be tied to one particular.
- **A located self** (Lowe; Hasker): location pairs it ([non-Cartesian dualism](#non-cartesian-dualism), [emergent dualism](#emergent-dualism)). Kim's rejoinders (2005): co-located exactly similar souls need an impenetrability principle; a located, extended soul looks like a strange material thing. Andrew Bailey, Joshua Rasmussen and Luke Van Horn ("No Pairing Problem", 2011) reply that a soul is not material, wherever it is, because its fundamental properties are mental.
- **Where it strains.** The argument needs the premise that all causing holds partly in virtue of a spatial relation. Kim draws it from physical cases; the dualist says that generalizes from the only cases we can survey, and Bailey and co-authors note it would also rule out any cause of the Big Bang singularity. Kim: without spatial pairing, "this soul caused this" has nothing to make it true. The dualist: the demand assumes the model of physical causation, which is the point at issue.
- **Hylomorphism** ([5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)): an interaction problem arises when two things, each complete in its kind, must be connected; soul and body are not two complete things, so there is nothing to pair. The hylomorphist concedes that this does not answer [closure](#causal-closure), which needs no second thing; [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md) takes that up.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Knowing that, knowing how, and acquaintance

**English marks three ways of knowing by grammar: a fact ("knows that p"), an ability ("knows how to V"), and a thing or quality ("knows N").** [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)'s frame for the replies to Mary.

| Kind | Takes | Example (1.3) |
|---|---|---|
| Knowing that | a fact, true or false, which telling can pass on | she knows that ripe tomatoes reflect mostly long-wavelength light |
| Knowing how | an ability | she knows how to pick a ripe tomato at a glance |
| Knowing by acquaintance | a thing or quality, as you know a city you have lived in | she knows the smell of ammonia |

- **"Knows what it is like" is the contested case.** Its grammar does not settle which kind it is, and the replies to the [knowledge argument](#knowledge-argument) differ over which kind Mary gains: a non-physical fact (Jackson, 1982), an ability ([ability hypothesis](#ability-hypothesis)), acquaintance ([acquaintance hypothesis](#acquaintance-hypothesis)), or an old fact under a new concept ([old fact, new mode](#old-fact-new-mode)).
- **The lines blur.** Jason Stanley and Timothy Williamson (2001) argue that knowing how is a kind of knowing that, under a practical mode.
- **Conee's acquaintance is not the classical foundationalist's** ([`epistemology` 2.2](../epistemology/lessons/02-02-foundationalism.md): error-proof awareness that justifies beliefs). His is knowledge neither factual nor practical.
- **Mary in the room can know that the sky looks blue to people with normal vision**, by being told. What she is said to lack is knowledge available only through the experience.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)

### Knowledge argument

**Mary knows every physical fact about colour vision, learns something on first seeing red, so not every fact is physical.** Frank Jackson, "Epiphenomenal Qualia" (1982) and "What Mary Didn't Know" (1986). Mary is a brilliant neuroscientist who has lived all her life in a black-and-white room and learned, from black-and-white books and monitors, all the physical information about colour vision. Released, she sees a ripe tomato. [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md) uses the explicit form of the *Stanford Encyclopedia*'s "Qualia: The Knowledge Argument":

1. **P1.** Before her release, Mary has complete physical knowledge about human colour vision. (Functional and psychological facts count, if stated without phenomenal terms.)
2. **C1.** So she knows all the physical facts about human colour vision.
3. **P2.** There is some knowledge concerning facts about human colour vision that she lacks before her release.
4. **C2.** So there are facts about human colour vision that she does not know before her release.
5. **C3.** There are non-physical facts about human colour vision (from C1 and C2).

- **C3 is about facts.** The weaker conclusion, that some knowledge is not physical knowledge, is compatible with physicalism; Jackson meant the stronger. Every physicalist grants that Mary's knowledge in the room was incomplete.
- **Jackson's conclusion (1982):** the extra facts concern [qualia](#qualia), so [property dualism](#property-dualism); he added [epiphenomenalism](#epiphenomenalism), a further claim about causation.
- **The replies** (1.3): some physical facts cannot be learned from books (a minority: Gilbert Harman, 1990; Owen Flanagan, 1992); the [ability hypothesis](#ability-hypothesis); the [acquaintance hypothesis](#acquaintance-hypothesis); Dennett's verdict that she learns nothing ([blue banana trick](#blue-banana-trick)); [old fact, new mode](#old-fact-new-mode), generalized as the [phenomenal concept strategy](#phenomenal-concept-strategy) and pressed by the [reference-fixing objection](#reference-fixing-objection); and Jackson's own later exit ([Jackson's recantation](#jacksons-recantation)). Which premise or step each targets is Boss 1's work and is not mapped here.
- **Hume's restored blind man** (*Enquiry* §II ¶15; 1.3's Source) gains a new idea, a way of conceiving colours, and Hume says nothing of a new fact. The argument lives in the gap between Hume's idea and Jackson's fact.
- **Precursor:** C. D. Broad's "mathematical archangel" (1925), who could not predict the smell of ammonia ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), cited in [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)).
- **The intuition is widely shared.** In one survey of non-philosophers, 82 percent said Mary in the room would not know what it is like to see red (Gregory, Hendrickx and Turner, 2022). Which details the verdict tracks is the intuition-pump question of [`philosophical-method` 3.3](../philosophical-method/lessons/03-03-thought-experiments.md).
- **Same shape as Moore's open question argument** ([`ethics` 6.1](../ethics/lessons/06-01-moral-realism.md)): from a gap between concepts to a gap between properties.
- **Its twin is the [zombie argument](#zombie-argument)**, which makes the same move through conceivability; whether the two stand or fall together is Boss 1's question.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)

### Qualia

**The felt qualities of experience: [what it is like](#what-it-is-like) to see red or feel a cramp.** In that broad sense, phenomenal character, almost no one denies them; Jackson's claim (1982) was that no physical information includes them ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)).

- **Narrow senses add more, and that is what deniers deny.** Dennett ("Quining Qualia", 1988) listed four marks qualia are supposed to have: ineffable, intrinsic, private, and directly apprehensible in consciousness ([quining qualia](#quining-qualia)). His coffee tasters Chase and Sanborn are meant to show that a quality cannot be told apart from a change in reaction, so the marks pull apart, the concept is confused, and there are no qualia ([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)). Denying qualia in a narrow sense is not denying that experience has a phenomenal character.
- **Against functionalism** ([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)): could a functional duplicate have no qualitative states at all? ([absent qualia](#absent-qualia)).
- **In perception** ([4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)): intentionalists who also accept non-representational qualia (Christopher Peacocke, 1983; Ned Block) reject strong intentionalism; the 2020 PhilPapers survey's "qualia theory" of perceptual experience, at 15 percent, is roughly theirs.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Ability hypothesis

**Knowing what an experience is like just is having the abilities to remember, imagine and recognize it: knowing how, not knowing that.** Laurence Nemirow (1980, 1990); David Lewis ("What Experience Teaches", 1988).

- **Lewis's case for it** ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)): its only rival is the [hypothesis of phenomenal information](#hypothesis-of-phenomenal-information); that rival contradicts physicalism and, by his Vegemite case, makes the information idle; and abilities explain everything it explains.
- **It grants Mary real knowledge**, knowing how, and denies only that she gains knowing that. The reply on which nothing new happens is Dennett's ([blue banana trick](#blue-banana-trick)).
- **The pressure.** Earl Conee (1994) and Michael Tye (2000) argue that the abilities are neither necessary nor sufficient for knowing what it is like (Tye's fine-grained shade, known while one looks and never recognizable or imaginable afterwards; Conee's Martha, who could imagine cherry red from the shades either side but has not). Stanley and Williamson (2001) argue that knowing how is a kind of knowing that. Loar: "like this" occurs in questions and conditionals (*if the sorbet tastes like this, there is durian in it*), which looks like a new thought, not a new skill. Martine Nida-Rümelin's Marianna has the abilities and can still be wrong about which slide looks the way the sky looks to others.
- **Its best line** (Yuri Cath, 2009): a new state of propositional knowledge, an old proposition under a practical mode, which the *Stanford Encyclopedia* notes puts the view under [old fact, new mode](#old-fact-new-mode).
- **Not the [phenomenal concept strategy](#phenomenal-concept-strategy)**, on which Mary does gain factual knowledge ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)

### Hypothesis of phenomenal information

**Experience gives information: it rules out possibilities that all the physical information left open.** David Lewis's name, in "What Experience Teaches" (1988), for the only rival to the [ability hypothesis](#ability-hypothesis); Jackson's 1982 view in effect affirms it ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)).

- **Lewis's case against it.** It contradicts physicalism. And by his Vegemite case it makes the information idle: two hypotheses about how Vegemite tastes fit all the same physical information, so which one holds makes no physical difference. Abilities explain everything it explains.
- **What would favour it:** a new state that can be mistaken in a way an ability cannot, or that embeds in questions and conditionals (Loar; Nida-Rümelin's Marianna).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)

### Acquaintance hypothesis

**On release Mary gains acquaintance, a third kind of knowledge, direct familiarity with a quality had only by experiencing it, and no new fact.** Earl Conee, "Phenomenal Knowledge" (1994). Michael Tye adopted it in *Consciousness Revisited* (2009), leaving his earlier [old-fact](#old-fact-new-mode) reply ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)).

- **Not [`epistemology` 2.2](../epistemology/lessons/02-02-foundationalism.md)'s acquaintance**, the error-proof awareness that justifies beliefs: Conee's is knowledge neither factual nor practical ([knowing that, knowing how, and acquaintance](#knowing-that-knowing-how-and-acquaintance)).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)

### Blue banana trick

**Shown a blue banana on release, Mary would see through the trick at once, because complete physical knowledge would tell her what to expect.** Daniel Dennett (*Consciousness Explained*, 1991): a Mary who knew everything physical could work out what red looks like, and the contrary verdict comes from failing to imagine "everything" ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)).

- **This, not the [ability hypothesis](#ability-hypothesis), is the reply on which she learns nothing.**
- **It anticipates [illusionism](#illusionism)** ([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)

### Old fact, new mode

**Mary gains new propositional knowledge, under a new, phenomenal concept, of a physical fact she already knew under a physical concept.** Terence Horgan (1984), Brian Loar (1990), Michael Tye (1995), John Perry (2001), David Papineau (2002). No a priori reasoning links the two concepts, which is why it looked like a new fact ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)).

- **The model is indexical.** You have known all morning that your train leaves at 15:04, and at 15:04 you learn that it is leaving *now*: new knowledge, old fact. Knowledge is finer-grained than facts.
- **Its new concept picks out a physical state without revealing its nature** ([2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)).
- **Main objection:** the [reference-fixing objection](#reference-fixing-objection).
- **Generalized**, it is the [phenomenal concept strategy](#phenomenal-concept-strategy) ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)). Tye later moved to the [acquaintance hypothesis](#acquaintance-hypothesis).
- **Its twin in ethics.** Cornell realism's synthetic identities answer Moore's open question argument as this view answers Jackson: two concepts, one property ([`ethics` 6.1](../ethics/lessons/06-01-moral-realism.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)

### Jackson's recantation

**Jackson later gave up his own argument: if qualia are epiphenomenal, they cannot cause our beliefs about them, so no one could know them, and the argument must fail somewhere.** First in print in 1995 ("Postscript on 'What Mary Didn't Know'"); the point had been pressed by Michael Watkins (1989). In "Mind and Illusion" (2003) and a 2007 paper Jackson blamed a false picture of experience and adopted strong representationalism: to be in a phenomenal state is to represent the world as some way, which has a physical account ([first-order representationalism](#first-order-representationalism)) ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)).

- **It attacks the price of Jackson's conclusion** ([epiphenomenal](#epiphenomenalism) qualia), not the verdict that Mary learns something.
- **He did not take up Lewis's abilities.**
- **An author's change of mind refutes nothing.** David Chalmers (*The Conscious Mind*, 1996) and others still defend the argument.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)

### First-order representationalism

**What an experience is like is fixed by what it represents, content of the right kind, and nothing more; no awareness of the state itself is needed.** [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md). Gilbert Harman ("The Intrinsic Quality of Experience", 1990) argued from the [transparency of experience](#transparency-of-experience): attend to your seeing a tomato and you find only more tomato. In outline: introspection finds no feature of an experience beyond those it presents its objects as having; if there were phenomenal features beyond content, attention could find them; so phenomenal character supervenes on representational content.

- **Which representing counts.** Fred Dretske (*Naturalizing the Mind*, 1995): sensory states whose evolved function is to indicate; conscious states are states we are conscious *with*, not *of*. Michael Tye (*Ten Problems of Consciousness*, 1995): PANIC, poised, abstract, nonconceptual intentional content. Tye and Lycan extend it to pain, which represents a disorder at a place in the body.
- **Jackson's later view** (2003, 2007), adopted to diagnose the [knowledge argument](#knowledge-argument) ([Jackson's recantation](#jacksons-recantation)).
- **Cases aimed at it** (3.2): the [inverted spectrum](#inverted-spectrum) (same content, different character) and Ned Block's [Inverted Earth](#inverted-earth) (same character, different content).
- **As a theory of what perceiving is**, the same idea is [intentionalism](#intentionalism) ([4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Reference-fixing objection

**Two modes of presentation of one thing ordinarily reach it through two properties; if the phenomenal mode works that way, its reference-fixing property is phenomenal, and the dualist's property returns one level up.** The strongest reply to [old fact, new mode](#old-fact-new-mode) (David Chalmers, 1996 and 2002; Stephen White, 2007). "Now" picks out 15:04 by a property: being the present moment ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)).

- **Loar's answer:** phenomenal concepts refer directly, with no property between.
- **The concessions** (1.3). The physicalist concedes that Mary learns something propositional, and owes a theory of concepts that refer unmediated. The dualist concedes that new knowledge alone does not entail a new fact, so the argument needs a further premise, which Philip Goff (2017) defends: that a phenomenal concept reveals the nature of what it picks out.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)

### Zombie argument

**A physical duplicate of our world with no experience in it is conceivable, so it is possible, so physicalism is false.** David Chalmers (*The Conscious Mind*, 1996), after Robert Kirk (1974); refined in "Does Conceivability Entail Possibility?" (2002) and "The Two-Dimensional Argument against Materialism" (2010). As [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) states it, with $P$ the complete physical truth (every microphysical fact and law) and $Q$ any truth about experience, such as that someone feels pain:

1. **P1.** $P \wedge \neg Q$ is conceivable.
2. **P2.** If $P \wedge \neg Q$ is conceivable, it is metaphysically possible.
3. **P3.** If $P \wedge \neg Q$ is metaphysically possible, physicalism is false.
4. **C.** Physicalism is false: consciousness is a further fact, so some form of [property dualism](#property-dualism) is true.

Kripke's picture: once God has fixed every physical fact, does he have more to do to make the creatures conscious? A zombie world is one where God stopped early.

- **A zombie duplicates every physical fact about you**, not just your behaviour. A machine that only acts like us raises [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)'s question, and a functional duplicate without experience is [absent qualia](#absent-qualia).
- **"The laws of nature rule zombies out" is no defence of physicalism.** Dualists agree (Chalmers posits fundamental psychophysical laws); P3 needs metaphysical possibility, not physical.
- **"Conceivable" must be strong:** ideal, positive and primary ([conceivability and possibility](#conceivability-and-possibility)). The step to possibility is defended with [two-dimensional semantics](#two-dimensional-semantics), and [Kripke's argument](#kripkes-argument) makes the same point.
- **Its conclusion is property dualism, not [epiphenomenalism](#epiphenomenalism).** A friend of zombies can be an interactionist who denies closure, or a [Russellian monist](#russellian-monism).
- **The physicalist replies** are sorted as [type-A and type-B physicalism](#type-a-and-type-b-physicalism); the Russellian exit is neither.
- **Where it is weakest** (1.4): the step from conceivability to possibility. The strongest objection: conceivability is a fact about concepts, possibility about the world, and Kripke himself showed they come apart. The strongest reply: every known case of their coming apart traces to a term with two intensions, and experience terms have one. The dualist concedes that this rests on a semantic thesis imagining cannot prove; the type-B physicalist concedes a real epistemic gap.
- **The symmetry check** is the [anti-zombie argument](#anti-zombie-argument).
- **Where the profession sits.** In the 2020 PhilPapers survey (N = 1,610), 16.4 percent accepted or leaned towards "inconceivable" for zombies, 36.5 percent "conceivable but not metaphysically possible", and 24.4 percent "metaphysically possible".
- **Russellian physicalism's reply** ([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)): what is conceivable is a duplicate of our structure only.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Conceivability and possibility

**Whether what can be coherently conceived is thereby possible, and which kind of conceiving would show it.** The step is risky: "water is not H2O" seems conceivable and is impossible ([`metaphysics` 3.1](../metaphysics/lessons/03-01-kinds-of-necessity.md)). Chalmers (2002) separates three contrasts, and the [zombie argument](#zombie-argument) needs the second member of each ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)):

| Contrast | First member | Second member |
|---|---|---|
| prima facie / ideal | conceivable at first look | survives ideal rational reflection |
| negative / positive | not ruled out a priori | one can imagine a situation that verifies the claim |
| secondary / primary | conceivable as a counterfactual | conceivable as how the actual world is |

- **"Ideal" makes precise Arnauld's demand**, against Descartes, that the conception be complete ([`modern-philosophy` 1.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/01-04-the-real-distinction-and-the-interaction-problem.md)).
- **[`metaphysics` 3.1](../metaphysics/lessons/03-01-kinds-of-necessity.md)'s checklist, run on the zombie** (1.4, Example 1). *State the scenario:* a world you take to verify $P \wedge \neg Q$, not merely a sentence free of contradiction. *What the conclusion needs:* metaphysical possibility; all sides deny physical possibility, narrow logical possibility is too cheap, and the 1996 book's "logically possible" is meant in a broad sense. *Misdescription test:* for water, Twin Earth was mistaken for the target; Kripke and Chalmers say nothing could be mistaken for the absence of experience but its absence; the type-B candidate is that you imagine a brain from outside and misdescribe the missing act of imagining the feeling as missing feeling (Hill and McLaughlin, 1999, blame distinct cognitive faculties). *Ideal reflection:* Dennett says the conceiving is only prima facie; the dualist, that adding physical detail never adds the feeling. *Symmetry:* the [anti-zombie argument](#anti-zombie-argument). The checklist locates the work in the misdescription and ideal-reflection steps; it settles nothing alone.
- **A priori is a property of a route** ([`epistemology` 4.1](../epistemology/lessons/04-01-a-priori-knowledge.md)).
- **Conceivable but physically impossible** ([6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)): [Blockhead](#blockhead), on any estimate of its size.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Two-dimensional semantics

**A term has two intensions: what it would pick out at a world if that world turned out to be actual (primary), and what, given its actual referent, it picks out at a world considered as counterfactual (secondary).** Chalmers, and Frank Jackson (1998), who built the same machinery. The semantics belongs to [`philosophy-of-language-and-logic`](../philosophy-of-language-and-logic/syllabus.md) (syllabus only); [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) carries the minimal step.

| "water" at | Earth (H2O in the lakes) | Twin Earth (XYZ in the lakes) |
|---|---|---|
| primary intension | H2O | XYZ |
| secondary intension | H2O | H2O |

- **"Water is H2O"** holds at both worlds on the secondary row, so it is necessary; it fails at Twin Earth on the primary row, so it is not a priori. Conceiving water without H2O is conceiving Twin Earth, a real primary possibility misdescribed.
- **Chalmers's step for the [zombie argument](#zombie-argument).** The misdescription move needs a gap between a term's intensions, and terms for experience have none, since whatever would feel like pain at a world is pain there. So for the zombie, primary possibility carries over to secondary possibility, unless [Russellian monism](#russellian-monism) is true: if physics' own terms pick out roles whose intrinsic occupants it never describes, a world could copy the roles without them.
- **Strong necessity.** Type-B physicalism needs a necessity that no world considered as actual violates and no a priori reasoning reveals, unlike water's ([type-A and type-B physicalism](#type-a-and-type-b-physicalism)).
- **Broad's archangel** (1.4, Example 2). "Readily soluble" has a primary intension that is a dispositional profile, which $P$ fixes, so an insoluble duplicate of ammonia is not ideally conceivable. Whether the primary intension of "smells like ammonia" is a functional profile or a felt quality is what the dualist and the type-A and type-B physicalists dispute.
- **Kripke's point and Chalmers's are one:** experience has no gap between how it appears and what it is ([Kripke's argument](#kripkes-argument)).
- **As narrow content** ([4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)): two-dimensional content is one candidate for the [narrow content](#narrow-and-wide-content) duplicates share.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Kripke's argument

**Pain is not C-fibre stimulation: if it were, the identity would be necessary, and its apparent contingency cannot be explained away as heat's can.** Saul Kripke, *Naming and Necessity* (1972/1980), Lecture III. The target is U. T. Place (1956) and J. J. C. Smart (1959), who called "sensations are brain processes" a contingent identity, discovered as lightning's being electrical discharge was ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)).

- **In outline.** "Pain" and "C-fibre stimulation" are [rigid designators](#rigid-designation), and a true identity between rigid designators is necessary. So if the identity holds, the felt possibility of pain without C-fibre stimulation is an [illusion of contingency](#illusion-of-contingency). For heat the illusion can be explained: the reference of "heat" is fixed by a contingent property, producing a certain sensation in us, so imagining heat without molecular motion is really imagining that sensation produced otherwise, or motion felt by no one, and both are possible. "Pain" is fixed by how pain feels, and whatever feels like pain is pain, so there is no stand-in to have mistaken for pain without C-fibres. The identity theorist needs another explanation, and Kripke doubted one would be found.
- **It runs on tokens too:** this pain, this brain state.
- **Kripke's point and Chalmers's are one** ([two-dimensional semantics](#two-dimensional-semantics)): experience has no gap between how it appears and what it is.
- **The physicalist responses** (1.4). The type-B physicalist grants that the identity is necessary and that it seems contingent, and explains the seeming by our concepts rather than by a contingent reference-fixer ([phenomenal concept strategy](#phenomenal-concept-strategy)). Making "pain" mean "whatever occupies the pain role" is the analytic functionalist's route, type-A in Chalmers's terms. Holding the identity contingent is Place and Smart's view before Kripke.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)

### Rigid designation

**A rigid designator picks out the same thing at every possible world where that thing exists.** Saul Kripke (1972/1980). A true identity between rigid designators is necessary ([`metaphysics` 3.1](../metaphysics/lessons/03-01-kinds-of-necessity.md)). The theory of reference belongs to [`philosophy-of-language-and-logic`](../philosophy-of-language-and-logic/syllabus.md) (syllabus only).

- **"Pain", "heat", "water" and "C-fibre stimulation"** are treated as rigid in [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md). So if both terms are rigid, an identity claim must be "necessary, known a posteriori", not contingent as Place and Smart said ([2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)).
- **Rigid is not the same as fixed by an essential property.** "Heat" is rigid though its reference is fixed by a contingent property; "pain", on Kripke's view, is fixed by how pain feels, which is essential to it ([Kripke's argument](#kripkes-argument)).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)

### Illusion of contingency

**The appearance that a necessary identity could have failed.** The literature's label for what Kripke calls the apparent contingency of the connection between a mental state and its brain state ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)).

- **When it can be explained away.** A term's reference is fixed by a contingent property, and what is really possible is the referent without that property, or the property without the referent: molecular motion felt by no one, or that sensation produced otherwise. A nearby possibility is misdescribed ([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)'s flashback runs it on "sound").
- **Kripke: pain's cannot be,** since its reference is fixed by how it feels ([Kripke's argument](#kripkes-argument)).
- **The type-B physicalist explains it otherwise:** phenomenal and physical concepts have no a priori ties, so the identity seems contingent while being necessary ([phenomenal concept strategy](#phenomenal-concept-strategy)).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)

### Type-A and type-B physicalism

**Chalmers's labels for physicalists, sorted by what they deny. Type-A physicalists deny that there is an epistemic gap: no zombie world is ideally conceivable. Type-B physicalists grant the epistemic gap and deny that it shows a gap in what exists: the zombie world is conceivable but not possible.** Chalmers, "Moving Forward on the Problem of Consciousness" (1997) and "Consciousness and its Place in Nature" (2002) ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)).

- **Type-A.** Whoever thinks they conceive a zombie has conceived less than a full physical duplicate (Daniel Dennett, "The Unimagined Preposterousness of Zombies", 1995). Chalmers classes Dennett as type-A (1997). On the hard problem: once every function is explained, including our disposition to insist something is left over, nothing is left, as with "life"; Chalmers replies that life was a function all along (3.1).
- **Type-B.** Experience is identical with a physical state, necessarily and a posteriori (Brian Loar, 1990; Christopher Hill, 1997; Ned Block and Robert Stalnaker, 1999; David Papineau, 2002). The [phenomenal concept strategy](#phenomenal-concept-strategy) is its explanation of why experience still seems separable. Chalmers replies that it needs a *strong* necessity, which no world considered as actual violates and no a priori reasoning reveals, unlike water's. Block and Stalnaker answer that scientific identities are no more derivable a priori, since their terms lack conceptual analyses too; identities are never explained (once Mark Twain is Sam Clemens, "why is he Clemens?" has no answer). Levine and Chalmers with Jackson reply that the scientific identities were explained, by role ([explaining and identifying](#explaining-and-identifying), 3.1).
- **Type-B accepts the necessity.** It denies only that the identity is knowable a priori; holding it contingent is Place and Smart's pre-Kripke view.
- **The letters label stances on the epistemic gap**, not [type and token identity](#type-and-token-identity): Chalmers counts type identity theorists (Hill, Papineau) and representationalists (Tye, Lycan) alike as type-B.
- **Neither type-A nor type-B:** the [Russellian monist](#russellian-monism) (Chalmers's type-F). Panprotopsychism is separated from type-B physicalism only by its clause that truths about the base entail truths about experience a priori ([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)).
- **Illusionists and realists** ([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)). A physicalist can be a realist about phenomenal properties. Chalmers (2018) places the distinctive illusionist among the type-A, with no epistemic gap, only the appearance of one.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Phenomenal concept strategy

**The physicalist's explanation of why experience seems separable from the brain: what is special is our concepts of experience, not the properties they pick out.** Daniel Stoljar's name (2005); the type-B explanation of the appearance ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)). Applied to Mary, it is [old fact, new mode](#old-fact-new-mode) ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)).

- **The concepts.** The concepts you use in thinking of a pain as *this* are recognitional and directly referring (Brian Loar), or quotational, using a sample of the experience (David Papineau, 2002). They have no a priori ties to physical concepts, so one can be conceived without the other though both pick out one state, and an identity needs no explanation.
- **Chalmers's dilemma** ("Phenomenal Concepts and the Explanatory Gap", 2007). Let $C$ be the features the strategy ascribes to these concepts. If $P \wedge \neg C$ is conceivable, $C$ is as physically inexplicable as experience, and the gap has only moved. If not, zombies have $C$ too, yet lack our epistemic situation, which includes knowing our experiences, so $C$ cannot explain it.
- **Not the [ability hypothesis](#ability-hypothesis)**, which denies that Mary gains factual knowledge at all.
- **A realist view, not illusionism** ([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)): phenomenal properties exist and are identified with physical ones.
- **Also a functionalist reply to [absent qualia](#absent-qualia)** ([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Anti-zombie argument

**Our physical duplicate, with nothing non-physical added, and conscious, seems just as conceivable as a zombie; if it is possible, zombies are not.** Keith Frankish, "The Anti-Zombie Argument" (2007): the symmetry step of [`metaphysics` 3.1](../metaphysics/lessons/03-01-kinds-of-necessity.md)'s checklist, run on the zombie ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)).

- **Chalmers's reply:** anti-zombies are not ideally positively conceivable, since we cannot positively conceive physics sufficing for experience ([conceivability and possibility](#conceivability-and-possibility)).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)

### Absent qualia

**Something could fill all your functional roles and have no qualitative states at all.** Ned Block ("Troubles with Functionalism", 1978), with the [China brain](#china-brain): a billion people with two-way radios each do the job of one entry in a machine table that describes you, so for an hour the system is your functional twin ([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)). In outline: the system fills your roles; there is doubt whether it has any [qualia](#qualia) at all, and no independent reason to think it does; functionalism says filling the roles suffices; so, unless the doubt can be explained away, do not accept functionalism about qualitative states. A principle about burdens, not a proof that the system feels nothing.

- **Not the [zombie argument](#zombie-argument)** ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)). A zombie is a *physical* duplicate and the target is physicalism; an absent-qualia case is a *functional* duplicate with different physics, and the target is [functionalism](#functionalism).
- **Replies** (2.2). The doubt is prejudice against systems of unfamiliar size, speed or spread (Dennett, *Brainstorms*, 1978; William Lycan, 1981); Block answers that we know brain-based systems have qualia, because we are such systems, and that the China brain was designed to mimic us. Sydney Shoemaker ("Functionalism and Qualia", 1975): a functional duplicate without qualia would believe everything you believe about yours, so your qualia would do nothing to make those beliefs true and you could not know you have any; since you can, absent qualia are impossible, though which qualia duplicates have might differ. The functionalist concedes that nothing in the case gives positive reason to credit the China brain with feeling; Block concedes that his argument is partly empirical.
- **Fading qualia** ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)): Chalmers (1995; 1996) answers it by running the brain-simulator case neuron by neuron inside one head ([fading qualia](#fading-qualia)).
- **The [inverted spectrum](#inverted-spectrum) and Inverted Earth** were also first aimed at functionalism ([3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

**Module 2: Physicalism and mental causation**

### Category mistake

**Taking something for one more item of a kind when it belongs to another logical type: the university is not one more building beside the colleges.**

Gilbert Ryle, *The Concept of Mind* (1949). A visitor is shown the colleges, libraries, playing fields and offices and then asks where the University is. He takes it for another item of the same kind as the buildings, when it is the way they are organized. Ryle charged the Cartesian picture, which he called the official doctrine and, with deliberate abusiveness, the dogma of "the Ghost in the Machine", with the same mistake: it treats a mind as a second, invisible thing behind the body's doings. To call someone clever, vain or careful is to say how they act and would act, not to report a hidden episode.

- **Ryle does not deny that people think and feel.** He denies that "mind" names a further thing behind what they do.
- **Whether he was a logical behaviourist is disputed.** He is usually filed there; the Stanford Encyclopedia's entry on him (revised 2026) argues he held none of the doctrine's main tenets, his dispositional statements being licences to infer, not analyses. See [logical behaviourism](#logical-behaviourism).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)

### Logical behaviourism

**Every meaningful statement about a mind can be rewritten, with no loss of meaning, as a statement about behaviour and dispositions to behave.**

Rudolf Carnap, "Psychologie in physikalischer Sprache" (1932/33); Carl Hempel, "The Logical Analysis of Psychology" (English version 1949). Its root is verificationism: what would verify a claim about another mind is behaviour. The form each analysis must take (2.1's P1): x is M if and only if x would do B in circumstances C, with B and C described in non-mental terms. Two things broke it: the [circularity](#circularity-of-behavioural-analyses) of belief and desire (Roderick Chisholm, *Perceiving*, 1957; Peter Geach, *Mental Acts*, 1957), and Putnam's [super-Spartans](#super-spartans) and perfect actor ("Brains and Behavior", 1963, published 1965). 2.1's conclusion: no mental predicate has a purely behavioural analysis. Hempel had abandoned the programme by 1966.

- **A thesis about meaning, not about method.** The case against it targets the logical kind only. John Watson's methodological rule (psychology studies behaviour) and B. F. Skinner's psychological behaviourism are untouched.
- **Revealed preference has both readings** ([`philosophy-of-economics` 2.1](../philosophy-of-economics/lessons/02-01-preference-and-revealed-preference.md)). Defining "preference" by choice is logical behaviourism about one word; the rule that economics models choices, not minds, is methodological.
- **Ryle's place in it is disputed** ([category mistake](#category-mistake)).
- **Bridge.** Judging a network only by its input-output behaviour treats it as the behaviourist treats a mind; probing its internal states is the identity theorist's move ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) takes this up with Othello-GPT).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)

### Circularity of behavioural analyses

**A belief shows up in behaviour only together with desires, and a desire only together with beliefs, so every behavioural analysis of one mental term smuggles in another.**

Chisholm (*Perceiving*, 1957) and Geach (*Mental Acts*, 1957); P2 of 2.1's case against [logical behaviourism](#logical-behaviourism). 2.1's Example 1: Isolde believes the lake ice is too thin to bear her, yet walks out after her dog when it goes through, believing all the while that the ice is thin. The repair "unless she wants something more than she wants to stay dry" names a desire, and analysing that desire brings a belief back.

- **The circle is not vicious in itself.** Belief and desire can be given behavioural content together, as two unknowns in one system of equations: the Ramsey-sentence move of [analytic functionalism](#analytic-functionalism). What it rules out is a term-by-term translation that leaves the mental vocabulary behind.
- **Bridge.** Reading an agent's reward off its trajectories meets the same circle: one trajectory fits many pairs of goal and world model. Daniel Hausman finds it in revealed preference ([`philosophy-of-economics` 2.1](../philosophy-of-economics/lessons/02-01-preference-and-revealed-preference.md)).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)

### Super-Spartans
