**Putnam's people who feel pain but have trained away every disposition to pain behaviour; with his perfect actor, they show that the behaviour is neither necessary nor sufficient for the pain.**

Hilary Putnam, "Brains and Behavior" (1963, published 1965). Super-Spartans suppress every involuntary sign of pain, with no wince, groan or flinch, and if asked will admit in a level voice that it hurts. Putnam then imagines descendants who no longer even say so. They answer the necessity of the behavioural condition (2.1's P3); the perfect actor, with every pain disposition and no pain, answers its sufficiency (P4).

- **Putnam's moral.** If they are in pain, the pain is what causes pain behaviour when there is any, and a cause is not built out of its effects.
- **The case removes every disposition.** No wince, no groan, no flinch, and in the descendants no report either; a case that removes only one disposition is weaker.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)

### Identity theory

**Sensations are brain processes, kind for kind, in the way lightning turned out to be electric discharge.**

U. T. Place, "Is Consciousness a Brain Process?" (*British Journal of Psychology*, 1956), which came out of arguments at Adelaide with C. B. Martin and with J. J. C. Smart, then a Rylean, whom it converted; Herbert Feigl, "The 'Mental' and the 'Physical'" (1958); Smart, "Sensations and Brain Processes" (*Philosophical Review*, 1959). The thesis concerns sensations and consciousness, offered as a contingent, discovered identity, like lightning's. Place kept Ryle's dispositions for belief and desire; D. M. Armstrong (*A Materialist Theory of the Mind*, 1968) identified those with brain states too. It is the founding [reductive physicalism](#reductive-physicalism), and a [type identity](#type-and-token-identity) is the strongest bridge a reduction can have ([`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md)). Smart's case for it is the [argument from simplicity](#argument-from-simplicity).

- **The meaning objection, and the reply.** People who know nothing of neurons talk perfectly well about their pains, so "pain" cannot mean "C-fibre firing". Place's reply, which Smart restated in terms of meaning and reference: grant the premise, deny the inference. "Lightning" never meant "electric discharge", and the identity theory is a claim about what is picked out.
- **The next objection** is the [distinct property argument](#distinct-property-argument), which Smart met with the [topic-neutral analysis](#topic-neutral-analysis).
- **"C-fibre firing"** is the philosophers' placeholder for whatever neural process turns up.
- **The founders called it contingent.** A true identity between rigid terms is necessary ([`metaphysics` 3.1](../metaphysics/lessons/03-01-kinds-of-necessity.md)), so if both terms are rigid the claim must be "necessary, known a posteriori", and [Kripke's argument](#kripkes-argument) presses on exactly that ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)): something else could look like lightning, but whatever feels like pain is pain.
- **It keeps pain.** It says what pain is, as kinetic theory kept temperature. Dropping the mental vocabulary is [eliminative materialism](#eliminative-materialism).
- **Weakest point (2.1): the correlation premise.** Putnam's [multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability) (1967) says pain occurs in creatures that share no kind of neural process with us. The strongest reply restricts the identities, one neural kind per species: Kim's local reductions, the restricted types Smart himself allowed, and Thomas Polger and Lawrence Shapiro (*The Multiple Realization Book*, 2016), who add that real realizers are less unlike than advertised. The identity theorist concedes that "pain" then names no single physical kind across species; the functionalist concedes that each actual pain may still be a neural process of a kind shared within its species, and that unlike realizers have to be shown, not merely imagined.
- **In 2.2's terms** the unrestricted theory is chauvinist about the octopus ([liberalism and chauvinism](#liberalism-and-chauvinism)), and realizer functionalism is an identity theory reached by a functional route ([role and realizer](#role-and-realizer)).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)

### Argument from simplicity

**Since each kind of sensation correlates with a kind of brain process, the simpler hypothesis is that each pair is one thing, which leaves science no dangling psychophysical laws.**

J. J. C. Smart (1959). Smart invoked Ockham's razor; Place (1956) did not. In outline (2.1's P5 to P8, conclusion C2):

1. Each kind of sensation is correlated with a kind of brain process (an empirical bet that neuroscience was expected to win).
2. If a sensation is not the process, each correlation is a fundamental law tying a complex neural process to something simple and non-physical, what Feigl called a "nomological dangler".
3. Everywhere else, science increasingly shows organisms to be physicochemical mechanisms.
4. Of two hypotheses that fit the same evidence, prefer the one with fewer fundamental entities and laws.
5. So each kind of sensation is a kind of brain process.

- **Weakest premise (2.1): the first.** [Multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability) denies there is one brain kind per sensation; the reply is restricted, species-relative identities ([identity theory](#identity-theory)).
- **A correlation is not an identity.** However exact and type-for-type, a correlation is what the identity hypothesis is offered to explain; a dualist accepts the same correlations and denies the identity.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)

### Distinct property argument

**If "pain" and "brain process" differ in meaning yet pick out one thing, "pain" must pick it out by some property of its own; if that property is irreducibly mental, dualism comes back at the level of properties.**

Max Black, reported by Smart in "Sensations and Brain Processes" (1959). "Lightning" picks out the discharge by a property of its own, a bright flash. By what property does "pain" pick out the brain process? If by an irreducibly mental one, the danglers of the [argument from simplicity](#argument-from-simplicity) come back as properties, and the view becomes a [property dualism](#property-dualism) about an event that is physical. Smart's answer was the [topic-neutral analysis](#topic-neutral-analysis).

- **Not the meaning objection.** That one says "pain" does not mean "brain process", and lightning answers it. Black grants the difference in meaning and asks what the different meaning picks the process out by.
- **Forward.** It is the ancestor of the explanatory gap ([3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Topic-neutral analysis

**A paraphrase of a sensation report that says only that something is going on in me like what goes on in the typical case, neutral between brain process and ghostly episode.**

Smart (1959), with a term adapted from Ryle. A report of a yellowish-orange after-image says only that something is going on in me like what goes on when I am awake, eyes open, with an orange in good light before me. "Something going on" is neutral between brain process and ghostly episode, so the report ascribes no mental property, and science says what the something is. It answers the [distinct property argument](#distinct-property-argument).

- **Chalmers's dilemma** (*The Conscious Mind*, 1996). Read broadly, the phrase fits countless processes that are no experience; narrowed to experiences, it is no longer neutral. Smart replied that "typically" does the narrowing.
- **Neutral means neutral.** The paraphrase earns its keep only if it uses no word, such as "experience", "seems" or "sensation", that puts the mental property back.
- **Where the lightning analogy stops.** Whether the paraphrase says everything "pain" says is a question about our concept, and the analogy assumed the answer. The identity theorist says nothing is left over; the property dualist says the felt quality is exactly what is left ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)).
- **Forward.** The bridge from here to functionalism runs through David Lewis's "An Argument for the Identity Theory" (1966) and Armstrong's causal analysis.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Multiple realizability

**One mental kind can be had by creatures whose physical make-up shares no relevant kind: the same pain in a human and in an octopus.**

The general notion (one higher-level kind, many physically unlike ways of being it), Fodor's argument and Shapiro's test are taught in [`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md); this course runs it on the mind. Hilary Putnam, "Psychological Predicates" (1967; reprinted as "The Nature of Mental States"), against the [identity theory](#identity-theory) taken as an empirical hypothesis, in outline: creatures with very different nervous systems can be in pain (mammals, birds, reptiles, molluscs; octopuses, he remarks, certainly feel pain); if pain is a type of brain state, one physical-chemical state is common to every creature in pain; there is probably no such state, and one psychological state realized differently would sink the hypothesis; so pain is not a type of brain state.

2.2's case: Robyn Crook's *Octopus bocki* (*iScience*, 2021), given dilute acetic acid in one arm, grooms the spot, avoids the chamber where it was hurt, and prefers one where a local anaesthetic relieved it; the anaesthetic also silences the arm's nerves. Its nervous system is built on a different plan from ours, with much of the control in the arms.

- **Shapiro's test.** Realizers are genuinely different only if they differ in properties that do the job. The octopus passes, since its control is distributed through the arms. A network's permuted hidden units do not: same job, labels moved ([functionalism](#functionalism)).
- **It does not by itself refute identity.** The replies are kind-relative identities (Lewis 1969: pain in humans is one neural state, in molluscs another), Kim's local reductions, and Polger and Shapiro's (2016) claim that real realizers are less unlike than advertised ([identity theory](#identity-theory)).
- **Not Davidson's reason.** [Anomalous monism](#anomalous-monism) also denies type identity, but because the mental answers to standards of rationality, not because one role has many realizers (2.3).
- **In the exclusion argument** it is the ground of the irreducibility claim: the decision is not the neural state that realizes it, since a differently built brain could make it ([exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument), 2.4).
- **Hylomorphism allows it (5.1).** Powers are individuated by acts and objects (q.77 a.3), so a fly's sight and a cat's are one power with two kinds of eye. What the hylomorphist denies is that the role is the soul.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Functionalism

**A mental state is whatever state plays a certain causal role: pain is what is caused by damage and causes guarding, avoidance and the wish that it stop, whatever it is made of.**

Beliefs and desires are defined the same way, by their relations to perception, behaviour and each other. The versions: Putnam's [machine functionalism](#machine-functionalism) (1967), then [analytic functionalism](#analytic-functionalism) and [psychofunctionalism](#psychofunctionalism), which differ over where the roles come from. [Role or realizer](#role-and-realizer) is the further question of what the mental property is. The objections are Ned Block's ("Troubles with Functionalism", 1978): [liberalism and chauvinism](#liberalism-and-chauvinism), and the [China brain](#china-brain)'s [absent qualia](#absent-qualia).

**2.2's network gloss.** Permute the $m$ hidden units of a one-hidden-layer network, each carrying its weights with it, and the output is unchanged: $m!$ weight settings for one computation. With ReLU units you can also multiply a unit's incoming weights and bias by any $\alpha > 0$ and its outgoing weight by $1/\alpha$, since $\max(0, \alpha z) = \alpha \max(0, z)$ for any pre-activation $z$. The pattern of inputs, internal states and outputs is a role; a weight setting is one realizer of it. (The many-settings point is the course's own gloss; the universal approximation theorem is [`deep-learning` 1.2](../deep-learning/lessons/01-02-universal-approximation.md).) By Shapiro's test a permutation is not a genuinely different realization: it does the job the same way with the labels moved.

- **Not behaviourism.** The states are defined partly by their relations to each other, and those internal relations are the difference.
- **Not a denial that pains are physical.** It denies [type identity](#type-and-token-identity) and keeps token physicalism, as Putnam and Fodor do. It is even officially neutral, since an immaterial state could fill a role; physicalists adopt it because they expect only physical states do.
- **Homonyms.** Not Durkheim's functional explanation ([`social-theory` 1.2](../social-theory/lessons/01-02-functional-explanation-and-its-critics.md)) and not proper functionalism ([`epistemology` 2.4](../epistemology/lessons/02-04-reliabilism.md)).
- **"Aristotle was the first functionalist" is disputed** ([`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 3.5](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/03-05-soul-and-intellect.md): Nussbaum and Putnam against Burnyeat). [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md) asks whether hylomorphism is [functionalism renamed](#functionalism-renamed-charge), and [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md) sets the functionalist against the Thomist on [vehicle and content](#vehicle-and-content).
- **Later qualia objections.** The inverted spectrum and Inverted Earth ([3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)) were first aimed at functionalism, as absent qualia were.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Machine functionalism

**Putnam's first version: model the organism as a probabilistic automaton and identify each mental state with a state in its machine table.**

Putnam, "Psychological Predicates" (1967). A probabilistic automaton is a [Turing machine](../theory-of-computation/lessons/03-01-turing-machines.md) with stochastic transitions; a state in its machine table is defined by how it takes inputs and the current state to outputs and the next state. Ned Block and Jerry Fodor ("What Psychological States Are Not", 1972) objected that finitely many machine states cannot match the open-ended range of thoughts, so later functionalists define each state by its place in a whole theory ([analytic functionalism](#analytic-functionalism), [psychofunctionalism](#psychofunctionalism)).

- **One form of functionalism, not the computational theory of mind.** [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) separates the two ([computational theory of mind](#computational-theory-of-mind)).
- **The China brain realizes your machine table,** so machine functionalism must say it is in pain whenever it is in the pain state (2.2, Example 2).

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Analytic functionalism

**The roles come from the platitudes of everyday psychology, so a functional definition says what the mental words mean.**

David Armstrong, *A Materialist Theory of the Mind* (1968); David Lewis, "Psychophysical and Theoretical Identifications" (1972). Conjoin the platitudes, replace each mental term with a variable and quantify: the theory's Ramsey sentence ([`philosophy-of-science` 5.3](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/05-03-structural-realism.md)). 2.2's toy version:

$$\exists x\,\exists y\,\big[\,\text{damage causes } x \;\wedge\; x \text{ causes } y \;\wedge\; x \text{ causes wincing}\,\big],$$

with $x$ in pain's place and $y$ in the place of the desire that it stop. A creature is in pain when it is in the state that fills the $x$-place in its realization of the whole sentence.

- **It dissolves 2.1's circle.** The mental terms are defined all at once, by the pattern, with no mental word left over: belief and desire become two unknowns in one system ([circularity of behavioural analyses](#circularity-of-behavioural-analyses)).
- **Only the mental terms become variables.** Words for damage, inputs and behaviour stay as they are.
- **Charge: liberal.** Platitude roles might be filled by the economy of Bolivia (Block), and the China system satisfies the Ramsey sentence of the platitudes ([liberalism and chauvinism](#liberalism-and-chauvinism)).

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Psychofunctionalism

**The roles come from empirical cognitive psychology instead of common sense, so they can be drawn finer than the platitudes.**

Jerry Fodor, *Psychological Explanation* (1968); Block and Fodor, "What Psychological States Are Not" (1972).

- **Charge: chauvinist.** Roles drawn at the grain of human psychology shut out creatures only roughly like us (Block).
- **The grain problem (2.2, Example 2).** If the roles include the fine timing and chemistry of human pain processing, the China system may fail them. But a grain fine enough to exclude it threatens to exclude the octopus too, whose psychology differs from ours in detail.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Role and realizer

**The role is the property of being in some state or other that does the job; the realizer is the state that actually does it in a given creature.**

Is pain the property of being in some state that fills the pain role, or the state that fills it? Role functionalists say the first: pain is a second-order property, having some state that fills the role, so an octopus and you share one property. Lewis (1966) and Armstrong said the second, and Lewis (1969, reviewing the volume in which Putnam's paper appeared) made the identities relative to a kind: pain in humans is one neural state, pain in molluscs another, the restricted identities of 2.1.

- **Realizer functionalism is not a halfway house.** It is an [identity theory](#identity-theory) with a functional recipe for finding the identities. Only role functionalism lets the octopus share your mental property.
- **Shoemaker's use.** Individuation by role is what Sydney Shoemaker's answer to the too-many-thinkers puzzle relies on: if a belief's role includes persistence conditions, the human animal, whose persistence conditions are biological, has no beliefs of its own. The verdict is [`metaphysics` 6.5](../metaphysics/lessons/06-05-what-a-person-is.md)'s.
- **The causal question it raises.** If pain is a role property, does the role or its realizer cause the wince? That is 2.3 and 2.4's topic ([exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument)).
- **5.1's functionalist premise** is that a functional state is individuated by its causal role, the premise the "functionalism renamed" charge starts from.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Liberalism and chauvinism

**A theory of mind is too liberal if it credits minds to things that lack them, and too chauvinist if it denies minds to things that have them.**

Ned Block, "Troubles with Functionalism" (1978). The [identity theory](#identity-theory) is chauvinist about the octopus. Functionalism, Block argued, overshoots: platitude roles ([analytic functionalism](#analytic-functionalism)) might be filled by the economy of Bolivia, while psychofunctional roles drawn at the grain of human psychology ([psychofunctionalism](#psychofunctionalism)) shut out creatures only roughly like us.

- **The dilemma is about grain.** Functionalism says what to look for, a role, but not at what grain it must be matched, and the grain is what the sides dispute. The functionalist says the best psychology, not an intuition, should fix it; Block says the theory has nothing in its favour that outweighs the doubt.
- **Each charge is relative to a verdict on a case.** Settle whether the case is in pain before naming the charge: a theory that counts in what the ordinary verdict leaves out is too liberal there, and one that leaves out what the verdict counts in is too chauvinist there.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### China brain

**Block's case: a billion people with radios run a machine table that describes you for an hour, and it is doubtful that the system feels anything.**

Ned Block, "Troubles with Functionalism" (1978). Each of a billion people (he chose China because it had a billion inhabitants) gets a two-way radio linking them to one another and to an artificial body; the current machine state is displayed on satellites; each person does the job of one entry in a machine table that describes you. For an hour the system is functionally equivalent to you.

Block's [absent qualia](#absent-qualia) argument, at his own strength, in outline: the China-body system fills the roles you fill; there is doubt whether it has any qualitative states at all, and no independent reason to think it does; functionalism says whatever fills your roles has your qualitative states; so, unless the doubt can be explained away, we should not accept functionalism about qualitative states. It is a principle about burdens, not a proof that the system feels nothing.

- **Weakest premise (2.2): the doubt,** which rests on an intuition. The prejudice reply (Daniel Dennett, *Brainstorms*, 1978; William Lycan, "Form, Function, and Feel", 1981): the intuition is bias against a system of unfamiliar size, speed or spread. Block grants that no mechanism, a brain least of all, looks like a seat of feeling, then draws two contrasts: we know brain-based systems can have qualia, because we are such systems; and the China brain was designed to mimic us, so its winces are best explained by the design.
- **Shoemaker's reply** ("Functionalism and Qualia", 1975). A functional duplicate lacking qualia would believe everything you believe about yours, so your qualia would do nothing to make those beliefs true, and you could not know you have any. Since you can, he concluded, absent qualia are impossible. He allowed that *which* qualia duplicates have might differ (the [inverted spectrum](#inverted-spectrum), 3.2).
- **What each side concedes.** The functionalist: nothing in the case gives positive reason to credit the system with feeling. Block: his argument is partly empirical and falls to any explanation that dissolves the doubt.
- **Not the zombie argument.** A [zombie](#zombie-argument) is a *physical* duplicate, aimed at physicalism; the China brain is a *functional* duplicate with very different physics, aimed at functionalism.
- **Not a neuron count.** Block chose China for its billion inhabitants, and each person implements an entry of a machine table, not a neuron; later glosses that wire people up as neurons are not Block's set-up.
- **Cousins.** Searle's [Chinese room](#chinese-room) ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)) asks the same question about understanding instead of feeling. For the hylomorphist ([5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)), a nation running the roles is not one substance, so it has no soul.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Anomalous monism

**Every mental event that causes or is caused is a physical event, yet there are no strict laws in mental terms: one kind of event, with the mental escaping strict law.**

Donald Davidson, "Mental Events" (1970), about propositional attitudes (believing, wanting, intending), not pains. He derives it from [Davidson's three principles](#davidsons-three-principles) together with extensional causation, so the monism comes a priori, while type theorists awaited correlations. The result is [token identity without type identity](#type-and-token-identity): each mental event is some physical event, though mental kinds need not match physical kinds. Davidson added [supervenience](#supervenience), which he later said was only [weak](#weak-supervenience).

- **It denies strict laws, not regularities.** Believers in rain usually reach for umbrellas, other things being equal ([strict law](#strict-law)).
- **Not less than physical.** Each mental event *is* a physical event; only kinds fail to line up. Type identity entails token identity, not the reverse.
- **Not [multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability).** Both deny type identity. Putnam's reason is that one role has many realizers; Davidson's is that the mental answers to standards of rationality, charity, that nothing physical answers to.
- **Scope.** The argument concerns attitudes; about sensations, the identity theorist's paradigm, it says nothing.
- **The charge it left.** Critics replied that mental events then cause only *as* physical events: the [quausation](#quausation) charge, which Kim's [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument) carries over to every nonreductive view (2.4).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Davidson's three principles

**Some mental events cause physical ones; causes fall under strict laws; there are no strict laws about the mental. They look inconsistent, and Davidson argues they entail monism.**

Davidson, "Mental Events" (1970), in 2.3's numbering:

1. **Causal interaction.** Some mental events cause, or are caused by, physical events.
2. **The nomological character of causality.** Events related as cause and effect fall under [strict laws](#strict-law).
3. **The anomalism of the mental.** There are no strict laws on the basis of which mental events can be predicted or explained.

The first two seem to demand strict psychophysical laws, which the third forbids. Two further premises resolve the tension. **P4:** causation is extensional, holding between events however described, while laws hold of events only as described (a balloon's bursting at a party is also the event that woke the baby, and only the first description appears in a law of gas pressure). **P5:** only physical vocabulary promises a closed system of strict laws. **Conclusion:** every causally interacting mental event has a physical description under which it falls under strict law, so it is a physical event ([anomalous monism](#anomalous-monism)).

- **The ground of anomalism is charity.** We interpret a person so as to make her largely consistent and right by our lights, revising any attribution in the light of all the rest; no physical predicate answers to that standard.
- **Weakest premise (2.3): the cause-law principle.** Elizabeth Anscombe (*Causality and Determination*, 1971) and Nancy Cartwright (*How the Laws of Physics Lie*, 1983) deny that singular causation needs strict laws, and nobody can state one. Davidson's strongest reply ("Laws and Cause", 1995): only predicates figuring in laws mark genuine changes, so causing presupposes law, and strict laws need only exist, not be known. The critic concedes that something general must back singular causal claims; Davidson concedes that we know no strict law, and that in 1970 he assumed the principle without argument.
- **Rivals leave at different premises (2.3's map).** The parallelist denies interaction; Anscombe and Cartwright deny the cause-law principle; the type-identity theorist denies anomalism. Kim grants the conclusion and asks what the mental then does (2.4).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Strict law

**An exceptionless law whose antecedent includes everything that could prevent the effect, as against a generalization that holds only other things being equal.**

Davidson's term. Anomalism denies strict laws about the mental and grants other-things-equal (ceteris paribus) generalizations about believers, whose exceptions are further attitudes, answerable to all the rest, so the list never closes (2.3, Example 1).

- **The pressure point.** Nobody can state a strict law, which is the case against the cause-law principle (Anscombe 1971; Cartwright 1983). Davidson: strict laws need only exist, not be known ("Laws and Cause", 1995).
- **Kim's point.** Strong supervenience over a Boolean-closed base yields one-way psychophysical laws strict enough to break anomalism ([strong supervenience](#strong-supervenience)).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Reasons as causes

**A reason explains an action by causing it: of several reasons that would each make an act intelligible, the one you acted on is the one that caused it.**

Davidson, "Actions, Reasons, and Causes" (1963). You expect rain, and you also want to hide your face from a neighbour. Either reason would make opening the umbrella intelligible, yet you may have acted on only one, and what makes it *the* reason is that it caused the action. So rationalizing is causal explanation under a mental description: the classic yes to the question that [`social-theory` 1.3](../social-theory/lessons/01-03-understanding-webers-interpretive-method.md) (Weber's two adequacies) and [`philosophy-of-science` 4.2](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-02-causal-explanation.md) (interventionist causes) send here.

- **Causes of actions, not yet of movements.** It makes reasons causes of actions, not yet of the bodily movements that the interaction principle of [Davidson's three principles](#davidsons-three-principles) needs.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Supervenience

**No mental difference without a physical difference: the dots fix the picture.**

Davidson (1970) added that no two events could be alike physically yet differ mentally; Jaegwon Kim ("Concepts of Supervenience", 1984) separated the readings. David Lewis's analogy: a dot-matrix picture's symmetry is nothing but the pattern of dots, so no two pictures alike dot for dot differ in symmetry. Supervenience makes the mental the symmetry and the physical the dots. The slogan hides choices: compare things within one world ([weak](#weak-supervenience)), across [possible worlds](../metaphysics/lessons/03-02-what-possible-worlds-are.md) ([strong](#strong-supervenience)), or as whole worlds ([global](#global-supervenience)); and with what force, nomological or metaphysical ([minimal physicalism](#minimal-physicalism)). Only one combination can state [physicalism](#physicalism).

**Notation (2.3).** $w, w'$ range over possible worlds and $x, y$ over individuals; $M$ is a family of mental properties and $P$ of physical (base) properties. Write $x_w \sim_P y_{w'}$ ("$x$ in $w$ is $P$-indiscernible from $y$ in $w'$") when for every $F$ in $P$, $x$ has $F$ in $w$ if and only if $y$ has $F$ in $w'$; likewise $\sim_M$ for $M$.

- **Covariation, not dependence or reduction.** A dualist with necessary psychophysical connections accepts it, and an emergentist too if the necessity is nomological. It reports covariation, not why it holds; why modal patterns underdetermine "in virtue of" is [`metaphysics` 2.4](../metaphysics/lessons/02-04-essence-and-grounding.md).
- **At least five theses.** Weak and strong individual; weak, intermediate and strong global. Davidson's is the weakest individual one; physicalism needs strong global.
- **Elsewhere in the library.** Mentalism makes justification supervene on mental states ([`epistemology` 1.4](../epistemology/lessons/01-04-internalism-and-externalism.md)); Lewis's Humean supervenience makes laws supervene on the mosaic ([`metaphysics` 4.1](../metaphysics/lessons/04-01-humes-challenge-and-the-regularity-theory.md)); the statue and its lump ([`metaphysics` 5.3](../metaphysics/lessons/05-03-coincidence-and-the-ship-of-theseus.md)) are where the weaker global variants were put to use. The [zombie argument](#zombie-argument) attacks the metaphysical supervenience of the phenomenal on the physical (1.4).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Weak supervenience

**Inside any one world, physical twins are mental twins.**

$$\forall w\;\forall x, y \in w\;(x_w \sim_P y_w \rightarrow x_w \sim_M y_w)$$

Kim (1984); notation at [supervenience](#supervenience). Davidson said his supervenience was only weak (1985; "Thinking Causes", 1993), after Kim argued that the strong reading would commit him to strict psychophysical laws.

- **It never compares individuals across worlds,** so the same physical type may go with different mental types in different worlds.
- **It cannot state physicalism (2.3, Example 2).** $P = \{N\}$, being in brain-state type $N$; $M = \{R\}$, believing that it is raining. In $w_1$, $x$ and $y$ are $N$ and $R$, and $u$ is neither; in $w_2$, $z$ and $z'$ are $N$ and not $R$, and $v$ is neither. Weak holds, strong fails, and the map $x \mapsto z$, $y \mapsto z'$, $u \mapsto v$ is $N$-preserving, so $w_2$ is a physical duplicate of $w_1$ in which nobody believes it is raining.
- **Weak without strong owes an explanation** of the [ban on mixed worlds](#ban-on-mixed-worlds).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)

### Strong supervenience

**Physical twins anywhere in modal space, in the same world or in different worlds, are mental twins.**

$$\forall w, w'\;\forall x \in w\;\forall y \in w'\;(x_w \sim_P y_{w'} \rightarrow x_w \sim_M y_{w'})$$

Kim (1984); notation at [supervenience](#supervenience).

- **Strong implies weak.** Setting $w' = w$ in strong gives weak: a ban on mismatches anywhere includes a ban on mismatches at home. The converse fails ([weak supervenience](#weak-supervenience), Example 2).
- **Kim's point against Davidson (1984).** Over a base closed under Boolean operations (negation, and infinite conjunction and disjunction), strong supervenience makes each mental property anything has necessitated by some physical property: a one-way psychophysical law strict enough to break anomalism. Davidson answered that his supervenience was only weak.
- **Checking a model.** Group the individuals of every world in play by their $P$-profile; strong supervenience fails exactly when two individuals with the same profile, in the same world or not, differ in $M$.
- **Force depends on the worlds in play.** Over the worlds with our laws of nature it is nomological supervenience, which a dualist with fundamental psychophysical laws accepts; physicalism needs it with metaphysical necessity ([minimal physicalism](#minimal-physicalism)).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)

### Global supervenience

**Compare whole worlds: worlds that match physically match mentally.**

An isomorphism $f$ from $w$ to $w'$ is a one-to-one correspondence between their individuals; it is $P$-preserving if $x_w \sim_P f(x)_{w'}$ for every $x$ in $w$, and $M$-preserving likewise.

- *Strong global:* every $P$-preserving isomorphism is $M$-preserving.
- *Intermediate:* if some isomorphism is $P$-preserving, some isomorphism preserves both families.
- *Weak global:* if some isomorphism is $P$-preserving, some is $M$-preserving.

*In words:* physically matching worlds match mentally under the same pairing always (strong), at least once (intermediate), or under some pairing or other (weak). **"Global" in this course means strong global.**

- **Entailments, stated in 2.3 without proof.** Strong individual entails strong global, which entails the other two; Boss 2 asks why. The converse, from strong global to strong individual, holds when both families are intrinsic (Shagrir 2002; Bennett 2004) or the base is closed under Boolean and quantificational operations (Stalnaker 1996), and fails for an extrinsic mental property over an intrinsic base (Paull and Sider 1992, who argued against the equivalence).
- **Why physicalists go global.** If [content externalism](#content-externalism) is right (4.3), belief fails to supervene on a person's intrinsic physical properties, harmlessly for physicalism.
- **Why strong global.** The weaker variants let physical twins swap mental lives.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Minimal physicalism

**Kim's name for mind-body supervenience: the commitment every physicalist shares, not itself a theory of how mind relates to body.**

Kim, *Mind in a Physical World* (1998). 2.3 lists what the physicalist's supervenience thesis needs:

1. **Metaphysical necessity.** A dualist with fundamental psychophysical laws accepts nomological supervenience, and strong emergence about the mind can be stated as supervenience with only nomological force (Chalmers 2006; the general notions are [`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md)).
2. **Global comparison,** because of [content externalism](#content-externalism).
3. **The strong global variant,** since the weaker ones let physical twins swap mental lives ([global supervenience](#global-supervenience)).
4. **A restriction,** since most physicalists think ghosts possible but not actual. Frank Jackson (*From Metaphysics to Ethics*, 1998) requires only that any world copying our physical facts and adding nothing be a duplicate of ours outright.

- **Necessary for physicalism, not sufficient.** It reports covariation, not why it holds, and a dualist who holds mental properties distinct but necessitated accepts it too.
- **The term is Kim's.** Jackson's contribution is the formulation in terms of a world that copies the physical facts and adds nothing.
- **What "physical" means.** [Hempel's dilemma](#hempels-dilemma) ([`apologetics-foundations` 3.1](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-01-naturalism-at-its-best.md)); replies tie "physical" to physical theory or to paradigm physical objects (Stoljar), or define it negatively, as not fundamentally mental (Montero and Papineau 2005; [via negativa](#via-negativa)).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Ban on mixed worlds

**If weak supervenience holds without strong, something must explain why no single world mixes the pairings that different worlds allow.**

Simon Blackburn pressed this demand against moral realism; 2.3 runs it on the mind. In [weak supervenience](#weak-supervenience)'s Example 2, $N$ goes with believing it is raining in $w_1$ and without it in $w_2$: what forbids one world containing both an $x$ and a $z$?

- **The [property dualist](#property-dualism) can answer it:** contingent psychophysical laws, uniform within each world and varying across worlds, yield exactly this pattern.
- **Davidson cannot answer it that way,** since anomalism denies such laws. His own line ("Thinking Causes", 1993) is a dependence holding only other things being equal, a ceteris paribus supervenience little examined since.
- **No verdict on $w_2$.** The dualist calls it a world with different bridging laws; the Davidsonian says nothing physical makes $w_1$'s pairing necessary; the physicalist must call it impossible, which takes strong supervenience and invites Kim's laws.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Content externalism

**What some beliefs are about depends partly on the world or the community around the believer, so two people identical inside, atom for atom, can believe different things.**

Taught in [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md). The content internalist says intrinsic duplicates must believe the same things. Two sources of external content: the natural environment, Putnam's [Twin Earth](#twin-earth) ("The Meaning of 'Meaning'", 1975, drawn for word meaning and carried to thought by Colin McGinn, 1977, and Tyler Burge, 1979, who unlike Putnam endorsed the extension outright); and the social environment, Burge's [arthritis case](#arthritis-case) ("Individualism and the Mental", 1979), which reaches any concept a thinker can understand incompletely (his *anti-individualism*). In the 2020 PhilPapers survey, 58 percent of the target group accepted or leaned towards content externalism and 26 percent towards internalism (N = 1,514). Internalist resources: [narrow content](#narrow-and-wide-content) and [phenomenal intentionality](#phenomenal-intentionality); the self-knowledge worry is [slow switching](#slow-switching).

- **Individuation is not location.** Davidson's sunburn (1987): a burn is a sunburn only if the sun caused it, yet it is located on the skin. Content externalism says beliefs are like sunburns; the [extended mind](#extended-mind) is a thesis about where the vehicles are. Teleosemantic externalism puts the content-fixing facts in the past and the environment while the representation stays in the head ([4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)).
- **Not justification externalism.** The internalism of [`epistemology` 1.4](../epistemology/lessons/01-04-internalism-and-externalism.md) is about what makes a belief justified, not what fixes its content.
- **Why physicalists state supervenience globally** (2.3): belief then fails to supervene on a person's intrinsic physical properties, harmlessly for physicalism ([global supervenience](#global-supervenience)).
- **Elsewhere.** It drives Inverted Earth ([3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)); externalism about experience is its cousin ([4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)); deference, by which your word "arthritis" refers through your community even when you misapply it, is a reply in the debate over language models ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Exclusion argument

**If every physical effect already has a sufficient physical cause, and effects are not caused twice over, a mental property distinct from its realizer has nothing left to do: reduce it, or it is idle.**

Jaegwon Kim, *Mind in a Physical World* (1998) and *Physicalism, or Something Near Enough* (2005), refining Norman Malcolm, "The Conceivability of Mechanism" (1968). Let *M* be a mental property, *P* the physical property realizing it on this occasion, and *E* a physical effect, such as a reach. The five claims, as 2.4 names them:

- **Mental causation:** an instance of *M* causes *E*.
- **Irreducibility:** *M* supervenes on physical properties and is realized here by *P*, but is identical to no physical property ([multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability) is the usual ground).
- **Closure:** every physical event that has a cause at a time has a sufficient physical cause at that time; here, *P* ([causal closure](#causal-closure)).
- **Exclusion:** no event has two distinct sufficient causes at one time unless it is a genuine case of overdetermination.
- **No [systematic overdetermination](#systematic-overdetermination):** mental and physical causes of behaviour are not such cases.

They cannot all be true, so either *M* is *P* or *M* causes nothing. The full skeleton is under Arguments.

**The supervenience argument** is Kim's fullest version. It starts from one thought causing another: a mental property can bring about a second one only by bringing about that property's physical base, so mental-to-mental causation becomes mental-to-physical, and the argument above takes over.

- **It does not say mental events cause nothing.** A token physicalist grants that the event that is your decision causes the reach. The question is whether it does so *in virtue of* being a decision, which token identity cannot answer; Kim's own exit needs type identities, if only local ones ([type and token identity](#type-and-token-identity), [functional reduction](#functional-reduction)).
- **Systematic, not one-off.** A firing squad's two bullets are a one-off coincidence. The premise concerns mind and brain both sufficing at every action, and whether that is a coincidence depends on whether the two causes are independent.
- **Ancestry.** It is the [quausation](#quausation) charge against Davidson without the strict laws, so it reaches every nonreductive view, functionalism included (3.2). The older ancestor is Elisabeth's objection to Descartes ([`modern-philosophy` 1.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/01-04-the-real-distinction-and-the-interaction-problem.md)) with the premise changed: she asked how something unextended could push a body; Kim asks what is left for the mind to do once the physical is sufficient. Overdetermination in single cases is [`metaphysics` 4.2](../metaphysics/lessons/04-02-counterfactual-causation.md)'s.
- **The responses 2.4 surveys.** Kim's local reduction ([functional reduction](#functional-reduction)); [epiphenomenalism](#epiphenomenalism), whose price is self-stultification; interactionist dualism and emergentism ([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)); overdetermination accepted, by Eugene Mills (1996) for a dualist and Barry Loewer (2002) for a physicalist; Stephen Yablo's and List and Menzies's [proportionality](#proportionality); and Block's and Baker's [generalization objection](#generalization-objection). Mapping each response onto the claims above is left to you (Boss 2(c)).
- **Two families decline the framing.** A Russellian monist ([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)) makes mental properties the grounds of physical dispositions, so the argument has nothing to exclude (Howell, 2015: the quiddities then ride along without causal work). A hylomorphist ([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)) denies that form competes with matter as an efficient cause.
- **Used in Module 3.** A realist about consciousness who says it causes our beliefs about it meets this argument ([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)).
- **Two statements of exclusion.** 2.4 separates exclusion from no systematic overdetermination and states exclusion in Kim's 2005 form (no two distinct sufficient causes). [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md)'s premise E folds both into one, closer to his 1998 form: no physical event regularly has, besides its sufficient physical cause, a distinct further cause. Both are Kim's.

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Quausation

**The charge that on anomalous monism a mental event causes only as a physical event, never in virtue of being mental.**

Pressed by Frederick Stoutland, Ted Honderich and Ernest Sosa; named "quausation", causing *qua* mental, by Terence Horgan ("Mental Quausation", 1989). Davidson answered ("Thinking Causes", 1993) that causation relates events however described, so nothing causes "qua" anything. Kim's [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument) is the charge without the strict laws.

- **The charge grants that mental events cause.** It asks about the properties in virtue of which they cause, which token identity leaves open ([anomalous monism](#anomalous-monism)).

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)

### Functional reduction

**Kim's way to keep mental causation: define the property by its causal role, find what plays the role in a kind of creature, and identify the property-in-that-kind with the occupant.**

Kim (1998, 2005); his own exit from the [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument). By the causal inheritance principle ([`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md)), an instance of *M* then has exactly its realizer's powers, so it causes what its realizer causes. 2.4, Example 1: in creatures built like you, deciding to drink is the occupant of the deciding role.

- **The price.** "Decision" names different properties in differently built creatures: the reductions are local, species-specific type identities.
- **The residue.** Kim held that qualia resist it, leaving an irreducible, causally idle residue: physicalism "near enough". [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md) takes up those qualia.
- **Kin.** It has the shape of Lewis's realizer functionalism, an identity theory reached by a functional route ([role and realizer](#role-and-realizer)).

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Proportionality

**A cause should be specific enough for its effect and no more: the pigeon pecks because the patch is red, not because it is scarlet.**

Stephen Yablo, "Mental Causation" (1992). Mental properties stand to their realizers as determinables to determinates, as red stands to scarlet, and a determinable does not compete with its determinates. **Yablo's pigeon (2.4, Example 2).** Sophie is trained to peck at red things and nothing else, and pecks at a scarlet patch. Had the patch been crimson she would still have pecked; had it not been red she would not have. Redness passes both halves and scarletness fails the second, so redness is the proportional cause; citing scarletness suggests she would ignore crimson. Yablo maps it onto the mind: the decision is redness, its exact neural realizer scarletness.

Christian List and Peter Menzies ("Nonreductive Physicalism and the Limits of the Exclusion Principle", 2009) recast this as difference-making: *F* makes a difference to *G* when relevantly similar situations with *F* have *G* and those without *F* lack it. On their test, which of a mental property and its realizer makes the difference is settled case by case, not a priori ([realization-insensitivity](#realization-insensitivity)).

- **No guarantee of mental causation.** Train a pigeon to peck at one shade only, a variant List and Menzies discuss, and the verdict flips: the determinate is the difference-maker and the determinable is not. The question becomes empirical: does behaviour survive small changes in which neurons realize the decision?
- **Not "both are causes".** When the effect is realization-insensitive, the realizer is not a difference-maker. Both make a difference only when the effect hinges on the exact realizer.
- **The determinable model is disputed** (Ehring 1996, Worley 1997, Funkhouser 2006): red and scarlet share a space of colours, and a belief and a firing pattern share none.
- **Kim's reply (2005).** His argument concerns causation as production, a stronger relation than counterfactual dependence, and dependence alone cannot tell a cause from something that rides along; the light from the patch into Sophie's eye did the producing. List and Menzies answer that production has never been explicated. The proportionalist concedes that the realizer physically produces the movement; Kim concedes that mental properties can satisfy the counterfactuals, and rests everything on dependence not being the relation at issue. Nothing in the pigeon case decides between the two pictures of causation.
- **Bridges.** A statue's shape, which survives a change of material, is the proportional difference-maker for its fall ([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)). The nonreductive reply to the argument from reason is this move ([`apologetics-foundations` 3.2](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-02-reason.md)). Difference-making and interventions as accounts of explanation are [`philosophy-of-science` 4.2](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-02-causal-explanation.md).

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Realization-insensitivity

**An effect is realization-insensitive when it would still have occurred had the same mental property been realized differently; then the mental property makes the difference and its exact realizer does not.**

List and Menzies (2009). If the effect survives a change of realizer, the mental property is the difference-maker and the realizer is not: exclusion runs downward, the higher-level property excluding its realizer. If the effect hinges on the exact realizer (realization-sensitive), both make a difference.

- **Trap.** [Proportionality](#proportionality) does not make the mental property and its realizer both causes in the insensitive case; it rules the realizer out.
- **An empirical matter.** Whether an effect is realization-insensitive turns on what happens in the nearest situations with the same mental property and a different realizer.

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Generalization objection

**If the exclusion argument works, it idles every multiply realized property of chemistry, biology and geology as well, and if there is no bottom level, causation drains away altogether.**

Ned Block, "Do Causal Powers Drain Away?" (2003), with Lynne Rudder Baker (1993) before him. The objection is that the [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument) proves too much, so something in it is wrong. The special sciences where it finds its examples, and causal inheritance, are [`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md).

- **Left to you here.** What the objection does with the argument's premises, and how Kim answers it, are Boss 2(c)'s question; 2.4's exposition and its P3 cover both.

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

### Levels and orders

**Kim's distinction between a higher-level property of a whole, built from its parts' properties, and a higher-order property, the property of having some property that plays a given role.**

Kim, *Mind in a Physical World* (1998), pp. 77-87. On Kim's account, exclusion pits a *second-order* property (having some property that plays a role) against its realizer, both had by one thing at one level. A table's mass of 10 kilograms is not second-order: it is a property of the whole, built from its parts' masses, with powers no part has, so nothing drains through it. What stays threatened is every functional property, mental or not, and Kim's cure for all of them is local reduction ([functional reduction](#functional-reduction)).

- **Level is not order.** A macroproperty of a whole has its own powers; a role property competes with its realizer.
- **In 5.3.** A statue's shape is an accidental form fixed by the bronze's arrangement, so even Kim grants it powers as a property of a whole built from its parts; the soul is a substantial form, which the hylomorphist denies is fixed by arrangement ([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)).

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

**Module 3: Consciousness**

### Creature and state consciousness

**"Conscious" is said of whole creatures (awake, responsive, a subject at all) and of particular states (this ache, that seeing).** David Rosenthal drew the line in "Two Concepts of Consciousness" (1986). A creature is conscious when it is awake and responsive, or, in Nagel's sense, when there is [something it is like](#what-it-is-like) to be it. A state is conscious when its owner is conscious *of* it, and a state can be conscious in two ways: [access and phenomenal](#phenomenal-and-access-consciousness).

- **A measure of wakefulness is a creature measure.** By itself it says nothing about which states are conscious or what they are like.
- **Waking versus sleep** is on Chalmers's list of [easy problems](#easy-problems).
- **Both theories of [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md) are theories of state consciousness,** and both aim at the phenomenal kind.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)

### Phenomenal and access consciousness

**A state is access-conscious when its content is available to reason, act and speak with; it is phenomenally conscious when there is something it is like to be in it.** Ned Block, "On a Confusion about a Function of Consciousness" (*Behavioral and Brain Sciences*, 1995), who called consciousness a "mongrel concept" and split it. Access: content poised for use as a premise in reasoning, for the rational control of action and for the rational control of speech. Phenomenal: [what it is like](#what-it-is-like) to be in the state.

- **The confusion of his title:** inferring a function of phenomenal consciousness from facts about access. [Blindsight](#blindsight) patients, Block argued, lack both for the blind field, so their deficit shows a function of access.
- **Phenomenal without access:** a pneumatic drill outside, heard all along and noticed only at noon. **Access without phenomenal:** "superblindsight", Block's hypothetical, which he doubted exists.
- **The distinction is conceptual.** In ordinary cases the two go together, and whether they ever separate is disputed. The [overflow debate](#overflow-debate) is about phenomenal outrunning access.
- **A report is evidence of access only.** A claim that there was something it was like, resting on a report, infers phenomenal from access and needs a bridging premise.
- **In the science ([3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)):** the experimenter learns that a stimulus was seen because the subject says so, so every contrast is at least a contrast in access. The [global workspace theory](#global-workspace-theory) identifies access with consciousness in its premise 4.
- **In higher-order theory ([3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)):** Block separates a "modest" higher-order theory of state consciousness from an "ambitious" one that claims to explain phenomenal consciousness (2011). Tye's "poised" clause builds an availability condition into an account of phenomenal character.
- **Illusionism ([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md))** denies phenomenal properties and keeps access. A study of what subjects noticed and said measures access.
- **Machines ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)):** most indicators of machine consciousness (broadcast, monitoring, control of attention) are functions of access. That they amount to experience is 3.3's premise 4 again.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Hard problem

**Why the performance of the brain's functions is accompanied by experience at all: a question that, Chalmers argues, is not about any function.** David Chalmers named it in "Facing Up to the Problem of Consciousness" (*Journal of Consciousness Studies*, 1995). C. D. Broad saw the split in 1925, with the mathematical archangel of [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md). The argument in outline:

1. To explain a phenomenon [reductively](#reductive-explanation) is to analyse it as the performance of a function and find the mechanism that performs it.
2. The [easy problems](#easy-problems) are problems about functions.
3. After every such function is explained, it remains an intelligible question why their performance is accompanied by experience.
4. So phenomenal consciousness cannot be explained by step 1's method; its explanation needs something beyond mechanisms.

- **Chalmers's own answer:** take experience as fundamental, tied to physical processes by psychophysical laws (his naturalistic dualism, *The Conscious Mind*, 1996, as [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md) names it).
- **The contested premise is 3.** The [type-A physicalist](#type-a-and-type-b-physicalism) (Dennett, as Chalmers classes him in "Moving Forward on the Problem of Consciousness", 1997, where he names type A and type B): once every function is explained, including our insistence that something is left over, nothing is left, as with "life". Chalmers: the vitalists doubted that mechanisms could perform life's functions; heat, life and light were functions all along; experience is the thing to be explained, not a posited explainer like vital spirit.
- **Each side's concession.** Chalmers: premise 3 rests on a first-person datum that no third-person argument can force on someone who denies it. The type-A physicalist: a nearly universal appearance is mistaken, and its persistence needs explaining (the [meta-problem](#meta-problem)).
- **A claim about explanation, not by itself a claim that consciousness is non-physical.** Levine did not conclude materialism false, type-B physicalists accept the [gap](#explanatory-gap) and keep physicalism, and illusionists deny the explanandum is what it seems: "explained away", which is not "explained".
- **Survey:** PhilPapers 2020, about 62 percent accepted or leaned towards "there is a hard problem", about 30 percent towards no (N = 998).
- **How Module 3's theories take it.** First-order and higher-order theories ([3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)) offer reductive answers. The science ([3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)) tests theories' biological implementations; whether it can test a core bridge, IIT's identity claim or the workspace's identification of experience with broadcast, is open. The illusionist ([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)) replaces it with the illusion problem, and only [strong illusionism](#strong-and-weak-illusionism) dissolves it. The Russellian monist and Strawson ([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)) keep the datum and place consciousness, or its makings, in the intrinsic nature physics leaves out.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Easy problems

**Problems of consciousness that are problems about functions, solved by finding the mechanism that performs the function.** Chalmers's list (1995): discriminating, categorizing and reacting to stimuli; integrating information; reporting mental states; a system's access to its own states; focusing attention; deliberate control of behaviour; the difference between waking and sleep.

- **"Easy" means only that we know what kind of answer would solve them,** a mechanism that performs the function. One of them can fill a career.
- **The [meta-problem](#meta-problem) is strictly an easy problem:** judgments and reports about consciousness are behaviour ([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)).
- **The illusionist's trade:** the real task, explaining why the brain represents itself as having phenomenal qualities and why that is so hard to disbelieve, is a question about mechanisms and reports, one of these.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Reductive explanation

**Explaining a phenomenon by analysing it as the performance of a function and then finding the mechanism that performs it.** Premise 1 of Chalmers's [hard problem](#hard-problem) argument (1995). The gene is whatever carries hereditary information, and stretches of DNA turn out to carry it.

- **Heat is the model success** (Levine): our concept of heat was a causal role, and kinetic theory showed how molecular motion plays it ([explanatory gap](#explanatory-gap)).
- **Whether it reaches experience** turns on whether our concept of experience is exhausted by a causal role. The heat case gives no verdict on that.
- **Not the D-N model.** Levine says deduction from laws is too weak for his sense of "explain", since his identities would support deductions ([`philosophy-of-science` 4.1](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-01-the-deductive-nomological-model.md)).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)

### Explanatory gap

**Even a true psychophysical identity seems to leave unexplained why the state feels the way it does.** Joseph Levine, "Materialism and Qualia: The Explanatory Gap" (*Pacific Philosophical Quarterly*, 1983), recasting Kripke's modal argument of [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md) as a claim about explanation. In outline:

1. "Heat is the motion of molecules" is fully explanatory: our concept of heat was a causal role, and physics shows how molecular motion plays it.
2. "Pain is C-fibre firing" explains pain's causal role but not why pain feels as it does.
3. Materialism requires that every phenomenon above the fundamental level be intelligible in terms of the mechanisms beneath it: brute facts belong in physics, not psychology.
4. So a psychophysical identity, even if true, leaves an explanatory gap: a problem for materialism but, Levine said, no proof that it is false.

- **Epistemic, not metaphysical.** Type-B physicalists accept the gap and keep physicalism: see [explaining and identifying](#explaining-and-identifying).
- **The heat case strains.** Heat's role began "whatever we feel as warmth". Kinetic theory explains why molecular motion causes that feeling, not why the feeling feels as it does; the gap closed for heat by moving the felt warmth out of the fire and into the perceiver. That is Locke's move with secondary qualities ([`modern-philosophy` 3.2](../modern-philosophy/lessons/03-02-locke-qualities-substance-and-essence.md)), which Levine says he is restating. (Strictly, radiant heat is not molecular motion.)
- **A neural correlate does not close it.** A correlation is a regularity and an identity a claim that there is one thing; neither by itself shows why the state is like that.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Explaining and identifying

**An identity can be true without making it intelligible why the thing has the features one of its names presents.** The type-B reply to the [explanatory gap](#explanatory-gap): David Papineau; Ned Block with Robert Stalnaker (1999). Identities are never explained: once Mark Twain is Sam Clemens, "why is he Clemens?" has no answer, so a gap is no evidence of two things.

- **Rejoinder:** Levine (*Purple Haze*, 2001) and Chalmers with Frank Jackson (2001): the scientific identities were explained. Heat was identified with molecular motion *because* molecular motion was shown to play heat's role.
- **The gap claim, restated:** a psychophysical identity would be a true identity that is not explanatory.
- **In the science ([3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)):** risky predictions that succeed can support an identity by inference to the best explanation, as with heat; that support does not by itself explain why the identity holds.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Transparency of experience

**Attending to an experience seems to reveal only the features of what is experienced, not features of the experience itself.** Look at a ripe tomato and try to attend to your *seeing*: you find more tomato, its redness, its shine, its place over there. Gilbert Harman ("The Intrinsic Quality of Experience", 1990) made it an argument for [first-order representationalism](#first-order-representationalism):

1. (Transparency.) Introspective attention to a perceptual experience finds no feature of it beyond the features it presents its objects as having.
2. If an experience had phenomenal features beyond its content, attention could find them.
3. So phenomenal character supervenes on representational content: no difference in what it is like without a difference in what is represented.

- **Not Moore's thesis.** It is traced to G. E. Moore, "The Refutation of Idealism" (*Mind*, 1903), who gave the image ("transparent", "diaphanous") but held that the awareness itself "*can* be distinguished if we look attentively enough". Harman's thesis, that the most careful attention finds nothing intrinsic to the experience, is the one Moore rejected.
- **Weakest premise: 1.** Block: some introspectible features belong to the representing, not to what is represented, "mental paint": the blur you notice when you take off your glasses, though nothing looks blurry; free-floating anxiety, which represents little and feels like a great deal. Reply: each case hides a content difference. Blurry seeing represents less, failing to represent sharp edges (Tye); anxiety represents that something bad is coming.
- **Each side's concession.** The representationalist: premise 1 must be redeemed case by case, and one clear counterexample sinks the conclusion. The critic: ordinary vision is overwhelmingly transparent (Brian Loar, a critic, grants it), and paint shows only under unusual attention.
- **Phenomenological cousin:** Husserl sees coloured things, not colour-sensations ([`phenomenology-and-personalism` 1.1](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/01-01-intentionality-from-brentano-to-husserl.md)).
- **In perception ([4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)):** transparency is the observation naive realists start from.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)

### Inverted spectrum

**Two subjects whose colour experiences are systematically swapped while their colour talk and sorting stay the same.** Locke (*Essay* II.xxxii.15) supposed a violet might give one man the idea a marigold gives another, undetectably. Turned on [first-order representationalism](#first-order-representationalism): Invert, inverted from birth, sorts colours as well as anyone and shares the community's colour words, so the tomato looks *red* to her too. Same content, different character, against the transparency argument's conclusion.

- **Replies:** one of the two misperceives after all (Tye); the case is conceivable but impossible (Lycan); the two represent different appearance properties (Shoemaker, 1994).
- **Trap:** an Invert to whom tomatoes look green is a difference in content, which the representationalist welcomes. The case needs the same represented colour with what it is like differing.
- **Undetectability is not required,** only sameness of content: the facts a content theory cares about (history, discrimination, language) must match.
- **First aimed at [functionalism](#functionalism)** ([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)), as [absent qualia](#absent-qualia) were.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)

### Inverted Earth

**Block's planet of complementary colours and inverted colour words: a traveller with colour-inverting lenses keeps what it is like while, in time, what his states represent changes.** Ned Block, "Inverted Earth" (1990). Fitted with inverting lenses, you are moved to a planet where the sky is yellow and the locals' "blue" means yellow, and you notice nothing. After long enough, Block argues, your contents shift to match theirs while what it is like stays put: same character, different content. The converse of the [inverted spectrum](#inverted-spectrum).

- **It needs [content externalism](#content-externalism)** ([4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)) to shift the contents, and a narrow phenomenal character to hold still.
- **Replies.** Phenomenal externalism (Dretske, Tye, Lycan): character is as wide as content, so molecular duplicates can differ in it, and it shifts too. Or Dretske's [teleology](#teleosemantics): your visual states keep the content evolution gave them, so you misperceive indefinitely, at the price that [Swampman](#swampman), with no evolutionary history, has no experiences ([4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)).
- **Aimed at functionalism and representationalism alike.**

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)

### Teleosemantics

**A state's content is fixed by biological function: it means the condition its users need to hold in order to succeed in the way that got them selected.** Taught in [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md), in Ruth Millikan's version (*Language, Thought, and Other Biological Categories*, 1984; "Biosemantics", 1989):

1. A representation sits between a [producer](#producer-and-consumer) that makes it and consumers that act on it, designed by selection or learning to work together.
2. Its content is the condition that must hold for the consumers to perform their proper functions in the Normal way, the way that historically explained their selection.

- **The function** is the selected-effect function of [`philosophy-of-science` 4.2](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-02-causal-explanation.md): what earlier tokens did that got the trait selected.
- **Variants.** David Papineau (1984; *Philosophical Naturalism*, 1993) starts from desires, whose content is the effect they were selected to produce, and derives belief content from them. Karen Neander (*A Mark of the Mental*, 2017) moves content to the input side: what the producer was selected to respond to.
- **Objection:** selection may not tell locally coextensive properties apart ([functional indeterminacy](#functional-indeterminacy), Fodor 1990).
- **[Swampman](#swampman):** no selection history, so no proper functions, so on teleosemantics his states have no content.
- **Trap:** it is a theory of what fixes content, not the claim that evolution favours true beliefs; [`apologetics-foundations` 3.2](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-02-reason.md) draws the link to truth when it uses the theory against Plantinga.
- **In 3.2:** Dretske's and Tye's experiential contents are teleological indicator contents. Dretske's teleology answers [Inverted Earth](#inverted-earth) at the cost that Swampman has no experiences. (In 4.2, Dretske's 1988 theory of belief recruits indicators by learning, so there Swampman's beliefs arrive as he learns.)
- **In 6.3:** Dimitri Coelho Mollo and Raphaël Millière ("The Vector Grounding Problem", 2023) hold that, on teleosemantics, a state refers if it stands in causal-informational relations to the world and has a selection history giving it the function of carrying that information. They treat training, especially preference tuning that rewards factual accuracy, as that history. Whether gradient descent is selection in the theory's sense is the open premise.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Transitivity principle

**A mental state is conscious only if its subject is in some way aware of being in it.** The higher-order theorist's starting premise. The higher-order argument, in outline:

1. Mental states of every kind, perceptions included, occur both consciously and unconsciously: Armstrong's absent-minded driver (1968), who "comes to" at her door having stopped at every red light, and the [blindsight](#blindsight) patient.
2. (Transitivity.) A state is conscious only if its subject is in some way aware of being in it.
3. So a state is conscious when its subject has a suitable higher-order representation of it.

- **Two ways to be aware of a state:** by an inner [perception](#higher-order-perception) or by a [thought](#higher-order-thought).
- **Denied outright by Dretske (1995):** conscious states are states we are conscious *with*, not *of*.
- **Modest and ambitious readings.** Read as an account of state consciousness only, it is a "modest" higher-order theory; read as an account of phenomenal consciousness, an "ambitious" one, the version Block (2011) attacks.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)

### Higher-order perception

**A state is conscious when an inner sense scans it and produces a perception-like representation of it.** D. M. Armstrong (*A Materialist Theory of the Mind*, 1968); William Lycan (*Consciousness and Experience*, 1996). A descendant of Locke's "reflection" ([`modern-philosophy` 3.1](../modern-philosophy/lessons/03-01-locke-against-innate-ideas.md)).

- **Objection:** inner sensing should then feel like something, and nothing does.
- **Lycan gave it up** (Sauret and Lycan, 2014).
- **With the driver** it agrees with [higher-order thought](#higher-order-thought), with an inner scan in place of the thought: the unattended seeing was unconscious.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)

### Higher-order thought

**A state is conscious when it is accompanied by a suitable thought that one is in it.** David Rosenthal ("Two Concepts of Consciousness", 1986; *Consciousness and Mind*, 2005). The thought is assertoric and not reached by conscious inference. It is normally itself unconscious, which stops a regress.

- **Not introspection.** Introspection is the rarer case where a third-order thought makes the higher-order thought conscious; the theory never says the driver was thinking about her seeing.
- **Actualist and dispositional.** Rosenthal requires an actual thought, and holds that one without a target still yields a conscious state. In Peter Carruthers's dispositional version (*Phenomenal Consciousness*, 2000) the state need only be *available* to such a thought, so it cannot occur without a target: availability needs something available.
- **Rosenthal's wine course (2005):** learning concepts changes how a wine tastes, which he takes as evidence that higher-order states shape what it is like. The first-order reply: "oaky" and "tannic" are concepts of the wine, not of the experience.
- **Objections:** the [empty higher-order state](#empty-higher-order-state) and the [animal and infant objection](#animal-and-infant-objection).
