- **Machines:** Butlin, Long and co-authors (2023) derive two indicators from computational higher-order theories, perception that can be noisy or generated from the top down and a metacognitive monitor; they report Hakwan Lau's suggestion (2022) that generative adversarial networks may have both ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)).

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Empty higher-order state

**A higher-order thought or perception that occurs without the first-order state it represents.** Pressed by Alex Byrne (1997), Karen Neander (1998) and Ned Block ("The Higher Order Approach to Consciousness Is Defunct", 2011). Is the subject conscious of seeing red when there is no seeing?

- **Rosenthal's dental patients (2005):** they report pain while the drill works on a tooth whose nerves are dead, and, once told, report only noise and vibration. (Not the dentist's chair of [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), which is pain without behaviour.)
- **Three verdicts on a targetless pain-thought.** Rosenthal: a conscious pain, since consciousness is how one's mental life appears to one. Rocco Gennaro (2012): nothing is conscious, since the theory says what makes a first-order state conscious and there is none. Block (2011): the "ambitious" higher-order theory is incoherent, since there is something it is like and yet no state is the target of a higher-order thought; and a mere thought of pain has no reason to inherit pain's awfulness.
- **Neander's division of phenomenal labour:** if the higher-order state can do the work alone, what does the first-order state ever contribute?
- **Dispositional theories escape:** availability needs something available.
- **Reports cannot settle a real case.** An empty thought, a real pain caused from the top down by fear, and a vivid imagining all yield the same sincere "it hurt".

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)

### Animal and infant objection

**If consciousness needs thoughts about one's own mental states, creatures unable to form them, many animals and newborns, would have no conscious experiences.** Pressed against higher-order thought theory by Dale Jamieson and Marc Bekoff (1992), Dretske (1995) and Tye (1995).

- **Not Carruthers's objection.** Peter Carruthers is the higher-order theorist who accepted the consequence ("Brute Experience", 1989; *Phenomenal Consciousness*, 2000), later arguing (2005) that it matters little for how animals are treated.
- **Gennaro's reply:** the higher-order states needed are simpler than those psychologists test for.
- **Trap:** the higher-order thought is normally unconscious, so the objection misfires if it assumes the creature must think consciously about its pain.
- **First-order representationalism has no such consequence:** a pain poised to make a difference to belief and desire is conscious without any higher-order state.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Contrastive method

**Hold a stimulus fixed, arrange for it to be seen on some trials and missed on others, and compare what the brain does in the two conditions.** The founding method of the science of consciousness; what it finds are [neural correlates of consciousness](#neural-correlates-of-consciousness). Its tools include [masking](#masking), [blindsight](#blindsight) (the lesion version) and [inattentional blindness](#inattentional-blindness).

- **Its confound:** "seen" is sorted by report, so every contrast is at least a contrast in [access](#phenomenal-and-access-consciousness), and the late event that separates seen from unseen might be the reporting: deciding, introspecting, pressing a key. [No-report paradigms](#no-report-paradigms) try to remove it.
- **It ranks candidates; it does not choose among them.** The same masking data fit the workspace reading and the [recurrent processing](#recurrent-processing-theory) reading.
- **Cogitate (2025) departed from it,** showing clearly visible pictures and asking where and how their contents were held ([adversarial collaboration](#adversarial-collaboration)).

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Neural correlates of consciousness

**Neural states that go with conscious states; on Chalmers's definition, a minimal neural system whose states suffice, under background conditions, for the matching conscious states.** David Chalmers (2000). "Minimal" is there because bare correlation also catches causes, effects and enabling conditions.

- **A correlate is not an explanation.** A correlation is a regularity, an identity a claim that there is one thing, and neither by itself shows why the state is like that ([explanatory gap](#explanatory-gap)).
- **Correlates found by contrast are correlates of report-sorted states:** Del Cul and colleagues call their late wave a correlate of "conscious reportability" ([masking](#masking)).
- **A correlate does not debunk an experience** by itself ([`philosophy-of-religion` 3.4](../philosophy-of-religion/lessons/03-04-the-argument-from-religious-experience.md)).

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Masking

**Making a briefly flashed target invisible by following it, after a short gap, with a second pattern, the mask.** A target shown for a few tens of milliseconds is hidden by short target-to-mask gaps, and near threshold the same display is seen on some trials and missed on others: the [contrastive method](#contrastive-method)'s cleanest tool. Taught in this course.

- **Del Cul, Baillet and Dehaene (*PLoS Biology*, 2007).** A digit flashed for 16 ms, then, after 16 to 100 ms, a mask of letters around its location. Rated visibility rose sharply between gaps of 33 and 66 ms (average threshold near 44 ms). The earliest scalp responses did not change with the gap and intermediate ones grew gradually; only a late wave, splitting seen from unseen trials from about 270 to 300 ms and coming from a distributed fronto-parieto-temporal network, rose as steeply as the ratings.
- **What it licenses:** the digit was processed for a quarter of a second whether or not it was reported; the late wave is, in the authors' words, a correlate of "conscious reportability", which the workspace theory reads as [ignition](#ignition).
- **What it does not:** that the late wave is the experience rather than its access or consequences. A [recurrent processing](#recurrent-processing-theory) theorist says the digit was experienced once intermediate activity turned recurrent, and the late wave is the cost of reporting it.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Blindsight

**After damage to primary visual cortex, patients point to or guess the location of stimuli in the blind field that they sincerely deny seeing.** Taught in [`neuroscience` 3.2](../neuroscience/lessons/03-02-vision.md); cited here.

- **Block ([3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)):** the patients lack both access and phenomenal consciousness for the blind field, he argued, so their deficit shows a function of access; reading off a function of phenomenal consciousness is the confusion of his 1995 title. "Superblindsight", access to blind-field content without phenomenal consciousness, is his hypothetical, and he doubted it exists.
- **Higher-order theories ([3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)):** with Armstrong's absent-minded driver, a motivating case for the premise that perception occurs unconsciously ([transitivity principle](#transitivity-principle)).
- **The science ([3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)):** the lesion version of the [contrastive method](#contrastive-method).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Global workspace theory

**A content is conscious when it wins entry to a limited-capacity workspace and is broadcast to memory, evaluation, planning and report at once.** The newsroom: specialist desks work in parallel, unseen, and a few stories make the front page, which every desk reads. Bernard Baars proposed it as a cognitive model (*A Cognitive Theory of Consciousness*, 1988). Stanislas Dehaene, Jean-Pierre Changeux and colleagues gave it neural form from 1998, the global neuronal workspace: long-range prefrontal and parietal neurons that a strong enough sensory representation recruits into sustained, recurrent activity ([ignition](#ignition)). The case is an inference to the best explanation:

1. Seen and unseen trials share early sensory processing and differ chiefly in a late, widespread, all-or-none frontoparietal event.
2. That event is what recruitment into a global broadcast system would look like.
3. What conscious contents can do and unconscious ones cannot (be reported, reasoned with, remembered, used flexibly) is what global availability provides.
4. Whatever marks the difference between conscious and unconscious states and explains what conscious states do is what consciousness consists in.
5. So a state is conscious just when its content is broadcast in the global workspace.

- **Weakest premise: 4.** Block: the data mark the difference between reported and unreported states, so the theory earns at most an account of access, and [recurrent processing](#recurrent-processing-theory) fits premise 1 as well if the late event is the cost of reporting. Cohen and Dennett: consciousness with no functional footprint is beyond any test, so premise 4 is the only criterion a science can use.
- **Each side's concession.** The workspace theorist: identifying access with experience is a thesis, not a measurement, which a realist about the [hard problem](#hard-problem) will not grant. The overflow theorist: her evidence arrives through access ([overflow debate](#overflow-debate)).
- **Cogitate (2025) presses premise 1:** generally no ignition when the stimulus disappeared, and little prefrontal information about some dimensions, such as identity ([adversarial collaboration](#adversarial-collaboration)).
- **No phenomenal-without-access category:** a strong but unattended content is [preconscious](#subliminal-preconscious-and-conscious), so not conscious.
- **Split brains:** the theory counts broadcasts, so two isolated workspaces give two streams in the lab ([unity of consciousness](#unity-of-consciousness)).
- **Machines ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)):** a source of indicators (specialist modules, a limited workspace, global broadcast, state-dependent attention). Butlin and co-authors doubt a transformer's residual stream is a workspace bottleneck.
- **Trap:** ignition belongs to Dehaene's neural version, not to Baars's 1988 model.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Ignition

**In the neural workspace theory, the sudden, nonlinear, all-or-none recruitment of long-range workspace neurons into sustained activity coding a content, which makes that content conscious.** Dehaene, Changeux and colleagues, from 1998. Step-like rather than graded, and late: in masking it is read into the wave that splits seen from unseen trials from about 270 to 300 ms ([masking](#masking)).

- **Dehaene's, not Baars's.** Baars's 1988 cognitive model has a workspace and broadcast but no ignition.
- **Cogitate (2025):** there was generally no ignition when the stimulus disappeared, a challenge to the [global workspace theory](#global-workspace-theory)'s premise 1.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Subliminal, preconscious and conscious

**Dehaene's three-way sorting of processing: subliminal (too weak to be accessed), preconscious (strong enough to be accessible but not accessed, typically for want of attention) and conscious (accessed and broadcast).** Dehaene, Changeux, Naccache, Sackur and Sergent (2006). Only the third is conscious on the [global workspace theory](#global-workspace-theory), which recognizes no phenomenal consciousness without access.

- **Trap:** a strong stimulus that is merely unattended is preconscious, not subliminal ([inattentional blindness](#inattentional-blindness)).
- **The overflow theorist redescribes** some preconscious contents as phenomenally conscious without access ([overflow debate](#overflow-debate)).

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Integrated information theory

**Consciousness is identical to maximally irreducible integrated information, $\Phi$: roughly, how far a system's causal structure as a whole exceeds what its parts carry separately.** Giulio Tononi, from 2004. It takes properties of experience as self-evident axioms (it exists for itself, is specific, is unified), moves from them to physical postulates, and makes an identity claim. $\Phi$ is named only in this course; no formula is given.

- **Predictions:** the human maximum lies in a posterior "hot zone"; a purely feedforward system has no consciousness, however well it behaves.
- **Cogitate (2025):** the predicted sustained synchronization within posterior cortex was missing ([adversarial collaboration](#adversarial-collaboration)).
- **The pseudoscience charge.** A PsyArXiv paper signed "IIT-Concerned" (September 2023, with Stephen Fleming, Hakwan Lau and many others) called IIT pseudoscience; its published version (*Nature Neuroscience*, 2025) argues that IIT is unscientific because its core claims are untestable even in principle. Keith Frankish replied, asking whether it is pseudoscience or "appropriately anomalous science": a live demarcation case ([`philosophy-of-science` 2.6](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/02-06-demarcation-after-popper.md)). The charge is contested, not settled.
- **Trap:** the axioms do not prove the identity claim; the step from axioms to physical postulates is defended as abduction.
- **Panpsychism ([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)):** Chalmers (2016) construes IIT as a form of emergent panpsychism, though it denies consciousness to rocks, whose particles integrate no information.
- **Machines ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)):** the hardware's physical causal structure decides, not the program. Feedforward hardware has no integrated information, brain-like hardware would be as conscious as a brain, and conventional hardware gives little. Incompatible with computational functionalism, it supplies no indicator to Butlin, Long and co-authors' list.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Recurrent processing theory

**Perceptual consciousness is recurrent (feedback) activity within sensory areas; entry into the global workspace is not needed.** Victor Lamme. Since sensory recurrence suffices, it allows experience without broadcast, which makes it an ally of the [overflow](#overflow-debate) thesis.

- **Its reading of masking:** the digit was experienced once intermediate activity turned recurrent, and the late frontoparietal wave is the cost of reporting an experience already had ([masking](#masking)).
- **The prefrontal dispute:** workspace and higher-order theorists look to the prefrontal cortex; recurrent processing theory places consciousness in sensory areas.
- **Machines ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)):** indicators of recurrence in input modules and organized percepts such as figure and ground.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### No-report paradigms

**Experiments that track what a subject experiences without asking for a report, using reflexive signs such as eye movements or pupil size.** Designed to strip the report confound from contrastive data: the deciding, introspecting and responding that go with an overt report.

- **Limits:** the proxy must be validated against report in each setting, so it does not wholly escape report; and seeing something still prompts thought that missing it does not, a point Block pressed in 2019.
- **Trap:** "no report" is not "no access": the reflex is itself a use of the content.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Overflow debate

**Whether phenomenal consciousness can outrun access, so that more is experienced than can be reported or used.** George Sperling (1960): shown a brief array of twelve letters, subjects reported only three or four, though they said they saw more; yet a tone just after the array vanished let them report most of whichever row it cued.

- **Overflow:** Ned Block, "Consciousness, Accessibility, and the Mesh between Psychology and Neuroscience" (2007): experience of all the letters, access to a few.
- **Against:** Michael Cohen and Daniel Dennett, "Consciousness Cannot Be Separated from Function" (2011): experiments detect consciousness only through something it does, so a phenomenal state with no access is untestable.
- **The overflow theorist's concession:** her evidence arrives through access. Sperling's subjects *said* they saw more.
- **Partial-report arithmetic.** If $N$ letters are available when an array of $r$ equal rows vanishes, and a row chosen at random is cued only afterwards, the cued row's expected score is $N/r$, so the score estimates $N$. Such an estimate counts letters available for report, which every side accepts; whether they were experienced is the dispute.
- **Same split elsewhere:** [inattentional blindness](#inattentional-blindness) and the split brain ([unity of consciousness](#unity-of-consciousness)) can each be read as experience without access or as no experience.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Adversarial collaboration

**A study in which proponents of rival theories agree in advance on predictions that would count against each, and theory-neutral teams run it.** The Cogitate Consortium (*Nature*, April 2025) tested [integrated information theory](#integrated-information-theory) against the global neuronal workspace: preregistered divergent predictions with pass and fail criteria; fMRI, MEG and intracranial EEG; 256 participants; clearly visible pictures shown for 0.5 to 1.5 seconds.

- **Results.** For IIT, the predicted sustained synchronization within posterior cortex was missing. For the workspace, generally no ignition when the stimulus disappeared, and little prefrontal information about some dimensions, such as identity. The authors: some predictions of each borne out, central claims of both seriously challenged.
- **Not a refutation of either core.** Each theory's mathematical or computational core is hard to test directly, so the tests targeted biological implementations, which a theorist can revise while keeping the core: Lakatos's research programmes put to work ([`philosophy-of-science` 2.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/02-05-lakatos-and-feyerabend.md)). The authors gave no verdict, and workspace proponents replied (*Neuroscience of Consciousness*, 2025).
- **Not the [contrastive method](#contrastive-method):** it asked where and how the contents of seen pictures are held.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Libet experiments

**The readiness potential, a slow scalp signal before a self-initiated movement, began before the moment subjects reported first feeling the urge to move.** Benjamin Libet and colleagues (*Brain*, 1983): subjects flexed a wrist when they chose and judged, from a moving clock spot, when they first felt the urge. In acts with no reported preplanning, the potential began about 350 ms on average before that reported moment.

- **The popular free-will reading:** the brain starts the act before you decide, so conscious will does not initiate it. Free will itself is [`metaphysics` Module 6](../metaphysics/lessons/06-01-determinism-and-the-consequence-argument.md).
- **Trap:** it compares a reported moment with a trial-averaged potential, so the free-will reading needs further premises. Aaron Schurger and co-authors (*PNAS*, 2012) model the potential as noise drifting to a threshold, which averaging over movement-locked trials turns into a slow rise; an average can have a shape no single trial had ([`neuroscience` 4.3](../neuroscience/lessons/04-03-attention-decision-making.md)).
- **The sense of agency,** felt authorship of one's acts, leans on report the same way.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Inattentional blindness

**Failing to notice a clearly visible object or event because attention is engaged elsewhere.** Daniel Simons and Christopher Chabris, "Gorillas in Our Midst" (1999): observers counting passes between basketball players often failed to notice a person in a gorilla suit walking through the scene.

- **Two readings.** On the [global workspace theory](#global-workspace-theory), a strong but unattended stimulus is [preconscious](#subliminal-preconscious-and-conscious): accessible, not accessed, so not consciously seen. On the [overflow](#overflow-debate) reading it may be experienced without being accessed.
- **Trap:** a strong, unattended stimulus is not subliminal.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Unity of consciousness

**Whether all of a subject's experiences at a time are experienced together, as parts of one experience.** The test case is the split brain. Callosotomy cuts the corpus callosum, the main fibre bridge between the hemispheres, to stop severe seizures spreading; each hemisphere then sees the opposite half of the visual field, and speech is usually the left's. In the lab, the voice names one word while the left hand, run by the right hemisphere, picks out the other; outside the lab such patients act as one person.

- **Models:** two streams, one per hemisphere; a switch model (Tim Bayne), one stream moving between hemispheres with attention; partial unity, each side's experiences unified with a shared third (an emotion, say) but not with each other, so co-experiencing is not transitive.
- **Nagel** ("Brain Bisection and the Unity of Consciousness", 1971) tried hypotheses of this kind and concluded that none fits: there is no answer to how many minds these patients have.
- **Where it strains:** the evidence is access disunity, the question phenomenal disunity. The workspace theory counts broadcasts; an overflow theorist can say access split while experience stayed whole.
- **Trap:** "how many subjects?" is the unity question. How many *persons*, and fission, belong to [`metaphysics` 6.5](../metaphysics/lessons/06-05-what-a-person-is.md) and [6.6](../metaphysics/lessons/06-06-personal-identity-over-time.md).
- **Historical source:** Kant's unity of apperception ([`modern-philosophy` 5.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/05-04-the-transcendental-deduction.md)), a claim about any possible experience, not about brains.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### User illusion

**Dennett's analogy: consciousness is like a computer desktop, whose icons are a useful simplification that misrepresents the machinery.** Open the case and there is no folder, only stored bits; the icon lets you use the machine without knowing how it works. Daniel Dennett (1942–2024).

- **[Illusionism](#illusionism) applies the point to introspection:** the states, the representing and the reports are real; the inner quality introspection presents is had by nothing, as nothing in the computer is a folder.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Illusionism

**Phenomenal properties do not exist: introspection represents our experiences as having them, and the task is to explain that misrepresentation.** Keith Frankish, "Illusionism as a Theory of Consciousness" (*Journal of Consciousness Studies*, 2016). Experiences, pains, seeings, reports and behaviour are all real. What is denied is the private, ineffable, intrinsic, directly known quality introspection presents. What experiences really have are [quasi-phenomenal properties](#quasi-phenomenal-properties). Explaining why introspection misrepresents them, and why the misrepresentation is so hard to disbelieve, is the illusion problem, a question about mechanisms and reports ([easy problems](#easy-problems)). The case in outline:

1. Introspection represents our experiences as having phenomenal properties: intrinsic, private, ineffable qualities, known directly.
2. If such properties existed, no physical or functional account could explain them.
3. A physical, functional explanation of why introspection represents our states this way is available, at least in principle.
4. When a seeming can be explained without supposing that what seems so is so, and what seems so would be anomalous, the better-supported view is that the seeming is an illusion.
5. So experiences have no phenomenal properties, only quasi-phenomenal ones that introspection misrepresents.

- **Weakest premise: 3.** No such explanation exists yet, and a self-model alone cannot be the source, since we model our own beliefs too and nobody finds a hard problem of belief (Chalmers 2018, §2). Reply: premise 3 needs only an explanation in principle, which anyone who thinks all behaviour physically explicable grants, and candidates exist: Derk Pereboom's thesis that introspection attributes primitive qualities to experience as colour vision does to surfaces (2011), and Michael Graziano's attention schema, on which awareness is the brain's simplified model of its own attention (2013). François Kammerer ("Can You Believe It?", 2018) argues that neither explains why the illusion is so hard to shake.
- **Each side's concession.** The realist: problem reports have a physical explanation, so the realist owes an account of how consciousness figures in it (the [meta-problem](#meta-problem) challenge). The illusionist: the mechanism is not in hand.
- **Eliminativist about phenomenal properties** ([eliminative materialism](#eliminative-materialism)), with a different diagnosis from [Quining Qualia](#quining-qualia): introspective misrepresentation, not a confused concept.
- **Where it sits:** Chalmers (2018, §6) places the distinctive illusionist among [type-A physicalists](#type-a-and-type-b-physicalism): no epistemic gap, only the appearance of one. He has written that if he were a materialist he would be an illusionist, and he thinks the view false.
- **Strength:** only the strong form dissolves the [hard problem](#hard-problem) ([strong and weak illusionism](#strong-and-weak-illusionism)).
- **A minority view:** in the PhilPapers 2020 survey, 4.5 percent accepted or leaned towards eliminativism about consciousness, the option nearest illusionism (the survey has none for it), and 62 percent said there is a hard problem.
- **Objections:** the [illusion-of-what objection](#illusion-of-what-objection) and its Moorean form.
- **Trap:** it does not say nobody is in pain or sees red. It affirms pain states and seeing and denies only phenomenal properties in the philosophers' sense.
- **Kin:** Dennett's [user illusion](#user-illusion) and [Cartesian Theater](#cartesian-theater); Mackie's [error theory](../ethics/lessons/06-03-error-theory.md) has the same shape for morality, a systematic error plus an explanation of why we make it.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Quining qualia

**Dennett's 1988 argument that nothing has the properties qualia are supposed to have, because the concept's marks pull apart.** Daniel Dennett, "Quining Qualia" (in Marcel and Bisiach, eds., *Consciousness in Contemporary Science*, 1988). The verb comes from a joke lexicon: to quine is to deny resolutely that something real or significant exists. Four marks of [qualia](#qualia) in the sense he denies: ineffable, intrinsic, private, directly apprehensible in consciousness.

- **Fifteen intuition pumps** ([`philosophical-method` 3.3](../philosophical-method/lessons/03-03-thought-experiments.md)), used on purpose. In one, the coffee tasters Chase and Sanborn have both stopped liking the coffee they once liked. Chase says the taste is unchanged and his standards moved; Sanborn says his standards are unchanged and the taste moved. Dennett argued that neither man's introspection can tell which story is true of him.
- **The step:** a quality that cannot be told apart from a change in reaction is not directly apprehensible, so the marks pull apart.
- **Conclusion:** there are no qualia at all. Eliminativism, on the ground that the concept is confused ([eliminative materialism](#eliminative-materialism)).
- **Not illusionism renamed:** same verdict, different diagnosis. Classify a claim by what it says, not by its author: Dennett later called himself an illusionist.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Heterophenomenology

**Dennett's method: treat what subjects say about their experience as data about how things seem to them, neutral on whether the seemings are accurate.** *Consciousness Explained* (1991); "Who's on First? Heterophenomenology Explained" (2003). Much as an anthropologist records a community's beliefs about a forest god without deciding whether the god exists (the lesson's analogy).

- **A method, not the illusionist verdict:** it leaves the seemings' accuracy open.
- **Realist objection:** recording every seeming as a belief is not neutral, since whether seeming is more than judging is the dispute. By design it records "it seems to me that I feel pain", never "I feel pain".

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Quasi-phenomenal properties

**The physical or functional properties of experiences that introspection detects and misrepresents as phenomenal.** Frankish (2016): on [illusionism](#illusionism), these are what experiences really have.

- **Not a weaker grade of phenomenal property.** They are physical or functional properties; the phenomenal ones they are represented as having do not exist.
- **Pain on this view** is a real state with quasi-phenomenal properties, not a mere word.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Strong and weak illusionism

**Strong: phenomenal consciousness does not exist. Weak: it exists, but some of its apparent features are illusory.** Frankish argues that only the strong form dissolves the [hard problem](#hard-problem), and Chalmers agrees (2018, §6): the hard problem needs only that there is [something it is like](#what-it-is-like) to be us, so weak illusionism leaves it standing.

- **Weak illusionism is a form of realism.** Chalmers accepts a version: the visual field seems detailed throughout, and he takes that to be an illusion that need be no extra experience, only a false judgment. Strong illusionism extends the point to all experience.
- **Evidence of introspective error supports the weak form only.** People are confidently wrong about features of their current experience (Eric Schwitzgebel, "The Unreliability of Naive Introspection", 2008), but a misdescribed experience can still be phenomenal.
- **The test for a claim:** if it says the experience it calls misdescribed is still one there is something it is like to have, it is weak illusionism, hence realism; if it denies there is anything phenomenal at all, strong.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Meta-problem

**The problem of explaining why we think there is a hard problem of consciousness.** David Chalmers, "The Meta-Problem of Consciousness" (*Journal of Consciousness Studies*, 2018). Its data are [problem intuitions](#problem-intuitions). Judgments and reports are behaviour, so the meta-problem is, strictly, an [easy problem](#easy-problems).

- **Neutral between realism and illusionism.** The illusionist's illusion problem is one way of taking it; realists owe a solution too.
- **The meta-problem challenge:** whatever a theory says underlies consciousness should help produce our judgments about it.
- **Chalmers counts debunking arguments built on it** as the best case for illusionism ([debunking argument](#debunking-argument)).
- **Trap:** a solution would not by itself prove illusionism. Explaining why we believe something explains it away only with a further premise (premise 4 of the case for [illusionism](#illusionism)).
- **Machines ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)):** Chalmers notes that a very simple program can mimic some of our puzzled reports, which is why self-report is confounded.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Problem intuitions

**Our dispositions to judge and report that consciousness poses a special problem.** Chalmers (2018): for instance, that consciousness cannot be explained physically, that it is non-physical, that Mary learns something new ([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)), that zombies are conceivable ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)). They are the data of the [meta-problem](#meta-problem).

- **What an illusionist mechanism must yield:** these intuitions and their stubbornness. A self-model alone does not, since we model our beliefs too and have no problem intuitions about belief.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Debunking argument

**An explanation of our beliefs about consciousness that is independent of consciousness would show those beliefs unjustified.** Chalmers (2018, §5), sharpening premises 3 and 4 of the case for [illusionism](#illusionism):

1. There is a correct explanation of our beliefs about consciousness that is independent of consciousness.
2. If there is, those beliefs are not justified.
3. So they are not justified.

- **Everything turns on "independent".** An explanation can be independent of X merely by not mentioning it (descriptive independence), or because X neither causes nor constitutes the processes that form the beliefs (causal and constitutive independence). Brain science explains table beliefs without mentioning tables, and those beliefs are fine.
- **Coincidence form:** if the explanation is independent and correct, the beliefs' being true is luck.
- **Realist answers:** consciousness realizes or causes the belief-forming processes, which raises the [zombie](#zombie-argument) question and the [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument) ([2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)); or the beliefs are justified by acquaintance, not by their causes.
- **Trap:** the conclusion concerns justification. That consciousness does not exist is a further step.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Illusion-of-what objection

**If phenomenal consciousness is an illusion, the illusion is itself something it is like to undergo, so phenomenal consciousness exists after all.** In outline:

1. An illusion is a case in which something seems to be F and is not.
2. When my experience seems to me to glow, that seeming is itself something it is like to undergo.
3. So if the glow is an illusion, the illusion has phenomenal character, and phenomenal consciousness exists after all.

- **Sharpest form:** John Searle (*The Mystery of Consciousness*, 1997): for consciousness there is no gap between seeming and being.
- **The illusionist denies premise 2:** a seeming can be a representation (a judgment, an intuition, a disposition to report, a self-model saying that a state has quality Q) with no phenomenal property of its own. Representing Q does not require having it; the Mach-band signal is not bright. Chalmers grants that strong illusionism is coherent (2018, §7).
- **Regrouped as the Moorean argument** (Chalmers 2018, §7): people feel pain; if strong illusionism is true, no one does; so it is false. Illusionist rejoinder: the theory predicts that the first premise will seem undeniable, so its seeming so is no independent evidence. Realist: the datum is the pain, not its seeming obvious.
- **The crux:** whether the datum is "I feel pain" or "it seems to me that I feel pain". Introspection cannot settle it, since its reliability is the question. The realist concedes coherence; the illusionist, that the view denies something more certain-seeming than any premise for it.
- **Ancestor:** C. D. Broad (1925) argued that a theory calling mentality itself delusive contradicts itself, since misperceiving and misjudging are mental ([1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md)); premise 2 is what carries that from mentality in general to phenomenal properties.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Cartesian Theater

**Dennett's name for the picture he rejects: a central place in the brain where everything comes together and is shown to an inner viewer.** Daniel Dennett (*Consciousness Explained*, 1991).

- **Bridge:** the normalization and evidence-accumulation models of [`neuroscience` 4.3](../neuroscience/lessons/04-03-attention-decision-making.md) explain selection without a chooser; the illusionist generalizes this to experience without an inner viewer.
- **Kin:** Hume looked for a self and found only perceptions ([`modern-philosophy` 4.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/04-04-the-self-belief-and-the-passions.md)).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Structuralism about physics

**Physics characterizes the world only by its structure and dynamics: what things do to one another, not what they intrinsically are.** R1 of [Russellian monism](#russellian-monism). The rules of chess define the bishop by how it may move and are silent on whether yours is boxwood, plastic or pixels. Bertrand Russell, *The Analysis of Matter* (1927): matter is a structure of events whose causal laws and spatio-temporal relations "are more or less known, but their intrinsic character is not known" (ch. XXXVII, p. 384). Arthur Eddington: "The physical atom is, like everything else in physics, a schedule of pointer readings" (*The Nature of the Physical World*, 1928, ch. XII, p. 259).

- **State it as "structure and dispositions", not "only dispositions".**
- **Newman's objection presses it:** pure structure says little beyond how many things there are, so the Russellian must mean a definite causal structure ([`philosophy-of-science` 5.3](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/05-03-structural-realism.md)).
- **Structural realism** reads R1 for the philosophy of science: the epistemic structural realist holds R1 and R2 without R3, and the ontic structural realist denies R2.
- **Trap:** "physics tells us only what matter does" is R1; "so there is nothing more to matter than what it does" denies R2 ([quiddity](#quiddity)). The "so" slides from what physics says to what there is, and "we know nothing else about it" is not "there is nothing else".

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Quiddity

**The intrinsic, categorical nature that underlies a basic physical disposition and that physics does not describe.** R2 of [Russellian monism](#russellian-monism), realism about quiddities: properties underlie the structure physics describes and categorically ground its basic dispositions. Categorical versus dispositional properties: [`metaphysics` 4.3](../metaphysics/lessons/04-03-powers-dispositions-and-laws.md).

- **Who denies them.** The dispositional essentialist (Alexander Bird): at bottom, attracting is what charge is, so a disposition needs no categorical bearer. The ontic structural realist: nothing stands behind the relations.
- **Who grants them but blocks the step to experience:** Rae Langton's Kantian humility, on which they exist and cannot be known ([`modern-philosophy` 6.1](../modern-philosophy/lessons/06-01-transcendental-idealism-and-the-thing-in-itself.md)).
- **Russell's "each other's washing"** (ch. XXXI, p. 325) is often cited for the need of bearers, though he wrote it about turning things into laws about other things.
- **Mental causation:** on Russellian physicalism consciousness is the categorical side of physical causes, so the [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument) has nothing to exclude; Robert Howell (2015) replies that quiddities then ride along without doing causal work.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Panpsychism

**Mentality, in the sense of the simplest experience and not thought, is fundamental and ubiquitous in nature.** Nobody says electrons believe anything, and a stone need not be a subject even if its particles are. Two routes in: from the intrinsic natures physics leaves out ([Russellian monism](#russellian-monism), the [intrinsic nature argument](#intrinsic-nature-argument)), and from below, Galen Strawson's [anti-emergence argument](#anti-emergence-argument).

- **Varieties:** [constitutive or emergentist](#constitutive-and-emergentist-panpsychism); micropsychism or [cosmopsychism](#cosmopsychism). [Panprotopsychism](#panprotopsychism) is a neighbour, not a variety: nothing at its base is experienced.
- **Not anti-physicalist by definition.** That depends on what "physical" means, and Strawson calls his view physicalism. What every panpsychist denies is that the physical is wholly non-experiential.
- **Its hardest problem,** friends and critics agree, is the [combination problem](#combination-problem).
- **Against the hard problem:** it keeps the datum the illusionist denies and spreads it through nature.
- **Least to say about thought** ([4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)), since it puts experience, not thought, at the base.
- **Survey:** PhilPapers 2020, 7.55 percent of the 1,020 respondents to the consciousness question accepted or leaned towards it.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Panprotopsychism

**The fundamental properties are not experiences, and there is nothing it is like to have one, but together they constitute experience, with truths about them entailing truths about experience a priori.** David Chalmers, "Panpsychism and Panprotopsychism" (2013; reprinted 2015). Chalmers stipulates the a priori entailment clause; without it every physicalist would qualify, as Daniel Stoljar pointed out, since every non-dualist holds that something non-experiential constitutes experience.

- **Not a milder panpsychism:** nothing is experienced at the base.
- **Separated from type-B physicalism** ([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)) by the a priori entailment clause, not by the claim that something non-experiential constitutes experience. Missing this is Stoljar's trap.
- **Its cost on 3.5's map ("revenge"):** could Mary learn all the protophenomenal truths in her room?
- **On Strawson's argument:** the panprotopsychist holds that heterogeneity (premise 5 of the [anti-emergence argument](#anti-emergence-argument)) is true only of our present concepts.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Constitutive and emergentist panpsychism

**Constitutive: your experience is grounded in, consists in, micro-experiences. Emergentist: micro-subjects cause a new fundamental subject that is not grounded in them.** Grounding is non-causal "consisting in", as a party consists in people dancing ([`metaphysics` 2.4](../metaphysics/lessons/02-04-essence-and-grounding.md)).

- **Constitutive micropsychism** suffers most from the [combination problem](#combination-problem), above all from [subject-summing](#subject-summing-problem).
- **Emergentist (layered) panpsychism:** Gregg Rosenberg (2004), Godehard Brüntrup (2016). It dodges some of the combination problem by positing new fundamental subjects. Fusion is a further option: micro-subjects cease to exist as they form a mind (Seager; Hedda Hassel Mørch).
- **Trap:** "consists wholly in" marks grounding, not causation; experiences being "arranged" does not make a view emergentist.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Cosmopsychism

**The universe as a whole is the one fundamental conscious subject, and your experience is grounded in it.** Jonathan Schaffer's priority monism (the cosmos is the one fundamental thing, and its parts are grounded in it) combined with panpsychism: Philip Goff, *Consciousness and Fundamental Reality* (2017). Grounding runs from the whole to the part.

- **Trades combination for decombination:** how does one cosmic subject ground many distinct subjects?
- **Not pantheism:** it gives the universe experience, not thought, agency or divinity.
- **A route out of [subject-summing](#subject-summing-problem).**

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Anti-emergence argument

**Experience cannot emerge from the wholly non-experiential, so it is present in the ultimate constituents of the physical.** Galen Strawson, "Realistic Monism: Why Physicalism Entails Panpsychism" (*Journal of Consciousness Studies*, 2006). The lesson's reconstruction:

1. (Realism.) Experience is concretely real.
2. (Physicalism.) Every real concrete phenomenon is physical, in a sense that does not assume the physical is non-experiential.
3. (No brute emergence.) If Y emerges from X, there is something about X, and X alone, in virtue of which Y emerges and which suffices for it.
4. (Homogeneity.) Intelligible emergence runs within one family of concepts: liquidity emerges from molecules sliding past one another, a story told wholly in terms of shape, size, mass, charge and motion.
5. (Heterogeneity.) No family of concepts spans both the experiential and the wholly non-experiential.
6. So experience does not emerge from the wholly non-experiential, and the ultimate constituents of the physical are themselves experiential.

- **Premise 1 sets the illusionist aside; it does not answer him.**
- **Weakest premise: 3.** Every theory bottoms out in brute laws, so a fundamental law by which some non-experiential configurations yield experience is no worse than any other: C. D. Broad's emergentism (1925) and Chalmers's naturalistic dualism (*The Conscious Mind*, 1996). Strawson's demand that emergence never be unintelligible "even to God" looks like a rationalist preference. Reply: such a law is a new fundamental fact about experience, so whoever posits it has given up premise 2's monism.
- **Each side's concession.** Strawson's side: the argument binds only physicalists who refuse brute psychophysical laws. The emergentist: the view adds such laws, which is property dualism, or [strong emergence](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md), under another name.
- **Ancestor:** Thomas Nagel, "Panpsychism" (1979), concluded only that basic matter has properties, perhaps not experiential, that imply mind.
- **The panprotopsychist** holds premise 5 true only of our present concepts ([panprotopsychism](#panprotopsychism)).

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Intrinsic nature argument

**Physics gives matter only a role; roles need bearers with natures; the one nature known from the inside is experience; so matter's nature is plausibly experiential or proto-experiential.** Roots in Russell (1927) and Eddington (1928), who asked why not attach the schedule of pointer readings to "something of spiritual nature of which a prominent characteristic is thought" (p. 259); developed by Philip Goff (2017). Run on charge:

1. Everything physics says about negative charge is relational or dispositional.
2. A disposition is had in virtue of something that is not merely a further disposition.
3. So charge has a categorical nature physics does not state, its [quiddity](#quiddity).
4. Assuming dualism false, the one categorical nature anyone knows without inference is the character of their own experience, which is the nature of some events in their brain.
5. Absent a reason to think otherwise, the simplest hypothesis is that the rest of matter has a nature of the same general kind (Goff's simplicity argument, 2017, ch. 7).
6. So the quiddity of charge is experiential or proto-experiential.

- **The exits.** The dispositional essentialist (Bird) denies 2. The ontic structural realist denies that 3 has a subject. Langton's Kantian humility grants 3 but blocks the conclusion, since we cannot know the natures. Newman's objection presses 1. The dualist denies 4, because experience is not the inside of brain matter, and so does the illusionist, because introspection misrepresents the brain.
- **Untouched by the [combination problem](#combination-problem):** it never claims to say how experiences combine.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Combination problem

**How micro-level experiences, each with its own subject, could combine into a single macro-level subject such as you.** Pressed by William James (*The Principles of Psychology*, 1890, ch. VI); named by William Seager (1995). Friends and critics agree it is panpsychism's hardest problem.

- **Chalmers's split** ("The Combination Problem for Panpsychism", 2016; the volume is sometimes dated 2017): subjects, how many points of view become one ([subject-summing](#subject-summing-problem)); qualities, how a few basic qualities yield smell, colour and pain; structure, why smooth experience sits on a grainy brain.
- **Mild and strong forms.** The mild form is a debt most panpsychists accept. Only the strong form, an argument that subjects cannot sum, claims a refutation.
- **Whom it hits:** constitutive views hardest; [emergentist panpsychism](#constitutive-and-emergentist-panpsychism) dodges some of it by positing new fundamental subjects; for [cosmopsychism](#cosmopsychism) it becomes decombination.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Subject-summing problem

**Whether distinct conscious subjects can sum into a further subject, and how.** The subjects part of the [combination problem](#combination-problem), and its hardest. Philip Goff (2009; 2017) turns it into an argument against constitutive micropsychism:

1. For any subjects with any experiences, it is conceivable that they exist and no further subject does.
2. So a micro-experiential zombie is conceivable: your physical duplicate, every particle experiencing, nothing experiencing at the scale of the whole.
3. If conceivability implies possibility, the micro-facts do not necessitate yours, so they do not ground it, and constitutive micropsychism is false.

- **Its own weapon:** step 3 is the one panpsychists used against physicalism in the [zombie argument](#zombie-argument). Grant it, and constitutive panpsychism falls; deny it, and the physicalist asks why the zombie argument still worked.
- **Sam Coleman (2014):** subject-summing is incoherent, since a point of view partly consists in excluding every other.
- **Defenders' routes:** a bonding relation, unknown to us in its nature, that makes micro-subjects compose a further subject (Goff, 2016); fusion, in which micro-subjects cease to exist as they form a mind (Seager; Mørch); or [cosmopsychism](#cosmopsychism), which trades combination for decombination.
- **The standoff:** the critic says panpsychism inherits the gap it was called in to close; the defender says an unsolved problem is not a refutation.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

**Module 4: Intentionality and perception**

### Intentionality

**A mental state's aboutness: its being of, about or directed at something, which need not exist.** You can fear a storm that never comes. A term of art, with nothing to do with doing something on purpose. Brentano's 1874 phrase for it is "intentional inexistence". His text, its readings and Husserl's revision are read in [`phenomenology-and-personalism` 1.1](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/01-01-intentionality-from-brentano-to-husserl.md); this course asks whether his thesis is true.

- **Module 4's questions about it.** Is it the mark of the mental ([Brentano's thesis](#brentanos-thesis), 4.1)? How can a physical state have it ([naturalizing content](#naturalizing-content), 4.2)? Is what fixes it in the head ([content externalism](#content-externalism), 4.3)?
- **Perception is aboutness at its most intimate** (4.4): a mug you see seems simply there, its blue and its shape given to you. Hallucination is the hard case ([argument from hallucination](#argument-from-hallucination)).
- **Two grades:** see [original and derived intentionality](#original-and-derived-intentionality).
- **It was one of the [marks of the mental](#marks-of-the-mental)** in 1.1, beside phenomenal consciousness and first-person access.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)

### Brentano's thesis

**Intentionality is the mark of the mental: all and only mental states are intentional.** Franz Brentano, *Psychologie vom empirischen Standpunkte* (1874), Book II ch. I §5, pp. 115-16, defines mental phenomena as "such phenomena as contain an object intentionally within themselves" (4.1's translation). The live debate states it in two halves, as Roderick Chisholm revived it (*Perceiving*, 1957):

1. **B1.** Every mental state is intentional. (Nothing in the mind is about nothing.)
2. **B2.** Only mental states are originally intentional; anything else that is about something borrows its aboutness from a mind. (The fuel gauge means "low" because drivers read it that way.)

- **Which test hits which half.** Pains and moods test B1: Hamilton's feelings are the first dispute Brentano meets, aimed at "all". Fuel gauges and sentences test B2. On moods, representationalists (Michael Tye, William Lycan) say a mood represents one's surroundings, or things in general, as being some way; Ned Block's dual view says some states have phenomenal character and no intentional content.
- **Brentano needs both halves for a definition,** which is why his "and so" can follow the exclusivity sentence: a mark that holds of all mental phenomena (against Bain), plus "only".
- **His "physical phenomenon" is a sensed colour or tone,** not a physical object. His "only" contrasts acts with what they present, not minds with thermostats, so a thermostat tests only the modern B2.
- **"Originally" is the modern repair:** words and gauges have [derived intentionality](#original-and-derived-intentionality), minds original (John Searle, John Haugeland, Fodor). Dennett denies the line is sharp.
- **Quine's dilemma** (*Word and Object*, 1960): if intentional idioms resist physical paraphrase, give up physicalism or count the idioms baseless. Fodor's realism, Churchland's eliminativism and Dennett's stance are three ways through ([folk psychology](#folk-psychology)).
- **George Molnar's "physical intentionality"** of powers would refute B2 if it were intentionality proper; [`metaphysics` 4.3](../metaphysics/lessons/04-03-powers-dispositions-and-laws.md) treats it as a borrowed analogy.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Propositional attitudes

**An attitude (believing, wanting, fearing) toward a content that a that-clause gives and that can be true or false.** Believing *that* it will rain; wanting *that* you stay dry. The states [Brentano's thesis](#brentanos-thesis) fits best and the states [folk psychology](#folk-psychology) works with. A state's content is the way it represents the world as being (4.3).

- **Attitude and content are separate dimensions:** one content can be believed, feared or hoped.
- **In 4.3** the that-clause reporting a belief is taken to give its content, and beliefs with different truth conditions are different beliefs: premise P3 of the [Twin Earth](#twin-earth) argument on belief, and the step its critics target.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Folk psychology

**The everyday practice of explaining and predicting behaviour by beliefs, desires and the other attitudes.** Its core generalization, roughly: if someone wants X and believes that doing Y is the best way to get X, then, other things equal, she will tend to do Y. She took the umbrella because she believes it will rain and wants to stay dry.

- **Three stances on its posits,** after three kinds of posit in old sciences. [Intentional realism](#intentional-realism) (Jerry Fodor): beliefs are like Mendel's factors, inner states later found (in DNA). [Eliminative materialism](#eliminative-materialism) (Paul Churchland): like phlogiston, which turned out not to exist. The [intentional stance](#intentional-stance) (Daniel Dennett): like a centre of gravity, real and predictive though no particle sits there.
- **4.1's map.** First, a conceptual question: does having a belief require a discrete inner state that causes behaviour because of its content? No: the stance. Yes: then an empirical question, will the science of cognition find such states? Yes: realism. No: eliminativism.
- **"Folk" does not mean false.** That folk psychology is a false theory is the eliminativist's thesis, not the definition.
- **Whether it is a theory at all** is [theory-theory and simulation](#theory-theory-and-simulation).
- **Belief-desire psychology** also drives the Humean theory of motivation, read in [`modern-philosophy` 4.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/04-04-the-self-belief-and-the-passions.md).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Intentional realism

**Beliefs and desires are real inner states that cause behaviour by virtue of their contents.** Jerry Fodor (*The Language of Thought*, 1975; *Psychosemantics*, 1987): beliefs are like genes, there to be found. Fodor adds the [representational theory of mind](#representational-theory-of-mind) (R1) and the [language of thought](#language-of-thought) (R2), and argues for R2 from [systematicity](#systematicity).

- **Realism is not the language of thought.** Realism says only that beliefs exist and cause behaviour by virtue of their content. LOT adds a claim about format, and many cognitive scientists posit representations without sentence-like structure (Nicholas Shea, 2018). A realist who denies LOT is still a realist.
- **The chess program** (4.1 Example 1). It holds explicit data structures for the board, so, for the realist, R1 is literally true of its belief that its queen is attacked. Its queen-out-early tendency is written nowhere. Fodor's answer (*Psychosemantics*, 1987, p. 25): a rule the machine merely conforms to need not be represented. Realist and stance theorist agree it believes its queen is attacked and disagree about what makes that true.
- **What would refute it:** finding no discrete, content-bearing, causally active states. Ramsey, Stich and Garon (1990) argued that if distributed networks model memory correctly, there are none ([connectionism](#connectionism)).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Intentional stance

**Predict a system by treating it as a rational agent with the beliefs and desires it ought to have; for Dennett, having beliefs is being reliably predictable this way.** Daniel Dennett, "Intentional Systems" (1971), "True Believers" (1981), *The Intentional Stance* (1987):

1. **D1.** The intentional strategy: treat a system as a rational agent, credit it with the beliefs and desires it ought to have, and predict that it will act on them.
2. **D2.** A system has beliefs just in case this strategy predicts its behaviour reliably and in detail, whatever is inside it.

- **Three stances on a chess computer** ("Intentional Systems"). Physical: compute its move from its circuitry, possible in principle and hopeless in practice. Design: trace its program. Intentional: assume it wants to win and believes what a good player in its position would.
- **Real patterns** (Dennett, 1991): beliefs are there in the behaviour, like a centre of gravity, not written inside. In 1977 Dennett reported a programmer's remark that a rival chess program "thinks" it should bring its queen out early, though nothing in it mentions queens. The real patterns reappear as Everettian branches in [`philosophy-of-physics`](../philosophy-of-physics/syllabus.md).
- **Not eliminativism, not behaviourism.** Beliefs are real patterns, and the test is a rationality-based strategy's success, not a behavioural definition of each belief ([logical behaviourism](#logical-behaviourism), 2.1). The live charge is that he explains beliefs away rather than explaining them.
- **Dennett held all intentionality to be derived** (6.2), so Searle's line between [original and derived intentionality](#original-and-derived-intentionality) marks no real difference for him. Dennett is also a source of the [illusionism](#illusionism) of 3.4.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Representational theory of mind

**To have an attitude toward a content is to stand in a functional relation to an inner representation with that content.** Fodor's R1, with $p$ standing for any declarative sentence: to believe that $p$ is to stand in a distinctive functional relation to a mental representation that means that $p$; likewise for the other attitudes.

- **The "belief box" is a role, not a place:** a functional role, as in [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md).
- **It leaves a debt** (4.2): if thinking is the manipulation of inner symbols, what makes a symbol mean *horse* rather than *cow*, or nothing? Paying it is [naturalizing content](#naturalizing-content).
- **6.1 adds computation to it:** the [computational theory of mind](#computational-theory-of-mind).
- **R2, the [language of thought](#language-of-thought),** is a further claim about the representations' format.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Language of thought

**We think in an inner code (Mentalese): mental representations with words and a syntax, which thinking processes respond to.** Fodor, *The Language of Thought* (1975). R2: thinking is a causal sequence of such representations, which have words and a syntax that the processes respond to.

- **A claim about format,** separable from [intentional realism](#intentional-realism) (a claim about existence and causal role).
- **The argument for it** is [systematicity](#systematicity) (Fodor and Pylyshyn, 1988); the rival is [connectionism](#connectionism).
- **Ockham's mental language** ([`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 8.4](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/08-04-ockham-nominalism-and-the-razor.md)) resembles Mentalese: a resemblance rather than a lineage, since Fodor's revival came from computing.
- **Fodor's concept nativism** (1975; restated 2008): concepts are acquired but never learned, since testing a hypothesis about what a concept applies to already requires the concept, so in his sense they are innate. It continues the dispute of [`modern-philosophy` 3.1](../modern-philosophy/lessons/03-01-locke-against-innate-ideas.md); whether developmental science vindicates it is a question for cognitive science.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Systematicity

**Thinkable thoughts come in families: whoever can think that the dog chases the cat can think that the cat chases the dog.** Jerry Fodor and Zenon Pylyshyn, "Connectionism and Cognitive Architecture" (1988), as the SEP reconstructs their argument for the [language of thought](#language-of-thought): (S1) thought is systematic by psychological law; (S2) a theory of cognition must explain why this holds by law, not by luck; (S3) only representations built from reusable constituents, processed by operations sensitive to that structure, explain it; so (C) a connectionist network either has such representations, and so implements a language of thought, or lacks them, and so fails S2.

- **What it does not say:** that networks cannot be systematic. It says that connectionism does not guarantee systematicity: a network that happens to be systematic explains nothing.
- **The data in 4.1.** Recurrent networks passed a held-out "jump around right" test but failed on commands longer than any in training (Loula, Baroni and Lake, 2018, as the SEP reports). Brenden Lake and Marco Baroni (*Nature*, 2023): a standard network trained by meta-learning on many small compositional tasks generalized as systematically as people in their instruction-learning experiment, presented as answering Fodor and Pylyshyn.
- **The dilemma on a success:** either the network learned reusable parts, and implements a language of thought, or its systematicity is an artefact of training, which could as easily have produced a failure (Fodor and McLaughlin, 1990). The classicist reads "trained for compositional skill" as a concession.
- **Replies.** A classical system can also be programmed to accept "John loves Mary" and refuse "Mary loves John", so S2 may demand a lawfulness no architecture supplies; and human systematicity is itself partial (Kent Johnson, 2004).
- **Where no test decides.** Constituents encoded in vectors and recoverable without being literal parts (Paul Smolensky's tensor products): Fodor and McLaughlin say this cannot do the classical causal work, Murat Aydede that it is a language of thought in another format. That turns on whether "classical" names a format or a structure.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Connectionism

**Thinking as activation spreading through networks of simple units, with knowledge stored in the connection weights.** The rival to the [language of thought](#language-of-thought), and the ancestor of the networks in [`deep-learning` 1.1](../deep-learning/lessons/01-01-from-linear-models-to-the-mlp.md).

- **Fodor and Pylyshyn's dilemma** ([systematicity](#systematicity)): connectionism is at best a theory of how the language of thought is wired.
- **"Representation" means something else in deep learning.** In [`deep-learning` 1.1](../deep-learning/lessons/01-01-from-linear-models-to-the-mlp.md) and [4.1](../deep-learning/lessons/04-01-autoencoders-and-representation-learning.md) it is a learned feature vector, judged by what a probe can read from it, not by accuracy conditions. Whether such vectors are about anything is the question of 4.2 and 6.3. Probe-readability shows information, not content that could be false.
- **The eliminativist's conditional.** Ramsey, Stich and Garon (1990): if distributed networks model memory correctly, nothing is a discrete inner state causing behaviour by virtue of its content, so nothing meets the conditions for belief ([eliminative materialism](#eliminative-materialism)).
- **Connectionism against the language of thought** is the old form of the question [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) asks about transformers.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Theory-theory and simulation

**Two accounts of how we attribute mental states: by applying a tacit folk theory, or by running our own decision-making offline on pretend beliefs and desires.** Theory-theory: Wilfrid Sellars (1956), and premise E1 of Churchland's argument for [eliminative materialism](#eliminative-materialism). Simulation theory: Robert Gordon (1986), Jane Heal (1986), Alvin Goldman (1989).

- **Simulation targets E1.** If we predict others by simulation, there may be no folk theory to be false, and Churchland's tests of a theory (explanatory failure, stagnation, poor fit with the sciences) have no target.
- **The strongest reply** (SEP, "Eliminative Materialism"): elimination needs only that our concept of belief sets conditions (a discrete inner state causing behaviour by virtue of its content) that nothing meets.
- **Concessions.** The eliminativist concedes that stagnation and explanatory failure then weigh little, so the case rests on what cognitive science finds. The simulation theorist concedes that simulation alone protects beliefs from nothing.
- **Sideways:** simulation theory is the analytic counterpart of the question about empathy in [`phenomenology-and-personalism` 4.3](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/04-03-stein-empathy.md).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Naturalizing content

**Saying what makes a state about something in terms that mention no meaning, intention or mind: only causes, laws, probabilities and biology.** It pays the debt the [representational theory of mind](#representational-theory-of-mind) leaves: if thinking is the manipulation of inner symbols, what makes a symbol mean *horse* rather than *cow*, or nothing?

- **The test every candidate faces is misrepresentation.** A theory on which every token is true has explained a thermometer, not a belief.
- **Each candidate adds one ingredient to information** ([natural meaning](#natural-meaning)): a learning period (Dretske); a dependence among laws ([asymmetric dependence](#asymmetric-dependence), Fodor); a biological function, what the state is for as selection made it ([teleosemantics](#teleosemantics): Millikan, Papineau); or, by a different route, a role in inference ([inferential-role semantics](#inferential-role-semantics)). The function is the selected-effect function of [`philosophy-of-science` 4.2](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-02-causal-explanation.md): what earlier tokens did that got the trait selected.
- **Three repairs, three facts.** On the cow-caused HORSE token, the learning period, asymmetric dependence and teleosemantics each make it false through a different fact (training, present laws, selection history), and each objection aims at that fact: learning has no end; proximal stimuli and smuggled meaning; [Swampman](#swampman) and coextensive properties.
- **Stakes.** Most physicalists want one theory to succeed. Defenders of [Brentano's thesis](#brentanos-thesis), [phenomenal-intentionality](#phenomenal-intentionality) theorists and hylomorphists (Module 5) expect it to fail somewhere, and 5.2's [determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument) says where.
- **The smuggling objection** (where 4.2's argument is weakest): each repair's ingredient is determinate only given meaning. Learning is determinate only if a teacher meant HORSE for horses (Fodor, 1984); the asymmetries seem explicable only by what the symbols mean; "selected for detecting dogs" wins only because we know what the rabbit is after. The teleosemantic reply: selection-for is causal, settled by which property explains the success, and biologists use it with no semantic help.
- **Concessions.** The naturalist: no theory yet shows how a thought about democracy or quarks gets its content. The critic: for simple signals, such as a bee's dance, selected function assigns content with no visible circularity.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)

### Natural meaning

**Meaning in the sense in which smoke means fire: a sign that cannot be false.** H. P. Grice, "Meaning" (1957). Twelve rings mean the tree was twelve years old. If the spots mean measles in this sense, the child has measles; the doctor's *belief* that she has measles can be false.

- **Natural meaning is information.** A theory of content must turn information into something that can be wrong: that is the job of [naturalizing content](#naturalizing-content).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Informational semantics

**A state means what it reliably signals: what it indicates.** Fred Dretske, *Knowledge and the Flow of Information* (1981). For a state type $R$ and a condition $C$, with $\Pr(C \mid R)$ the probability that $C$ obtains given that $R$ is tokened:

$$R \text{ indicates } C \iff \Pr(C \mid R) = 1 \text{, given the channel conditions,}$$

where the channel conditions are the stable background the signal's reliability depends on. In its simplest form the theory says $R$ represents that $C$ just when $R$ indicates $C$.

- **Indication entails truth: it cannot misfire.** Hence the [disjunction problem](#disjunction-problem).
- **Not Shannon's information.** Indication requires probability 1 and is all-or-nothing; mutual information ([`information-theory` 1.3](../information-theory/lessons/01-03-mutual-information.md)) is graded and symmetric. Smoke tells you about fire exactly as much as fire tells you about smoke, and aboutness runs one way.
- **The smoke detector** (4.2 Example 1). In a kitchen where steaks get seared, the probability of fire given the alarm is below 1, so the alarm carries no information that there is a fire, even on the night there is one, though it raises the probability of fire. A false alarm is a real error only because the detector is *for* fire, a function borrowed from its designers.
- **Dretske's two later theories.** First a learning period (1981): a state means what it indicated while the creature was learning to token it, so later deviant tokens are false. Fodor (1984): learning has no principled end, and a cow seen during training would have caused HORSE then too. Then the indicator-function theory ("Misrepresentation", 1986; *Explaining Behavior*, 1988): content is what a state has the *function* to indicate, with functions recruited by learning. The 1981 indication theory and the 1986/88 indicator-function theory are different theories, and only the second is teleological. It meets the problem of [distal content](#distal-content).
- **Probes.** A linear probe in [`deep-learning` 4.1](../deep-learning/lessons/04-01-autoencoders-and-representation-learning.md) asks what a layer carries information about: Dretske's question, not yet Millikan's.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Disjunction problem

**If a symbol means whatever reliably causes it, its mistaken causes are folded into its content, so it can never be wrong.** Named by Jerry Fodor, "Semantics, Wisconsin Style" (1984); the horse version is usually told after his *Psychosemantics* (1987). Against [informational semantics](#informational-semantics) in its simplest form, in outline: content is indication (P1); HORSE, the inner symbol, is tokened by horses and also by cows glimpsed on a dark night (P2); if the cows fall within the channel conditions, given HORSE the probability of a horse is below 1 and of horse-or-cow-on-a-dark-night it is 1 (P3); so HORSE means *horse or cow-on-a-dark-night* and the cow-caused token is true (C1); a state none of whose tokens can be false is not a belief or a perception (P4); so information alone does not give mental content (C2).

- **It concerns whether error is possible, not how often errors happen.**
- **C2 is common ground** among the repairs; see [naturalizing content](#naturalizing-content) for which fact each repair uses to make the cow token false.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Asymmetric dependence

**HORSE means horse if horses cause it by law, and every other cause of it does so only because horses do, not the other way round.** Jerry Fodor, *Psychosemantics* (1987); "A Theory of Content" (1990). The conditions:

1. **A1.** It is a law that horses cause HORSE tokens. (The content is a cause.)
2. **A2.** Whatever else causes HORSE (cows on dark nights, the question "what pulls a hansom cab?") does so only because horses do, and not the other way round. (Cows trigger HORSE by looking like horses; better lighting breaks only the cow link.)
3. **A3.** The dependence holds among the laws now, not in their history. (How the symbol was acquired is irrelevant.)

- **Counterfactual, and synchronic.** Because of A3 the theory is not historical: on it [Swampman](#swampman) has content, and makes mistakes, from the start.
- **The objections:** drugs or microelectrodes could trigger any brain state without depending on horses; where perception runs through a single sensory channel, every link depends on the one from the proximal stimulus, so content comes out proximal ([distal content](#distal-content)); only meaning seems to explain the asymmetries (Seager, 1993; Adams and Aizawa, 1994).
- **On the smoke detector** (4.2 Example 1): fire and steak reach the alarm only through particles. Break the fire link and the steak link survives; both depend on the particle link. If the alarm means anything on this theory, it means particles.
- **It is the semantics Fodor offered** the [representational theory of mind](#representational-theory-of-mind).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Distal content

**Which link in the causal chain leading to a representation is its content: the distal object, or a stimulus nearer the senses?** Dretske met it with his magnetotactic bacteria ("Misrepresentation", 1986). Their chains of tiny magnets steer them along the Earth's field, down into the oxygen-poor sediment they need. Hold a bar magnet above them, turned against the Earth's field, and they swim up to their death. Their magnets indicate magnetic north and oxygen-poor water at once: which do they have the function to indicate, and is the swim up a mistake or correct tracking of the field?

- **Against [asymmetric dependence](#asymmetric-dependence):** where perception runs through a single sensory channel, every link depends on the one from the proximal stimulus, so content comes out proximal (the single-channel objection).
- **Teleosemantic theories differ on where to look** ([producer and consumer](#producer-and-consumer)): Millikan at the condition the consumers need, Neander at what the producer was selected to respond to.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Producer and consumer

**Millikan's model of a representation: it sits between a system that makes it and systems that act on it, designed by selection or learning to work together.** Ruth Millikan, *Language, Thought, and Other Biological Categories* (1984); "Biosemantics" (1989). Her [teleosemantics](#teleosemantics) in two theses:

1. **B1.** A representation sits between a producer that makes it and consumers that act on it, designed to work together. (A signal is a message inside a system.)
2. **B2.** Its content is the condition that must hold for the consumers to perform their proper functions in the Normal way, the way that historically explained their selection. (A state means what the world must be like for its users to succeed as they were selected to.)

- **Her stock case is the frog** (4.2's exposition). The visual system produces, the prey-catching system consumes, and the content is *frog food here now*, since food is what ancestral snapping succeeded by.
- **"Normal",** capitalized, means the way that historically explained the consumers' selection.
