- **Which side fixes content.** Consumer side: Millikan; David Papineau ("Representation and Explanation", 1984; *Philosophical Naturalism*, 1993) starts from desires, whose content is the effect they were selected to produce, and derives belief content from them. Producer (input) side: Karen Neander (*A Mark of the Mental*, 2017) keeps selection and makes content what the producer was selected to respond to. Giving Millikan a producer-side content is giving her Neander's view.
- **Not the claim that evolution favours true beliefs.** It is a theory of what fixes content; [`apologetics-foundations` 3.2](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-02-reason.md) uses it against Plantinga and draws the link to truth there.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Functional indeterminacy

**The charge that selection cannot tell apart properties that were coextensive where the selection happened, so it cannot fix a single content.** Jerry Fodor (1990), against [teleosemantics](#teleosemantics). 4.2's illustration (the SEP's, not Fodor's own example): if rabbits that freeze at dogs survived, so did rabbits that freeze at dog-lookalikes, so one history fits both descriptions.

- **The reply** (Millikan, 1991; Neander, 1995): dogs, not lookalikes, eat rabbits, so only one description explains the success.
- **The critic's rejoinder** is the smuggling objection ([naturalizing content](#naturalizing-content)): "selected for detecting dogs" wins only because we know what the rabbit is after. The teleosemanticist answers that selection-for is causal.
- **Pressed to the limit in 5.2:** Ross argues that no physical fact fixes some contents at all ([determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument)).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Swampman

**A molecule-for-molecule duplicate of a person, formed by chance and with no history: does he have thoughts?** Donald Davidson, "Knowing One's Own Mind" (1987): lightning in a swamp reduces Davidson to his elements and turns a nearby dead tree into his exact physical replica, which goes on as he would have. Davidson denied that the replica has thoughts or that its words mean anything, since it lacks the causal history meaning requires.

- **Verdicts by theory** (4.2 Example 2). [Teleosemantics](#teleosemantics): his parts have no selection history, so no proper functions, so no content; his "there's my friend" is neither true nor false. Simple [indication](#informational-semantics): a present relation, so his states indicate what Davidson's did from the first instant, but that version cannot make him wrong. Dretske's indicator-function theory: his beliefs arrive as he learns. [Asymmetric dependence](#asymmetric-dependence): content, and mistakes, from the start.
- **The case decides nothing until you say what a theory of content is a theory of.** Millikan ("On Swampkinds", 1996) and Neander (1996): a real-nature theory; content is a historical kind, like being a species, and the intuition that Swampman believes is like the old conviction that water could fail to be H2O, natural and false. Papineau (2001; 2016): the intuition may be true but is irrelevant, since teleosemantics is a claim about actual believers. The critic: an account of what content is must cover possible believers, and a conscious Swampman has at least the content of how things look to him ([phenomenal intentionality](#phenomenal-intentionality)). Nothing in the case settles whether the intuition is evidence.
- **4.3's addition:** Swampman is a historical twin case. If Horgan and Tienson's H1 and H2 hold, Swampman, your intrinsic duplicate, has whatever phenomenal contents you have, with no history to supply them.
- **Not a case against physicalism.** He is a physical duplicate; the case divides historical from synchronic physicalist theories of content.
- **Elsewhere:** [`epistemology` 2.4](../epistemology/lessons/02-04-reliabilism.md) sets him against proper functionalism about warrant. A brand-new mutation, with a Cummins function and no selected one ([`philosophy-of-science` 4.2](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-02-causal-explanation.md)), is Swampman's problem in a single organ.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Inferential-role semantics

**A symbol's content is its role in reasoning: its inferential relations to other states (and, on some versions, to perception and action).** Also called conceptual role semantics. An inner "&" means *and* because believing P&Q leads to believing P and believing Q, and believing both leads back. Gilbert Harman ("(Nonsolipsistic) Conceptual Role Semantics", 1987) lets the roles reach out to perception and action. Ned Block ("Advertisement for a Semantics for Psychology", 1986) discusses two-factor theories, which pair an inner role with a referential factor that a tracking theory must supply.

- **The objections:** holism, since if every inference counts, any change of belief changes every meaning (Fodor and Lepore, *Holism: A Shopper's Guide*, 1992); and roles alone fix no truth conditions. It fits *and* better than *horse*.
- **In 4.3** it is a candidate for [narrow content](#narrow-and-wide-content) (Block, 1986).
- **In 6.3,** on a language model: a model that reliably draws the inferences a word licenses has the word's role, but roles fix no truth conditions, so a two-factor theorist still needs a referential factor. Tracking and role come apart most sharply there: a model can be rich in role and poor in reference.
- **Inferentialism about linguistic meaning** is [`philosophy-of-language-and-logic`](../philosophy-of-language-and-logic/syllabus.md) 4.2's.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Twin Earth

**Putnam's planet exactly like Earth except that its watery stuff is a look-alike, XYZ, not H2O: are you and your molecule-for-molecule twin there thinking the same thing?** Hilary Putnam, "The Meaning of 'Meaning'" (1975), drew the case for word meaning. Colin McGinn (1977) and Tyler Burge (1979) carried it to thought, and Burge, unlike Putnam, endorsed the extension outright. 4.3 runs it on belief, in outline:

1. **P1.** In 1750, before chemistry could tell the liquids apart, Oscar on Earth and Twin Oscar on Twin Earth are intrinsic duplicates; the watery stuff is H2O on Earth and XYZ on Twin Earth.
2. **P2.** "Water" refers to whatever shares the hidden nature of the samples the speaker's community calls water, so Oscar's word picks out H2O and Twin Oscar's picks out XYZ.
3. **P3.** The that-clause that reports a belief gives its content, and beliefs with different truth conditions are different beliefs.
4. **C.** Oscar believes that water is wet and Twin Oscar does not, so a person's intrinsic states do not fix what she believes ([content externalism](#content-externalism)).

- **Where it is weakest:** P3, the step from words to thoughts, which Putnam himself did not endorse. The strongest objection is Fodor's: content explains behaviour, causal powers are intrinsic, and the twins act alike, so psychology needs a shared content ([narrow and wide content](#narrow-and-wide-content)). The strongest reply is Burge's (1986, 1989): working psychology, such as Marr's theory of vision, individuates states by their normal environment.
- **Concessions.** The externalist: something shared explains why the twins act alike, and an account is owed of why it is not content. The internalist: the shared item fixes no truth conditions until a context is added (Stalnaker; Block).
- **The conclusion is about individuation, not location.** A burn is a sunburn only if the sun caused it, yet it is located on the skin (Davidson, 1987).
- **Owned elsewhere.** Twin Earth for kind terms, the division of linguistic labour and two-dimensional semantics are [`philosophy-of-language-and-logic`](../philosophy-of-language-and-logic/syllabus.md) 3.3 (unbuilt); the necessity of water's being H2O is [`metaphysics` 3.1](../metaphysics/lessons/03-01-kinds-of-necessity.md). Horgan and Timmons's moral twin earth ([`ethics` 6.1](../ethics/lessons/06-01-moral-realism.md)) runs the same test on moral terms.
- **The 2020 PhilPapers survey** (target group, N = 1,514): 58 percent accepted or leaned towards content externalism, 26 percent towards internalism.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Arthritis case

**Burge's case that makes the environment social: a patient's false belief that he has arthritis in his thigh is still an arthritis-belief, because he defers to his doctor.** Tyler Burge, "Individualism and the Mental" (1979). Three steps:

1. **Actual.** A patient with many true beliefs about arthritis tells his doctor he has it in his thigh. Arthritis is an inflammation of the joints, so the belief is false, but it is an arthritis-belief, because he would accept the doctor's correction.
2. **Counterfactual.** Same intrinsic history, but in a community whose doctors apply "arthritis" to rheumatoid ailments generally, thighs included.
3. **Verdict.** There his belief is true and not about arthritis. Only the community changed, so content depends on the community.

- **Deference is the hinge.** 4.3 Example 1 reruns the pattern with "sciatica" (pain along the sciatic nerve, from the lower back down the leg).
- **Its reach.** Burge ran the pattern on sofas and contracts too, so his *anti-individualism* reaches any concept a thinker can understand incompletely, not just natural-kind concepts.
- **The extreme internalist's reply** (Tim Crane, 1991; Gabriel Segal, 2000): the patient never had the experts' concept, only one that covers thighs. The cost: the ordinary report of his belief comes out false, and readiness to defer becomes readiness to change concepts.
- **In 6.3:** on content externalism your word "arthritis" refers through your community even when you misapply it. Mandelkern and Linzen (2023; 2024) argue that a language model's words can inherit reference the same way, since the text it learns from has a natural history.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Narrow and wide content

**Wide (broad) content depends partly on the environment; narrow content is whatever content any intrinsic duplicate must share.** An *intrinsic duplicate* is identical in every property that does not depend on its surroundings. After [Twin Earth](#twin-earth) and the [arthritis case](#arthritis-case) there are three internalist-leaning answers:

- **Extreme internalism** (Tim Crane, 1991; Gabriel Segal, *A Slim Book about Narrow Content*, 2000) denies the externalist verdicts: ordinary content is already narrow.
- **Moderates** grant wide content and add a narrow content duplicates share. Jerry Fodor (*Psychosemantics*, 1987) made narrow content a function from contexts to wide contents, yielding "water is wet" on Earth and "XYZ is wet" on Twin Earth. Robert Stalnaker (1989) and Ned Block (1991) objected that a function with no truth conditions of its own is not content at all. Rival candidates: [inferential role](#inferential-role-semantics) (4.2) and [two-dimensional](#two-dimensional-semantics) content (1.4).
- **[Phenomenal intentionality](#phenomenal-intentionality)** is the internalist's newest resource: the content fixed by phenomenal character alone is narrow.
- **Fodor's argument for narrow content:** content explains behaviour, causal powers are intrinsic, and the twins act alike, so psychology needs a shared content. Burge replies that working psychology (Marr's theory of vision) individuates states by their normal environment.
- **Content internalism is not justification internalism.** [`epistemology` 1.4](../epistemology/lessons/01-04-internalism-and-externalism.md)'s internalism is about what justifies a belief; a reliabilist about justification can be an internalist about content.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Slow switching

**Someone moved unawares to Twin Earth thinks about water at first but, after years among XYZ, thinks about XYZ, with no change she could notice.** Introduced by Tyler Burge, "Individualism and Self-Knowledge" (1988); turned against self-knowledge by Paul Boghossian, "Content and Self-Knowledge" (1989).

- **The switch is gradual,** a matter of years among XYZ, not of the move itself.
- **What switching removes,** on Burge's account, is only the ability to tell a water content from an XYZ content.
- **Its uses:** [Boghossian's memory argument](#boghossians-memory-argument) against privileged self-knowledge, and Burge's reply by [self-verifying judgments](#self-verifying-judgments).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Boghossian's memory argument

**Slow switching is used to argue that an externalist lacks privileged knowledge of what her own thoughts are about, even before any switch.** Paul Boghossian, "Content and Self-Knowledge" (1989). The skeleton, with $t_1$ the time of a thought on Earth and $t_2$ a time after the [slow switch](#slow-switching) when it is recalled:

1. **P1.** If she forgets nothing between $t_1$ and $t_2$, then what she knew at $t_1$ she knows at $t_2$.
2. **P2.** She forgets nothing.
3. **P3.** At $t_2$ she does not know that at $t_1$ she thought a *water* thought, rather than an XYZ thought.
4. **C.** So at $t_1$ she did not know what she thought.

- **Replies, by premise.** Deny P3: Burge (1998), preservative memory keeps the content a thought had when it was formed. Deny P1: Anthony Brueckner (1997), knowledge can be lost without forgetting, for instance when a defeater arrives; a relevant-alternatives theorist can take this route. Deny P2: the shift in memory is itself a kind of forgetting.
- **Burge's other resource:** [self-verifying judgments](#self-verifying-judgments).
- **Neighbouring argument:** McKinsey-style arguments, that externalism plus privileged access would give armchair knowledge of the environment; Wright's transmission failure is his diagnosis of them ([`epistemology` 3.3](../epistemology/lessons/03-03-moore-and-the-dogmatist.md)).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Self-verifying judgments

**Judgments like "I am thinking, with this very thought, that water is wet", which come out true whatever the content of the first-order thought turns out to be.** Tyler Burge (1988). The judgment inherits the content of the thought it is about, so basic self-knowledge of one's occurrent thoughts survives [slow switching](#slow-switching).

- **What switching removes** is only the ability to tell two contents apart.
- **Burge offers it as the reply** to the self-knowledge worry that [Boghossian's memory argument](#boghossians-memory-argument) presses.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Phenomenal intentionality

**Intentionality constituted by phenomenal consciousness: on the phenomenal intentionality theory (PIT), how a state feels fixes, by itself, much of what it is about.** Terence Horgan and John Tienson (2002); Brian Loar (2003); Uriah Kriegel (2011, 2013). Their argument for narrow content:

1. **H1.** There is pervasive intentional content that depends constitutively on phenomenal character alone.
2. **H2.** Phenomenal character depends only on narrow factors. (Intrinsic duplicates feel alike.)
3. **H3.** So there is pervasive [narrow content](#narrow-and-wide-content). (Duplicates share much of what they think.)

- **The support:** a brain-in-a-vat duplicate (Horgan, Tienson and George Graham, 2004) seems to share your experiences and so to believe, falsely, that it lies on a beach; a false belief needs a content. The vat is [`epistemology` 3.1](../epistemology/lessons/03-01-the-closure-argument.md)'s skeptical case.
- **Two views of wide content within PIT.** *Derivativists* (Loar; Horgan and Tienson) grant wide content and build it from narrow content plus the environment. *Eliminativists* (Kriegel, 2007; Katalin Farkas, 2008) give the twins one content and let context do the rest. Only eliminativists deny that Oscar's thought is about H2O.
- **Who denies which premise.** Phenomenal externalists (Fred Dretske, 1996; William Lycan, 1996) deny H2: phenomenal externalism is the intentionalist's version of denying it ([intentionalism](#intentionalism), 4.5).
- **On [Swampman](#swampman)** (4.2): the critic says a conscious Swampman has at least the content of how things look to him; if H1 and H2 hold, he has whatever phenomenal contents you have, with no history to supply them.
- **Against [naturalizing content](#naturalizing-content):** PIT theorists expect the naturalistic theories to fail somewhere.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Extended mind

**Some vehicles of mental states lie partly outside the body: a notebook can hold part of a belief.** Andy Clark and David Chalmers, "The Extended Mind" (1998). A *vehicle* is what carries a content, such as a brain state or a notebook entry. Inga recalls that the Museum of Modern Art is on 53rd Street and walks there; Otto, who has Alzheimer's disease, looks it up in his notebook and walks there. In outline:

1. **E1.** Before recalling it, Inga already believed the museum was on 53rd Street, as a standing belief in memory.
2. **E2.** Otto's entry plays the same role for Otto, provided the notebook is a constant in his life, its contents are directly available, he endorses what he reads automatically, and he consciously endorsed it when he wrote it.
3. **E3.** The [parity principle](#parity-principle).
4. **C2.** Otto believed the museum was on 53rd Street before he looked, and the notebook is part of the belief's vehicle.

- **The four conditions:** constant, directly available, automatically endorsed, consciously endorsed when entered. Clark and Chalmers call the fourth arguable. Drop it and the mind bloats to whatever one trusts; keep it and belief becomes partly historical, as their own footnote notes.
- **Their name for it** is [active externalism](#active-and-passive-externalism). Also called vehicle externalism.
- **The objections:** the [coupling-constitution objection](#coupling-constitution-objection), and that the notebook's marks have only [derived intentionality](#original-and-derived-intentionality).
- **Not a stronger content externalism.** The two questions cross: content externalism says what fixes a belief's content, the extended mind where its vehicle is, and a view's answer to one fixes nothing about its answer to the other. Davidson's analogy: content externalism makes beliefs like sunburns (individuated from outside, located on the skin); the extended mind makes some beliefs like a long division done half on paper.
- **Neighbours:** [embodied cognition](#embodied-cognition); Heidegger's equipment and Dreyfus's critique of symbolic AI ([`phenomenology-and-personalism` 2.2](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/02-02-heidegger-dasein-and-being-in-the-world.md)) are its continental cousin.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Parity principle

**If a part of the world functions in a task as a process we would count as cognitive were it done in the head, it is part of the cognitive process.** Premise E3 of Clark and Chalmers's [extended-mind](#extended-mind) argument (1998). The idea is theirs; the name is later usage, not a label in the 1998 paper. In words: location alone does not disqualify.

- **What it leaves open** is which differences between inner and outer processes matter: the defender counts only functional role, while the critic counts, for instance, that the user must *see* the screen, whose marks mean anything only because he wrote them.
- **Its opponents:** the [coupling-constitution objection](#coupling-constitution-objection) and [derived intentionality](#original-and-derived-intentionality).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Active and passive externalism

**Clark and Chalmers's contrast: their external factors are present, coupled and drive behaviour now (active); Putnam's and Burge's are distal and idle in what the twins now do (passive).** Clark and Chalmers (1998). Putnam's and Burge's twins act alike. Clark and Chalmers's own twin Otto, identical from the skin in but with a notebook saying 51st Street, walks somewhere else.

- **Active externalism is the [extended mind](#extended-mind)** (a thesis about vehicles); passive externalism is [content externalism](#content-externalism) (a thesis about what fixes content).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Coupling-constitution objection

**Being causally coupled to a cognitive process does not make a thing part of it.** Fred Adams and Ken Aizawa ("The Bounds of Cognition", 2001; *The Bounds of Cognition*, 2008) and Robert Rupert (2004), against the [extended mind](#extended-mind). On their view the notebook is *embedded*, not extended. Its marks, they add, have only [derived intentionality](#original-and-derived-intentionality): they mean what they do because users write and read them that way.

- **Where the dispute stops** (4.3 Example 2): the defender counts only functional role ([parity](#parity-principle)); the critic counts differences such as having to *see* the page, whose marks mean anything only because the user wrote them.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Original and derived intentionality

**Original intentionality is aboutness a state has of itself; derived intentionality is aboutness borrowed from a mind that uses or interprets the thing.** The fuel gauge means "low" because drivers read it that way. The distinction is the modern repair of B2 of [Brentano's thesis](#brentanos-thesis) (John Searle, John Haugeland, Fodor). Dennett denies that the line is sharp.

- **Against the extended mind** (4.3): the notebook's marks have only derived intentionality, so they are not a belief ([coupling-constitution objection](#coupling-constitution-objection)).
- **In the Chinese room** (6.2): Searle's line between the original intentionality of minds and the derived intentionality of symbols. Dennett held all intentionality derived, so for him the line marks no real difference.
- **In 6.3:** the [symbol-grounding problem](#symbol-grounding-problem) (Harnad, 1990) is the question asked of a whole system: how can its interpretation be intrinsic to it rather than parasitic on the meanings in our heads? 6.3 also asks whether a language model's words have content of their own or only derived content.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Embodied cognition

**A research programme on which the body's make-up and its traffic with the world constitute or contribute to cognition.** Named in 4.3 beside the [extended mind](#extended-mind) (SEP, "Embodied Cognition", 2021).

- **It says where cognitive processes run,** not what a body is. 5.1: the hylomorphist's body is not the embodied-cognition theorist's body; the hylomorphist says what a body is (matter that is this body only as informed), and the Thomist adds a power with no organ ([soul as form](#soul-as-form)).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Illusion and hallucination

**Illusion: a real object is perceived but looks other than it is. Hallucination: nothing is perceived, yet it seems as if something were.** Definitions as in Tim Crane and Craig French (SEP, "The Problem of Perception", rev. 2021). A white wall that looks yellow under a sodium lamp is an illusion. The philosophers' hallucination is a perfect one: indistinguishable by introspection from a veridical perception (premise H1 of the [argument from hallucination](#argument-from-hallucination)).

- **The test is whether an ordinary object is perceived,** not whether what it looks like exists. An object seen wrongly is an illusion: the mannequin taken for a woman in [`phenomenology-and-personalism` 1.2](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/01-02-the-anatomy-of-an-experience.md) (something was there, seen wrongly), or the grey square that looks darker on a white surround (4.4 Example 1).
- **Perceptual variation is neither.** An object looks different from different standpoints and nobody is fooled: Russell's table, which has no one colour "in itself" (*The Problems of Philosophy*, 1912, ch. I, 4.4's Source).
- **Each error drives an argument with two moves.** A *base case* shows that in the bad case you are not presented with the ordinary object; a *spreading step* gives the good case the same account, since from the inside the two can be alike. Illusion drives the [argument from illusion](#argument-from-illusion), hallucination the argument from hallucination.
- **Where the definitions are silent:** a dead star seen by old light ([time-lag argument](#time-lag-argument)). Both definitions assume the object exists when seen.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)

### Naive realism

**In seeing, ordinary objects are themselves directly presented to you, and how they look is fixed partly by how they are.** 4.4's default, the ordinary conception of perception: a mug you see seems simply there, its blue and its shape given to you. 4.5's contemporary form, also called the [relational view](#relational-view) (M. G. F. Martin, "The Transparency of Experience", 2002, and later papers; John Campbell, *Reference and Consciousness*, 2002; Bill Brewer, *Perception and its Objects*, 2011): in veridical perception the objects and their features are constituents of the experience and fix its character. Campbell makes the relation three-place: subject, standpoint, object.

- **4.5's analogy:** a handshake needs two hands. The same movement of one arm into empty air is not a worse handshake but a different event.
- **Two arguments for it.** Campbell's: experience is what lets you know which thing "that" refers to, and a representation cannot do this, since conceptual content presupposes the grasp it was meant to explain and non-conceptual content lacks the resources. Martin's, from introspection: experience seems to set the world itself before you, so naive realism lets the good cases be as they seem, while its rivals make every experience misleading about its own nature. Its starting observation is the [transparency of experience](#transparency-of-experience) (3.2).
- **With [disjunctivism](#disjunctivism).** The naive realist grants that hallucinations present nothing and rejects the [common-kind assumption](#common-kind-assumption): naive realist disjunctivism. Its bad case, for Martin, has a character consisting only in its indiscriminability from the good case ([negative epistemic conception](#negative-epistemic-conception)); for McDowell it is a mere appearance.
- **It does not say things always look as they are.** It is a thesis about the structure of experience, with its own accounts of illusion. Brewer: the object itself is presented, and how it looks from here, in these conditions, is a real relational feature of it (4.4 Example 1); in 4.5's terms, it has visually relevant similarities with paradigm cases and so objectively looks that way.
- **Its rivals' charge and its reply:** see [screening-off argument](#screening-off-argument) and [causal argument against disjunctivism](#causal-argument-against-disjunctivism). Making the object a constituent is externalism about experience itself, a cousin of [content externalism](#content-externalism).
- **Reid is a direct realist, not a naive realist** ([`modern-philosophy` 4.5](../modern-philosophy/lessons/04-05-reid-common-sense-against-the-way-of-ideas.md)); his reply to an argument from hallucination is now glossed as disjunctivist. In Module 5 the hylomorphist joins the direct realists: Aquinas held that the likeness is that *by which* sight sees (ST I q.85 a.2, read in [`thomistic-synthesis` 4.1](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/04-01-how-a-human-intellect-knows.md)).
- **Husserl** holds that we see the thing itself, one-sidedly ([`phenomenology-and-personalism` 1.2](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/01-02-the-anatomy-of-an-experience.md)).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Relational view

**Another name for [naive realism](#naive-realism) in its contemporary form: perceptual experience as a relation whose relata include the perceived object itself (for Campbell, three-place: subject, standpoint, object).** See [the entry](#naive-realism).

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Sense-datum theory

**In every experience you are directly aware of a non-ordinary item, a sense-datum, that really has the qualities on show; ordinary objects are perceived, if at all, indirectly through it.** It accepts the conclusion of the arguments from illusion and hallucination and adds that something is always presented. G. E. Moore introduced the term in 1909 ("The Subject-Matter of Psychology"), and Russell's *The Problems of Philosophy* (1912) made it standard.

- **Not mental by definition.** Moore's term was neutral, and Russell said his colour arguments did not prove the colour mental (*Problems*, ch. IV; see [`modern-philosophy` 3.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/03-04-berkeley-immaterialism.md)). Today's defenders, Howard Robinson (*Perception*, 1994) and John Foster (*The Nature of Perception*, 2000), take sense-data to be mind-dependent and accept the cost to naturalism.
- **It accepts the [Phenomenal Principle](#phenomenal-principle):** where something looks darker, a darker sense-datum is present.
- **Not a name for Locke's ideas** ([`modern-philosophy` 3.2](../modern-philosophy/lessons/03-02-locke-qualities-substance-and-essence.md)).
- **Further support:** the [time-lag argument](#time-lag-argument), pressed for sense-data by Robinson (1994, ch. 3).
- **The 2020 PhilPapers survey** (perceptual experience, N = 1,323): about 5 percent accept or lean towards it.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)

### Phenomenal Principle

**If something sensibly appears F to you, then there is something F of which you are aware.** Premise P2 of the [argument from illusion](#argument-from-illusion) in Crane and French's form, after Howard Robinson (*Perception*, 1994): for a subject S and a sensible quality F, if it sensibly seems to S that something is F, then S is directly presented with something that is F.

- **Who accepts it:** [sense-datum theory](#sense-datum-theory). **Who rejects it:** [adverbialism](#adverbialism), [intentionalism](#intentionalism) and [naive realism](#naive-realism). On a grey square that looks darker the theories part here: a darker sense-datum; sensing darker-greyly; the square represented as darker; the square itself, whose look in this surround is a real relational feature (Brewer).
- **Rejecting it blocks only the argument from illusion.** The [argument from hallucination](#argument-from-hallucination) never uses it: its base case follows from what a hallucination is. Adverbialists and intentionalists still accept that argument's conclusion, and keep direct *perception* only by adding a causal condition (H. P. Grice, 1961).
- **The objection** (W. H. F. Barnes, 1944-45; G. E. M. Anscombe, 1965): it is a fallacy. "Looks yellow" no more entails a yellow thing than "looking for a unicorn" (4.4's gloss) entails a unicorn.
- **The reply:** it is a claim about experience, not logic, backed by explanation. C. D. Broad (*Scientific Thought*, 1923) asked why a penny looks elliptical rather than any other shape if nothing elliptical is before the mind.
- **Concessions.** The defender: it is a substantive thesis, deniable without contradiction. The objector: she owes another explanation of the specific look (a representation, a way of sensing, or the object's own look from here).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Argument from illusion

**In an illusion something looks F that the perceived object is not; if whatever looks F is an F thing you are aware of, that thing is not the object; give every experience the same account, and you never directly see ordinary objects.** In Tim Crane and Craig French's form (SEP, rev. 2021, after Howard Robinson, *Perception*, 1994), for a subject S:

1. **P1.** In an illusion it seems to S that something has a sensible quality F that the perceived object lacks.
2. **P2.** The [Phenomenal Principle](#phenomenal-principle): if it sensibly seems to S that something is F, then S is directly presented with something that is F.
3. **P3.** So in an illusion S is not directly presented with the perceived object (the F thing is not the object, by Leibniz's law). *Base case.*
4. **P4.** The same account of experience applies to veridical and illusory experiences. *Spreading step.*
5. **P5.** If S is not directly presented with ordinary objects in veridical experience, S never is.
6. **C.** We are never directly presented with ordinary objects.

- **A gap at P3** (Craig French and Lee Walters, 2018): P1, P2 and Leibniz's law yield only an F thing *distinct from* the object, so P3 also needs their *Exclusion Assumption*: being presented with that thing excludes being presented with the object.
- **Support for P4:** continuous series (A. J. Ayer, 1940). Slide the surround of a grey square smoothly from white to black and its look changes by degrees, with no point where one kind of experience gives way to another.
- **Where it is weakest:** P2 (see the [Phenomenal Principle](#phenomenal-principle) for objection, reply and concessions). Pressure then moves to the [argument from hallucination](#argument-from-hallucination), which does not use P2.
- **Cases.** The textbook stick in water is used in [`modern-philosophy` 4.5](../modern-philosophy/lessons/04-05-reid-common-sense-against-the-way-of-ideas.md); 4.4 runs simultaneous contrast, two identical greys on black and white surrounds, whose mechanism is [`neuroscience` 3.2](../neuroscience/lessons/03-02-vision.md).
- **Intentionalism** rejects P2, so on it the argument fails (4.5).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Argument from hallucination

**A perfect hallucination presents no ordinary object; a perception you cannot tell from it is the same kind of event; so perception presents no ordinary object either.** 4.4's skeleton:

1. **H1.** For any veridical experience of an F, a hallucination indistinguishable from it by introspection is possible.
2. **H2.** In such a hallucination, S is directly presented with no ordinary object. *Base case:* it follows from what a hallucination is, so no Phenomenal Principle is needed.
3. **H3.** The [common-kind assumption](#common-kind-assumption): a veridical experience and its indistinguishable hallucination are fundamentally the same kind of experience, differing only in their causes. *Spreading step.*
4. **H4.** So veridical experience is not, by its nature, the direct presentation of an ordinary object.
5. With P5 of the [argument from illusion](#argument-from-illusion) (if not in veridical experience, never): **C.** We are never directly presented with ordinary objects.

- **Who accepts the conclusion.** Sense-datum theory, adverbialism and intentionalism deny no premise and differ over what experience is instead. Naive realism with [disjunctivism](#disjunctivism) denies H3. Since H2 needs no Phenomenal Principle, only denying H1 or the spreading step stops the argument.
- **Intentionalism accepts it** and so gives up direct *presentation*, while keeping direct perception in a causal sense ([intentionalism](#intentionalism)).
- **H1's ancestor** is the Academic claim that any true impression has an indistinguishable false twin, aimed at the Stoics' knowledge rather than at presentation ([`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 4.3](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/04-03-the-skeptics.md)). Reid's case against the ideal system, whose P3 already states the common-kind argument for a fever, is [`modern-philosophy` 4.5](../modern-philosophy/lessons/04-05-reid-common-sense-against-the-way-of-ideas.md).
- **4.1's point that a state can be about what does not exist** is what lets intentionalism give a hallucination content.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Common-kind assumption

**A veridical perception and a hallucination you could not tell from it are fundamentally the same kind of experience, differing only in their causes.** Premise H3, the spreading step, of the [argument from hallucination](#argument-from-hallucination): one kind of event occurs in you either way.

- **Accepted** by [sense-datum theory](#sense-datum-theory), [adverbialism](#adverbialism) and [intentionalism](#intentionalism), which differ over what the shared event is: awareness of a sense-datum, a manner of sensing, or a content. **Denied** by [disjunctivism](#disjunctivism).
- **Intentionalists accept it.** What they reject is the [Phenomenal Principle](#phenomenal-principle).
- **Its defences against disjunctivism:** Robinson's [causal argument](#causal-argument-against-disjunctivism), and Martin's [screening-off argument](#screening-off-argument), which runs in reverse: whatever kind occurs in the matched hallucination occurs in the perception too.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Time-lag argument

**Light and every other causal signal take time to arrive, so what you directly perceive now cannot be the object as it now is, which may no longer exist.** A star 2,000 light-years away is seen by light 2,000 years old, and may have died since; C. D. Broad raised the case (*The Mind and Its Place in Nature*, 1925, ch. IV). Russell already said it of the sun (*Problems*, 1912, ch. III): "if the physical sun had ceased to exist within the last eight minutes, that would make no difference to the sense-data". Pressed for sense-data by Robinson (1994, ch. 3):

1. **T1.** What you see now depends on light that left the star long ago, and the star may be gone.
2. **T2.** What is directly presented exists, as presented, when the experience occurs.
3. **T3.** So the star is not what you are directly presented with.
4. **T4.** Every perception takes time: about 8.3 minutes for sunlight, under 2 nanoseconds for a mug half a metre away, plus the brain's delay.
5. **C.** Nothing you directly perceive is an ordinary object as it now is.

- **Replies.** The naive realist denies T2: seeing reaches into the past, as hearing thunder after the lightning does (Russell's own example), and the star's having sent that light makes it a seeing. The intentionalist: the experience misrepresents when.
- **The sense-datum theorist's rejoinder,** with Russell: an experience that would be the same whether or not the star survived cannot have the star in its nature. That is the thought behind 4.5's [screening-off argument](#screening-off-argument).
- **Concessions.** One side gives up "what you see is there now"; the other grants that a lag proves nothing without that causal premise.
- **Left open:** whether seeing a dead star is veridical, illusory or hallucinatory ([illusion and hallucination](#illusion-and-hallucination)).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)

### Adverbialism

**Experiencing yellow is a way of sensing (sensing yellowly), not a relation to a yellow object.** Adverbialism about experience: C. J. Ducasse (1942); Roderick Chisholm, *Perceiving* (1957). When the wall looks yellow nothing yellow is present.

- **It rejects the [Phenomenal Principle](#phenomenal-principle),** so it blocks the [argument from illusion](#argument-from-illusion). It accepts the [common-kind assumption](#common-kind-assumption), so it accepts the conclusion of the [argument from hallucination](#argument-from-hallucination), which never used the principle.
- **Main objection:** the [many-property problem](#many-property-problem).
- **Not the "adverbialism" of [`metaphysics` 5.4](../metaphysics/lessons/05-04-endurance-perdurance-and-stages.md),** which is about temporary intrinsics.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)

### Many-property problem

**The adverbial description of seeing a brown square beside a green triangle fits seeing a green square beside a brown triangle equally well, so adverbialism cannot say which quality goes with which.** Frank Jackson (1975; *Perception*, 1977, ch. 3), then a sense-datum theorist, and the Jackson of the [knowledge argument](#knowledge-argument) (1.3). The adverbial description is *sensing brownly, squarely, greenly and triangularly*.

- **What binds** brown to square seems to be an object having both, or a field with places.
- **Adverbialists** (Michael Tye, 1984, among them) try to supply the binding without objects.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)

### Intentionalism

**Perceptual experience represents the world as being some way: it has accuracy conditions, and a matching hallucination shares the perception's content and is usually just inaccurate.** Also called representationalism about perceptual experience. Gilbert Harman (1990); Michael Tye (*Ten Problems of Consciousness*, 1995; *Consciousness, Color, and Content*, 2000); Fred Dretske (*Naturalizing the Mind*, 1995); Alex Byrne ("Intentionalism Defended", 2001). 4.5's analogy: a radar blip at bearing 040 says there is an aircraft there and can say so falsely; what a true blip and a false one share is not an aircraft but a content.

1. **I1.** Every perceptual experience represents the world as being some way, and it is accurate just if the world is that way.
2. **I2.** A veridical experience, an illusion and a matching hallucination can share their content. (The common kind is a content, and hallucination is usually misrepresentation, which needs no object.)
3. **I3** (strong intentionalism). No two experiences differ in what they are like without differing in content (Byrne's form); Tye identifies the character with the properties represented.

- **Where it stands on 4.4's arguments.** It rejects the [Phenomenal Principle](#phenomenal-principle), so the [argument from illusion](#argument-from-illusion) fails. It accepts the [common-kind assumption](#common-kind-assumption), and with it the conclusion of the [argument from hallucination](#argument-from-hallucination): experience never directly *presents* an object. It keeps direct realism in a causal sense: you see the post box directly when your experience represents it and is caused by it in the right way.
- **Strong and weak.** Intentionalists who also accept non-representational [qualia](#qualia) (Christopher Peacocke, 1983; Ned Block) reject I3; the 2020 survey's "qualia theory", at 15 percent, is roughly theirs.
- **Not a belief theory.** The Müller-Lyer lines look unequal while you believe them equal. D. M. Armstrong's belief theory (1968) retreated to "potential belief"; most intentionalists make experience its own kind of representation.
- **Not indirect realism.** It posits no inner object and rejects the Phenomenal Principle. What it gives up is direct presentation, not direct perception.
- **On illusion** (4.5 Example 2) it is untroubled: two identical greys, the one on black looks lighter, so the experience represents that patch as lighter than it is, and its content is inaccurate. Against Brewer it says "looks lighter" is content under another name: something must say the patch is lighter, or nothing is wrong.
- **Relatives.** [First-order representationalism](#first-order-representationalism) (3.2) is the same idea as a theory of what experience is like; here it is a theory of what perceiving is. Phenomenal externalism (Dretske, Lycan) is the intentionalist's version of denying H2 of the [phenomenal-intentionality](#phenomenal-intentionality) argument (4.3). Its accuracy conditions are what the naturalistic theories of 4.2 try to supply.
- **The 2020 PhilPapers survey** (perceptual experience, N = 1,323): 39 percent accept or lean towards representationalism.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Accuracy conditions

**The way the world must be for an experience to be accurate: what the experience says.** [Intentionalism](#intentionalism)'s notion of content: a way the world would have to be for the experience, like a radar screen, to be right.

- **The same notion as [`epistemology` 2.2](../epistemology/lessons/02-02-foundationalism.md)'s "content":** what can be accurate or inaccurate. Whether it must be conceptual is left open in both courses.
- **Error on intentionalism.** An illusion is an experience with inaccurate content about a real object; a hallucination is one whose conditions usually fail.
- **"Accuracy" is not "probe accuracy".** A deep-learning representation is judged by what a probe can read from it, not by accuracy conditions (4.1; [connectionism](#connectionism)).

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Disjunctivism

**Seeing and a hallucination you could not tell from it are not the same fundamental kind of event; the most that is true of both is a disjunction: either I see an F, or it merely seems to me as if I do.** J. M. Hinton ("Visual Experiences", 1967; *Experiences*, 1973); Paul Snowdon ("Perception, Vision and Causation", 1980-81); John McDowell ("Criteria, Defeasibility, and Knowledge", 1982); M. G. F. Martin. It is the negative thesis that rejects the [common-kind assumption](#common-kind-assumption), held for different reasons: Martin's are phenomenological ([naive realism](#naive-realism)), McDowell's epistemological ([epistemological disjunctivism](#epistemological-disjunctivism)).

- **Not "nothing in common."** Both cases are, trivially, indiscriminable from seeing; disjunctivism denies that a shared kind explains their likeness. Hilary Putnam's 1999 gloss overstates it.
- **Not the same thesis as naive realism.** Tye (2007) is a disjunctivist about content, the good case's content involving the object and the bad case's having a gap, while keeping a common character.
- **What it makes of the hallucinator** (4.5 Example 1). On the common-kind views she is in your very state, minus the world's co-operation; on disjunctivism she is not, though she cannot tell. For Martin the bad case's character is exhausted by its indiscriminability from the good case ([negative epistemic conception](#negative-epistemic-conception)); for McDowell it is a mere appearance.
- **On illusion it gives no verdict by itself,** since rejecting the common kind was a claim about hallucination. Its defenders split: for Snowdon the good disjunct is that something looks some way, so an illusion falls with perception; McDowell's "mere appearance" seems to group illusion with hallucination; Martin treats an illusory aspect as a partial hallucination; Brewer keeps illusion wholly perceptual (the object has visually relevant similarities with paradigm cases, and objectively looks that way).
- **The arguments against it:** the [causal argument](#causal-argument-against-disjunctivism) and the [screening-off argument](#screening-off-argument).
- **Reid's reply** to an argument from hallucination, read historically in [`modern-philosophy` 4.5](../modern-philosophy/lessons/04-05-reid-common-sense-against-the-way-of-ideas.md), is now glossed as disjunctivist.
- **The 2020 PhilPapers survey** (perceptual experience, N = 1,323): 16 percent accept or lean towards it.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Epistemological disjunctivism

**McDowell's disjunctivism: an appearance is either a mere appearance or a fact making itself perceptually manifest, so a perceiver's grounds can be better than a matched hallucinator's.** John McDowell, "Criteria, Defeasibility, and Knowledge" (1982). It rejects the [common-kind assumption](#common-kind-assumption) for epistemological reasons.

- **The verdict on grounds.** McDowell concludes that the hallucinator's grounds are worse than the perceiver's; an internalist moved by the new evil demon ([`epistemology` 1.4](../epistemology/lessons/01-04-internalism-and-externalism.md)) says they are equal.
- **Epistemology by other means:** E = K in [`epistemology` 1.3](../epistemology/lessons/01-03-why-knowledge.md), dogmatism about experience in [3.3](../epistemology/lessons/03-03-moore-and-the-dogmatist.md). Whether experience justifies belief stays with `epistemology`.
- **On illusion,** McDowell's "mere appearance" seems to group illusion with hallucination ([disjunctivism](#disjunctivism)).

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Causally matching hallucination

**A hallucination produced by the same proximal brain state as a veridical perception, and so one you could not tell from it.** 4.5's perfect laboratory case: a good enough stimulator in your visual cortex gives you an experience you cannot tell from seeing a red post box, with no post box anywhere.

- **The case two arguments turn on:** the [causal argument](#causal-argument-against-disjunctivism) and the [screening-off argument](#screening-off-argument).
- **On [intentionalism](#intentionalism)** it shares the perception's content. **On Martin's disjunctivism** its character consists only in its being introspectively indiscriminable from a veridical perception ([negative epistemic conception](#negative-epistemic-conception)).

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Causal argument against disjunctivism

**A hallucination with the same proximal brain cause as a perception is possible, and the same proximal cause yields the same kind of immediate effect, so the kind of event that occurs in seeing can occur in hallucination.** Howard Robinson (1985). In outline: (i) a [causally matching hallucination](#causally-matching-hallucination) is possible; (ii) the same proximal cause yields the same kind of immediate effect; (iii) so the kind of event in the perception can occur in the hallucination, which is the [common-kind assumption](#common-kind-assumption); so naive realism fails.

- **Martin's reply (2004)** denies the principle for perception: the post box is a non-causal, constitutive condition of seeing it.
- **Martin then states a stronger argument** against his own view, the [screening-off argument](#screening-off-argument), whose revised causal principle survives that reply.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Screening-off argument

**If whatever kind of event occurs in a matched hallucination also occurs in the perception and explains what it is like, then the naive realist's relation to the object explains nothing about what seeing is like: it is screened off.** M. G. F. Martin (2004, 2006), stated against his own [naive realism](#naive-realism):

1. **P1.** Let *h* be a [causally matching hallucination](#causally-matching-hallucination) of a red post box, and *H* the fundamental kind of event it is.
2. **P2.** An event of kind *H* needs nothing beyond the brain state.
3. **P3.** The same proximal cause, in circumstances lacking no non-causal condition an effect of kind *K* needs, produces an effect of kind *K* (the revised causal principle, which survives Martin's reply to the [causal argument](#causal-argument-against-disjunctivism)).
4. **C1.** So the veridical perception is of kind *H* too.
5. **P4.** Being of kind *H* explains why *h* is, for its subject, like seeing a red post box.
6. **C2.** That explanation is available in the veridical case as well, so the relation to the post box explains nothing about what seeing is like.

- **Its direction:** it runs from the bad case back to the good, arguing that the bad-case kind is present in the good case too.
- **Where it is weakest:** P4, on Martin's [negative epistemic conception](#negative-epistemic-conception), which gives *H* no positive nature of its own.
- **Concessions.** The naive realist: the matched hallucination has no character of its own. The critic: screening-off needs a positive theory of hallucination to get started.
- **Its forerunner** is the sense-datum theorist's rejoinder in the [time-lag argument](#time-lag-argument): an experience that would be the same whether or not the star survived cannot have the star in its nature.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Negative epistemic conception

**Martin's account of a causally matching hallucination: its character consists only in its being impossible to tell apart, by introspection, from a veridical perception.** M. G. F. Martin (2004, 2006). The hallucination kind has no positive nature of its own.

- **How it answers screening-off.** That property gets whatever explanatory power it has from the perceptions it is indiscriminable from, so it cannot make them idle (against P4 of the [screening-off argument](#screening-off-argument)).
- **Siegel's objection** ("Indiscriminability and the Phenomenal", 2004). A dog that cannot judge its own experiences cannot rule out seeing a tomato, so its state counts as indiscriminable from a tomato perception whatever it hallucinates; and a state with no phenomenal character could meet the condition too.
- **Martin's reply** (2006): indiscriminability is impersonal, fixed by what introspection as such could reveal from the subject's point of view, not by her judgements.
- **On illusion** Martin treats the illusory aspect as a partial hallucination: the patch is seen, and its lightness is merely indiscriminable from a perceived lightness.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Cognitive penetration

**A subject's beliefs or knowledge change how things perceptually look, not just what she judges.** Taught for justification in [`epistemology` 2.2](../epistemology/lessons/02-02-foundationalism.md) (Siegel's Jill and Jack). 4.5 asks the mind-side question.

- **It tests what experience is.** If beliefs can change how things look, each theory of perception must say where the change sits.
- **Whether it happens is disputed.** Zenon Pylyshyn (1999) argued that early vision is cognitively impenetrable; Chaz Firestone and Brian Scholl (2016), that hundreds of reported top-down effects fall to a handful of pitfalls.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

**Module 5: Soul as form: the hylomorphic view**

### Soul as form

**In a living thing the form is the soul: the principle by which this matter is one living thing of a kind, with that kind's powers to feed, sense, want, move and, in us, understand.** It is not one more item on the list of what the body is made of, nor the list's arrangement, which a corpse keeps for a while. "Soul" means the principle of life, so a fern has one. Aristotle defines it as "the first actuality of a natural body furnished with organs" (*De Anima* II.1, 412b5-6, tr. R. D. Hicks, 1907; [first actuality](#first-actuality)). Aquinas argues that the principle by which a human being understands is the form of the body (ST I q.76 a.1; [unity of substantial form](#unity-of-substantial-form)). Applied to minds, this is [hylomorphism](#hylomorphism).

- **The coin and the wax.** Nobody asks how a coin's shape grips the metal: the shape is not a second thing acting on the metal but the way this metal is a coin. Aristotle said the same of soul and body with wax and an imprint. If that holds, Descartes's question, how the soul reaches the body it moves, is malformed rather than hard.
- **No interaction problem** (5.1):
  1. **P5.** An interaction problem arises when two things, each complete in its kind, must be causally connected by some contact or relation. *In words:* Elisabeth's question to Descartes ([`modern-philosophy` 1.4](../modern-philosophy/lessons/01-04-the-real-distinction-and-the-interaction-problem.md)) and Kim's [pairing problem](#pairing-problem).
  2. **P6.** Soul and body are not two complete things: the matter is this body only as informed, and the form is this matter's actuality. *In words:* the wax and the imprint.
  3. **C3.** There is no question how the soul grips the body. *In words:* the cat's wanting commands its legs without reaching across to them; wanting and stalking are acts of one animal (q.75 a.3 ad 3).

  Edward Feser (*Philosophy of Mind: A Beginner's Guide*, 2006), as SEP "Dualism" §3.6 reports, makes mind-body interaction one case of the form-matter relation found throughout nature.
- **Where it is weakest: P5.** It fits Elisabeth and pairing but not [causal closure](#causal-closure), which needs no second thing: if every physical event that has a cause has a sufficient physical cause, a form that changes what the body does either competes with physics or does nothing, and "formal" changes only the name (the SEP entry presses this). The reply: a formal cause is no rival for the same effect ([formal and efficient causation](#formal-and-efficient-causation)). The hylomorphist concedes that dissolving the contact puzzle does not answer closure; the critic concedes that "how does the unextended push?" has no grip on a form and its matter. [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md) takes closure up.
- **For Aquinas the soul is the body's form and also moves it.** It informs the body by its essence and moves it "by the motive power" (q.76 a.4 ad 2), so the formal-cause reply covers only half of Aquinas ([command and commanded act](#command-and-commanded-act)).
- **Trap: substance dualism in Aristotelian dress.** A form is not a thing beside its matter, and even Aquinas's subsistent soul is not the human being (q.75 a.4; [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.3](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-03-the-human-soul-in-the-system.md)): what survives is a part ([subsistent intellect](#subsistent-intellect)). Whether Aquinas is a dualist at all is disputed ([hylomorphic dualism](#hylomorphic-dualism)).
- **Trap: the embodied-cognition theorist's body.** [Embodied cognition](#embodied-cognition) says where cognitive processes run: the body and its dealings with the world constitute or contribute to cognition. The hylomorphist says what a body is, matter that is this body only as informed, and the Thomist adds a power with no organ.
- **Who thinks** is in [powers of the soul](#powers-of-the-soul): for Aquinas the soul is the subject of intellect, though the man understands through it.
- **Owners.** The definition is [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 3.5](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/03-05-soul-and-intellect.md)'s; form in general is [`metaphysics` 2.3](../metaphysics/lessons/02-03-hylomorphism-and-composition.md)'s; Aquinas's one substantial form is [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.3](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-03-the-human-soul-in-the-system.md)'s. The soul as form as doctrine, with its level, is [`creation-grace-last-things` 2.2](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md)'s; no doctrinal weight is assigned in this course. Whether the integration of body and psyche needs a soul as form is asked in [`phenomenology-and-personalism` 5.5](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/05-05-integration-participation-and-the-bridge.md).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### First actuality

**A standing capacity, present even when it is not being exercised; second actuality is its exercise.** A dozing cat has its powers of seeing and stalking as first actuality; stalking, it has them as second. Aristotle's definition makes the soul a first actuality, "the first actuality of a natural body furnished with organs" (*De Anima* II.1, 412b5-6, tr. Hicks, 1907), so the soul is present in sleep.

- **What 5.1 uses it for.** The soul is the body's standing capacity to live in its kind's ways, not an ingredient of the body ([soul as form](#soul-as-form)).
- **Owner.** The definition and its reading are [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 3.5](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/03-05-soul-and-intellect.md)'s.
- **Where the distinction returns.** In ST I q.84 a.7 ([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)), "actually" means in exercise: lesions hinder the exercise of understanding, not knowledge held as a habit ([dependence of intellect on imagination](#dependence-of-intellect-on-imagination)).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Powers of the soul

**The soul's capacities, ranked by how their operations stand to the body: vegetative, sensitive, rational.** Plants have the first, animals two, humans all three. The test is Aquinas's (ST I q.78 a.1):

| Rank | Uses an organ? | Works by virtue of a bodily quality? | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| Vegetative | yes | yes | digestion, which works by heat |
| Sensitive | yes | no | seeing, which is not a heating or a cooling |
| Rational | no: "not even performed by any corporeal organ" | no | understanding |

*In words:* each rank rises further above what bodies do anyway.

- **Interior senses are sensitive powers.** The estimative power grasps what no sense presents, as a sheep grasps the wolf as an enemy (q.78 a.4); imagination (phantasy) keeps and reworks forms received through the senses. Both have organs. Aquinas calls the human form of the estimative power, the cogitative, "particular reason", yet it grasps individuals, not universals, and has an organ, so it is not intellect.
- **Powers are individuated by their acts and objects** (q.77 a.3): a power is what it does, to what. So a fly's sight and a cat's are one power with two kinds of eye, and hylomorphism need not deny [multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability); what it denies is that the role is the soul. The same premise opens the [functionalism-renamed charge](#functionalism-renamed-charge).
- **Subject: who has the power.** Sensitive and vegetative powers have the composite as subject (q.77 a.5, as [`apologetics-foundations` 3.3](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-03-consciousness.md) uses it). Intellect and will use no organ, so their subject is the soul itself (q.77 a.5), and they remain in the separated soul (a.8).
- **Yet the act is the man's.** "It is more correct to say that man understands through the soul" (q.75 a.2 ad 2): the datum *hic homo intelligit* ("this man understands") of q.76 a.1, read against Averroes in [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 7.1](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/07-01-faith-reason-and-the-paris-crisis.md). That is how the act is attributed, not what the power's subject is.
- **Trap: "forms do not think, composites do."** That is the generic hylomorphist's line ([`metaphysics` 6.5](../metaphysics/lessons/06-05-what-a-person-is.md)), not Aquinas's: for him the soul is the subject of intellect, though the man understands through it.
- **Capacity and exercise.** Dozing, the cat has its powers as [first actuality](#first-actuality); stalking, as second.
- **Is the rational power really organless?** That is [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)'s question ([argument from the scope of intellect](#argument-from-the-scope-of-intellect), [argument from universals](#argument-from-universals)).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Unity of substantial form

**A human being has one soul, which does the vegetative, sensitive and rational work: not three souls, one per rank.** A cat has one sensitive soul, which does the vegetative work too (q.76 a.3). 5.1's argument (ST I q.76 a.1, opening of the *respondeo*; a.3, first reason):

1. **P1.** That by which a thing primarily acts is a form of it, since "nothing acts except so far as it is in act". *In words:* what you primarily act by is part of what you are, not a tool you hold.
2. **P2.** The soul is that by which a living body primarily nourishes, senses, moves and understands. *In words:* one answer covers "what makes this body alive?" for every kind of living it does.
3. **C1.** The principle by which a human being understands is the form of the body. *In words:* it is not lodged in the body; it makes the body human.
4. **P3.** A thing is one without qualification only by one form, since it has existence and unity from the same source. *In words:* two forms give two things, or one thing with an attribute added, as a white man is.
5. **P4.** A human being is one thing without qualification.
6. **C2.** The vegetative, sensitive and rational principles in a human being are numerically one soul. *In words:* one form does all of it.

- **Where the dispute with the dualist sits.** Aquinas grants that Plato's three souls would be coherent if the soul were joined to the body "not as its form, but as its motor" (a.3).
- **The third reason of a.3.** When one operation of the soul is intense it impedes another, which could not happen unless the principle of action were essentially one. 5.1 P3 reads the passage and tests it against two programs slowing each other on one processor.
- **"Contains virtually"** (a.3): the intellectual soul has whatever the sensitive and nutritive souls would give, without those souls being present as further forms.
- **Article 3 is not article 4.** Article 3 argues against a plurality of souls; article 4's stronger thesis, no other substantial form at all, is [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.3](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-03-the-human-soul-in-the-system.md)'s.
- **Why it matters for mind.** The one who understands is the one who digests and sees: seeing, wanting and stalking are acts of one animal ([soul as form](#soul-as-form)).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Hylomorphic dualism

**Hylomorphism that keeps Aquinas's immaterial intellect: the soul is the body's form, and one of its powers works through no organ.** David Oderberg ("Hylemorphic Dualism", 2005) accepts the word dualism for the Thomist view; Edward Feser (*Philosophy of Mind: A Beginner's Guide*, 2006) defends the Thomist package. Whether the intellect really uses no organ is [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)'s question.

- **The other family.** Structural hylomorphists need no immaterial power. William Jaworski (*Structure and the Metaphysics of Mind*, 2016) gives structure the role of form ([structure as difference-maker](#structure-as-difference-maker)); William M. R. Simpson (*Hylomorphism*, 2023) argues that a substantial form so transforms its matter that the powers of the parts are grounded in the whole.
- **Is Aquinas a substance dualist?** Commentators split (SEP "Dualism" §3.6; [substance dualism](#substance-dualism)). Against the easy yes: a form is not a thing beside its matter, and the subsistent soul is a part, not the human being (q.75 a.4).
- **On closure, hylomorphists split** (5.3). SEP "Dualism" §3.6 calls rejecting [closure](#causal-closure), with the bottom-up picture behind it, the natural response for hylomorphic dualists, noting that doubts about closure would help other dualists as much. Jaworski keeps closure. Aquinas's texts admit both readings ([command and commanded act](#command-and-commanded-act)).
- **Not emergent dualism.** For William Hasker the brain generates a new individual, the self; the hylomorphist counts one substance, whose form makes the matter a brain ([emergent dualism](#emergent-dualism)).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Functionalism-renamed charge

**The objection that a hylomorphic form is just functional organization under an older name.** The SEP entry "Functionalism" roots the doctrine in Aristotle's soul, and Martha Nussbaum and Hilary Putnam ("Changing Aristotle's Mind", 1992) read Aristotle as near kin to it ([`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 3.5](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/03-05-soul-and-intellect.md) sets that exegetical dispute). 5.1 Example 2 aims the charge at the living view:

1. **F1.** Aquinas individuates powers by their acts and objects (q.77 a.3). *In words:* a power is what it does, to what.
2. **F2.** A functional state is individuated by its causal role ([role and realizer](#role-and-realizer)). *In words:* a state is what causes it and what it causes.
3. **F3.** Every power of the cat can be written as such a role, realized in organs.
4. **C.** "Form" names functional organization, with nothing added but the word.

The reply has three moves:

- **R1, matter.** A role is realized by states whose nature is fixed apart from the role; a form makes its matter the matter it is. The hylomorphist turns [Ackrill's problem](#ackrills-problem) into a thesis: no realizer is specifiable apart from the form. For Simpson the parts' powers are grounded in the whole.
- **R2, subject.** Hylomorphism begins with one substance whose powers these are. A nation running the roles, the [China brain](#china-brain), is not one substance, so it has no soul.
- **R3, intellect (Thomists only).** Understanding uses no organ (q.78 a.1; q.76 a.1 ad 1), so it has no realizer, and F3 fails for the power that matters most, if [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)'s argument works.

- **On a structural hylomorphist.** Jaworski has no organless power and grants that physical constitution necessitates the mental, so his difference must be R1: the base necessitates the structure without explaining it, and the structured individual, not the base, has the mental powers.
- **Where the account stops.** William Seager (review of Jaworski, *Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews*, 2016) presses that a physicalist can identify the structured individual with its base, so the difference may be verbal. The hylomorphist answers that necessitation is not explanation. Nothing in the brain decides between "necessitated and explained" and "necessitated, not explained" until one says what explanation is.
- **Where the charge aims** (5.1's map). Hylomorphism counts substances as the functionalist does (one) and answers "what is the soul?" as neither the dualist nor the [functionalist](#functionalism) does: the first actuality of an organic body, not a substance that could exist complete alone, nor a system of roles realized in tissue.
- **Trap: multiple realizability is not the dividing line.** Powers are individuated by acts and objects, so both sides allow one power exercised through differently built organs ([multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability)).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Ackrill's problem

**J. L. Ackrill's objection (1972/73): if a soulless body is a body only in name, then the organic body is ensouled essentially.** Form and matter were meant to be two principles; on this reading the body cannot be specified as the matter it is apart from the soul that informs it.

- **5.1 turns it into a thesis** (R1 of the reply to the [functionalism-renamed charge](#functionalism-renamed-charge)): no realizer is specifiable apart from the form, so the soul is not a role played by states whose nature is fixed independently. Reading Ackrill's problem against a functionalist realizer is 5.1's own gloss.
- **The structural hylomorphist's version.** For Simpson (2023) a substantial form so transforms its matter that the powers of the parts are grounded in the whole: a strong-emergence claim in the sense of [`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md).
- **Matter, near and far** (5.1 Example 1). Proximately the cat's matter is its organic body, a cat's only while it lives; remotely, cells and molecules, which outlast it.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Argument from the scope of intellect

**Aquinas's argument that the power by which you can know the natures of all bodies cannot itself be a body, nor work through a bodily organ, and so is subsistent.** ST I q.75 a.2, *respondeo*: Aquinas's version of Aristotle's case for an unmixed intellect (*De Anima* III.4; [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 3.5](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/03-05-soul-and-intellect.md)). As 5.2 numbers it:

1. **P1.** By the intellect a human can know the natures of all bodies. *In words:* no kind of body is closed to understanding.
2. **P2.** Whatever can know things of a kind cannot have any of them in its own nature, since what it has naturally would impede its knowing the rest. *In words:* a receiver must not already be what it receives.
3. **P3.** Every body has its own determinate nature. *In words:* to be a body is to be some particular kind of body.
4. **C1.** The intellectual principle is not a body.
5. **P4.** The same holds of any bodily organ through which it might understand: the organ's determinate nature would impede likewise. *In words:* no bodily instrument can do the receiving either.
6. **C2.** The intellect has an operation *per se*, in which no body shares.
7. **P5.** Only what subsists can operate *per se*, since nothing operates but what is actual. *In words:* to act on your own you must exist on your own.
8. **C3.** The human soul is incorporeal and [subsistent](#subsistent-intellect).

- **Run on a geologist** (5.2 Example 1). She understands what quartz is, a nature true of any sample anywhere, and could as well understand garnet or a neuron. Her eyes and imagination take in this vein; the intellect takes from that image the nature, without what makes it this vein ([abstraction](#abstraction)).
- **What it does not claim.** It does not make her brain idle: she understands only by turning to images (q.84 a.7; [dependence of intellect on imagination](#dependence-of-intellect-on-imagination)). Nor is her soul the geologist: subsistent is not complete, and "it is more correct to say that man understands through the soul" (q.75 a.2 ad 2).
- **Trap: Aquinas's "determinate" is not Ross's.** For Aquinas a body is too determinate: a fixed nature limits what it can receive. For Ross a physical process is not determinate enough: it fits rival functions ([determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument)). They share only the claim that thought has a scope or form no bodily state fixes (5.2; 6.1's flashback).
- **Its companions.** The [argument from universals](#argument-from-universals) (q.75 a.5), which SEP's "Thomas Aquinas" (2022) lists beside it, and Ross's argument: 5.2's map runs all three into one claim, that what is bodily is limited, too particular or too finite. The main reply to that claim is [vehicle and content](#vehicle-and-content).
- **Left to you.** Boss 5 reads the *respondeo* itself. Mapping its text and its examples onto these premises, and saying which premise a functionalist who grants scope must deny, are Boss 5's questions; the card gives the skeleton only.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Argument from universals

**Aquinas's argument that the intellect, which knows natures as such, cannot receive them in matter, since matter individualizes whatever it receives.** ST I q.75 a.5, *respondeo* (tr. English Dominican Province, second and revised edition, 1922, p. 14). Its shape: a principle about receivers, a datum about knowing, a contrast with sense.

1. **Receivers.** "Whatever is received into something is received according to the condition of the recipient."
2. **Knowing.** A thing is known in so far as its form is in the knower, and the intellectual soul knows a stone "absolutely as a stone".
3. **Sense.** The sensitive powers "receive forms in a corporeal organ" and so know only the individual, "since matter is the principle by which forms are individualized".
4. **So** the intellectual soul is not composed of matter and form: if it were, it would receive forms as individuals and know only the individual.

- **Two words decide it.** "Absolutely": the stone-nature as such, without what makes it this stone. "Individualized" carries the load: the argument works only if reception in matter individualizes the *content* received, not merely the receiving state.
- **Its conclusion, and a second use.** The article concludes that the soul has no matter; the same premise gives the argument that intellect works through no organ, which SEP's "Thomas Aquinas" (2022) lists beside q.75 a.2 ([argument from the scope of intellect](#argument-from-the-scope-of-intellect)).
- **What it needs.** [Abstraction](#abstraction): the intellect takes the nature from a sensory image without what makes it this one.
- **The reply.** [Vehicle and content](#vehicle-and-content): a particular state can carry general content, as the word "red" is not red.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Abstraction

**The intellect's taking the nature from a sensory image (a phantasm), without what makes it this individual.** The geologist's eyes and imagination take in this vein of quartz; her intellect takes from that image what quartz is, true of any sample anywhere (5.2 Example 1).

- **Owner.** Abstraction from phantasms is [`thomistic-synthesis` 4.1](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/04-01-how-a-human-intellect-knows.md)'s. 5.2 uses it as the machinery the [argument from universals](#argument-from-universals) needs.
- **Abstraction is not independence.** In this life the intellect understands only by turning to images (q.84 a.7), so damage to imagination or memory hinders it ([dependence of intellect on imagination](#dependence-of-intellect-on-imagination), 5.3).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Subsistent intellect

**The claim that the intellectual principle has an operation of its own, in which no body shares, and so exists in its own right.** C2 and C3 of the [argument from the scope of intellect](#argument-from-the-scope-of-intellect) (ST I q.75 a.2): only what subsists can operate *per se*, since nothing operates but what is actual, so the human soul is incorporeal and subsistent.
