- **Subsistent is not complete.** "It is more correct to say that man understands through the soul" (q.75 a.2 ad 2), and even the subsistent soul is not the human being (q.75 a.4): what survives is a part ([5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)).
- **Where intellect lives.** For Aquinas intellect and will have the soul as subject (q.77 a.5) and remain in the separated soul (a.8) ([powers of the soul](#powers-of-the-soul)).
- **Not an idle brain.** The intellect understands only by turning to images (q.84 a.7), so an organless intellect still predicts dependence on the brain ([dependence of intellect on imagination](#dependence-of-intellect-on-imagination)).
- **Trap: three questions, not one.** That something of a human can exist without the body (C3); whether that separated soul is the person ([corruptionism and survivalism](#corruptionism-and-survivalism), [`metaphysics` 6.5](../metaphysics/lessons/06-05-what-a-person-is.md)); and immortality as doctrine ([`creation-grace-last-things` 2.3](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)), on which no doctrinal weight is assigned here.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Determinacy argument

**James Ross's argument that some thinking, such as adding, is determinate in a way no physical process is, so such thinking is not wholly physical.** "Immaterial Aspects of Thought", *Journal of Philosophy* 89 (1992). He borrows Kripke's case ([plus and quus](#plus-and-quus)):

1. **R1.** Some thinking, such as adding, is determinate: a single act of adding has the form of addition and of no rival function. *In words:* when you add, it is a fact that you are adding.
2. **R2.** No physical process, and no function among physical states, is determinate in that way: every physical record, counterfactuals included, fits incompossible functions. *In words:* physics never settles which function is computed.
3. **R3.** Such thinking is not wholly physical.

- **Consequences Ross accepts, so not reductios.** A machine adds only "the way puppets walk"; the brain cannot be the organ of thought as the eye is of sight, though brain states may be its media (n. 13); n. 3 traces this to Aristotle's *De Anima* III.4. An objection must find a consequence he rejects.
- **The calculator** (5.2 Example 2). An eight-digit calculator's ripple-carry circuit ([`digital-logic` 2.3](../digital-logic/lessons/02-03-arithmetic-circuits.md)) and every answer it would give fit addition, and equally a function that agrees with addition wherever the display can answer (up to 99,999,999) and differs beyond. So it never adds, though it is perfectly reliable.
- **The child.** Ross separates adding incorrectly from guessing, estimating or following a routine, so a child who always drops carries, and answers 115 to 68 + 57, still adds. A dispositionalist says nearly the reverse ([finitude and error problems](#finitude-and-error-problems)).
- **The strain.** A child who runs the column method by rote, getting 125 without grasping what the columns are for: adding, or following a routine? Ross grants that correct answers do not always show the ability. The account says what would make her an adder, not how anyone could tell. The Rossian: determinacy belongs to the act, not to evidence about it. The critic: a fact no evidence reaches cannot carry an argument.
- **The replies** (5.2): [vehicle and content](#vehicle-and-content), against the shared claim that what is bodily is limited; the [proves-too-much objection](#proves-too-much-objection), which turns R2 on the intellect; dispositions, pressed by the [finitude and error problems](#finitude-and-error-problems); and [naturalness](#naturalness-reply). Dispositions and naturalness deny R2.
- **Not only Thomist.** Paul Boghossian (2015) argues from the finitude problem that determinate meaning cannot supervene on non-semantic facts.
- **The human-adder charge.** To the charge that we do not really add either, Ross's §III answers that the price is all of logic and mathematics, and that defining a [pure function](#pure-function) is itself determinate thought. Boss 5(d) asks for the strongest form of that charge and Ross's reply; the card gives the outline only.
- **Trap: "not computable."** Addition is computable; Ross's claim is that no physical system determinately realizes it. Running "not computable" together with "not physical" turns him into the Gödelian argument of [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md) ([Lucas-Penrose argument](#lucas-penrose-argument)).
- **Trap: Ross's "determinate" is not Aquinas's.** See [argument from the scope of intellect](#argument-from-the-scope-of-intellect).
- **Elsewhere.** Ross runs Goodman's grue ([`philosophy-of-science` 1.2](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/01-02-the-new-riddle-of-induction.md)) on computation. As a premise against naturalism the argument is [`apologetics-foundations` 3.2](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-02-reason.md)'s. With the [triviality problem](#triviality-problem) it is one worry about physical computation from two directions.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Plus and quus

**Kripke's rival to addition: a function that agrees with plus on every case so far and parts company beyond, so any finite record of sums fits both.** Saul Kripke, *Wittgenstein on Rules and Private Language* (1982). For natural numbers $x$ and $y$, define quus, written $\oplus$, by

$$x \oplus y = \begin{cases} x + y & \text{if } x, y < 57 \\ 5 & \text{otherwise.} \end{cases}$$

So $68 + 57 = 125$ but $68 \oplus 57 = 5$, and a record of sums on numbers below 57 fits both.

- **Grue for functions.** Every green emerald examined so far is also grue ([`philosophy-of-science` 1.2](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/01-02-the-new-riddle-of-induction.md)); every finite record of sums is also a record of quus.
- **Owner.** The paradox and its replies belong to [`philosophy-of-language-and-logic`](../philosophy-of-language-and-logic/syllabus.md) 4.3. 5.2 borrows the case for Ross's R2 ([determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument)).
- **What could fix plus over quus**, on 5.2's replies: dispositions ([finitude and error problems](#finitude-and-error-problems)), naturalness ([naturalness reply](#naturalness-reply)), or, for Ross, nothing physical. Kripke also considered a primitive state of meaning (1982: 51) and called it mysterious ([proves-too-much objection](#proves-too-much-objection)).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Pure function

**Ross's term for an abstract form such as addition, wholly present in a single act of computing it.** The array of inputs and outputs is not what the function is but its "logical tail of the comet" (Ross, 1992). Determinacy, for Ross, is a feature of the single act, not of any record of answers.

- **Why R1 needs it.** A single act of adding has the form of addition and of no rival ([determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument)), so what is determinate is the act, whatever the outputs show.
- **Realizing versus seeming to.** A machine whose outputs track addition adds only "the way puppets walk".
- **§III.** Defining a pure function is itself determinate thought: half of Ross's answer to the charge that we do not add either.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Vehicle and content

**A representing state (the vehicle) is one thing, what it represents (its content) another: the word "red" is not red.** [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md) drew the distinction for belief: a content needs something that carries it, such as a brain state. 5.2 turns it on the arguments that intellect is no bodily power.

- **The reply** (5.2). A limited, particular vehicle can carry content of unlimited range and exact form. Representing a nature needs no absence of that nature in the vehicle, and a finite state can encode a rule with infinitely many cases. It is the [functionalist](#functionalism)'s reply to Aquinas and to Ross alike, aimed at the claim they share: that what is bodily is limited in a way thought is not.
- **The Thomist's answer.** Knowing is receiving a form, so the question is whether a bodily receiver's own conditions limit what it receives ([argument from universals](#argument-from-universals)).
- **The strongest reply to it** (5.2's "where weakest"). The objection presupposes some fact that fixes the vehicle's content, and every physical candidate (history, wiring, dispositions) is finite and fits rival contents, so the objector owes a theory of content that survives finitude and error ([finitude and error problems](#finitude-and-error-problems)).
- **Concessions.** The Thomist and Ross concede that physical states are thought's vehicles, and that correct answers do not always show who adds. The functionalist concedes that the content-fixing facts must reach cases no system can process, which no reductive account has done to its critics' satisfaction.
- **Left to you.** Which numbered premise of the [scope argument](#argument-from-the-scope-of-intellect) this reply denies is Boss 5(b)'s question.
- **A different vehicle question.** Where vehicles are is the [extended mind](#extended-mind)'s (4.3), independent of what fixes content.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Proves-too-much objection

**The objection that Ross's reasoning, run through Kripke's sceptic, undoes an immaterial intellect as much as a brain.** Kripke's sceptic found no fact of any kind, mental included, that fixes plus over quus ([plus and quus](#plus-and-quus)); so if R2's reasoning works against brains, it works against any intellect (5.2's map: it turns R2 on the intellect).

- **Feser's answer.** Edward Feser, who develops Ross ("Kripke, Ross, and the Immaterial Aspects of Thought", 2013), answers in a 2017 restatement: words, behaviour and images are signs needing interpretation, while a thought is its content.
- **The crux.** Kripke did consider a primitive state of meaning (1982: 51), calling it mysterious; the Thomist must answer that dismissal. [`apologetics-foundations` 3.2](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-02-reason.md) sets this crux as a problem; this course cites it there.
- **Trap: not the human-adder charge.** "We do not really add either" is a different charge, which Ross answers in §III ([determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument)) and Boss 5(d) sets.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Finitude and error problems

**Kripke's two objections to saying a system means plus because of what it is disposed to do: dispositions run out before the numbers do, and they include systematic mistakes.** The dispositional reply to Ross says a system adds because of what it would do, and so denies R2 ([determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument)); these problems press it.

- **Finitude.** Nobody is disposed to answer for numbers too large to process, so dispositions fit any function that diverges from plus only there.
- **Error.** Dispositions include systematic mistakes: a child who always drops carries, answering 115 to 68 + 57, is disposed toward some function other than plus.
- **Reverse verdicts** (5.2 Example 2). For Ross the carry-dropper adds, incorrectly, and the calculator never adds. The dispositionalist says nearly the reverse: the calculator is disposed to give sums, the carry-dropper toward another function.
- **Boghossian** (2015) argues from the finitude problem that determinate meaning cannot supervene on non-semantic facts.
- **The debt they leave.** Whoever answers Ross with a theory of content must survive finitude and error; the functionalist concedes that the content-fixing facts must reach cases no system can process ([vehicle and content](#vehicle-and-content)).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Naturalness reply

**David Lewis's reply to Kripke's sceptic: quus is plus with a gerrymandered exception, so the best interpretation of any system assigns plus.** "New Work for a Theory of Universals" (1983). Content goes to the most natural function that fits the facts, so the reply denies Ross's R2 ([determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument)).

- **Its consequence.** A calculator computes plus as determinately as we do. Ross says it never adds, and accepts that.
- **The crux.** 5.2 P3 sets an invented exchange between a Lewisian functionalist and a Rossian; the card leaves the crux to you.
- **Bridge.** Lewis's naturalness reply and 6.2's [causal account of implementation](#causal-account-of-implementation) answer alike, by ruling some interpretations out ([triviality problem](#triviality-problem)).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Corruptionism and survivalism

**If the rational soul persists without the body, is the separated soul the person (survivalism), or does the person cease and await the body's restoration (corruptionism)?** A dispute inside the hylomorphic view of persons, owned by [`metaphysics` 6.5](../metaphysics/lessons/06-05-what-a-person-is.md). Immortality as doctrine is [`creation-grace-last-things` 2.3](../creation-grace-last-things/lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)'s; no doctrinal weight is assigned in this course.

- **Where 5.2 meets it.** If C3 of the [scope argument](#argument-from-the-scope-of-intellect) holds, something of a human can exist without the body. Whether that separated soul is the person is this dispute, and the argument does not settle it.
- **Trap.** The subsistence of the intellect is not the survival of the person, and neither is immortality as doctrine. Even Aquinas's subsistent soul is a part, not the human being (q.75 a.4; [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md); [subsistent intellect](#subsistent-intellect)).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Triviality problem

**The worry that if any mapping under which physical transitions mirror computational ones counts as implementation, every ordinary physical system implements every computation, and "cognition is computation" says nothing.** Hilary Putnam (*Representation and Reality*, 1988) argued that every ordinary open system implements every finite-state automaton without inputs and outputs. John Searle ("Is the Brain a Digital Computer?", his 1990 APA presidential address) claimed that a wall contains some pattern of molecular motion with the formal structure of WordStar, an early word processor ([observer-relativity of syntax](#observer-relativity-of-syntax)).

- **What it rests on.** The [simple mapping account](#simple-mapping-account): any mapping under which physical transitions mirror computational ones counts.
- **The sunlit wall** (6.2 Example 2). Read every ten minutes from 8:00, a warming wall gives 11.0, 12.4, 13.7, 14.9, 16.0 and 17.0 °C. Map A to {11.0, 14.9}, B to {12.4, 16.0}, C to {13.7, 17.0}: all five recorded transitions mirror a three-state counter A → B → C → A. Since no reading repeats, regrouping fits the five-step run of any automaton without inputs.
- **The standard answer.** The [causal account of implementation](#causal-account-of-implementation) (David Chalmers, 1996; Jack Copeland, 1996): physical states must cause their successors and support counterfactuals. The wall fails: at 16:00 the cooling wall reads 14.9, an A-state, and ten minutes later 14.1, in no class; and the sun, not the earlier temperature, moved it from 11.0 to 12.4. Copeland adds that the mapping was built after the record; Peter Godfrey-Smith (2009), that it lumps 11.0 with 14.9 while 12.4, between them, sits elsewhere. A three-flip-flop counter on a clock passes every test.
- **Where it strains.** Chalmers accepts that every physical system implements *some* computation; the restriction removes only the claim that each implements every one. Searle can answer that the observer has moved, not vanished: which causal states count as which symbols is still assigned. Chalmers answers that the causal organization is there whatever we call it. Even if implementation is objective, Searle's 1984 third axiom, [syntax is not semantics](#syntax-is-not-semantics), is untouched, since the [Chinese room](#chinese-room) grants that the program runs.
- **Bridge from 5.2.** Ross says no physical system computes any function determinately ([determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument)); triviality says every system computes too many. Lewis's [naturalness reply](#naturalness-reply) and the causal account answer alike, by ruling some interpretations out. [`apologetics-foundations` 3.2](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-02-reason.md) draws the same bridge.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Formal and efficient causation

**A formal cause says what a thing or event is; an efficient cause says what brought it about. They answer different questions, so they do not compete.** Why did the tower of blocks fall? The cat knocked it (efficient). Why is it a tower, not a heap? The way the blocks are stacked (formal). The four causes are a map of questions, not four forces ([`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 3.2](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/03-02-nature-and-the-four-causes.md); [`metaphysics` 2.3](../metaphysics/lessons/02-03-hylomorphism-and-composition.md)).

**The dilemma it answers** (5.3; [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md) promised it, and [`apologetics-foundations` 4.4](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/04-04-divine-action-miracles-and-quantum-indeterminacy.md) runs it on God):

1. **K1.** If the soul makes a difference to what a body does, it is either one more sufficient cause of the bodily event or no cause of it. *In words:* a difference-maker is a push.
2. **K2.** If one more sufficient cause, closure fails or every action is overdetermined (2.4's P3-P5). *In words:* a second push means a gap or a double.
3. **K3.** If no cause, the event is fixed without it. *In words:* physics does everything.
4. **K.** The soul breaks [closure](#causal-closure), overdetermines every action, or idles.

**The hylomorphist's reply:**

1. **H1.** "Cause" answers several questions: what brought this about (efficient), what it is (formal), what it is made of, what it is for.
2. **H2.** A formal cause makes a difference without pushing, by making an event the event it is. *In words:* the soul is why these firings are a reach.
3. **H3.** Exclusion (2.4's P4) bars two sufficient causes of one effect of the same kind; a formal and an efficient cause do not compete ([exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument)).
4. **C.** K1 is false: as form, the soul is neither a second push nor idle. *In words:* the soul shapes the act without pushing it.

- **Two bronze horses** (5.3 Example 1). An earthquake topples the rearing horse, not the one lying down. Efficient: the shaking and gravity acting on the bronze. Formal: its shape, a high centre of mass over a narrow base. Cast lying, the same bronze would have stood; a rearing marble horse would also have fallen, so the shape is the [proportional](#proportionality) difference-maker. It adds no force, so closure is untouched and nothing is overdetermined, yet it is not idle.
- **Why the statue is easy.** Shape is an accidental form, fixed by the bronze's arrangement, and even Kim grants powers to properties of wholes built from their parts ([levels and orders](#levels-and-orders)). The soul is a substantial form, which the hylomorphist denies is fixed by arrangement. So the statue shows H2 coherent, not that it reaches the soul; the physicalist says it does only by making the soul as physical as the shape.
- **For Aquinas the soul is also an efficient cause.** It moves the body "by the motive power", not "by its essence, as the form of the body" (q.76 a.4 ad 2). The formal-cause reply covers half of Aquinas; the other half is [command and commanded act](#command-and-commanded-act).
- **Where it is weakest: H3.** The objection, in Kim's spirit and pressed by Seager on Jaworski: a cause of another kind either changes which physical events occur, and then competes after all, or changes only how we describe them, explaining the reach as a reach without making the mental a cause: explained, but explained away. The replies: the objection assumes the sufficient physical cause is what it is apart from the organism whose state it is, the bottom-up picture Simpson denies (5.1); or, with Jaworski and in the spirit of Tyler Burge ("Mind-Body Causation and Explanatory Practice", 1993), that our best explanations cite rational causes is the evidence about what causes there are ([causal pluralism](#causal-pluralism)). The hylomorphist concedes that a form adds no force, and that a command changing which events occur makes closure fail in brains, an empirical bet. The physicalist concedes that exclusion as stated governs efficient causes, so K1 needs an argument that every difference-maker produces.
- **Trap: formal versus efficient is not mental versus physical.** Aristotle's own efficient cause includes the man who gave advice, and a shape is a formal cause of a physical outcome.
- **Before 5.3.** 5.1 named this reply to the closure objection: a formal cause is no rival for the same effect, since it says what the event is an event of, not what pushed it ([soul as form](#soul-as-form)).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Structure as difference-maker

**William Jaworski's thesis that structure, in form's role, is an irreducible principle that accounts for what things can do: "structure makes a difference".** *Structure and the Metaphysics of Mind* (2016). The lessons report the book through William Seager's review (*Notre Dame Philosophical Reviews*, 2016), and every claim here is attributed that way.

- **A structural hylomorphist.** Jaworski has no immaterial power; he grants that physical constitution necessitates the mental, yet calls his view anti-physicalist (5.1).
- **No new forces.** He grants that higher-level behaviour never violates lower-level physical laws, and denies that structures exert new forces moving matter as it otherwise would not (5.3). He keeps [closure](#causal-closure).
- **Two kinds of cause.** Seager reads him as a [causal pluralist](#causal-pluralism): physical and rational causes are distinct kinds and cannot overdetermine an action.
- **Against the functionalism-renamed charge** his difference must be R1: the base necessitates the structure without explaining it, and the structured individual, not the base, has the mental powers ([functionalism-renamed charge](#functionalism-renamed-charge)).
- **Seager's objections.** A physicalist can identify the structured individual with its base, so the difference may be verbal (5.1); and a cause of another kind either competes or explains away (5.3; [formal and efficient causation](#formal-and-efficient-causation)).
- **The other structural hylomorphist.** William M. R. Simpson (*Hylomorphism*, 2023): a substantial form so transforms its matter that the powers of the parts are grounded in the whole ([Ackrill's problem](#ackrills-problem)).
- **Trap.** Do not give Jaworski the Thomist package or a denial of closure; that is [hylomorphic dualism](#hylomorphic-dualism)'s territory, and hylomorphists split.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Causal pluralism

**There is more than one kind of causal relation, so causes of different kinds need not compete for one effect.** Seager reads Jaworski as a causal pluralist: physical and rational causes are distinct kinds and cannot overdetermine an action (5.3; [structure as difference-maker](#structure-as-difference-maker)).

- **What it denies.** Kim's dilemma treats every difference-maker as a push (K1 of [formal and efficient causation](#formal-and-efficient-causation)): one kind of causal arrow, so two sufficient causes of one event compete or overdetermine ([exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument), [systematic overdetermination](#systematic-overdetermination)).
- **Its evidence.** With Jaworski and in the spirit of Tyler Burge ("Mind-Body Causation and Explanatory Practice", 1993): that our best explanations cite rational causes is the evidence about what causes there are.
- **The objection.** A rational cause either changes which physical events occur, and then competes, or changes only how we describe them: explained, but explained away (Seager, in Kim's spirit).
- **Trap: not Yablo.** Stephen Yablo restricts exclusion because mental and neural properties are determinable and determinate ([proportionality](#proportionality)), not because they are causes of different kinds.
- **Trap: not a denial of closure.** Jaworski grants that higher-level behaviour never violates lower-level physical laws.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Command and commanded act

**Aquinas's thesis that a higher power's command and the lower power's act it moves are one human act, the lower act standing to the higher as matter to form.** Intellect directs movement without executing it (ST I q.79 a.11 ad 1), yet directing and executing make one act: "the act of a lower power is in the position of matter in regard to the act of a higher power", so that command and commanded act "are one human act" (ST I-II q.17 a.4).

- **The soul also moves the body.** It "does not move the body by its essence, as the form of the body, but by the motive power" (q.76 a.4 ad 2); united to the body "by its very being" as its form, it still "guides and moves the body by its power and virtue" (q.76 a.6 ad 3). For Aquinas the soul is the body's form and also moves it.
- **Union by being comes first.** The motive power's act "presupposes the body to be already actualized by the soul" (q.76 a.4 ad 2). Whether moving the body reopens the interaction problem of [soul as form](#soul-as-form) is 5.3's flashback question.
- **Does the Thomist deny closure?** The text allows two readings (5.3). If the lower power's acting "in virtue of the higher power moving it" means the executing brain events would not occur, from the same physical state, without the command, the Thomist denies [closure](#causal-closure) for them. If the command is only the form of an act physically caused throughout, closure stands, and the question is what the command does.
- **Trap: primary causation is not available.** For God's ordinary action, a primary cause does not compete with creatures ([`apologetics-foundations` 3.1](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-01-naturalism-at-its-best.md), [4.4](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/04-04-divine-action-miracles-and-quantum-indeterminacy.md)). That reply is closed to a soul, a creature among creatures.
- **Who else.** SEP "Dualism" §3.6 calls rejecting closure natural for [hylomorphic dualists](#hylomorphic-dualism); Jaworski keeps it ([structure as difference-maker](#structure-as-difference-maker)).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Dependence of intellect on imagination

**Aquinas's thesis that in this life the intellect, though it uses no organ, cannot actually understand without the acts of imagination and memory, which do use organs; so lesions hinder thinking.** ST I q.84 a.7, *respondeo*, first reason (tr. English Dominican Province, second and revised edition, 1922): lesions would not hinder intellect "if for its act there were not required the act of some power that does make use of a corporeal organ"; the need holds "not only when it acquires fresh knowledge, but also when it applies knowledge already acquired". Frenzy hinders imagination, lethargy memory, and with them a man's actually understanding what he knew before.

- **Its shape.** A counterfactual with a failed antecedent: lesions would not hinder intellect *if* it needed no bodily power; it needs one, so they do.
- **Three words decide it.** "Actually": the hindrance falls on the exercise, not on knowledge held as a habit ([first actuality](#first-actuality)). "Applies": dependence lasts beyond learning, so no stock of concepts frees thinking from images. "Hindered", not destroyed. The images are phantasms ([`thomistic-synthesis` 4.1](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/04-01-how-a-human-intellect-knows.md)).
- **The objection** (5.3):
  1. **B1.** Brain damage can impair or abolish every intellectual operation we can test.
  2. **B2.** A power that uses no organ would not be impaired by damage to an organ.
  3. **B3.** Intellect uses an organ, and 5.2's conclusion fails.

  Aquinas raised it against himself (q.75 a.2 obj. 3) and denies B2: "The body is necessary for the action of the intellect, not as its origin of action, but on the part of the object" (ad 3).
- **What it predicts.** Deficits wherever imagination and memory fail, dementia included; what remains of the person is [`metaphysics` 6.5](../metaphysics/lessons/06-05-what-a-person-is.md)'s question, and the memory systems dementia erodes are [`neuroscience` 4.2](../neuroscience/lessons/04-02-memory-systems.md)'s.
- **Agrammatic but numerate** (Varley and co-authors, *PNAS*, 2005; 5.3 Example 2). Three men with large left-hemisphere lesions, two from vascular lesions in the left middle cerebral artery's territory and one after an infection, did no better than chance on sentences where only word order says who did what to whom. Yet they solved reversed subtractions and divisions, $90 - 60$ against $60 - 90$, and bracket expressions such as $90 - [(3 + 17) \times 3]$. The physicalist sees structure-sensitive thought dividing along neural lines; the Thomist reads it through q.84 a.7, words being sensible signs handled by imagination and memory. Neither predicted where the boundary would fall, and only a deficit the two readings classify differently could decide.
- **The objection at strength.** A reply that blames every deficit on images risks predicting nothing, and some deficits look like losses of concepts themselves. Boss 5(e) weighs that; the card leaves the weighing to you.
- **Trap: brain-dependence does not by itself refute an organless intellect.** Aquinas predicts it and raises it against himself. Lesion evidence shows necessity, not implementation ([`neuroscience` 4.4](../neuroscience/lessons/04-04-disease-a-taste.md)); the objection needs a deficit his reading cannot absorb.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

**Module 6: Machine minds**

### Imitation game

**Turing's replacement for "Can machines think?": an interrogator exchanges typed messages with a hidden person and a hidden machine and must say which is which.** The Turing test. Alan Turing, "Computing Machinery and Intelligence", *Mind* (1950), set the original question aside as too meaningless to discuss; in the game the machine tries to pass as the person. He predicted that within about fifty years machines would leave an average interrogator no more than a 70 per cent chance of the right identification after five minutes, and that educated opinion would by then allow talk of machines thinking without contradiction.

- **Turing's case is an argument from parity** ([6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), T1–T3). Our grounds for holding that other people think are, in the end, what they say and do; a machine that cannot be told from a person under sustained questioning by someone trying to unmask it supplies grounds of the same kind and strength; so, without a reason that would not also count against people, we should say it thinks. Refusing the machine is solipsism applied to one case ([problem of other minds](#problem-of-other-minds)).
- **Two readings.** As a *definition*, passing is logically sufficient for thinking; [Blockhead](#blockhead) targets this reading. As *evidence*, passing is defeasible, and its strength depends on game length, interrogator skill and the rival explanations ruled out. 6.1's weakest point is T2's "same kind and strength": Block's objection is that the same behaviour can come from lookup or from thought; the *Stanford Encyclopedia*'s reply is that no Blockhead can exist in our world, so passing long, expert questioning is good evidence of thought, though not its definition.
- **What each side concedes** (6.1). The Turing side: the evidence is defeasible and can be overridden by knowing how the passer works. The [psychologist](#psychologism): for other people behaviour is all we have, and we use it.
- **Turing answered nine objections;** 6.1 takes four. The [argument from consciousness](#argument-from-consciousness); [Lovelace's objection](#lovelaces-objection); the mathematical objection (Gödel's and Turing's results show each machine fails some question; reply: no one has shown humans free of such limits, and we beat one machine at a time), ancestor of the [Lucas-Penrose argument](#lucas-penrose-argument); and continuity in the nervous system (neurons are not discrete-state devices; reply: a digital machine can mimic a continuous one past an interrogator's power to tell). The rest: theological, "heads in the sand", various disabilities (it will never be kind, or enjoy strawberries and cream), informality of behaviour, extrasensory perception.
- **The 2025 game** (Cameron Jones and Benjamin Bergen, arXiv preprint, March 2025). Preregistered, run on two populations, 284 people, five-minute games against one human and one machine witness. GPT-4.5, prompted to play an introverted young person fluent in internet slang, was picked as the human 73% of the time, so interrogators were right 27% of the time, under Turing's 70%; LLaMa-3.1-405B with the same prompt won 56%; ELIZA, Joseph Weizenbaum's 1966 pattern-matcher, 23%. Only 12% of interrogators asked the knowledge and reasoning questions Turing imagined; without the persona prompt the models passed in only one of the two populations; the authors conclude that the test measures humanlikeness more than intelligence. 6.1: T2's antecedent holds, but only for five-minute games with lay interrogators, and the inside question stays open.
- **Ancestor:** Descartes's first test for machines, about language use (*Discourse on Method* Part V, 1637). The passage and his second test are Boss 6's close reading, not this card's.
- **In 6.3** Bender and Koller's octopus passes small talk, a weak imitation game ([octopus test](#octopus-test)); behaviour is the first of the three questions in "does it understand?" ([understanding as degree](#understanding-as-degree)).
- **Trap.** Turing did not say machines can think: he replaced the question with the game, made predictions, and conceded he had no very convincing positive arguments.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Argument from consciousness

**Turing's name for the objection that a machine does not think unless it feels what it expresses and knows what it has done.** His source: Professor Jefferson's Lister Oration (1949), that no machine equals a brain until it writes a sonnet from emotions felt and knows it wrote it ([6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)).

- **Turing's reply:** that standard leaves only solipsism. On it we could know that no one thinks but ourselves, yet we grant other people thought on the same kind of evidence ([problem of other minds](#problem-of-other-minds)).
- **On 6.1's map it attacks the parity premise** of Turing's argument ([imitation game](#imitation-game)), that minds are judged by behaviour.
- **Not the argument from various disabilities** (it will never be kind, or enjoy strawberries and cream). Turing says those are often disguised forms of this one, but Jefferson's sonnet is this one.
- **Forward.** [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) turns it into the question of what could show that a machine is conscious, and its Connections note that the [gaming problem](#gaming-problem) is why Turing's parity answer weakens for a system trained on our words.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Lovelace's objection

**A machine does only what we know how to order it to do, so it never originates anything; in Turing's variant, it never takes us by surprise.** Ada, Countess of Lovelace, Note G to her translation of L. F. Menabrea's *Sketch of the Analytical Engine*, Taylor's *Scientific Memoirs*, vol. 3 (1843):

> The Analytical Engine has no pretensions whatever to *originate* any thing. It can do whatever we know how to order it to perform.

- **Read in context** (6.1's Source). The note opens by warning against "exaggerated ideas" of the engine, so "pretensions" are claims fairly made for this engine, not a theorem about all machines. "Originate" carries the objection; "we know how to order" credits the programmer.
- **Turing's replies:** machines often surprise him, and a learning machine's teacher is largely ignorant of what goes on inside it.
- **Two targets.** Lovelace's wording is about origination; Turing's variant is about surprise. 6.3's flashback tests a machine against each.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Computational theory of mind

**Thinking is computation: the mind literally is a computing system, whose core processes are rule-governed operations on representations, sensitive to their form.** The classical version is Putnam's [machine functionalism](#machine-functionalism) (1967) generalized, computing over Fodor's [language of thought](#language-of-thought) (1975): the [representational theory of mind](#representational-theory-of-mind) plus computation. [Connectionists](#connectionism) keep the computing and drop the inner sentences. 6.1's argument:

1. **C1.** Thinking is computation.
2. **C2.** Any such computation can be run by a universal machine given time and memory. (By the [Church-Turing thesis](#church-turing-thesis) every effective procedure is Turing-computable, and one machine runs every Turing machine.)
3. **C3.** So a suitably programmed digital computer could think. (The program is what matters; Searle calls the claim that the right program suffices [strong AI](#strong-and-weak-ai).)

- **Two routes to a thinking machine** (6.1's map): through behaviour ([imitation game](#imitation-game)) and through what thinking is (C1–C3). Each objection cuts one.
- **Its critics in Module 6.** The [Lucas-Penrose argument](#lucas-penrose-argument) denies C1. The [Chinese room](#chinese-room) denies that C1's kind of computation suffices for understanding. The [triviality problem](#triviality-problem) threatens to make "cognition is computation" say nothing ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)). Hubert Dreyfus (*What Computers Can't Do*, 1972) turned Heidegger's analysis of skilled coping against its symbolic version ([`phenomenology-and-personalism` 2.2](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/02-02-heidegger-dasein-and-being-in-the-world.md)).
- **Not the Church-Turing thesis.** That thesis is about algorithms; that thinking is one is C1, a further premise.
- **Not [computational functionalism](#computational-functionalism)** ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)), which says that implementing the right computations is necessary and sufficient for consciousness. This theory is about thinking.
- **Connectionism against the language of thought** ([4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)) is the old form of the question [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) asks about transformers.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Church-Turing thesis

**Every effective procedure is Turing-computable: whatever an algorithm computes, some Turing machine computes.** Taught in [`theory-of-computation` 3.3](../theory-of-computation/lessons/03-03-church-turing-thesis-and-the-universal-machine.md) with the universal machine, which reads another machine's program as data and imitates it step by step; Turing machines and the halting problem are its [3.1](../theory-of-computation/lessons/03-01-turing-machines.md).

- **6.1's use:** premise C2 of the [computational theory of mind](#computational-theory-of-mind)'s argument, with universality.
- **6.2's use:** the man in the [Chinese room](#chinese-room) is a universal machine with a rulebook, so whatever program is offered, he can run it by hand.
- **Trap.** It does not say the mind is a Turing machine. It is about algorithms; that thinking is one is C1.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Lucas-Penrose argument

**Gödel's first incompleteness theorem shows that no machine models the human mind: whatever machine is offered, the mind sees a truth it cannot prove.** J. R. Lucas, "Minds, Machines and Gödel", *Philosophy* (1961). The theorems as 6.1 needs them ([`mathematical-logic` 5.1](../mathematical-logic/lessons/05-01-incompleteness-first-theorem.md), [5.2](../mathematical-logic/lessons/05-02-second-theorem-undecidability.md)): let $T$ be a consistent, effectively axiomatized theory containing enough arithmetic, $G_T$ its Gödel sentence (provably equivalent in $T$ to "$G_T$ is not provable in $T$"), and $\operatorname{Con}(T)$ the sentence saying $T$ proves no contradiction; $T \vdash \varphi$ means $T$ proves $\varphi$, and $T \nvdash \varphi$ that it does not. Then

$$\text{(F1)}\ \ T \nvdash G_T, \qquad \text{(F2)}\ \ T \vdash \operatorname{Con}(T) \rightarrow G_T, \qquad \text{(F3)}\ \ T \nvdash \operatorname{Con}(T).$$

F1 needs only consistency (the first theorem's other half, $T \nvdash \neg G_T$, needs more); F2 is F1 proved inside $T$; F3 is the second theorem. Lucas needs only F1.

1. **L1.** Suppose a machine $M$ models my mind: what I can establish in arithmetic is what a consistent formal system $T_M$ proves. (Mechanism, assumed for reductio; $G_M$ is $T_M$'s Gödel sentence.)
2. **L2.** By F1, $T_M \nvdash G_M$.
3. **L3.** I can see that $G_M$ is true.
4. **L4.** So I outrun $M$, so $M$ does not model me; and $M$ was arbitrary.

- **Penrose's version** (*The Emperor's New Mind*, 1989; *Shadows of the Mind*, 1994) is recast for a reasoner who knows she is sound, and infers non-computable physics in the brain, perhaps in neurons' microtubules. Soundness asks more than consistency, since consistency does not guarantee truth: $\mathsf{PA}$ plus the axiom $\neg\operatorname{Con}(\mathsf{PA})$ is consistent if $\mathsf{PA}$ (Peano arithmetic) is, yet refutes its own true Gödel sentence.
- **Ancestor:** Turing's mathematical objection (1950) and his reply, that it is only asserted that humans have no such limits, and we beat one machine at a time ([imitation game](#imitation-game)).
- **The main reply** is the [consistency reply](#consistency-reply). The *Stanford Encyclopedia* entry on the computational theory of mind (2024) reports a wide consensus that the Gödelian critique fails.
- **Against a named system** (6.1 Example 2). Against $\mathsf{PA}$ Lucas wins: $G_{\mathsf{PA}}$ is true because $\mathsf{PA}$ is consistent, as ZFC proves and Gentzen proved, so L3 rested on knowing $\operatorname{Con}(\mathsf{PA})$ from outside. Against a system $T^*$ too vast for anyone to survey, no outside proof is in view (Paul Benacerraf, "God, the Devil, and Gödel", 1967). Benacerraf: at most it follows that either no formal system captures our arithmetic or we could not recognize one that does.
- **Traps.** "Not computable" is not "not physical": Penrose holds the mind physical and non-computable. Ross's [determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument) ([5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)) is a different claim: Ross holds addition computable but determinately realized by nothing physical, where Lucas holds that no machine's output matches the mind's.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Consistency reply

**Lucas can see that the Gödel sentence of the system said to model him is true only if he knows that system is consistent, and if he is that system he cannot prove it.** Put to Lucas by Rogers and by Hilary Putnam ("Minds and Machines", 1960); it turns on the second theorem.

- **The reply** ([6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), in the notation of the [Lucas-Penrose argument](#lucas-penrose-argument)). What I can see is F2's conditional, $\operatorname{Con}(T_M) \rightarrow G_M$, which $T_M$ also proves. Detaching $G_M$ needs $\operatorname{Con}(T_M)$, and if L1 is true F3 says I cannot prove it. So L3 either asserts the conditional, which the machine shares, or assumes self-knowledge that mechanism denies.
- **Lucas's answers:** our inconsistencies are mistakes we withdraw, not policies (1961); we must assume our consistency to think at all (1976).
- **Where the account stops** (6.1 Example 2). Lucas says we may assume our consistency, since we withdraw contradictions when found. The mechanist says assuming is not knowing, and withdrawing the contradictions you find does not show that none are left.
- **Traps.** The reply does not say $G_M$ is false or deny F1; its target is L3. And F3 limits proof within $T_M$; by itself it leaves open other ways of knowing $\operatorname{Con}(T_M)$.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Blockhead

**Block's imagined machine that passes a conversation test of fixed length by looking up a reply its programmers wrote in advance for every possible sequence of the judge's messages.** Ned Block, "Psychologism and Behaviorism", *Philosophical Review* (1981):

1. **B1.** If passing the test were logically sufficient for intelligence, anything that could pass would be intelligent.
2. **B2.** A machine could pass that stores, for a test of fixed length, a sensible reply to every possible sequence of the judge's messages, and looks each one up.
3. **B3.** That machine is not intelligent: Block gave it the intelligence of a toaster.
4. **B4.** So passing is not logically sufficient: whether behaviour is intelligent depends on what produces it, Block's [psychologism](#psychologism).

- **Its size** ([6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)). If the judge sends $k$ messages, each one of $N$ possible strings, the table needs one entry per judge history, $S(k) = N + N^2 + \cdots + N^k$; the machine's replies are fixed by the history and add no branches. On 6.1's undercount (27 symbols, messages of 1 to 300 characters, $k = 5$), $S(5) \approx 10^{2147}$, against roughly $10^{80}$ atoms in the observable universe. The count is worked under Formal definitions.
- **Conceivable and, on any estimate of its size, physically impossible:** logical against physical possibility, [`metaphysics` 3.1](../metaphysics/lessons/03-01-kinds-of-necessity.md)'s distinction ([conceivability and possibility](#conceivability-and-possibility)).
- **What it hits.** It targets only the definition reading of the [imitation game](#imitation-game); being physically impossible, it leaves passing as evidence in our world.
- **The 2025 systems are not Blockheads:** they answer messages no one wrote replies for (6.1 Example 1). What they are instead is 6.2–6.3's question.
- **Trap.** It does not show the test is worthless.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Psychologism

**Block's thesis that whether behaviour is intelligent depends on the internal processing that produces it, not on the behaviour alone.** The conclusion of the [Blockhead](#blockhead) argument (Block, "Psychologism and Behaviorism", 1981), against reading the [imitation game](#imitation-game) as a definition of intelligence.

- **Its concession** ([6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)): for other people behaviour is all we have, and we use it. The Turing side concedes in turn that the evidence can be overridden by knowing how the passer works.
- **Trap.** Not the psychologism about logic that Frege and Husserl attacked ([`phenomenology-and-personalism` 1.1](../phenomenology-and-personalism/lessons/01-01-intentionality-from-brentano-to-husserl.md)): same word, different thesis.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Strong and weak AI

**Strong AI: an appropriately programmed computer literally has a mind, so with the right program it understands, as you do. Weak AI: computers are powerful tools for modelling the mind.** John Searle's distinction ("Minds, Brains, and Programs", 1980). Searle had no quarrel with weak AI.

- **Strong AI is a sufficiency claim:** that a program, run on anything, is enough. Searle did not say that no machine can think: brains are machines, and brains think, and a machine with causal powers equivalent to the brain's could think ([biological naturalism](#biological-naturalism)).
- **6.1's C3**, "a suitably programmed digital computer could think", is the computationalist route to it; 6.1's gloss, that the program is what matters, carries the sufficiency ([computational theory of mind](#computational-theory-of-mind)).
- **Searle's first targets** were Roger Schank's programs (Schank and Abelson, 1977), which answered questions about short restaurant stories from stored "scripts".
- **Redefining it** as whatever artificially produces cognition is the [many mansions reply](#many-mansions-reply).
- **Trap.** "Strong" has nothing to do with the strong emergence of [`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Chinese room

**A man who reads no Chinese runs, by hand, a program that gives fluent Chinese answers, and understands nothing; so, Searle argued, running a program is not enough for understanding.** John Searle (1932–2025), "Minds, Brains, and Programs", *Behavioral and Brain Sciences* 3 (1980). He is locked in a room with baskets of Chinese characters (the database) and a rulebook in English (the program) that says, shape by shape, which characters to hand out when certain characters come in. Questions in Chinese arrive under the door, fluent answers go back out, and the people outside take him for a native speaker.

- **The man is doing what a computer does** ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)): a universal machine reads another machine's program as data and imitates it step by step ([`theory-of-computation` 3.3](../theory-of-computation/lessons/03-03-church-turing-thesis-and-the-universal-machine.md)). Whatever program is said to produce understanding, he can run it, slowly, answering to the shapes of the characters and never to their meaning.
- **Its target is [strong AI](#strong-and-weak-ai)**, that the right program suffices, whatever runs it. 6.2 gives the *Stanford Encyclopedia*'s reconstruction in numbered premises; reconstructing it is Boss 6(b), so the card gives only this outline. Searle's later general form is [syntax is not semantics](#syntax-is-not-semantics).
- **It also bears on 6.1.** The room passes a Turing test in Chinese, so if it does not understand, passing the [imitation game](#imitation-game) is not sufficient, and the [computational theory of mind](#computational-theory-of-mind) is in trouble.
- **The replies Searle answered in 1980:** the [systems](#systems-reply), [robot](#robot-reply), [brain simulator](#brain-simulator-reply), [combination](#combination-reply), [other minds](#other-minds-reply) and [many mansions](#many-mansions-reply) replies, plus the later [virtual mind](#virtual-mind-reply) form of the systems reply. 6.2's map says where each attaches; which premise or step the systems and robot replies target is Boss 6(b)'s question and is not mapped here.
- **Relatives.** Cousin: [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)'s [China brain](#china-brain), many people realizing a machine table and asked about feeling. Ancestor: Leibniz's mill (Boss 1): walk inside the machine and find nothing that explains a perception. Dennett called the room an intuition pump, the hazard [`philosophical-method` 3.3](../philosophical-method/lessons/03-03-thought-experiments.md) teaches you to test by turning the knobs.
- **Families.** A dualist or a Thomist can accept its conclusion and go further: [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)'s Ross denies that any physical process determinately computes anything.
- **Traps.** It does not show that no machine can think. Its conclusion is not that understanding is non-physical or not computable: the man computes the very function a speaker does, and [biological naturalism](#biological-naturalism) makes understanding a biological feature of brains. "Not produced by computation alone" is neither "not computable" nor "not physical". The 1990 wall argument is a different argument ([observer-relativity of syntax](#observer-relativity-of-syntax)).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Syntax is not semantics

**Formal symbol manipulation by itself neither constitutes nor suffices for meaning.** The third axiom of the general form Searle gave the argument in *Minds, Brains and Science* (1984):

1. **A1.** Programs are purely formal, or syntactic.
2. **A2.** Minds have contents, or semantics.
3. **A3.** Syntax by itself neither constitutes nor suffices for semantics.
4. **C.** So programs by themselves neither constitute nor suffice for minds.

- **The [Chinese room](#chinese-room) is the evidence for A3** ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)). "Syntax" here means the formal shapes of symbols and rules over them.
- **The 1990 argument goes further** ([observer-relativity of syntax](#observer-relativity-of-syntax)): it denies that syntax is intrinsic to physics at all. Even if implementation turns out objective ([causal account of implementation](#causal-account-of-implementation)), A3 is untouched, since the room grants that the program runs.
- **For language models** ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)): a model is the room's heir with one twist, that nobody wrote its rules, which were learned from text, so the question becomes whether content can be learned from form ([octopus test](#octopus-test), [symbol-grounding problem](#symbol-grounding-problem)).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Systems reply

**The man is only one part, like a CPU, of a system that includes the rulebook, the baskets and the scratch paper; the system understands.** One of the replies Searle answered in 1980 ([Chinese room](#chinese-room)).

- **Its developed form** is the [virtual mind reply](#virtual-mind-reply): the understander is neither the man nor the hardware but one the running program creates.
- **Left to you.** Which premise or step it denies, and Searle's answer to it, are Boss 6(b)'s question; 6.2's "The argument" and "Where the argument is weakest" cover both.
- **Trap.** Not the [other minds reply](#other-minds-reply): the systems reply is about which entity understands, the other minds reply about evidence.
- **Harnad,** of the [symbol-grounding problem](#symbol-grounding-problem), defended Searle against it ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Virtual mind reply

**Running the program may create a distinct understander, neither the man nor the hardware, whose answers these are.** Marvin Minsky (1980); David Cole (1991). 6.2's evidence: asked about its home town, the room names one the man has never seen, so the answers are not his.

- **A form of the [systems reply](#systems-reply);** 6.2 rates this form the strongest objection to the room. Its exchange with Searle, including Cole and Ned Block's two-minds-in-one-head rejoinder, is in 6.2's "Where the argument is weakest"; which step it targets is Boss 6(b)'s question.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Robot reply

**Put the computer in a robot with cameras and arms, so that its symbols are causally tied to hamburgers and the other things they are about; then it understands.** One of the replies Searle answered in 1980 ([Chinese room](#chinese-room)).

- **Its allies** are the causal theories of content in [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md) ([naturalizing content](#naturalizing-content), [teleosemantics](#teleosemantics)).
- **Searle's answer:** put the room inside the robot; the camera's output arrives as more symbols, and the man is no wiser.
- **Left to you.** Whether it denies any premise of the room argument, or does something else, is Boss 6(b)'s question; 6.2's exposition answers it.
- **Descendants** ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)). The room and the robot reply led Stevan Harnad to the [symbol-grounding problem](#symbol-grounding-problem) (1990); [multimodal grounding](#multimodal-grounding) is the reply as engineering.
- **With a brain simulator inside** it is the [combination reply](#combination-reply).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Brain simulator reply

**Let the program simulate the firing of every neuron in a native speaker's brain; then it does what her brain does, and understands, as her brain does though no single neuron does.** Paul and Patricia Churchland pressed a version (1990).

- **Searle's answer** ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)): let the man work water pipes and valves arranged as the neurons are; nothing understands, because the simulation copies the formal structure of the firings, not the causal powers that produce understanding. Simulation is not duplication: a simulated storm leaves nothing wet.
- **It returns in [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)** as gradual neural replacement: [fading qualia](#fading-qualia) runs it neuron by neuron inside one head, a case Zenon Pylyshyn put to Searle in 1980.
- **Trap.** Talk of what the whole program does does not make it the systems reply: its claim is about a program that copies a brain neuron by neuron.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Combination reply

**A brain simulator inside a robot, taken as a whole, understands.** Searle (1980): we would credit it with understanding only until we learned that a program drove it ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)).

- **It joins the [robot reply](#robot-reply) and the [brain simulator reply](#brain-simulator-reply);** since the robot carries a brain simulator and is judged as a whole, it is not the robot reply alone.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Other minds reply

**You know that others understand only from their behaviour, and the room behaves as well, so consistency requires crediting it too.** One of the replies Searle answered in 1980 ([Chinese room](#chinese-room)); it presses the parity of Turing's argument ([imitation game](#imitation-game)).

- **Searle's answer** ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)): the question is not how I know that others have cognitive states but what I attribute when I attribute them, and a program and its outputs can exist without that.
- **Traps.** It is about evidence, not about which entity understands (that is the [systems reply](#systems-reply)). Stated as "understanding is just behaving appropriately", it becomes [logical behaviourism](#logical-behaviourism), the very thing Searle's answer rebuts.
- **Its human case** is the [problem of other minds](#problem-of-other-minds).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Problem of other minds

**How, if at all, can you know that anything besides yourself has a mind, when all you observe is behaviour and physical make-up?** [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md): it is the human case of the evidence question Module 6 puts to machines.

- **Routes to other minds** ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)). You credit other people with experience because they are built like you and say so, and an octopus on thinner grounds: shared ancestry, nerves, behaviour hard to watch. A machine breaks both routes: inside, it is unlike any animal; outside, it was built, often by training on billions of human sentences, to act like us.
- **In Module 6.** Turing answered the [argument from consciousness](#argument-from-consciousness) by noting that we grant other people minds on the same kind of evidence. The [other minds reply](#other-minds-reply) presses that parity against Searle. The [gaming problem](#gaming-problem) is why the answer weakens for a system trained on our words (6.4 Connections).
- **Elsewhere:** whether our grasp of others' minds is a theory or a simulation is [theory-theory and simulation](#theory-theory-and-simulation) ([4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Many mansions reply

**Whatever processes produce understanding in brains, engineers will one day build devices that have them, and that will be artificial intelligence.** Searle (1980): perhaps, but that redefines [strong AI](#strong-and-weak-ai) as whatever artificially produces cognition, giving up the claim the room attacks, that a program suffices ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)).

- **Trap.** Answering it with "Searle denies machines can think": he allows any machine with causal powers equivalent to the brain's ([biological naturalism](#biological-naturalism)).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Biological naturalism

**Consciousness and intentionality are caused by neurobiological processes and are higher-level biological features of the brain, like digestion.** John Searle's name for his positive view in *The Rediscovery of the Mind* (1992). A machine could think if it had causal powers equivalent to the brain's; no program confers them ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)).

- **Simulation is not duplication** ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)): brains cause consciousness by specific biological powers, which a program only simulates, as a simulation of digestion digests nothing. Duplicating those powers would do; running a program would not.
- **Charge and denial.** Critics call it property dualism renamed; Searle denied it ("Why I Am Not a Property Dualist"). 6.4: Searle, no dualist, denied [substrate independence](#substrate-independence).
- **Anil Seth's version** ("Conscious artificial intelligence and biological naturalism", *Behavioral and Brain Sciences*, online April 2025) ties consciousness to being alive, and judges conscious AI unlikely on current trajectories but more plausible as AI grows more brain-like or life-like.
- **In 6.4.** On uploads: no, a simulation is no duplicate. On the [indicator approach](#indicator-approach): the biological naturalist sets the audit aside, his credence in [computational functionalism](#computational-functionalism) being near zero. On [fading qualia](#fading-qualia): Searle (1992) accepted the replacement case, with experience shrinking while behaviour goes on.
- **Traps.** It does not say understanding is non-physical or not computable, and it does not deny that machines can think.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Observer-relativity of syntax

**Searle's 1990 claim that syntax, and so computation, is not intrinsic to physics: a voltage is a "1" only relative to someone who treats it as one, so whether a system runs a program depends on an observer.** "Is the Brain a Digital Computer?" (his 1990 APA presidential address).

- **The argument in outline** ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)). Computation is defined syntactically; syntax, he argued, is not intrinsic to physics; so whether a system runs a program is observer-relative (a wall, he claimed, contains some pattern of molecular motion with the formal structure of WordStar, an early word processor). Understanding is not observer-relative, so "the brain is a computer" is not even a clear hypothesis.
- **Its partner** is Putnam's [triviality problem](#triviality-problem) (*Representation and Reality*, 1988), resting on the [simple mapping account](#simple-mapping-account).
- **The standard answer** restricts implementation: the [causal account of implementation](#causal-account-of-implementation), where Searle's rejoinder and Chalmers's answer are given.
- **Trap.** Not the room again. The room grants that the program runs and denies that running suffices for meaning ([syntax is not semantics](#syntax-is-not-semantics)); the wall denies that "runs this program" is an intrinsic fact at all.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Simple mapping account

**A physical system implements a computation if some mapping from its physical states to the computational states makes the physical transitions mirror the computational ones.** Very liberal: with distinct states at distinct times, a record can be regrouped to fit the run of any automaton without inputs, which is Putnam's [triviality](#triviality-problem) result in miniature, and Searle's wall ([observer-relativity of syntax](#observer-relativity-of-syntax)).

- **6.2's sunlit wall** (Example 2). Read every ten minutes from 8:00, a patch of wall gives 11.0, 12.4, 13.7, 14.9, 16.0 and 17.0 °C. Target: a three-state counter with no inputs, A → B → C → A. Map readings by position: A is {11.0, 14.9}, B is {12.4, 16.0}, C is {13.7, 17.0}. All five recorded transitions mirror the counter's. Since no reading repeats, regrouping fits the five-step run of any automaton without inputs.
- **What it ignores:** what would have happened, and what caused what. The [causal account of implementation](#causal-account-of-implementation) adds both.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Causal account of implementation

**A physical system implements a computation only if its physical states cause their successors in the computation's pattern, and would do so in counterfactual circumstances too.** The standard answer to the [triviality problem](#triviality-problem): David Chalmers (1996), Jack Copeland (1996).

- **On 6.2's wall** ([simple mapping account](#simple-mapping-account)). Counterfactuals: at 16:00 the cooling wall reads 14.9, an A-state, and ten minutes later 14.1, which is in no class, let alone B. Causation: the sun, not the earlier temperature, moved the wall from 11.0 to 12.4. Copeland adds that the mapping was built after the record, and Peter Godfrey-Smith (2009) that it lumps 11.0 with 14.9 while 12.4, between them, sits elsewhere. A three-flip-flop counter on a clock passes every test: set it to any state and it goes to the right next one.
- **What it leaves.** Chalmers accepts that every physical system implements *some* computation; the restriction removes only the claim that each implements every one, which is what made the theory of mind empty.
- **Where it strains** ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)). Searle can answer that the observer has moved, not vanished: which causal states count as which symbols is still assigned. Chalmers answers that the causal organization is there whatever we call it. Nothing in the wall decides whether "syntax" names that organization or our reading of it. And even if implementation is objective, [syntax is not semantics](#syntax-is-not-semantics) is untouched.
- **One worry from two directions** (6.2 Connections). Ross ([5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)) says no physical system computes any function determinately; triviality, that every system computes too many. Lewis's [naturalness reply](#naturalness-reply) there and the causal account here answer alike, by ruling some interpretations out. [`apologetics-foundations` 3.2](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-02-reason.md) draws the same bridge, and [`philosophy-of-science` 4.5](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/04-05-reduction-and-emergence.md)'s Game of Life embeds a universal computer in a grid whose cells really do cause their successors.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Next-token prediction

**Training a language model to predict each token of a text from the tokens before it.** Taught in [`deep-learning` 4.5](../deep-learning/lessons/04-05-llms-self-supervision-and-scaling-laws.md), on the decoder-only transformer of [`deep-learning` 3.6](../deep-learning/lessons/03-06-full-transformer-architectures.md); the model is then post-trained on demonstrations and human preferences. [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) assumes it and asks what, if anything, the result understands.

- **The objective says what training rewards, not what the trained network computes.** "It is only next-token prediction" describes the mechanism; describing the mechanism is not explaining understanding away. Equally, a fluent answer to a novel question settles nothing about grounding (6.3 Watch out).
- **deep-learning 4.5's line** that a good predictor "must model syntax, then facts, then arithmetic" is, for 6.3, a hypothesis about what the network computes, not a premise; that lesson's careful word is "capability", not "understanding".
- **The training signal is form,** the observable strings: the first premise of Bender and Koller's argument ([octopus test](#octopus-test)).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Symbol-grounding problem

**How can a symbol system's interpretation be intrinsic to it, rather than parasitic on the meanings in our heads?** Stevan Harnad, "The Symbol Grounding Problem", *Physica D* (1990). It is [original versus derived intentionality](#original-and-derived-intentionality) asked of a whole system ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)).

- **Harnad's dictionary.** A learner given only a Chinese–Chinese dictionary can trace every word to other words and never get out: symbols defined only by symbols ride a "merry-go-round". Add pictures, or a teacher who points, and she can.
- **His remedy was bottom-up:** elementary symbols name categories learned from sensory projections, a connectionist network doing the learning, and the rest are composed ("zebra" is "horse" and "stripes").
- **Where it came from.** The [Chinese room](#chinese-room) and the [robot reply](#robot-reply) led Harnad to it ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)), and he defended Searle against the systems reply.
- **For language models.** Emily Bender and Alexander Koller aimed it at them ([octopus test](#octopus-test)); its restatement for networks computing over vectors is the [vector grounding problem](#vector-grounding-problem); Harnad's own route is [multimodal grounding](#multimodal-grounding).
- **Harnad's grounding is sensorimotor.** Mollo and Millière distinguish five kinds of grounding and hold that only one matters: referential grounding, words hooking onto things.
- **Bridge from perception** ([4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)): Campbell's claim that conscious acquaintance with a thing is what lets you think about it returns here as the question what, if anything, ties a model's symbols to things it has never perceived.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Octopus test

**Bender and Koller's thought experiment: an octopus who learns only the statistics of a telegraph conversation can mimic one speaker, but cannot learn what the words mean.** Emily Bender and Alexander Koller, "Climbing towards NLU" (ACL 2020). A and B, stranded on two uninhabited islands, find old telegraphs joined by an undersea cable and chat. O, a hyper-intelligent deep-sea octopus who can neither visit nor see the islands, taps the cable. He knows no English but is very good at statistical patterns, and learns to predict B's replies. Lonely, he cuts the cable and plays B ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) Example 1).

- **Small talk:** O plausibly passes. Replies need only be coherent, and A does the work of attributing intent: a weak [imitation game](#imitation-game).
- **The coconut catapult:** A sends plans for a catapult she has invented; O, who has never met rope or coconuts, sends "Cool idea, great job!", which B used to say. A takes it as meant; the meaning is hers.
- **The bear:** chased by a bear, holding two sticks, A asks how to make a weapon. O fails: the answer needs words mapped onto things.

The argument it illustrates (6.3):

1. **P1.** A language model's training signal is form alone: strings, unpaired with things or with what speakers meant.
2. **P2.** Meaning is a relation between form and something outside language: the communicative intents expressions can convey, and standing meanings that can be tested for truth against a model of the world.
3. **P3.** A relation between form and X cannot be learned from data containing form alone.
4. **C.** A system trained only on form cannot learn meaning; the sense we find in its output is ours.

- **What the test isolates.** P1 holds by construction and P2 is the paper's working definition, so C follows if P3 does. O is as clever as you like, so the test concerns the training signal, not the learner, and predicts failure only where a task turns on reference. A reply must deny P3 or P2; a smarter octopus changes nothing.
- **The replies divide by premise** (6.3). Against P1, add grounding ([multimodal grounding](#multimodal-grounding)). Against P3, [teleosemantics](#teleosemantics) ([vector grounding problem](#vector-grounding-problem)). Against P2, deference: on [content externalism](#content-externalism) your word "arthritis" refers through your community even when you misapply it ([arthritis case](#arthritis-case)), and Matthew Mandelkern and Tal Linzen ("Do Language Models' Words Refer?", arXiv 2023; *Computational Linguistics*, 2024) argue that a model's words can inherit reference the same way, since its text has a natural history: meaning need not be learned if it can be borrowed. And [inferential-role semantics](#inferential-role-semantics), on which a model can be rich in role and poor in reference ([understanding as degree](#understanding-as-degree)).
- **Its slogan form** is the [stochastic parrot](#stochastic-parrots).
- **Where the argument is weakest** (6.3): P3. The objection is that text is a projection of a structured world through the people describing it, and a learner pressed to predict it can come to carry information about what produced it, as Othello-GPT does in a toy world ([world-model probing](#world-model-probing)). The reply: carrying information is Dretske's question, not Millikan's ([4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)); a state can covary with talk of coconuts without referring to coconuts, and the octopus still cannot tell a coconut from a mango. The argument's defenders concede that form-only training can yield internal states that track, and are causally used for, the structure of whatever generated the data. Its critics concede that tracking counts as reference only on a contested theory of content, and that a game board is fixed by its move list as no text fixes the world.
- **Trap.** No relation to the octopus of [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), the [multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability) case.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Stochastic parrots

**A language model described as stitching together linguistic forms by how they probabilistically combine, without reference to meaning, so that coherence is in the eye of the beholder.** Emily Bender, Timnit Gebru and co-authors, "On the Dangers of Stochastic Parrots" (FAccT 2021, §6.1), putting the conclusion of the form-only argument as a slogan ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)).

- **A slogan, not an argument:** the argument is Bender and Koller's ([octopus test](#octopus-test)), and the slogan stands or falls with its premises.
- **It puts the meaning in the reader,** on the derived side of [original and derived intentionality](#original-and-derived-intentionality).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Multimodal grounding

**Tying a model's words to something besides text, such as images paired with captions or a robot's success at acting, so that its symbols connect to things and not only to other symbols.** [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md): Harnad's own route, and [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)'s [robot reply](#robot-reply) as engineering.

- **The reply that targets P1** of Bender and Koller's argument ([octopus test](#octopus-test)). They concede (§9) that meaning could then be learned, to the extent the data represents what speakers mean.
- **Not needed, on Mollo and Millière's view:** they argue for grounding without senses or a body ([vector grounding problem](#vector-grounding-problem)).
- **Trap.** "It sees" does not settle grounding on every theory: each account of grounding has its own condition, and 6.3's P2 runs them on a system trained on photographs and captions.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Vector grounding problem

**The symbol-grounding problem for networks that compute over vectors: can a language model's internal states be about the world, independently of our interpretation?** Dimitri Coelho Mollo and Raphaël Millière, "The Vector Grounding Problem" (arXiv 2023, revised December 2025) ([symbol-grounding problem](#symbol-grounding-problem)).

- **Five kinds of grounding, one that matters:** referential grounding, words hooking onto things.
- **Their answer, by [teleosemantics](#teleosemantics)** ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)). A state refers if it (1) stands in causal-informational relations to the world and (2) has a selection history giving it the function of carrying that information. For them, text is a causal trace of a world people describe, and preference tuning that rewards factual accuracy selects states for tracking how things are. Hence grounding without senses or a body.
- **The premise they say needs most defence** is condition (2): that training is selection in the needed sense.
- **Against which premise:** P3 of Bender and Koller's argument ([octopus test](#octopus-test)).
- **On Othello-GPT** (6.3 Example 2): on teleosemantics the probed state is about the board if gradient descent counts as selection, since later layers consume it and their success at proposing legal moves needs the board to be as represented; Mollo and Millière allow a game world as a world. A defender of Bender and Koller replies that it is no counterexample: P2 concerns language whose meaning lies outside its form, and move notation has no such outside ([world-model probing](#world-model-probing)).
- **Reference, not understanding or consciousness:** Mollo and Millière argue for reference while bracketing consciousness (6.3 Watch out).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Understanding as degree
