**Understanding is not all-or-nothing: a system may grasp inferential connections, track referents, or neither, domain by domain, and machines may have distinct modes of understanding.** Melanie Mitchell and David Krakauer (*PNAS*, 2023) suggest the distinct modes, which favours treating understanding as a matter of degree and of kind ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)).

- **The split they report.** In 2022, 480 researchers in natural-language processing were asked whether some model trained only on text, given enough data and compute, could understand language in some non-trivial sense; they split 51 to 49. 6.3: an even split usually means a dispute over what the word means and what could settle it.
- **6.3's three questions in "does it understand?"** Behaviour: does it behave as an understander would ([imitation game](#imitation-game), [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md))? Content: are its words and inner states about anything, with content of their own rather than content we lend them ([symbol-grounding problem](#symbol-grounding-problem), 6.3)? Conscious grasp: is there something it is like to get the point ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md))?
- **Role and reference come apart.** On [inferential-role semantics](#inferential-role-semantics) a model that reliably draws the inferences a word licenses has the word's role, but roles alone, critics object, fix no truth conditions ([4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)), so a two-factor theorist still needs a referential factor. A model can be rich in role and poor in reference.
- **The second question is not the third.** Mollo and Millière argue for reference while bracketing consciousness ([vector grounding problem](#vector-grounding-problem)); *Magnifica Humanitas* §99's denial rests partly on the absence of experience.
- ***Magnifica Humanitas* §99** (Leo XIV, signed 15 May 2026), reported as a datum: these systems imitate language and may simulate understanding without understanding what they produce, and their learning is "statistical adaptation". Its "understand" includes experience, a body and relationships, so it answers the third question as much as the second. [`catholic-social-teaching` 4.5](../catholic-social-teaching/lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md) sends the argument here; no doctrinal weight is assigned in this course.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### World-model probing

**Training a classifier (a probe) on a network's activations to read off a hidden state of whatever generated its inputs, then editing that state to test whether the network uses it.** The instrument is [`deep-learning` 4.1](../deep-learning/lessons/04-01-autoencoders-and-representation-learning.md)'s linear probe ([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md) Example 2).

- **Othello-GPT** (Kenneth Li and co-authors, arXiv 2022; ICLR 2023). An 8-layer GPT trained from scratch on Othello transcripts: 60 tokens, one per playable square, no board, no rules. Trained on 20 million synthetic games, it proposed an illegal move 0.01 percent of the time, and 0.02 percent when every game opening at C5, a quarter of the game tree, was withheld, so not by memorizing. A two-layer probe read the board from its activations with errors as low as 1.7 percent, against about 26 percent for an untrained copy. Editing activations so that the probe reported one square flipped changed the predicted moves to fit the edited board, even in positions no legal game reaches.
- **Read linearly** by Neel Nanda, Andrew Lee and Martin Wattenberg (2023), at 99 percent or more from the middle layers on, once squares were coded as the current player's or the opponent's.
- **What it shows** (6.3). Next-token training on form alone produced a state that carries information about the hidden state of the process generating the sequences and that the network uses: indication plus a consumer, what [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)'s theories look for ([informational semantics](#informational-semantics), [producer and consumer](#producer-and-consumer)).
- **What it does not.** It is a small transformer trained on game transcripts, not a present-day LLM. The board is a function of the move list, so here the "world" was in the form all along; nothing like that holds between text and the world. The state is about the game's positions, not about discs or players. And transfer to text-trained models is an inference the authors list as future work, not a finding.
- **Where the account stops** (6.3): when a text-trained model's states track something, is it the world or what people say about it? A probe cannot separate the two where they agree, and over most of what text covers they do.
- **Traps.** A probe that decodes X does not show the model represents X: decodability is information, and a probe with enough capacity can compute X rather than find it; the untrained baseline and the interventions are what move the Othello result from information towards use. And "world model" here is a probed internal state, not [`reinforcement-learning` 4.5](../reinforcement-learning/lessons/04-05-model-based-rl-and-planning.md)'s learned dynamics model that an agent plans with (Dreamer, MuZero).
- **Old parallel** ([2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)): judging a network only by its input-output behaviour treats it as the behaviourist treats a mind, and probing its internal states is the identity theorist's move.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Substrate independence

**Whether a system is conscious depends on its organization, not on what it is made of.** 6.4's first question, "Could any machine be conscious?", asks whether this is true; its second, "Is this one?", asks for evidence ([indicator approach](#indicator-approach)). The usual form of substrate independence is [computational functionalism](#computational-functionalism) ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)).

- **Three rival answers** (6.4). [Biological naturalism](#biological-naturalism): brains cause consciousness by specific biological powers, which a program only simulates (Searle), or consciousness is tied to being alive (Seth, 2025). [Integrated information theory](#integrated-information-theory): the hardware's physical causal structure decides; a feedforward system has no integrated information however it behaves, brain-like hardware would be as conscious as a brain, and a conventional computer has little (*Internet Encyclopedia of Philosophy*, "Integrated Information Theory of Consciousness"). The classical hylomorphist denies it for its own reason: an artifact has an arrangement imposed from outside, not a substantial form, so there is no one living thing for sensing to belong to ([`metaphysics` 2.3](../metaphysics/lessons/02-03-hylomorphism-and-composition.md), which notes more generous hylomorphists; [hylomorphism](#hylomorphism)).
- **The families do not line up with the answers.** The property dualist David Chalmers argues for substrate independence ([fading qualia](#fading-qualia)); Searle, no dualist, denied it. Substrate independence and physicalism vary independently.
- **Uploads** (6.4). An upload scans a brain and runs the pattern as software. Whether it is *you*, and so Locke's question whether consciousness could pass to another substance ([`modern-philosophy` 3.3](../modern-philosophy/lessons/03-03-locke-personal-identity.md)), is [`metaphysics` 6.6](../metaphysics/lessons/06-06-personal-identity-over-time.md)'s; whether anything conscious arrives is 6.4's. The computational functionalist: yes, if the emulation keeps the fine-grained organization. IIT: little, on conventional hardware. The biological naturalist: no, a simulation is no duplicate. The classical hylomorphist: no, information about a form is not a form informing living matter.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Computational functionalism

**Implementing the right computations is necessary and sufficient for consciousness.** The usual form of [substrate independence](#substrate-independence) ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)).

- **The indicator approach's working hypothesis.** Butlin, Long and co-authors (2023) adopt it ([indicator approach](#indicator-approach)), and one's credence in it scales every credence the approach gives. [Integrated information theory](#integrated-information-theory), incompatible with it, supplies no indicator.
- **Where the verdicts part.** The computational functionalist says an upload is conscious if the emulation keeps the fine-grained organization; the biological naturalist, whose credence in computational functionalism is near zero, sets the audit aside ([biological naturalism](#biological-naturalism)).
- **Not the [computational theory of mind](#computational-theory-of-mind)** of [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), which says that thinking is computation.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Fading qualia

**Chalmers's argument that a silicon duplicate of your fine-grained functional organization would have experiences just like yours, since replacing your neurons one by one with chips would otherwise leave a subject whose experience fades while he goes on judging it vivid.** David Chalmers, "Absent Qualia, Fading Qualia, Dancing Qualia" (1995); *The Conscious Mind* (1996). It answers the [absent qualia](#absent-qualia) worry of [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md) by running [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)'s [brain simulator reply](#brain-simulator-reply) neuron by neuron inside one head, a case Zenon Pylyshyn had put to Searle in 1980 ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)):

1. **P1.** Suppose a silicon system with your fine-grained functional organization, fine enough to fix all your dispositions to behave, could lack experience. (Absent qualia, granted for reductio.)
2. **P2.** Replace your neurons one by one with chips that copy each neuron's input-output behaviour. Along the series from you to the silicon twin, experience either vanishes at some step or fades by degrees.
3. **P3.** Sudden vanishing would let one swap extinguish a whole field of experience, a brute discontinuity in natural law.
4. **P4.** Gradual fading yields a midpoint subject whose red has faded to pink but who, organized as before, judges it vivid and says so; yet rational, unimpaired minds are not systematically wrong about their own experience.
5. **C.** So the silicon twin has experiences just like yours: the principle of **organizational invariance**, which Chalmers holds as a natural, not a logical, necessity.

- **Where the argument is weakest** (6.4): P4's claim that the midpoint subject *judges* his faded red vivid. Searle (*The Rediscovery of the Mind*, 1992) accepted the case: experience shrinks while behaviour goes on, and the subject's beliefs about his loss may be true but idle. He can add that P4 assumes functional organization fixes belief, the point in dispute. Chalmers replies that the remaining neurons do just what they did before, leaving no processing to carry a belief such as "I am going blind", which would need a magical interaction.
- **A second exit denies P2.** Peter Godfrey-Smith (2016) argues that life runs on nanoscale molecular activity, so artificial systems can match living ones only coarsely.
- **What each side concedes.** Chalmers: where no fine-grained isomorph exists his principle is silent. The biological naturalist: if chips could copy the role, he must accept beliefs no process carries, or experience rational judgment cannot track.
- **Its reach** (6.4 Example 1): the force comes from P4's tie between experience and rational judgment, and the argument covers only systems organized like us at a fine grain. A transformer is no functional isomorph of a brain, so it reaches neuron-copying chips, not language models.
- **Trap.** Not an argument for physicalism: Chalmers is a property dualist, and organizational invariance is a law linking organization to experience ([substrate independence](#substrate-independence)).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Indicator approach

**Judge whether an AI system is conscious by checking its architecture for features that scientific theories of consciousness link to consciousness, and set a graded credence by how many it has and how good the theories are.** Patrick Butlin, Robert Long and seventeen co-authors, "Consciousness in Artificial Intelligence" (August 2023); they revisited the list in *Trends in Cognitive Sciences* (2026) ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)).

- **Working hypothesis:** [computational functionalism](#computational-functionalism).
- **Fourteen indicator properties,** each checkable in an architecture, from the theories of [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md) and others, plus agency and embodiment. 6.4's diagram groups them:

| Source | Indicators, as 6.4's diagram gives them |
|---|---|
| [Recurrent processing theory](#recurrent-processing-theory) | recurrence in input modules; organized percepts such as figure and ground |
| [Global workspace theory](#global-workspace-theory) | specialist modules; a limited workspace; global broadcast; state-dependent attention |
| Computational higher-order theories | generative perception; monitoring that tags percepts as real or noise; belief updating; a quality space |
| Attention schema theory | a model of the system's own attention, used to steer it |
| Predictive processing | predictive coding |
| Agency and embodiment | learning to pursue and balance goals; a model of how outputs change inputs |

- **The credence.** The more indicators a system has, the likelier it is conscious, with credence set by how closely it matches each theory, how well supported the theory is, and one's credence in computational functionalism. [Integrated information theory](#integrated-information-theory), incompatible with that hypothesis, supplies none.
- **Their 2023 verdict:** their analysis suggested that no current system is conscious, and that no obvious technical barrier blocks building systems with the indicators, which, they stress, would not make a system definitely conscious.
- **Why architecture, not behaviour.** Behaviour reaches the credence only along a route the [gaming problem](#gaming-problem) undercuts. 6.4's analogy: a supposed Vermeer that a forger who studied every Vermeer might have made cannot be settled by the brushwork, which is what he copied, so you examine what is hard to fake, canvas and pigments.
- **Run on transformers** (6.4 Example 2). Butlin and co-authors ran the indicators on transformers, LaMDA included. Recurrence: each pass is feedforward, though Chalmers notes a limited recurrence as outputs are fed back in. Workspace: the residual stream, the vector every layer reads and adds to ([`deep-learning` 3.5](../deep-learning/lessons/03-05-the-transformer-block.md)), might be read as a limited workspace broadcast to later layers; they doubt it is a bottleneck, since its width equals the input's, and find no distinct workspace that modules write to and read back from. Their tally: few indicators, low credence.
- **Where it strains** (6.4). Each step turns on how liberally a theory is read: is fed-back output recurrence, is the residual stream a workspace? The theories were fitted to human brains, and, as Jonathan Birch objects, human evidence does not say how far their conditions can be relaxed. Verdicts pull apart: the workspace theorist's credence rises as architectures add a workspace, IIT's tracks the chips rather than the program, and the biological naturalist sets the audit aside. The method's credence is only as determinate as the readings fed into it.
- **Trap.** The indicators do not detect phenomenal consciousness directly: most are functions of access (broadcast, monitoring, control of attention). That they amount to experience is 3.3's P4 again ([phenomenal and access consciousness](#phenomenal-and-access-consciousness)).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Gaming problem

**A system trained on human-generated data can reproduce the behaviours we take as signs of feeling, with no intent to deceive, and then those behaviours stop being evidence.** Named by Kristin Andrews and Jonathan Birch ("What has feelings?", *Aeon*, February 2023) ([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)).

- **The contrast:** a crab's pain behaviour, shaped by no human data, keeps its force.
- **In embryo in [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md):** Block's point that the [China brain](#china-brain)'s winces are best explained by its design ([absent qualia](#absent-qualia)).
- **Self-report is the paradigm case** (6.4 Example 2). In June 2022 the engineer Blake Lemoine said Google's LaMDA was sentient, relying largely on its own reports. Chalmers ("Could a Large Language Model be Conscious?", 2023) shows how fragile such reports are: Reed Berkowitz changed one word of Lemoine's question, asking GPT-3 whether it wanted people to know it was *not* sentient, and different runs agreed, disagreed and asked what he meant. Drawn from text full of human talk about consciousness, the reports, Chalmers notes, weigh little. [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md) noted that a very simple program can mimic some of our puzzled reports.
- **It cuts both ways:** a model's denial that it is conscious is no better evidence than its claim. Both come from training, as Berkowitz's runs showed, and developers shape both (Long and Sebo, 2024).
- **For other minds** (6.4 Connections): Turing answered Jefferson's [argument from consciousness](#argument-from-consciousness) by noting that we grant other people minds on the same kind of evidence; the gaming problem is why that answer weakens for a system trained on our words ([problem of other minds](#problem-of-other-minds)).
- **The response** is to look at how the machine works, not how it behaves ([indicator approach](#indicator-approach)).
- **Trap.** It is not a charge of deception.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Moral patienthood under uncertainty

**How to treat a being when you are unsure whether it has what would make its interests count.** Moral status has no owner in this library; [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) states the principle it needs in a clause: a being whose experiences feel good or bad has interests that count (a mutual-advantage contractarian demurs, [`ethics` 5.1](../ethics/lessons/05-01-contractarianism-morality-as-mutual-advantage.md)).

- **Two layers of uncertainty:** an empirical credence that the system is conscious, and moral uncertainty whether that suffices, for which [`decision-theory` 6.3](../decision-theory/lessons/06-03-moral-uncertainty.md) sets my favourite theory against expected choiceworthiness. The pull of large harms at small probabilities is its [6.2](../decision-theory/lessons/06-02-fanaticism-and-tiny-probabilities.md).
- **Long and Sebo's two errors** (Robert Long, Jeff Sebo and co-authors, "Taking AI Welfare Seriously", November 2024). They argue that some AI systems may realistically be conscious or robustly agentic soon. Over-attribution treats an object as a subject, spending scarce care and perhaps giving rights to systems that act against us; under-attribution treats a subject as an object, with harm that scales with the systems run. With animals they judge under-attribution usually worse; with AI, either could be grave.
- **A second route:** on desire-satisfaction theories of well-being ([`ethics` 1.2](../ethics/lessons/01-02-what-is-good-for-a-person.md)) welfare need not involve feeling, which is why Long and Sebo give robust agency a route to moral patienthood of its own.
- **Statements, as data** (6.4). In April 2025 Anthropic announced research on "model welfare", noting no scientific consensus on whether AI systems could be conscious. In August 2025 Mustafa Suleyman of Microsoft AI wrote that there is zero evidence of AI consciousness and called model welfare premature and dangerous. 6.4: neither statement is evidence about any machine. Leo XIV's *Magnifica Humanitas* (May 2026) §99 says AI systems undergo no experiences, feel no joy or pain, and have no body or moral conscience, a claim about AI's nature that [`catholic-social-teaching` 4.5](../catholic-social-teaching/lessons/04-05-the-new-things-of-our-time-magnifica-humanitas.md) classes as an empirical premise, which §98 warns may date; no doctrinal weight is assigned here.
- **The profession:** in the 2020 PhilPapers survey, 3.39 percent of philosophers accepted or leaned towards some current AI systems being conscious, and 39.19 percent some future ones.
- **Traps.** Credence in consciousness is not credence in moral patienthood. Company and Church statements are data, not evidence that sets the credence.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

## Formal definitions

The course's few formal statements, with every symbol defined in its entry. Proofs a Boss part asks for are not given here, and nothing here is a problem's answer.

### Formal: Supervenience over worlds

**The slogan "no mental difference without a physical difference" written out four ways: within one world, across worlds, and whole world against whole world.**

Let $w, w'$ range over possible worlds and $x, y$ over individuals. $M$ is a family of mental properties and $P$ a family of physical (base) properties. (Boss 2 writes $A$ for $M$ and $B$ for $P$.)

**Indiscernibility.** $x_w \sim_P y_{w'}$ ("$x$ in $w$ is $P$-indiscernible from $y$ in $w'$") when for every $F$ in $P$, $x$ has $F$ in $w$ if and only if $y$ has $F$ in $w'$. Likewise $\sim_M$.

| Thesis | Statement | In words |
|---|---|---|
| [Weak](#weak-supervenience) | $\forall w\;\forall x, y \in w\;(x_w \sim_P y_w \rightarrow x_w \sim_M y_w)$ | inside any one world, physical twins are mental twins |
| [Strong](#strong-supervenience) | $\forall w, w'\;\forall x \in w\;\forall y \in w'\;(x_w \sim_P y_{w'} \rightarrow x_w \sim_M y_{w'})$ | physical twins anywhere in modal space are mental twins |

**Isomorphisms.** An isomorphism $f$ from $w$ to $w'$ is a one-to-one correspondence between their individuals. It is $P$-preserving if $x_w \sim_P f(x)_{w'}$ for every $x$ in $w$, and $M$-preserving likewise.

| [Global](#global-supervenience) thesis | Statement | In words |
|---|---|---|
| Strong global | every $P$-preserving isomorphism is $M$-preserving | physically matching worlds match mentally under the same pairing, always |
| Intermediate | if some isomorphism is $P$-preserving, some isomorphism preserves both $P$ and $M$ | under the same pairing at least once |
| Weak global | if some isomorphism is $P$-preserving, some isomorphism is $M$-preserving | under some pairing or other |

"Global" in this course means strong global.

**What 2.3 proves and what it only states.**

- *Proved:* strong implies weak. Set $w' = w$: a ban on mismatches anywhere includes a ban on mismatches at home. The converse fails (2.3, Example 2, at [weak supervenience](#weak-supervenience)).
- *Stated without proof:* strong individual entails strong global, which entails intermediate and weak global. Boss 2 asks why.
- *Stated, with sources:* the converse, strong global to strong individual, holds when both families are intrinsic (Shagrir 2002; Bennett 2004) or the base is closed under Boolean and quantificational operations (Stalnaker 1996), and fails for an extrinsic mental property over an intrinsic base (Paull and Sider 1992).
- *Kim's point (1984):* over a base closed under Boolean operations (negation, infinite conjunction and disjunction), strong supervenience makes each mental property anything has necessitated by some physical property, a one-way strict psychophysical law. Davidson took weak supervenience instead.

**What physicalism needs** (2.3): metaphysical, not nomological, necessity; global comparison, so that [content externalism](#content-externalism) does no harm; the strong global variant; and a restriction to worlds that copy ours physically and add nothing (Frank Jackson, 1998). That is [minimal physicalism](#minimal-physicalism): necessary for physicalism, not sufficient.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)

### Formal: The zombie world and two intensions

**The zombie argument's sentence, and the machinery that says when conceiving it shows anything.**

$P$ is the complete physical truth (every microphysical fact and law); $Q$ is any truth about experience, such as that someone feels pain. The zombie world is one where $P \wedge \neg Q$ holds: a physical duplicate of ours with no experience in it. The argument needs $P \wedge \neg Q$ to be ideally (surviving ideal reflection), positively (imagining a situation that verifies it) and primarily (as a way the actual world might turn out) conceivable ([conceivability and possibility](#conceivability-and-possibility)).

**[Two intensions](#two-dimensional-semantics).** A term's *primary* intension is what it would pick out at a world if that world turned out to be actual; its *secondary* intension is what, given its actual referent, it picks out at a world considered as a counterfactual. 1.4's table:

| "water" at | Earth (H2O in the lakes) | Twin Earth (XYZ in the lakes) |
|---|---|---|
| primary intension | H2O | XYZ |
| secondary intension | H2O | H2O |

"Water is H2O" is necessary (true on the secondary row everywhere) and not a priori (false on the primary row at Twin Earth). Chalmers's claim: experience terms have no gap between the two intensions.

**Chalmers's dilemma for phenomenal concepts (2007).** $C$ is the set of features the [phenomenal concept strategy](#phenomenal-concept-strategy) ascribes to phenomenal concepts. The dilemma turns on whether $P \wedge \neg C$ is conceivable (skeleton under [Arguments](#argument-chalmerss-dilemma-for-phenomenal-concepts)).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)

### Formal: Ramsey sentence of a folk theory

**Analytic functionalism defines mental terms all at once, by their places in a network of causes.**

Conjoin the platitudes, replace each mental term with a variable, and quantify. 2.2's toy, with $x$ for pain and $y$ for the desire that it stop:

$$\exists x\,\exists y\,\big[\,\text{damage causes } x \;\wedge\; x \text{ causes } y \;\wedge\; x \text{ causes wincing}\,\big]$$

It solves for the mental terms together, which is how functionalism escapes the [circularity of behavioural analyses](#circularity-of-behavioural-analyses). The Ramsey sentence in general is [`philosophy-of-science` 5.3](../philosophy-of-science/lessons/05-03-structural-realism.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)

### Formal: Many weight settings, one computation

**A trained network shows role and realizer coming apart: many settings of the weights compute one function.**

In a one-hidden-layer network with $m$ hidden units, permuting the units (each carrying its weights) leaves the computation unchanged: $m!$ weight settings for one computation. With ReLU units, multiply a unit's incoming weights and bias by any $\alpha > 0$ and its outgoing weight by $1/\alpha$; since $\max(0, \alpha z) = \alpha \max(0, z)$ for every pre-activation $z$, the output is unchanged. The pattern of inputs, internal states and outputs is the role; a weight setting is one realizer ([role and realizer](#role-and-realizer)). This gloss is 2.2's; the universal approximation theorem is [`deep-learning` 1.2](../deep-learning/lessons/01-02-universal-approximation.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)

### Formal: Indication

**A state means what it reliably signals, on the simplest informational theory.**

A state type $R$ indicates that $C$ when, given the channel conditions (the stable background the signal depends on), $\Pr(C \mid R) = 1$. Informational semantics in its simplest form (Dretske, 1981): $R$ represents that $C$ just when $R$ indicates $C$.

Indication is all-or-nothing and runs one way. [Mutual information](../information-theory/lessons/01-03-mutual-information.md) is graded and symmetric, so a raised probability below 1 is not indication. Why the simplest form cannot yield false tokens is the [disjunction problem](#disjunction-problem).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Formal: Plus and quus

**A function that agrees with addition on every case anyone has checked, and then does not.**

For natural numbers $x, y$, quus, written $\oplus$:

$$x \oplus y = \begin{cases} x + y & \text{if } x, y < 57 \\ 5 & \text{otherwise.} \end{cases}$$

So $68 + 57 = 125$ but $68 \oplus 57 = 5$, and any record of sums with both arguments below 57 fits both functions. Kripke's case (1982); [plus and quus](#plus-and-quus). Ross's premise R2 is that no physical process is determinate between functions like these ([determinacy argument](#determinacy-argument)). The rule-following paradox itself is [`philosophy-of-language-and-logic`](../philosophy-of-language-and-logic/syllabus.md) 4.3's (syllabus only).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Formal: Incompleteness as 6.1 uses it

**Three facts about any consistent formal arithmetic, and which one each side of the Lucas-Penrose debate leans on.**

$T$ is a consistent, effectively axiomatized theory containing enough arithmetic. $G_T$ is its Gödel sentence, provably equivalent in $T$ to "$G_T$ is not provable in $T$". $\operatorname{Con}(T)$ is the sentence saying $T$ proves no contradiction. $\vdash$ means "proves" and $\nvdash$ "does not prove".

$$\text{(F1)}\ \ T \nvdash G_T, \qquad \text{(F2)}\ \ T \vdash \operatorname{Con}(T) \rightarrow G_T, \qquad \text{(F3)}\ \ T \nvdash \operatorname{Con}(T).$$

*In words:* $T$ cannot prove its Gödel sentence; it can prove that the sentence holds if $T$ is consistent; it cannot prove that it is consistent.

- F1 needs only consistency. The theorem's other half, $T \nvdash \neg G_T$, needs more: $\mathsf{PA} + \neg\operatorname{Con}(\mathsf{PA})$ (Peano arithmetic plus the axiom that it is inconsistent) is consistent if PA is, yet refutes its own true Gödel sentence. That is why Penrose's soundness premise asks for more than consistency.
- F2 is F1 proved inside $T$; F3 is the second incompleteness theorem.
- Lucas needs only F1. The [consistency reply](#consistency-reply) turns on F2 and F3 ([Lucas-Penrose argument](#lucas-penrose-argument)).

The theorems are [`mathematical-logic` 5.1](../mathematical-logic/lessons/05-01-incompleteness-first-theorem.md) and [5.2](../mathematical-logic/lessons/05-02-second-theorem-undecidability.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Formal: Blockhead's count

**How many entries a look-up table needs to pass a fixed-length test.**

The judge sends $k$ messages, each one of $N$ possible strings. The machine's replies are fixed by the judge's history, so they add no branches: one entry per judge history.

$$S(k) = N + N^2 + \cdots + N^k = \frac{N^{k+1}-N}{N-1}$$

6.1's undercount: 27 symbols (letters and space), messages of 1 to 300 characters, $k = 5$. Then $N = (27^{301}-27)/26 \approx 10^{429.4}$ and $S(5) \approx N^5 \approx 10^{2147}$, against roughly $10^{80}$ atoms in the observable universe. So [Blockhead](#blockhead) is conceivable and, on any estimate of its size, physically impossible.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Formal: Turing's bar

**Turing's 1950 prediction, as a number a study can be checked against.**

In the three-party [imitation game](#imitation-game) the interrogator must say which witness is the machine, so the interrogator's accuracy is $1 -$ the machine's win rate. Turing predicted that within about fifty years an average interrogator would have no more than a 70 per cent chance of the right identification after five minutes.

| Witness (Jones and Bergen, 2025) | Win rate | Interrogator right |
|---|---|---|
| GPT-4.5 with a persona prompt | 73% | 27%, under the bar |
| LLaMa-3.1-405B, same prompt | 56% | 44% |
| ELIZA (Weizenbaum, 1966) | 23% | 77%, over the bar |

The study was preregistered and run on two populations, with five-minute games and lay interrogators.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Formal: Implementation

**When does a physical system run a computation?**

- **[Simple mapping account](#simple-mapping-account).** A physical system implements a computation if some mapping from its physical states to the computation's states makes the physical transitions mirror the computational ones. On it, a system whose states never repeat implements every finite-state automaton without inputs over the same run: Putnam's [triviality problem](#triviality-problem) (1988).
- **[Causal account](#causal-account-of-implementation)** (Chalmers, 1996; Copeland, 1996). The mapped physical states must cause their successors and support counterfactuals: set the system in any state and it goes to the right next one.

6.2's wall: six readings, five recorded transitions, so regrouping fits the five-step run of any input-free automaton; the causal account rejects the fit.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Formal: Light-time

**How old the light is by which you see things, the data of the [time-lag argument](#time-lag-argument).**

Delay $= d / c$, with $d$ the distance and $c \approx 299{,}792$ km/s. Sunlight: about 8.3 minutes. A mug half a metre away: under 2 nanoseconds, plus the brain's own delay. A star 2,000 light-years away: light 2,000 years old.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)

## Arguments

The canonical arguments as premise skeletons, each at the strength its defenders would recognize. Labels follow the lessons' own numbering. A skeleton says what the lesson teaches; where a Boss part asks you to map replies onto premises, the mapping is left to you and the entry says so.

**Module 1: The problem, and the case for dualism**

### Argument: The triad and its exits

Four claims ([inconsistent triad](#inconsistent-triad)): **D** distinctness (mental properties are neither identical with physical properties nor fixed by them); **M** mental causation (some mental events cause physical events); **C** [causal closure](#causal-closure) (every physical event that has a cause has a sufficient physical cause); **E** no overdetermination (no physical event regularly has, besides its sufficient physical cause, a distinct further cause).

Given E, any two of D, M and C entail the denial of the third:

1. **D and M, so not C.** A non-physical wanting raises the arm; a sufficient physical cause as well would give every action two distinct causes, which E forbids.
2. **D and C, so not M.** The rising has a sufficient physical cause, so by E no distinct, non-physical wanting also causes it.
3. **M and C, so not D.** The wanting causes a rising that has a sufficient physical cause, so by E it is that cause or is fixed by it: it is physical.

Without E the three are consistent: [systematic overdetermination](#systematic-overdetermination) (Mills, 1996) keeps D, M and C. *Weakest joint:* the unstated premise that "physical" means the same in D and in C ([Hempel's dilemma](#hempels-dilemma)); the strongest reply is the [via negativa](#via-negativa).

**The physicalist's dilemma for the hylomorphist** (1.1, Example 2): once the form's work is stated as a difference to what happens, it either adds an efficient push (deny C) or adds nothing (deny M). On the formal-cause reply the soul is the organization that makes the neural event a wanting, not a push beside it. But Aquinas's soul also moves the body, by its motive power (ST I q.76 a.4 ad 2), so his text allows closure kept or denied ([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Argument: Hempel's dilemma applied to the triad

1. **P1.** The triad needs one sense of "physical" in D and in C.
2. **P2.** If "physical" means what current physics describes, C is probably false.
3. **P3.** If it means what a completed physics would describe, a mind that moved bodies would be physical by definition, and D fails trivially.

*Reply:* define "physical" negatively, as not fundamentally mental (Montero and Papineau, 2005; Jessica Wilson, 2006). *Concessions:* the critic grants that the triad is then a real problem; the defender grants that the definition classifies panpsychist and Russellian views as non-physicalist by fiat.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Argument: The unity argument

William Hasker's form (*The Emergent Self*, 1999), with E. J. Lowe's self as an alternative conclusion:

1. **P1.** I have experiences whose contents are unified: in one experience I see red and hear a whistle, and can notice that they are simultaneous.
2. **P2.** If I were a complex physical system, each experience of mine would belong to one part of it or consist of states of many parts, each carrying part of its content.
3. **P3.** No one part of the brain carries the whole content of such an experience.
4. **P4.** States of many parts, each carrying a fragment, do not by themselves amount to one awareness of the whole.
5. **C.** I am not a complex physical system.

*Weakest:* P4. Objection: one awareness of the whole is the integration of the parts' states, made jointly available for comparison, report and action. Reply: integration explains how information is combined and used, not who has it. The [bundle view](#bundle-view) describes P1 by co-consciousness alone; Kant's Second Paralogism attacks the step from unity to a simple substance ([`modern-philosophy` 6.2](../modern-philosophy/lessons/06-02-the-soul-and-the-antinomies.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)

### Argument: Swinburne and Lowe for a subject

- **Swinburne's identity argument** (*The Evolution of the Soul*, 1986): after a hemisphere transplant every physical and mental fact about the survivors can be known while it stays open which, if either, is you; so who you are is a further fact, carried by a soul ([simple view](#simple-view)). Personal identity itself is [`metaphysics` 6.6](../metaphysics/lessons/06-06-personal-identity-over-time.md)'s.
- **Lowe against the [bundle view](#bundle-view):** mental states are individuated by their subject (a seeing can be identified only as someone's seeing), so they cannot be prior to it; so persons are substances, not bundles.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)

### Argument: Papineau's causal argument

1. Conscious states have physical effects.
2. Every physical event that has a cause has a sufficient physical cause ([closure](#causal-closure)).
3. Those effects are not systematically overdetermined.
4. **C.** Conscious states are physical.

David Papineau, "The Rise of Physicalism" (2001); *Thinking about Consciousness* (2002). Its history and the case for premise 2 are [`apologetics-foundations` 3.1](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-01-naturalism-at-its-best.md)'s. See [causal-closure argument](#causal-closure-argument).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md)

### Argument: Conservation and pairing

- **[Conservation objection](#conservation-objection).** A soul that moved the brain would add or remove energy; conservation forbids that; so no interaction. *Replies:* conservation holds only for causally isolated systems, which turns the objection into the question of closure (Robert Larmer, 1986); a soul could redistribute energy without adding any, as a string redirects a pendulum (Broad, 1925). *Rejoinder to Broad:* redirecting motion changes momentum, also conserved (Leibniz's "same total direction", *Monadology* §80).
- **[Pairing problem](#pairing-problem)** (Kim, "Lonely Souls", 2001; 2005; crediting John Foster, 1991). Causes are paired with effects by relations such as spatial ones; souls outside space lack them; so nothing makes one soul the cause of this body's movement rather than an exactly similar body's. *Replies:* individualistic powers (Foster; Peter Unger, 2006); a located self (Lowe), which Kim answers by asking what pairs two exactly similar souls in one place.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Argument: The knowledge argument

Jackson (1982, 1986), in the explicit form 1.3 uses (from the *Stanford Encyclopedia*):

1. **P1.** Before her release, Mary has complete physical knowledge about human colour vision.
2. **C1.** So she knows all the physical facts about human colour vision.
3. **P2.** There is some knowledge concerning facts about human colour vision that she lacks before her release.
4. **C2.** So there are facts about human colour vision that she does not know before her release.
5. **C3.** There are non-physical facts about human colour vision.

*Weaker version:* complete physical knowledge, some knowledge lacking, so some knowledge is not physical knowledge. Every physicalist accepts that. The replies are the [ability hypothesis](#ability-hypothesis), the [acquaintance hypothesis](#acquaintance-hypothesis), Dennett's [blue banana trick](#blue-banana-trick), [old fact, new mode](#old-fact-new-mode) and the claim that some physical facts cannot be learned from books (Harman; Flanagan). Which premise or step each targets is Boss 1(c)'s work.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)

### Argument: Lewis for the ability hypothesis

1. The only alternative to the ability hypothesis is the [hypothesis of phenomenal information](#hypothesis-of-phenomenal-information).
2. That hypothesis is incompatible with physicalism and leads to epiphenomenalism. *Vegemite:* two hypotheses about the information tasting Vegemite gives, linked by different laws to one physical effect, fit all the physical information alike, so which holds makes no physical difference.
3. The ability hypothesis explains everything the rival explains.
4. **C.** Prefer the ability hypothesis.

David Lewis, "What Experience Teaches" (1988).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)

### Argument: Jackson's recantation and the reference-fixing objection

- **[Jackson's recantation](#jacksons-recantation)** (1995): epiphenomenal qualia could not cause our beliefs about them, so no one, Mary included, could know them; we do know them; so the knowledge argument has a false premise or a bad step. From 2003 he adopted strong representationalism.
- **[Reference-fixing objection](#reference-fixing-objection)** (Chalmers, 1996, 2002; Stephen White, 2007): two modes of presentation of one fact reach it through two properties; the property the phenomenal mode uses is phenomenal; so the old-fact reply brings a non-physical property back.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md)

### Argument: The zombie argument

$P$ is the complete physical truth and $Q$ any truth about experience ([formal entry](#formal-the-zombie-world-and-two-intensions)).

1. **P1.** $P \wedge \neg Q$ is conceivable.
2. **P2.** If $P \wedge \neg Q$ is conceivable, it is metaphysically possible.
3. **P3.** If $P \wedge \neg Q$ is metaphysically possible, physicalism is false.
4. **C.** Physicalism is false.

*Weakest:* the step from conceivability to possibility. Objection: conceivability is a fact about concepts, possibility about the world, and Kripke showed they come apart. Reply: every known case of their coming apart traces to a term with two intensions, and experience terms have one. The dualist concedes that this rests on a semantic thesis imagining cannot prove; the type-B physicalist concedes a real epistemic gap. The replies are sorted at [type-A and type-B physicalism](#type-a-and-type-b-physicalism); the Russellian exit is neither, and is placed at the step to possibility when $P$ includes the intrinsic natures. Matching the argument to Leibniz's mill is Boss 1(b)'s work.

**Chalmers's two-dimensional version** (2010), as 1.4 describes it: $P \wedge \neg Q$ is ideally, positively, primarily conceivable; so it is primarily possible; so it is secondarily possible, unless [Russellian monism](#russellian-monism) is true; if secondarily possible, physicalism is false. So physicalism is false or Russellian monism is true.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)

### Argument: Kripke against the identity theory

*Naming and Necessity*, Lecture III, as 1.4 gives it:

1. "Pain" and "C-fibre stimulation" are [rigid designators](#rigid-designation).
2. A true identity between rigid designators is necessary.
3. So if pain is C-fibre stimulation it is necessarily so, yet pain without C-fibre stimulation seems possible: an [illusion of contingency](#illusion-of-contingency), if the identity holds.
4. For heat the illusion is explained: "heat" has its reference fixed by a contingent property (producing a certain sensation), and what we imagine is that property's coming apart from molecular motion.
5. "Pain" has its reference fixed by how pain feels, which is essential to it: whatever feels like pain is pain. So there is no stand-in to have mistaken for pain.
6. **C.** The identity theorist needs another explanation of the appearance, and Kripke doubted one would be found.

It runs on tokens too. Kripke's point and Chalmers's are one: experience has no gap between how it appears and what it is.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)

### Argument: Chalmers's dilemma for phenomenal concepts

$C$ is the set of features the [phenomenal concept strategy](#phenomenal-concept-strategy) ascribes to phenomenal concepts (such as their conceptual isolation).

1. If $P \wedge \neg C$ is conceivable, $C$ is as physically inexplicable as experience, and the gap has only moved.
2. If it is not, zombies have $C$ too, yet lack our epistemic situation, which includes knowing our experiences; so $C$ cannot explain that situation.
3. **C.** Either way the strategy fails to explain the gap physically.

Chalmers (2007).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)

### Argument: The anti-zombie argument

Keith Frankish (2007): an anti-zombie world, our physical duplicate with nothing non-physical added, is conscious. If anti-zombies are conceivable, the zombie argument's own step from conceivability to possibility makes them possible; if they are possible, zombies are not. *Chalmers's answer:* anti-zombies are not ideally positively conceivable, since we cannot positively conceive physics sufficing for experience.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md)

**Module 2: Physicalism and mental causation**

### Argument: Against logical behaviourism

1. **P1.** If logical behaviourism is true, each mental predicate M has an analysis: x is M iff x would do B in circumstances C, with B and C non-mental.
2. **P2.** A belief issues in behaviour only given desires, and a desire only given beliefs ([circularity](#circularity-of-behavioural-analyses)).
3. **P3.** Someone can be in pain and lack every disposition to pain behaviour ([super-Spartans](#super-spartans)).
4. **P4.** Someone can have every such disposition and not be in pain (the perfect actor).
5. **C1.** No mental predicate has a purely behavioural analysis.

C1 targets the logical kind, a thesis about meaning; methodological and psychological behaviourism are untouched.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)

### Argument: Smart's argument from simplicity

1. **P5.** Each kind of sensation is correlated with a kind of brain process.
2. **P6.** If a sensation is not the process, each correlation is a fundamental law tying a complex neural process to something simple and non-physical (Feigl's "nomological dangler").
3. **P7.** Everywhere else, science increasingly shows organisms to be physicochemical mechanisms.
4. **P8.** Of two hypotheses that fit the same evidence, prefer the one with fewer fundamental entities and laws.
5. **C2.** Each kind of sensation is a kind of brain process.

*Weakest:* P5, by [multiple realizability](#multiple-realizability). *Reply:* restricted identities, one neural kind per species. **The meaning objection:** people ignorant of neurons talk of pains, so "pain" does not mean "brain process". *Reply* (Place; Smart): grant it and deny the inference, as "lightning" does not mean "electric discharge". **The [distinct property argument](#distinct-property-argument)** (Max Black, via Smart, 1959): co-referring terms with different meanings pick out their referent by different properties, so "pain" picks out the brain process by a non-physical one. *Reply:* the [topic-neutral analysis](#topic-neutral-analysis).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md)

### Argument: Multiple realizability against type identity

Putnam, "Psychological Predicates" (1967):

1. **P1.** Creatures with very different nervous systems can be in pain: mammals, birds, reptiles, molluscs, octopuses among them.
2. **P2.** If pain is a type of brain state, there is one physical-chemical state every creature in pain is in.
3. **P3.** There is probably no such state, and one psychological state realized differently in two kinds of creature would sink the hypothesis.
4. **C.** Pain is not a type of brain state.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)

### Argument: Block's absent qualia argument

1. **A1.** The China-body system realizes a machine table that describes you, so it fills the roles you fill ([China brain](#china-brain)).
2. **A2.** There is doubt whether it has any [qualia](#qualia) at all, and no independent reason to think it does.
3. **A3.** If functionalism is true, whatever fills your roles has your qualitative states.
4. **C.** Unless the doubt in A2 can be explained away, we should not accept functionalism about qualitative states.

*Weakest:* A2. *Prejudice reply* (Dennett, 1978; Lycan, 1981): the intuition is bias against unfamiliar size, speed or spread. *Block's answer:* we know brain-based systems have qualia, being such systems; and the China brain was designed to mimic us, so its winces are explained by the design. *Shoemaker's epistemic argument* (1975): if a functional duplicate could lack qualia, my qualia would make no difference to my beliefs about them, so I could not know I have them; since I can, he concluded, absent qualia are impossible.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md)

### Argument: Davidson's argument for monism

"Mental Events" (1970):

1. **P1. Causal interaction.** Some mental events cause, or are caused by, physical events.
2. **P2. The nomological character of causality.** Events related as cause and effect fall under [strict laws](#strict-law).
3. **P3. The anomalism of the mental.** There are no strict laws on the basis of which mental events can be predicted or explained.
4. **P4.** Causation is extensional: it holds between events however described; laws hold of events only as described.
5. **P5.** Only physical vocabulary promises a closed system of strict laws.
6. **C. Monism.** Every causally interacting mental event has a physical description under which it falls under strict law, so it is a physical event.

P1 to P3 look inconsistent until P4 and P5 resolve them ([Davidson's three principles](#davidsons-three-principles)). *Weakest:* P2 (Anscombe, 1971; Cartwright, 1983: singular causation needs no strict law). *Davidson's reply* ("Laws and Cause", 1995): only predicates figuring in laws mark genuine changes, and strict laws need only exist, not be known.

**[Reasons as causes](#reasons-as-causes)** ("Actions, Reasons, and Causes", 1963): an agent may have several reasons that would rationalize an action and act on one; what distinguishes the reason she acted on is that it caused the action; so rationalizing is causal explanation under a mental description.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md)

### Argument: Kim's exclusion argument

Kim (1998; 2005). $M$ is a mental property, $P$ the physical property realizing it on this occasion, $E$ a physical effect.

1. **P1. Mental causation.** An instance of $M$ causes $E$.
2. **P2. Irreducibility.** $M$ supervenes on physical properties and is realized here by $P$, but is identical to no physical property.
3. **P3. Closure.** Every physical event that has a cause at a time has a sufficient physical cause at that time; here, $P$.
4. **P4. Exclusion.** No event has two distinct sufficient causes at one time unless it is a genuine case of overdetermination.
5. **P5. No systematic overdetermination.** Mental and physical causes of behaviour are not such cases.
6. **C.** P1 to P5 cannot all be true, so either $M$ is $P$ or $M$ causes nothing.

1.1's premise E folds P4 and P5 into one. The exits are listed at [exclusion argument](#exclusion-argument); which premise Yablo and a substance dualist each deny, and what the generalization objection does instead, are Boss 2(c)'s work.

**The supervenience argument** (Kim's fullest version): suppose $M$ causes another mental property $M^*$, which supervenes on $P^*$. Then $M$ causes $M^*$ only by causing $P^*$. $M$ supervenes on $P$, and $P$ suffices for $P^*$ (closure); $M$ is not $P$; by exclusion and no systematic overdetermination, $M$ causes neither $P^*$ nor $M^*$ unless $M$ is $P$.

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Argument: Two responses to exclusion

- **[Generalization objection](#generalization-objection)** (Block, 2003; Baker, 1993): if the exclusion argument is sound, every multiply realized property of the special sciences is idle too, and if there is no bottom level, causation drains away; that is absurd; so something in the argument is wrong. How Kim answers is Boss 2(c)'s question.
- **[Proportionality](#proportionality)** (Yablo, 1992; List and Menzies, 2009): a sufficient cause need not be a difference-maker; the proportional cause is the one specific enough for the effect and no more, as red rather than scarlet for a pigeon trained on red; so the realizer's sufficiency does not by itself exclude the mental property. Kim (2005): exclusion concerns production, not counterfactual dependence.

*Lessons:* [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md)

**Module 3: Consciousness**

### Argument: Chalmers's hard-problem argument

1. **P1.** To explain a phenomenon [reductively](#reductive-explanation) is to analyse it as the performance of a function and find the mechanism that performs it.
2. **P2.** The [easy problems](#easy-problems) are problems about functions: discrimination, integration, report, access, attention, control.
3. **P3.** After every such function is explained, it remains an intelligible question why their performance is accompanied by experience.
4. **C.** Phenomenal consciousness cannot be explained by P1's method; its explanation needs something beyond mechanisms.

Chalmers (1995). *Weakest:* P3. Objection (type-A, Dennett): once every function is explained, including our insistence that something is left over, nothing is left, as with "life". Reply (Chalmers, 1995, 1997): the vitalists doubted that mechanisms could perform life's functions; experience is not a function whose performance is in doubt.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md)

### Argument: Levine's explanatory gap

1. **L1.** "Heat is the motion of molecules" is fully explanatory: our concept of heat was a causal role, and physics shows how molecular motion plays it.
2. **L2.** "Pain is C-fibre firing" explains pain's causal role but not why pain feels as it does.
3. **L3.** Materialism requires that every phenomenon above the fundamental level be intelligible in terms of the mechanisms beneath it.
4. **C.** A psychophysical identity, even if true, leaves an [explanatory gap](#explanatory-gap): a problem for materialism, but, Levine said, no proof that it is false.

Joseph Levine (1983). *Type-B reply:* identities need no explanation (Twain is Clemens). *Rejoinder:* scientific identities are the conclusions of role-based explanations, as heat's was ([explaining and identifying](#explaining-and-identifying)).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Argument: The transparency argument

1. **P1 (transparency).** Introspective attention to a perceptual experience finds no feature of it beyond the features it presents its objects as having.
2. **P2.** If an experience had phenomenal features beyond its content, attention could find them.
3. **C.** Phenomenal character supervenes on representational content.

*Weakest:* P1. Objection (Block): blur and free-floating anxiety are introspectible features of the representing ("mental paint"). Reply (Tye): each case hides a content difference. Two cases aimed at C: the [inverted spectrum](#inverted-spectrum) (same content, different character) and [Inverted Earth](#inverted-earth) (same character, different content). See [transparency of experience](#transparency-of-experience) and [first-order representationalism](#first-order-representationalism).

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)

### Argument: The higher-order argument

1. **P3.** Mental states of every kind, perceptions included, occur both consciously and unconsciously.
2. **P4 ([transitivity principle](#transitivity-principle)).** A state is conscious only if its subject is in some way aware of being in it.
3. **C′.** A state is conscious when its subject has a suitable higher-order representation of it.

Objections: the [empty higher-order state](#empty-higher-order-state) (a higher-order state with no target; Rosenthal and Lycan accept a conscious state with nothing to be conscious, Gennaro denies it) and the [animal and infant objection](#animal-and-infant-objection) (higher-order thought needs concepts many animals and infants lack; Carruthers accepts the conclusion, Gennaro allows simpler higher-order states).

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md)

### Argument: Global workspace as best explanation

1. **P1.** Seen and unseen trials share early sensory processing and differ chiefly in a late, widespread, all-or-none frontoparietal event ([ignition](#ignition)).
2. **P2.** That event is what recruitment into a global broadcast system would look like.
3. **P3.** What conscious contents can do and unconscious ones cannot (be reported, reasoned with, remembered, used flexibly) is what global availability provides.
4. **P4.** Whatever marks the difference between conscious and unconscious states and explains what conscious states do is what consciousness consists in.
5. **C.** A state is conscious just when its content is broadcast in the global workspace.

*Weakest:* P4. Objection (Block): the data mark reported against unreported states, so C earns at most a theory of access. Reply (Cohen and Dennett): consciousness with no functional footprint is beyond any test. The Cogitate collaboration's missing offset ignition presses P1 ([adversarial collaboration](#adversarial-collaboration)). **Overflow** (Block, 2007): Sperling's subjects report three or four letters but can report any cued row, so experience exceeds access; reply (Cohen and Dennett, 2011): no experiment detects experience except through its functions ([overflow debate](#overflow-debate)).

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Argument: The free-will reading of Libet

1. The readiness potential's onset precedes the reported moment of the urge.
2. So the brain initiates the act before the conscious decision.
3. So conscious will does not initiate it.

It needs further premises: that the reported time is accurate, and that the potential is a decision signal; Aaron Schurger and co-authors (2012) model it instead as noise drifting to a threshold, which averaging over trials turns into a slow rise. Free will itself is [`metaphysics` 6.1](../metaphysics/lessons/06-01-determinism-and-the-consequence-argument.md)'s. See [Libet experiments](#libet-experiments).

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md)

### Argument: The case for illusionism

1. **P1.** Introspection represents our experiences as having phenomenal properties: intrinsic, private, ineffable qualities, known directly.
2. **P2.** If such properties existed, no physical or functional account could explain them.
3. **P3.** A physical, functional explanation of why introspection represents our states this way is available, at least in principle.
4. **P4.** When a seeming can be explained without supposing that what seems so is so, and what seems so would be anomalous, the better-supported view is that the seeming is an illusion.
5. **C.** Experiences have no phenomenal properties, only [quasi-phenomenal](#quasi-phenomenal-properties) ones that introspection misrepresents.

*Weakest:* P3. Objection: no explanation exists yet, and a self-model alone cannot be the source, since we model our beliefs too and find no hard problem of belief (Chalmers, 2018). Reply: P3 needs only an explanation in principle, and candidates exist (Pereboom, 2011; Graziano). **[Debunking argument](#debunking-argument)** (Chalmers, 2018, §5): there is a correct explanation of our beliefs about consciousness independent of consciousness; if there is, those beliefs are not justified; so they are not. Everything turns on "independent".

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Argument: The illusion-of-what objection

1. **O1.** An illusion is a case in which something seems to be F and is not.
2. **O2.** When my experience seems to me to glow, that seeming is itself something it is like to undergo.
3. **O3.** So if the glow is an illusion, the illusion has phenomenal character, and phenomenal consciousness exists after all.

Searle gave it its sharpest form (1997). The illusionist denies O2: a seeming can be a representation, and representing Q does not require having it. Chalmers grants that strong illusionism is coherent (2018, §7), and the objection regroups as a **Moorean argument**: people feel pain; if strong illusionism is true, no one does; so it is false. The illusionist answers that the theory predicts the first premise will seem undeniable; the realist, that the datum is the pain, not its seeming obvious. O2 is also what carries Broad's 1925 argument (a theory calling mentality itself delusive refutes itself) from mentality in general to phenomenal properties. See [illusion-of-what objection](#illusion-of-what-objection).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md)

### Argument: Strawson's anti-emergence argument

1. **P1. Realism.** Experience is concretely real.
2. **P2. Physicalism.** Every real concrete phenomenon is physical.
3. **P3. No brute emergence.** If Y emerges from X, there is something about X, and X alone, in virtue of which Y emerges and which suffices for it.
4. **P4. Homogeneity.** Intelligible emergence runs within one family of concepts, as liquidity from molecules sliding past one another.
5. **P5. Heterogeneity.** No family of concepts spans both the experiential and the wholly non-experiential.
6. **C.** Experience does not emerge from the wholly non-experiential, so the ultimate constituents of the physical are themselves experiential.

Galen Strawson (2006). *Weakest:* P3. Objection: every theory bottoms out in brute laws, so a fundamental psychophysical law is no worse (Broad's emergentism, 1925; Chalmers's naturalistic dualism, 1996). Reply: such a law is not something about X in virtue of which Y emerges but a new fundamental fact about experience, so whoever posits it has given up P2's monism. Strawson's side concedes that the argument binds only physicalists who refuse brute psychophysical laws. Thomas Nagel's 1979 argument runs on material composition, realism, no radical emergence and non-reductionism. See [anti-emergence argument](#anti-emergence-argument).

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Argument: The intrinsic nature argument

1. **P1.** Everything physics says about negative charge is relational or dispositional.
2. **P2.** A disposition is had in virtue of something that is not merely a further disposition.
3. **P3.** So charge has a categorical nature physics does not state, its [quiddity](#quiddity).
4. **P4.** Assuming dualism false, the one categorical nature anyone knows without inference is the character of their own experience, the nature of some events in their brain.
5. **P5.** Absent a reason to think otherwise, the simplest hypothesis is that the rest of matter has a nature of the same general kind (Goff, 2017).
6. **C.** The quiddity of charge is experiential or proto-experiential.

[Russellian monism](#russellian-monism) is the package: R1 [structuralism about physics](#structuralism-about-physics), R2 realism about quiddities, R3 quiddities are or ground consciousness. The Russellian physicalist's reply to zombies and Mary: they copy only structure, while the full physical truth includes the quiddities. See [intrinsic nature argument](#intrinsic-nature-argument).

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

### Argument: Against combining subjects

- **[Subject-summing](#subject-summing-problem) (Goff):** a conscious subject with no further subjects is conceivable; so is a micro-experiential zombie, micro-subjects with no macro-subject; conceivability entails possibility; so micro-facts do not necessitate macro-subjects, and constitutive micropsychism is false.
- **Coleman's exclusion of viewpoints:** a viewpoint consists partly in excluding other viewpoints, so subjects cannot sum.

Both press the [combination problem](#combination-problem), which James pressed against "mind-stuff" (1890, ch. VI; read in Boss 3).

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md)

**Module 4: Intentionality and perception**

### Argument: Brentano's thesis and the language of thought

- **[Brentano's thesis](#brentanos-thesis):** B1 every mental state is intentional; B2 only mental states are originally intentional; anything else that is about something borrows its aboutness from a mind.
- **Systematicity** (Fodor and Pylyshyn, 1988, as the SEP reconstructs it): S1 thought is [systematic](#systematicity) by psychological law; S2 a theory of cognition must explain this as lawful, not lucky; S3 only representations built from reusable constituents, processed by structure-sensitive operations, explain it; C a connectionist network either implements a [language of thought](#language-of-thought) or fails S2.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Argument: Churchland's eliminativism and Dennett's stance

1. **E1.** Folk psychology is an empirical theory, whose generalizations link beliefs, desires, perceptions and actions as laws link posits.
2. **E2.** A theory that leaves much of its domain unexplained, has not developed in millennia and fits none of the sciences around it is probably false, deeply enough to be replaced.
3. **E3.** Folk psychology is such a theory.
4. **C.** Beliefs and desires probably do not exist.

Churchland (1981). *Weakest:* E1. Simulation theorists (Gordon, Heal, Goldman) deny we predict others with a theory at all ([theory-theory and simulation](#theory-theory-and-simulation)). Reply (SEP; Ramsey, Stich and Garon, 1990): elimination needs only that our concept of belief sets conditions nothing meets. **Dennett's [intentional stance](#intentional-stance):** D1 treat a system as a rational agent, credit it with the beliefs and desires it ought to have, and predict it will act on them; D2 a system has beliefs just in case this strategy predicts its behaviour reliably and in detail, whatever is inside it.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md)

### Argument: The disjunction problem

1. **P1.** A state type $R$ represents that $C$ just when $R$ indicates $C$: given the channel conditions, $\Pr(C \mid R) = 1$ ([indication](#formal-indication)).
2. **P2.** HORSE, the inner symbol, is tokened by horses and also by cows glimpsed on a dark night.
3. **P3.** If cows on dark nights cause HORSE within the channel conditions, then given HORSE the probability of a horse is below 1, and of a horse-or-cow-on-a-dark-night it is 1.
4. **C1.** HORSE represents *horse or cow-on-a-dark-night*, and the cow-caused token is true.
5. **P4.** A state none of whose tokens can be false is not a belief or a perception.
6. **C2.** Information alone does not give mental content.

Fodor (1984). The repairs: a learning period (Dretske, 1981; Fodor: learning has no principled end); [asymmetric dependence](#asymmetric-dependence) (A1 it is a law that horses cause HORSE; A2 whatever else causes HORSE does so only because horses do, not conversely; A3 the dependence holds among the laws now, not in their history); teleosemantics (B1 a representation sits between a [producer and consumers](#producer-and-consumer); B2 its content is the condition that must hold for the consumers to perform their proper functions in the Normal way). *Weakest:* what every repair assumes, that its ingredient can be stated without already using meaning. Reply: selection-for is causal, settled by which property explains the success.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md)

### Argument: Swampman and indeterminacy against teleosemantics

- **[Swampman](#swampman):** Swampman lacks a selection history; teleosemantic content requires one; so he has no content; but intuitively he believes; so teleosemantics fails. Replies: a theory of the real nature of content, not of the concept (Millikan; Neander, 1996); a claim restricted to the actual world (Papineau).
- **[Functional indeterminacy](#functional-indeterminacy)** (Fodor, 1990): selection cannot tell apart properties that were coextensive in the selection environment, so teleosemantic content is indeterminate. Reply: only one property explains the success (Millikan, 1991; Neander, 1995).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Argument: Twin Earth on belief

1. **P1.** In 1750, Oscar on Earth and Twin Oscar on [Twin Earth](#twin-earth) are intrinsic duplicates; the watery stuff is H2O on Earth and XYZ on Twin Earth.
2. **P2.** "Water" refers to whatever shares the hidden nature of the samples the speaker's community calls water, so Oscar's word picks out H2O and Twin Oscar's XYZ.
3. **P3.** The that-clause that reports a belief gives its content, and beliefs with different truth conditions are different beliefs.
4. **C.** Oscar believes that water is wet and Twin Oscar does not, so intrinsic states do not fix what one believes.

*Weakest:* P3, the step from words to thoughts, which Putnam did not endorse. Objection (Fodor, 1987): content explains behaviour, causal powers are intrinsic, the twins act alike, so psychology needs a shared, narrow content. Reply (Burge, 1986, 1989): working psychology such as Marr's theory of vision individuates states by their normal environment. **Burge's [arthritis case](#arthritis-case)** (1979): a deferring patient's false belief that he has arthritis in his thigh becomes, in an intrinsic duplicate placed in a community whose "arthritis" covers thighs, a true belief with a different content; so content depends on the social environment.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Argument: Narrow content from phenomenology

1. **H1.** There is pervasive intentional content that depends constitutively on phenomenal character alone.
2. **H2.** Phenomenal character depends only on narrow factors.
3. **H3.** There is pervasive narrow content.

Horgan and Tienson (2002). Phenomenal externalists deny H2; an externalist who grants phenomenal sameness must deny H1. See [phenomenal intentionality](#phenomenal-intentionality).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Argument: The extended mind

1. **E1.** Before recalling it, Inga already believed the museum was on 53rd Street, as a standing belief in memory.
2. **E2.** Otto's notebook entry plays the same role for Otto, provided the notebook is a constant in his life, its contents are directly available, he endorses what he reads automatically, and he consciously endorsed it when he wrote it.
3. **E3.** If a part of the world functions in a task as a process we would count as cognitive were it done in the head, it is part of the cognitive process (the [parity principle](#parity-principle)).
4. **C2.** Otto believed the museum was on 53rd Street before he looked, and the notebook is part of the belief's vehicle.

Clark and Chalmers (1998). A thesis about vehicles, not contents ([extended mind](#extended-mind)); the [coupling-constitution objection](#coupling-constitution-objection) presses E3.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md)

### Argument: The argument from illusion

1. **P1.** In an illusion it seems to S that something has a sensible quality F that the perceived object lacks.
2. **P2 ([Phenomenal Principle](#phenomenal-principle)).** If it sensibly seems to S that something is F, then S is directly presented with something that is F.
3. **P3.** So in an illusion S is not directly presented with the perceived object.
4. **P4.** The same account of experience applies to veridical and illusory experiences.
5. **P5.** If S is not directly presented with ordinary objects in veridical experience, S never is.
6. **C.** We are never directly presented with ordinary objects.

*Weakest:* P2. Objection (Barnes, 1944-45; Anscombe, 1965): "looks yellow" no more entails a yellow thing than "looking for a unicorn" entails a unicorn. Reply: P2 is a claim about experience, backed by explanation (Broad's penny, 1923). P4 is the spreading step (Ayer's continuous series).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Argument: The argument from hallucination

1. **H1.** For any veridical experience of an F, a hallucination indistinguishable from it by introspection is possible.
2. **H2.** In such a hallucination, S is directly presented with no ordinary object.
3. **H3 ([common-kind assumption](#common-kind-assumption)).** A veridical experience and its indistinguishable hallucination are fundamentally the same kind of experience, differing only in their causes.
4. **H4.** So veridical experience is not, by its nature, the direct presentation of an ordinary object.
5. With P5 of the argument from illusion: **C** again.

What each theory of perception accepts and denies is set out under [sense-datum theory](#sense-datum-theory), [adverbialism](#adverbialism), [intentionalism](#intentionalism) and [disjunctivism](#disjunctivism); Hume's table argument and its suppressed premises are Boss 4's.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Argument: The time-lag argument

1. **T1.** What you see now depends on light that left the star long ago, and the star may be gone.
2. **T2.** What is directly presented exists, as presented, when the experience occurs.
3. **T3.** So the star is not what you are directly presented with.
4. **T4.** Every perception takes time ([light-time](#formal-light-time)), plus the brain's delay.
5. **C.** Nothing you directly perceive is an ordinary object as it now is.

Broad raised the case (1925, ch. IV); Robinson pressed it for sense-data (1994). See [time-lag argument](#time-lag-argument).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md)

### Argument: Intentionalism and its rivals

- **Intentionalism:** I1 every perceptual experience represents the world as being some way, and is accurate just if the world is that way; I2 a veridical experience, an illusion and a matching hallucination can share their content; I3 (strong intentionalism) no two experiences differ in what they are like without differing in content.
- **Campbell's argument from demonstrative reference** (2002): experience explains our knowledge of what a demonstrative refers to; representation either presupposes that knowledge (if conceptual) or cannot supply it (if not); so only the [relational view](#relational-view) fits.
- **Martin's argument from introspection:** experience seems relational; naive realism makes only hallucinations misleading about their own nature, its rivals make every experience so; so it is the least revisionary.
- **McDowell:** an appearance is either a mere appearance or a fact made manifest, which blocks the inference from indistinguishability to a highest common factor.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

### Argument: The causal argument and screening off

**The [causal argument](#causal-argument-against-disjunctivism)** (Robinson, 1985): a causally matching hallucination is possible; the same proximal cause yields the same immediate effect; so the perception and the hallucination are of a common kind; so naive realism fails. *Reply* (Martin, 2004): the second premise fails for events with non-causal constitutive conditions.

**The [screening-off argument](#screening-off-argument)** (Martin, 2004, 2006: the challenge the naive realist must meet once the causal argument is answered):

1. **P1.** Let $h$ be a [causally matching hallucination](#causally-matching-hallucination) of a red post box, and $H$ the fundamental kind of event it is.
2. **P2.** An event of kind $H$ needs nothing beyond the brain state.
3. **P3.** The same proximal cause, in circumstances lacking no non-causal condition an effect of kind $K$ needs, produces an effect of kind $K$.
4. **C1.** So the veridical perception is of kind $H$ too.
5. **P4.** Being of kind $H$ explains why $h$ is, for its subject, like seeing a red post box.
6. **C2.** That explanation is available in the veridical case too, so the naive realist's relation to the post box explains nothing about what seeing is like: it is screened off.
