*Weakest:* P4, on Martin's [negative epistemic conception](#negative-epistemic-conception): $H$ has no positive nature beyond being introspectively indiscriminable from a veridical perception. Objection (Siegel, 2004): a dog cannot judge indiscriminability, yet hallucinates. Counter-reply (Martin, 2006): indiscriminability is impersonal.

*Lessons:* [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md)

**Module 5: Soul as form: the hylomorphic view**

### Argument: One soul, the form of the body

ST I q.76 a.1 and a.3, as 5.1 reconstructs them:

1. **P1.** That by which a thing primarily acts is a form of it, since "nothing acts except so far as it is in act".
2. **P2.** The soul is that by which a living body primarily nourishes, senses, moves and understands.
3. **C1.** The principle by which a human being understands is the form of the body.
4. **P3.** A thing is one without qualification only by one form, since it has existence and unity from the same source.
5. **P4.** A human being is one thing without qualification.
6. **C2.** The vegetative, sensitive and rational principles in a human being are numerically one soul.

See [soul as form](#soul-as-form) and [unity of substantial form](#unity-of-substantial-form). The text is [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 3.5](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/03-05-soul-and-intellect.md)'s for Aristotle; the general theory is [`metaphysics` 2.3](../metaphysics/lessons/02-03-hylomorphism-and-composition.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Argument: No interaction problem

1. **P5.** An interaction problem arises when two things, each complete in its kind, must be causally connected by some contact or relation.
2. **P6.** Soul and body are not two complete things: the matter is this body only as informed, and the form is this matter's actuality.
3. **C3.** There is no question how the soul grips the body.

*Weakest:* P5. It fits Elisabeth's objection and the pairing problem, not closure: if every physical event that has a cause has a sufficient physical cause, a form that changes what the body does either competes with physics or does nothing. *Reply:* a formal cause is no rival for the same effect. Aquinas adds that the soul also moves the body, by its motive power, not by its essence as form (q.76 a.4 ad 2), so the formal-cause reply covers half of his view ([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Argument: The functionalism-renamed charge

1. **F1.** Aquinas individuates powers by their acts and objects (q.77 a.3).
2. **F2.** A functional state is individuated by its causal role ([role and realizer](#role-and-realizer)).
3. **F3.** Every power can be written as such a role, realized in organs.
4. **C.** "Form" names functional organization, with nothing added but the word.

The reply has three moves: **R1, matter** (a form makes its matter the matter it is, where a realizer's nature is fixed apart from the role; [Ackrill's problem](#ackrills-problem) becomes a thesis); **R2, subject** (one substance whose powers these are; the [China brain](#china-brain) is no substance); **R3, intellect**, Thomists only (understanding uses no organ, so F3 fails for it). Seager presses that the difference may be verbal; the hylomorphist answers that necessitation is not explanation. See [functionalism renamed](#functionalism-renamed-charge).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md)

### Argument: The scope argument, in outline

ST I q.75 a.2, *respondeo*: by the intellect a human can know the natures of all bodies; what can know things of a kind cannot have any of them in its own nature; every body, and every bodily organ, has a determinate nature; so the intellectual principle is not a body and understands through no organ, so it has an operation *per se*, and since only what subsists operates *per se*, it is incorporeal and [subsistent](#subsistent-intellect). 5.2 states it in numbered premises. Reconstructing the *respondeo* and saying what the sick man's tongue and the coloured glass vase each support is Boss 5(a)'s work, and the functionalist's crux with it is 5(b)'s. See [argument from the scope of intellect](#argument-from-the-scope-of-intellect).

**The [argument from universals](#argument-from-universals)** (q.75 a.5): whatever is received is received according to the condition of the recipient; the intellect knows a nature absolutely, without what makes it this one; reception in matter individualizes; so the intellect is not material. It works only if reception in matter individualizes the *content* received, not merely the receiving state. See also [abstraction](#abstraction).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Argument: Ross's determinacy argument

1. **R1.** Some thinking, such as adding, is determinate: a single act of adding has the form of addition and of no rival function.
2. **R2.** No physical process, and no function among physical states, is determinate in that way: every physical record, counterfactuals included, fits incompossible functions ([plus and quus](#formal-plus-and-quus)).
3. **R3.** Such thinking is not wholly physical.

James Ross, "Immaterial Aspects of Thought" (1992). He accepts as consequences, not objections, that machines do not really add and that the brain is not the organ of thought. Replies, as 5.2 gives them: [vehicle and content](#vehicle-and-content) (a finite vehicle can carry content of unlimited scope); [proves too much](#proves-too-much-objection) (Kripke's sceptic spares no fact, mental included, so the argument undoes an immaterial intellect too; that crux is set in [`apologetics-foundations` 3.2](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/03-02-reason.md)); dispositions, met by Kripke's [finitude and error problems](#finitude-and-error-problems); and Lewis's [naturalness reply](#naturalness-reply) (quus is gerrymandered, so the best interpretation assigns plus). A different proves-too-much charge, about every human adder, is Boss 5(d)'s.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md)

### Argument: Push or idle, and the hylomorphist's reply

The dilemma (K):

1. **K1.** If the soul makes a difference to what a body does, it is either one more sufficient cause of the bodily event or no cause of it.
2. **K2.** If one more sufficient cause, closure fails or every action is overdetermined (2.4's P3-P5).
3. **K3.** If no cause, the event is fixed without it.
4. **K.** The soul breaks closure, overdetermines every action, or idles.

The reply (H):

1. **H1.** "Cause" answers several questions: what brought this about (efficient), what it is (formal), what it is made of, what it is for ([formal and efficient causation](#formal-and-efficient-causation)).
2. **H2.** A formal cause makes a difference without pushing, by making an event the event it is ([structure as difference-maker](#structure-as-difference-maker)).
3. **H3.** Exclusion (2.4's P4) bars two sufficient causes of one effect of the same kind; a formal and an efficient cause do not compete.
4. **C.** K1 is false: as form, the soul is neither a second push nor idle.

*Weakest:* H3. Objection (Kim's spirit; Seager on Jaworski): a cause of another kind either changes which physical events occur, and competes, or only how we describe them, and is explained away. Replies: the sufficient physical cause is not what it is apart from the organism (Simpson); explanatory practice is the evidence about causes (Jaworski; Burge). Because Aquinas's soul also moves the body ([command and commanded act](#command-and-commanded-act)), his text allows closure kept or denied.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

### Argument: Dependence on the brain

1. **B1.** Brain damage can impair or abolish every intellectual operation we can test.
2. **B2.** A power that uses no organ would not be impaired by damage to an organ.
3. **B3.** Intellect uses an organ, and 5.2's conclusion fails.

Aquinas raised the worry against himself (q.75 a.2 obj. 3) and denies B2: the body is necessary for the intellect's action "on the part of the object", not as the origin of the action (ad 3); in this life the intellect understands only by turning to phantasms, so a lesion of the organ of imagination or memory hinders understanding (q.84 a.7; [dependence of intellect on imagination](#dependence-of-intellect-on-imagination)). How much the lesion evidence counts is Boss 5(e)'s question. **Emergent dualism and hylomorphism** differ over the direction of dependence (the brain generates Hasker's self; the form makes the matter a brain), the count of individuals, the origin of the human soul (created, for Aquinas), and, for some hylomorphists, closure.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md)

**Module 6: Machine minds**

### Argument: Turing's parity argument

1. **T1.** Our grounds for holding that other people think are, in the end, what they say and do.
2. **T2.** A machine that cannot be told from a person under sustained questioning by someone trying to unmask it supplies grounds of the same kind and strength.
3. **T3.** Without a reason that would not also count against people, we should say it thinks.

*Weakest:* T2's "same kind and strength". Objection (Block): the same behaviour can come from look-up or from thought. Reply (the SEP entry): no Blockhead can exist in our world, so passing long, expert questioning is good evidence of thought, though not its definition. The Turing side concedes the evidence is defeasible; the psychologist, that for other people behaviour is all we have. See [imitation game](#imitation-game), [Lovelace's objection](#lovelaces-objection), [argument from consciousness](#argument-from-consciousness).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Argument: Computationalism

1. **C1.** Thinking is computation: rule-governed operations on representations, sensitive to their form.
2. **C2.** Any such computation can be run by a universal machine given time and memory ([Church-Turing thesis](#church-turing-thesis) plus universality).
3. **C3.** A suitably programmed digital computer could think.

See [computational theory of mind](#computational-theory-of-mind).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Argument: Lucas-Penrose and the consistency reply

1. **L1.** Suppose a machine $M$ models my mind: what I can establish in arithmetic is what a consistent formal system $T_M$ proves.
2. **L2.** By F1, $T_M \nvdash G_M$ ([incompleteness](#formal-incompleteness-as-61-uses-it)).
3. **L3.** I can see that $G_M$ is true.
4. **L4.** I outrun $M$, so $M$ does not model me; and $M$ was arbitrary.

**[Consistency reply](#consistency-reply)** (Rogers; Putnam, 1960): what I can see is F2's conditional, which $T_M$ also proves; detaching $G_M$ needs $\operatorname{Con}(T_M)$, which by F3 I cannot prove if L1 is true. So L3 either asserts the shared conditional or assumes self-knowledge mechanism denies. Lucas: our inconsistencies are mistakes we withdraw, and (1976) we must assume our consistency to think at all. Benacerraf (1967): at most, no formal system captures our arithmetic or we could not recognize one that does. See [Lucas-Penrose argument](#lucas-penrose-argument).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md)

### Argument: Blockhead

1. **B1.** If passing the test were logically sufficient for intelligence, anything that could pass would be intelligent.
2. **B2.** A machine could pass that stores, for a test of fixed length, a sensible reply to every possible sequence of the judge's messages, and looks each one up.
3. **B3.** That machine is not intelligent.
4. **B4.** Passing is not logically sufficient: whether behaviour is intelligent depends on what produces it ([psychologism](#psychologism)).

Ned Block (1981). The table's size is at [Blockhead's count](#formal-blockheads-count). It targets only the definition reading of the test, not its use as evidence.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Argument: The Chinese room, in outline

Searle (1980), as 6.2 reconstructs it from the room: if strong AI is true, some program for Chinese is such that anything running it understands Chinese; a man who knows no Chinese could run any such program by hand; running it, he would not understand; so strong AI is false. The 1984 form (*Minds, Brains and Science*): programs are syntactic; minds have semantics; syntax by itself neither constitutes nor suffices for semantics ([syntax is not semantics](#syntax-is-not-semantics)); so programs by themselves neither constitute nor suffice for minds. The room is the evidence for the third claim.

Searle's answers to the replies: brain simulator, water pipes arranged as neurons understand nothing, since simulation copies formal structure, not causal powers; other minds, the question is what is attributed, not how it is known; combination, we would credit it only until we learned a program drove it; many mansions, that gives up the claim that a program suffices. The systems and robot replies are at their entries; which premise or inference each denies, or whether it concedes the conclusion, is Boss 6(b)'s work.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Argument: Observer-relativity and triviality

- **[Observer-relativity of syntax](#observer-relativity-of-syntax)** (Searle, 1990): computation is defined syntactically; syntax, he argued, is not intrinsic to physics (a voltage is a "1" only relative to someone who treats it as one); so computation is observer-relative; understanding is not; so "the brain is a computer" is not even a clear hypothesis.
- **[Triviality problem](#triviality-problem)** (Putnam, 1988): on the simple mapping account every ordinary open system implements every finite-state automaton without inputs and outputs, so "cognition is computation" says nothing. Reply: implementation needs states that cause their successors and support counterfactuals ([implementation](#formal-implementation)). Chalmers grants that every system implements some computation; Searle answers that the observer has moved, not vanished.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md)

### Argument: The form-only argument

1. **P1.** A language model's training signal is form alone: strings, unpaired with things or with what speakers meant.
2. **P2.** Meaning is a relation between form and something outside language: communicative intents, and standing meanings testable against a model of the world.
3. **P3.** A relation between form and X cannot be learned from data containing form alone.
4. **C.** A system trained only on form cannot learn meaning; the sense we find in its output is ours.

Bender and Koller (2020); "stochastic parrots" is its slogan form ([stochastic parrots](#stochastic-parrots)). The replies divide by premise in 6.3: against P1, add grounding data ([multimodal grounding](#multimodal-grounding), which Bender and Koller concede could work); against P3, teleosemantics (Mollo and Millière: text is a causal trace of the world, and preference tuning selects tracking states; [vector grounding problem](#vector-grounding-problem)); against P2's demand that meaning be learned, deference (Mandelkern and Linzen: reference can be borrowed); [inferential-role semantics](#inferential-role-semantics), on which roles alone, critics object, fix no truth conditions.

*Weakest:* P3. Objection: text projects a structured world, and prediction pressure can yield states carrying information about what produced it, as Othello-GPT does in a toy world ([world-model probing](#world-model-probing)). Reply: carrying information is Dretske's question, not Millikan's. The argument's defenders concede that form-only training can yield states that track, and are causally used for, the structure behind the data; its critics concede that tracking is reference only on a contested theory, and that a game board is fixed by its move list as no text fixes the world. The [octopus test](#octopus-test) has P1 by construction and P2 by definition, so its verdict stands or falls with P3.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md)

### Argument: Fading qualia

1. **P1.** Suppose a silicon system with your fine-grained functional organization could lack experience.
2. **P2.** Replace your neurons one by one with chips that copy each neuron's input-output behaviour: along the series, experience either vanishes at some step or fades by degrees.
3. **P3.** Sudden vanishing would be a brute discontinuity in natural law.
4. **P4.** Gradual fading yields a midpoint subject whose red has faded to pink but who, organized as before, judges it vivid; yet rational, unimpaired minds are not systematically wrong about their own experience.
5. **C.** The silicon twin has experiences just like yours: organizational invariance, a natural, not a logical, necessity.

Chalmers (1995, 1996). *Weakest:* P4's claim that the subject *judges* his faded red vivid. Searle (1992): the beliefs may be true but idle, and P4 assumes functional organization fixes belief. Chalmers: the remaining neurons do just what they did, leaving no processing to carry a new belief. Second exit: deny that P2's isomorph is possible (Godfrey-Smith, 2016). See [fading qualia](#fading-qualia), [substrate independence](#substrate-independence), [biological naturalism](#biological-naturalism).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

### Argument: Evidence for machine consciousness

- **The [gaming problem](#gaming-problem):** a system trained on human-generated data that includes the behaviours taken as markers of feeling can produce the markers without the underlying process; so the markers lose evidential force for that system, self-report above all; so turn to architecture.
- **The [indicator approach](#indicator-approach)** (Butlin, Long and co-authors, 2023): credence that a system is conscious rises with how closely its processing matches the properties a theory of consciousness names, with the evidence for that theory, and with credence in [computational functionalism](#computational-functionalism). Their tally for current language models: few indicators, low credence.
- **[Moral patienthood under uncertainty](#moral-patienthood-under-uncertainty):** stated in a clause, with acting under moral uncertainty cited to `ethics` and `decision-theory`.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md)

Alphabetical. The thesis column is the lessons' one-line version, not a verdict. Dates are of the works the lessons cite. Modern works are paraphrased here; the public-domain texts read in the Boss problems (Leibniz, Huxley, James, Hume, Aquinas, Descartes) are named, not quoted.

| Kristin Andrews and Jonathan Birch | 2023 | "What has feelings?" (*Aeon*) | the gaming problem for behavioural markers | [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md) |

Broad | 1923; 1925 | *Scientific Thought*; *The Mind and Its Place in Nature* | the elliptical penny; the mathematical archangel; conservation of energy is irrelevant to interaction; a theory calling mentality delusive refutes itself; emergent laws | [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md) |

## Pitfalls

Each is a wrong belief and its correction, one line, grouped by theme. The syllabus names the course's commonest graded error as a conflation; most of these are conflations.

### Trap: The map of views

- **"Distinctness, mental causation and closure are inconsistent by themselves."** Only with a fourth premise, no systematic overdetermination; Mills keeps the three and denies it. *([1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md))*
- **"Closure alone shuts out a non-physical cause."** As worded it asks only for a sufficient physical cause; the exclusion needs the no-overdetermination premise. *([1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md))*
- **"Property dualism is epiphenomenalism."** One is about what there is, the other about what causes what; a property dualist can deny mental causation or deny closure. *([1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md))*
- **"Nonreductive physicalists accept distinctness, since they deny identity."** They deny type identity, not token identity, and hold the mental fixed by the physical. *([1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md))*
- **"Eliminative materialism says nothing is mental."** Its target is beliefs and desires; denying phenomenal properties is illusionism, and Broad argued that calling mentality itself delusive refutes itself. *([1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md))*
- **"Every dualism posits a soul."** Property dualists posit non-physical properties of a physical subject, and Papineau's argument hits them too. *([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md))*
- **"Neural correlates favour physicalism."** Every view on the map predicts the same correlations; correlation does not entail identity. *([1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md))*
- **"Privileged access means infallibility."** First-person access is special, not error-proof (Nisbett and Wilson, 1977). *([1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md))*

### Trap: The case for dualism

- **"Substance dualism today is Descartes's."** Lowe's self has physical characteristics and a location, and Hasker's is generated by a brain. *([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md))*
- **"The unity argument is Descartes's divisibility argument restated."** It argues at P4 that no assembly of fragments is one awareness, so the Paralogism objection must be re-aimed there. *([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md))*
- **"The conservation objection is Elisabeth's."** "Energy" is a nineteenth-century concept; she asked how something unextended could push. *([1.2](lessons/01-02-substance-dualism-today.md))*
- **"The knowledge argument concludes that Mary's knowledge was incomplete."** Every physicalist grants that; it concludes there are non-physical facts. *([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md))*
- **"The ability hypothesis says Mary learns nothing."** It grants her know-how and denies only new knowing-that; Dennett is the one who says nothing new happens. *([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md))*
- **"Jackson's recantation refuted the knowledge argument."** An argument does not fail because its author leaves it; Chalmers and others still defend it. *([1.3](lessons/01-03-the-knowledge-argument.md))*
- **"A zombie is anything that behaves like us."** It duplicates every physical fact; a functional duplicate without experience is absent qualia. *([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md))*
- **"The laws of nature rule zombies out, so physicalism is safe."** Dualists agree; physicalism needs metaphysical necessity. *([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md))*
- **"Accepting zombies makes you an epiphenomenalist."** The conclusion is property dualism; a friend of zombies can be an interactionist or a Russellian monist. *([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md))*
- **"Type-A and type-B are versions of type and token identity."** The letters label stances on the epistemic gap; type identity theorists and representationalists alike are type-B. *([1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md))*

### Trap: Identity, function and supervenience

- **"Token identity is a weaker type identity."** It makes no claim about kinds; type identity entails token identity, not conversely. *([2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md))*
- **"Place and Smart offered a necessary truth."** They called the identity contingent; if both terms are rigid it must be necessary a posteriori, where Kripke presses. *([2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-zombies-conceivability-and-kripke.md))*
- **"The identity theory abolishes pain."** It says what pain is and keeps it; dropping the vocabulary is eliminativism. *([2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md))*
- **"The circularity and super-Spartan arguments refute behaviourism."** They target the logical kind, a thesis about meaning; methodological and psychological behaviourism are untouched. *([2.1](lessons/02-01-behaviourism-and-the-identity-theory.md))*
- **"The absent-qualia argument is the zombie argument."** The China brain is a functional duplicate with different physics, aimed at functionalism, not physicalism. *([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md))*
- **"Functionalism denies that pains are physical."** It denies type identity and keeps token physicalism, and is officially neutral. *([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md))*
- **"Realizer functionalism is a halfway house."** It is an identity theory with a functional recipe; only role functionalism lets the octopus share your mental property. *([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md))*
- **"Aristotle was the first functionalist."** Disputed (Nussbaum and Putnam against Burnyeat), and 5.1 asks whether hylomorphism is functionalism renamed. *([2.2](lessons/02-02-functionalism.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md))*
- **"Anomalous monism denies psychophysical regularities."** It denies only strict laws; other-things-equal generalizations stand. *([2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md))*
- **"Supervenience is dependence or reduction."** It is covariation, which a dualist with necessary connections, or an emergentist with nomological ones, accepts. *([2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md))*
- **"'No mental difference without a physical difference' is one thesis."** It is at least five; Davidson's is the weakest individual one, and physicalism needs strong global. *([2.3](lessons/02-03-anomalous-monism-and-supervenience.md))*

### Trap: Mental causation

- **"The exclusion argument says mental events cause nothing."** A token physicalist grants the decision causes the reach; the question is whether it does so in virtue of being a decision. *([2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md))*
- **"Proportionality makes the mental property and its realizer both causes."** When the effect is realization-insensitive the realizer is not a difference-maker; both count only when the effect hinges on the exact realizer. *([2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md))*
- **"The no-overdetermination premise bans overdetermination."** It bans systematic overdetermination; a firing squad is a one-off. *([2.4](lessons/02-04-mental-causation-and-the-exclusion-problem.md))*
- **"The hylomorphist denies closure."** Hylomorphists split: Jaworski rejects new forces, the SEP calls denial natural for hylomorphic dualists, and Aquinas's texts admit both readings. *([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md), [1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md))*
- **"Formal against efficient cause is mental against physical."** Aristotle's efficient causes include the man who gave advice, and a shape is a formal cause of a physical outcome. *([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md))*

### Trap: Consciousness and its theories

- **"Phenomenal and access consciousness come apart all the time."** The distinction is conceptual; whether they ever separate is disputed. *([3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md))*
- **"The hard problem says consciousness is non-physical."** It is a claim about explanation; type-B physicalists accept the gap, and illusionists explain the explanandum away. *([3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md))*
- **"The easy problems are easy."** Only in that we know what kind of answer would solve them. *([3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md))*
- **"A neural correlate or a proved identity closes the gap."** A correlation is a regularity and an identity a claim of one thing; neither shows why the state is like that. *([3.1](lessons/03-01-the-hard-problem.md))*
- **"Transparency is Moore's thesis."** Moore supplied the image and denied the strong thesis: the awareness can be told apart if we look attentively enough. *([3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md))*
- **"A higher-order thought is an act of introspection."** It is normally unconscious; introspection is the rarer third-order case. *([3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md))*
- **"An inverted subject is one to whom tomatoes look green."** That is a content difference the representationalist welcomes; the case needs the tomato to look red to both. *([3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md))*
- **"A theory of how we become aware of our states is a theory of what it is like."** That is the phenomenal/access conflation in higher-order dress. *([3.2](lessons/03-02-representationalism-and-higher-order-theories.md))*
- **"Workspace data from conscious trials show it explains experience."** The trials are sorted by report, so the data fix the basis of access; that access is experience is a thesis. *([3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md))*
- **"Cogitate refuted both theories."** It challenged preregistered predictions, its authors gave no verdict, and each core can survive a revised implementation. *([3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md))*
- **"Libet showed the brain decides before you do."** He compared a reported moment with a trial-averaged potential; the free-will reading needs further premises. *([3.3](lessons/03-03-global-workspace-and-the-science-of-consciousness.md))*

### Trap: Illusionism and panpsychism

- **"Solving the meta-problem would prove illusionism."** The meta-problem is neutral; explaining a belief explains its object away only with a further premise. *([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md))*
- **"Illusionism is 'Quining Qualia' renamed."** They share the verdict; Dennett found the concept confused, Frankish finds introspection misrepresenting. *([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md))*
- **"Evidence of introspective error supports illusionism."** It supports weak illusionism, which realists accept. *([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md))*
- **"Heterophenomenology is illusionism."** It is a method that leaves the seemings' accuracy open. *([3.4](lessons/03-04-illusionism.md))*
- **"Russell was a panpsychist."** *The Analysis of Matter* defends neutral monism; the experiential reading came later (Maxwell, Lockwood). *([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md))*
- **"Panprotopsychism is a milder panpsychism."** It denies there is anything it is like to have a protophenomenal property; the a priori entailment clause separates it from type-B physicalism. *([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md))*
- **"Panpsychism is anti-physicalist."** That depends on what "physical" means; what every panpsychist denies is that the physical is wholly non-experiential. *([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md))*
- **"The combination problem refutes panpsychism."** Its mild form is an accepted debt; only the strong form, that subjects cannot sum, claims a refutation, and it aims at constitutive views. *([3.5](lessons/03-05-panpsychism-and-russellian-monism.md))*

### Trap: Intentionality and content

- **"Intentional realism is the language-of-thought hypothesis."** Realism says beliefs exist and cause by their content; LOT adds a claim about format. *([4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md))*
- **"Dennett is an eliminativist or a behaviourist."** He counts beliefs as real patterns, tested by a rationality-based strategy's success. *([4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md))*
- **"A thermostat refutes Brentano."** His physical phenomena are sensed qualities; the thermostat tests only the modern B2, answered with derived intentionality. *([4.1](lessons/04-01-intentionality-and-folk-psychology.md))*
- **"Dretske's information is Shannon's."** Indication requires probability 1; mutual information is graded and symmetric. *([4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md))*
- **"Teleosemantics says evolution favours true beliefs."** It is a theory of what fixes content. *([4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md))*
- **"Swampman is a case against physicalism."** He is a physical duplicate; the case divides historical from synchronic physicalist theories of content. *([4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md))*
- **"Content externalism is the extended mind."** One says what fixes a belief's content, the other where its vehicle is; they are independent. *([4.2](lessons/04-02-naturalizing-content.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md))*
- **"Content externalism puts thoughts outside the head."** It concerns individuation, not location, as with sunburn. *([4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md))*
- **"Content internalism is epistemology's internalism."** That one is about justification. *([4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md))*
- **"A phenomenal-intentionality theorist must deny Oscar's thought is about H2O."** Derivativists grant the wide content and build it from the narrow; only eliminativists deny it. *([4.3](lessons/04-03-externalism-narrow-content-and-the-extended-mind.md))*

### Trap: Perception

- **"Any perceptual error is a hallucination."** An object seen wrongly is an illusion; a hallucination has no perceived object. *([4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md))*
- **"A sense-datum is by definition a mental image."** Moore's term was neutral, and sense-data are not Locke's ideas. *([4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md))*
- **"Naive realism says things are always as they look."** It is a thesis about the structure of experience, with its own accounts of illusion. *([4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md))*
- **"Intentionalists reject the common-kind assumption."** They accept it and reject the Phenomenal Principle; disjunctivists deny the common-kind assumption. *([4.4](lessons/04-04-the-problem-of-perception.md), [4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md))*
- **"Disjunctivism says the good and bad cases have nothing in common."** Both are indiscriminable from seeing; it denies that a shared kind explains their likeness, and it is not the same view as naive realism. *([4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md))*
- **"Intentionalism makes experience a belief."** The Müller-Lyer lines look unequal while you believe them equal. *([4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md))*
- **"Intentionalism is indirect realism."** It posits no inner object; what it gives up is direct presentation, not direct perception. *([4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md))*
- **"Cognitive penetration is only epistemology's problem."** It tests what experience is, and whether it happens is disputed. *([4.5](lessons/04-05-intentionalism-naive-realism-and-disjunctivism.md))*

### Trap: Soul as form

- **"Hylomorphism is substance dualism in Aristotelian dress."** A form is not a thing beside its matter, and even Aquinas's subsistent soul is a part, not the human being. *([5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md))*
- **"The soul, as form, does not move the body."** For Aquinas it is the body's form and also moves it, by its motive power (q.76 a.4 ad 2); the formal-cause reply covers half his view. *([5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md))*
- **"The composite, not the soul, is what understands for Aquinas."** Intellect and will have the soul itself as subject, sensing the composite, and the act is the man's: he understands through the soul. That composites think and forms do not is the generic hylomorphist's line, not Aquinas's. *([1.1](lessons/01-01-the-mind-body-problem.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md))*
- **"The hylomorphist's body is the embodied-cognition theorist's body."** Embodied cognition says where cognition runs; the hylomorphist says what a body is. *([5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md))*
- **"Soul as form of this body rules out multiple realizability."** Powers are individuated by acts and objects, so a fly's sight and a cat's are one power. *([5.1](lessons/05-01-the-soul-as-the-form-of-the-body.md))*
- **"Aquinas and Ross mean the same by 'determinate'."** For Aquinas a body is too determinate to receive all forms; for Ross a physical process is not determinate enough. *([5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md))*
- **"Ross says addition is not computable."** It is computable; his claim is that nothing physical determinately realizes it. *([5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md))*
- **"'Then calculators never add' refutes Ross."** He asserts it, and that the brain is not the organ of thought. *([5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md))*
- **"Brain-dependence refutes an organless intellect."** Aquinas predicts it and raises it against himself (q.84 a.7). *([5.3](lessons/05-03-hylomorphism-tested.md))*

### Trap: Machine minds

- **"Turing said machines can think."** He replaced the question with the game, made predictions, and conceded he had no very convincing positive arguments. *([6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md))*
- **"The Church-Turing thesis says the mind is a Turing machine."** It is about algorithms; that thinking is one is a further premise. *([6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md))*
- **"Not computable means not physical."** Penrose holds the mind physical and non-computable; Ross holds addition computable and realized determinately by nothing physical. *([6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-is-the-intellect-a-bodily-power.md))*
- **"Blockhead shows the test is worthless."** It targets the definition reading; being physically impossible, it leaves passing as evidence. *([6.1](lessons/06-01-computationalism-and-the-turing-test.md))*
- **"The room shows no machine can think."** Searle held that brains are machines that think; the target is that running a program suffices. *([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md))*
- **"Searle concludes understanding is not physical, or not computable."** Biological naturalism makes it a biological feature of brains. *([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md))*
- **"The 1990 wall argument is the room again."** The room grants the program runs and denies that running suffices; the wall denies that running it is an intrinsic fact. *([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-chinese-room.md))*
- **"A probe that decodes X shows the model represents X."** Decodability is information; baselines and interventions move a result towards use. *([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md))*
- **"'World model' means one thing."** In reinforcement learning it is a learned dynamics model; in 6.3 it is a probed internal state. *([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md))*
- **"'It is only next-token prediction' settles it."** The objective says what training rewards, not what the network computes; a fluent answer settles nothing about grounding either. *([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md))*
- **"Grounding and conscious grasp stand or fall together."** Reference can be argued for while consciousness is bracketed. *([6.3](lessons/06-03-do-language-models-understand.md))*
- **"Fading qualia is an argument for physicalism."** Chalmers is a property dualist; substrate independence and physicalism vary independently. *([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md))*
- **"The indicators detect phenomenal consciousness directly."** Most are functions of access. *([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md))*
- **"A model's denial that it is conscious beats its claim."** Both come from training, and developers shape both. *([6.4](lessons/06-04-could-a-machine-be-conscious.md))*
